The Dashwoods were a family of substance, long rooted in the county of Sussex. Their home, Norland Park, sat proudly in the heart of their extensive lands, a residence that for generations had commanded the warm regard of their neighbors. The estate's late proprietor, a bachelor who lived to a ripe old age, had found his final years filled with the companionship of his sister, who served as his constant housekeeper until her death a decade prior. Grieving her loss, he opened his doors to his nephew, Mr. Henry Dashwood, the rightful heir, along with his wife and their three daughters. In the lively company of his nephew's family, the old gentleman's days became a tapestry of comfort. The unwavering kindness of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Dashwood, driven by genuine affection rather than mere duty, provided him with the solace age craves, while the boundless cheerfulness of the children seasoned his existence with delight.

Mr. Henry Dashwood had one son from a previous marriage, a steady and respectable youth who, upon reaching maturity, inherited a substantial fortune from his mother and further augmented it through his own marriage. Consequently, the succession to Norland held less weight for him than for his three sisters. Their mother possessed no dowry, and their father held only seven thousand pounds in his own name; the remainder of his first wife's fortune was legally secured to their brother, leaving him with only a life interest.

When the old gentleman passed, his will was read, bringing a mixture of relief and profound disappointment. He was neither unjust nor ungrateful in leaving the estate to his nephew, yet the conditions attached to the bequest stripped it of nearly half its value. Mr. Dashwood had coveted the property primarily for the sake of his wife and daughters, hoping to secure their future. However, the will stipulated that the estate pass to him and his son, and to the son's unborn child—a toddler of four years old. This arrangement left Mr. Dashwood powerless to provide for those he loved most, barring any charges against the estate or the sale of its valuable timberlands. The entire property was effectively locked away for the benefit of the child, who, during occasional visits, had charmed his uncle with the irresistible allure of early childhood: a stammering speech, a fierce desire for autonomy, cunning tricks, and endless noise. These youthful charms outweighed years of devoted care from his aunt and nieces. Though he meant no harm, he did leave each of the three girls a thousand pounds as a token of his affection.

Mr. Dashwood's initial despair was severe, yet his naturally cheerful and sanguine temperament offered hope. He reasoned that he could live many more years, managing the estate frugally to accumulate a significant sum. But fate intervened swiftly; the fortune that seemed so distant arrived only to vanish just as quickly. He survived his uncle by no more than a year. Ten thousand pounds, inclusive of the legacies, remained for his widow and daughters.

Upon learning of his father's critical condition, Mr. John Dashwood was summoned immediately. With the urgency of illness, his dying father implored him to ensure the comfort of his mother-in-law and sisters. Mr. John, lacking the deep emotional resonance of his family, was nonetheless moved by the gravity of the request and promised to use every means at his disposal to make them comfortable. This assurance eased his father's mind, granting Mr. John the quiet space to calculate the prudent limits of his generosity.

He was not a malicious soul, though perhaps somewhat cold-hearted and self-regarding; generally, he was well-respected for his propriety in daily affairs. Had he married a woman of greater warmth, he might have become more amiable himself, for he was young and fond of his wife. Yet, Mrs. John Dashwood was a sharp caricature of her husband—far narrower in her mind and more selfish in her nature.

In his private deliberations, Mr. John had initially envisioned bestowing a thousand pounds upon each of his sisters, a sum he genuinely believed he could afford. The prospect of adding four thousand pounds a year to his income, alongside the residual half of his mother's fortune, had warmed his heart and inspired a sense of capability. "Three thousand pounds," he had mused, "would be liberal and handsome! It would render them entirely secure. I can spare such a sum with little inconvenience." He had entertained this thought for days, finding no cause for regret.

However, the moment his father's funeral concluded, Mrs. John Dashwood arrived at Norland with her child and retinue, sending no prior notice to her mother-in-law. While no one could contest her right to enter the house, which now belonged to her husband, the indelicacy of her action was glaring. For a woman of Mrs. Dashwood's station, possessing only common feelings, such behavior would have been deeply offensive. Yet, in her own mind, Mrs. John Dashwood possessed a sense of honor so rigid and a generosity so romantic that any slight, whether given or received, provoked an immovable disgust. She had never been a favorite among her husband's family, but until this moment, she had lacked the opportunity to demonstrate how little regard she held for the comfort of others.

Mrs. Dashwood felt this ungracious conduct with acute pain, despising her daughter-in-law with such earnestness that she considered abandoning the house forever. Only the entreaties of her eldest daughter persuaded her to reconsider the propriety of staying, and her tender love for her three children ultimately anchored her decision to remain, avoiding a breach with her son for their sakes.

Elinor, the eldest daughter, whose counsel proved so effective, possessed a strength of understanding and coolness of judgment that qualified her, at nineteen, to guide her mother. She often countered the impulsiveness that might otherwise have led to imprudence, steering the family toward safety. Her heart was excellent; her disposition affectionate and her feelings strong, yet she knew how to govern them—a wisdom her mother had yet to acquire, and which one of her sisters had resolved never to learn.

Marianne's abilities rivaled Elinor's in many ways. She was sensible and clever, yet eager in everything; her sorrows and joys knew no moderation. Generous, amiable, and deeply interesting, she was everything but prudent. The resemblance between her and her mother was striking.

Elinor observed her sister's excessive sensibility with concern, but Mrs. Dashwood cherished and encouraged it. Together, they now indulged the violence of their grief, voluntarily renewing their sorrow, seeking out new causes for wretchedness, and resolving against ever accepting consolation. They surrendered wholly to their mourning, feeding their pain with every reflection that could deepen it. Elinor, too, was deeply afflicted, yet she retained the strength to struggle, to exert herself. She could consult with her brother, receive his wife with proper attention, and strive to rouse her mother to similar forbearance, encouraging her to endure with dignity.

Margaret, the youngest sister, was a good-humored and well-disposed girl. Having absorbed much of Marianne's romanticism without acquiring her sister's sense, she did not, at thirteen, show signs of equalling her sisters in later years.

Mrs. John Dashwood settled into her new dominion at Norland, transforming the estate into a kingdom where she reigned supreme. Her mother and her sisters-in-law were relegated to the status of distinguished guests, a title that, while polite, served as a gentle reminder of their diminished standing. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood maintained a veneer of quiet civility toward them, and her husband, Mr. John Dashwood, offered them a kindness that was as genuine as it was limited, extending to everyone except himself, his wife, and their beloved infant son. With earnestness, he urged them to view Norland as their permanent home, a sentiment that Mrs. Dashwood eagerly embraced. For her, staying amidst the echoes of her past joys was the only logical course until she could secure a residence nearby.

Mrs. Dashwood possessed a heart that was a mirror to her circumstances. In times of mirth, her spirits soared with a buoyancy that seemed to generate its own happiness; conversely, in moments of sorrow, her imagination carried her so far beyond the reach of consolation that grief itself seemed impossible to sustain.

However, the prospect of aiding her sisters weighed heavily on her conscience. The idea of stripping three thousand pounds from the inheritance of their "dear little boy" struck her as a monstrous act of cruelty. She implored her husband to reconsider. How could he justify robbing his only child of such a substantial sum? Furthermore, she argued, what claim did the Miss Dashwoods hold against him? They were connected only by half-blood, a relationship she deemed virtually non-existent. It was common knowledge that affection rarely bridged the gap between children of different marriages. Why should he ruin his own future and that of his poor little Harry by squandering his fortune on distant relatives?

"It was my father's final request," Mr. Dashwood replied solemnly.

"My father was likely light-headed at the time," Mrs. Dashwood countered sharply. "Had he been in his right mind, he would never have asked you to give away half your fortune from your own child."

"He made no specific stipulation regarding the amount, my dear Fanny," he explained gently. "He merely requested that I assist them and improve their situation to the best of my ability. Perhaps he would have left it entirely to my discretion. But since he demanded a promise, I felt bound to give it. The vow stands, and it must be kept. We must do something for them once they depart Norland."

"Very well," Mrs. Dashwood conceded, though her tone remained skeptical. "Let us do something. But let it not be three thousand pounds. Remember, once money is parted with, it returns to us no more. Your sisters will marry, and it will vanish forever. Unless..."

"Unless what?"

"Unless it could be restored to our poor little boy."

Mr. Dashwood's face fell into a grave expression. "Indeed, that would alter matters significantly. There may come a time when Harry regrets such a large sum being dispersed. Should he one day have a numerous family, this capital would prove a most convenient addition."

"To be sure, it would."

"Then perhaps it would be wiser for all concerned if we reduced the sum by half. Five hundred pounds per sister would be a prodigious windfall!"

"Oh! Beyond anything great!" Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed, feigning surprise. "What brother in his right mind would offer half so much, even if they were truly his sisters? And considering they are only half-sisters—"

"But you possess such a generous spirit!"

"I would never wish to appear mean," he replied. "On such occasions, one would rather err on the side of excess than deficiency. No one could possibly think I have done enough; even they could hardly expect more."

"They may expect anything," she retorted. "But we must look to your capacity, not theirs. Certainly, I believe I can afford to give them five hundred pounds apiece. As it stands, upon their mother's death, they will each inherit about three thousand pounds—a very comfortable fortune for any young woman."

"To be sure," she agreed. "And indeed, it seems they require no further aid. Divided among them, they will hold ten thousand pounds. Whether they marry or remain single, they will live comfortably on the interest alone."

"That is true," Mr. Dashwood admitted. "Therefore, I wonder if it would be more prudent to assist their mother while she lives, rather than providing a lump sum for her daughters. Perhaps an annuity?"

Mrs. Dashwood paused, weighing the suggestion.

"Yes," she said slowly. "It is preferable to parting with fifteen hundred pounds at once. However, if Mrs. Dashwood lives fifteen years, we shall be utterly ruined."

"Fifteen years! My dear Fanny, her life is hardly worth half that purchase."

"Certainly not; yet, people seem to live forever when an annuity is involved. She is stout, healthy, and hardly forty. An annuity is a binding contract; it recurs annually and cannot be escaped. You are unaware of the trouble you invite. I have suffered greatly under similar obligations. My mother was burdened by three annuities to old servants left by my father. Twice a year, payments had to be made, accompanied by the tedious journey to deliver them. Then came the false reports of death, only to be corrected later. My mother grew weary of it all. She felt her income was no longer her own, shackled by perpetual claims. It bred such an abhorrence in me that I would not agree to pay an annuity for all the world."

"It is certainly an unpleasant drain on one's resources," Mr. Dashwood acknowledged. "As your mother noted, one's fortune ceases to be entirely one's own. Being tethered to regular payments strips away one's independence."

"Undoubtedly. And worse still, there is no gratitude to be gained. They assume it is their due; you perform no more than is expected, and no thanks are offered. If I were you, I would act entirely at my own discretion. I would not bind myself to a yearly allowance. It may become inconvenient to spare a hundred, or even fifty pounds from our own expenses in lean years."

"You are right, my love," Mr. Dashwood conceded. "It is better to avoid an annuity. Any occasional gift will be of far greater assistance than a fixed income, for they might simply elevate their standard of living without becoming truly richer. A sporadic present of fifty pounds will prevent distress and, I believe, amply fulfill my father's wish."

"To be sure," Mrs. Dashwood said, her voice softening into persuasion. "In truth, I am convinced your father never intended for you to give them a penny. The assistance he envisioned was merely practical: finding a modest house, helping with the move, sending seasonal gifts of fish and game. I wager my life he meant nothing more. It would be strange and unreasonable to expect financial ruin. Consider how comfortably they will live on the interest of seven thousand pounds, plus the fifty pounds a year each girl receives from her share. Altogether, they will have five hundred pounds annually. What more could four women possibly need? They will live frugally—no carriages, no horses, few servants, no social engagements. Five hundred a year is ample. I cannot imagine how they would spend half of it. To suggest you give more is absurd; they will be far more capable of giving *you* something."

"Upon my word," Mr. Dashwood said, his resolve crumbling under her logic, "you are perfectly right. My father certainly meant nothing more than you describe. I now clearly understand his intent, and I shall strictly fulfill my engagement through acts of kindness and neighborly assistance as you have outlined. When your mother moves, I shall gladly help accommodate her, and perhaps offer a small gift of furniture."

"Certainly," Mrs. Dashwood replied. "But there is one final matter. When your father and mother moved to Norland, although the furniture of Stanhill was sold, all the china, plate, and linen were preserved and left to your mother. Her new home will be almost fully furnished the moment she arrives."

"A valuable legacy, indeed," Mr. Dashwood murmured. "Though some of that plate would have been a pleasant addition to our own household."

"Yes; and the breakfast china is twice as exquisite as ours. Too handsome, in my opinion, for any home they could ever afford. But so it is. Your father thought only of them. And I must add, you owe him no particular gratitude, nor strict adherence to his wishes. We both know that if he had been able, he would have left almost everything in the world to them."

The argument was irrefutable. It supplied the final decision Mr. Dashwood lacked, and he resolved that it would be not only unnecessary but indecorous to provide the widow and her children with more than the mere gestures of neighborly kindness his wife had suggested.

Mrs. Dashwood lingered at Norland for several months, not because she relished the prospect of leaving, but because her heart was still too raw to bear the journey. Every familiar corner of the estate struck a fresh chord of sorrow, and she found herself unable to summon the strength to move until her spirits began to mend. Once her mind cleared of the paralyzing grief of recent loss, she grew impatient to depart, driven by a fierce desire to secure a home near Norland. To distance herself from the place where her husband had breathed his last was unthinkable. Yet, despite her earnest inquiries, she could find no dwelling that balanced her need for comfort with the strict financial prudence of her eldest daughter, Elinor. While Mrs. Dashwood might have welcomed a larger estate, Elinor's steady judgment rejected every option as too costly for their diminished income.

Throughout this period, Mrs. Dashwood held fast to a solemn promise made by her late husband regarding a legacy for his son, John. She trusted his word implicitly, just as he had trusted himself, finding solace in the hope it offered for her daughters' future. Though she privately believed that a sum far less than seven thousand pounds would suffice for her own comfortable retirement, she rejoiced for her children's sake and for the memory of her husband's integrity. She reproached herself for having previously underestimated John's capacity for generosity, a belief now dispelled by his attentive care for his widowed mother and sisters.

However, the shadow of Mrs. John Dashwood loomed large. The contempt Mrs. Dashwood had felt for her daughter-in-law upon first meeting had deepened over half a year of shared residence. Despite the bonds of politeness and maternal duty, the two women seemed destined to clash. Yet, a fortuitous circumstance arose that made their continued proximity at Norland seem not only bearable but desirable. This was the blossoming romance between Elinor and Edward Ferrars, the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood.

Introduced shortly after his sister's arrival, Edward had become a constant presence at Norland. Some might have encouraged this match for financial gain, noting that Edward was the heir to a wealthy father, while others might have discouraged it due to the precarious nature of his fortune, which rested entirely on his mother's will. Mrs. Dashwood, however, cared for neither wealth nor caution. To her, it was sufficient that Edward possessed an amiable nature, that he loved Elinor, and that she returned his affection. She held the conviction that differences in fortune should never bar a union founded on mutual understanding and kindness.

Edward Ferrars lacked the striking features or polished manners that often captivate the eye. He was neither handsome nor naturally charismatic, possessing a diffidence that made him seem reserved to strangers. Yet, once his natural shyness melted away, he revealed a heart of genuine warmth and openness. His mind was sound and well-educated, though he lacked the ambition his mother and sister craved. They dreamed of seeing him rise to prominence, perhaps entering Parliament or associating with the elite, while Mrs. John Dashwood simply hoped to see him driving a fashionable carriage. Edward, however, possessed no interest in public life or grand displays; his contentment lay entirely in the quiet comfort of domestic life.

For weeks, Edward's quiet demeanor failed to capture Mrs. Dashwood's full attention, as she was consumed by her own sorrow. She merely noted his unobtrusive nature, appreciating that he did not intrude upon her grief with ill-timed conversation. It was Elinor who first drew her mother's deeper admiration, pointing out the stark contrast between Edward and his sister, Fanny.

"It is enough," Mrs. Dashwood declared one day, "to say that he is unlike Fanny. That implies everything amiable. I love him already."

"You will like him," Elinor replied gently, "once you know him better."

"Like him!" her mother smiled, her eyes brightening. "I feel no sentiment of approbation inferior to love."

"You may esteem him."

"I have never yet known what it was to separate esteem and love."

Determined to know him fully, Mrs. Dashwood made an effort to engage with Edward. Her own engaging manners quickly banished his reserve, allowing her to discern his true merits. Perhaps her belief in his regard for Elinor aided her perception, but she was genuinely convinced of his worth. Even his quiet manner, which initially seemed lacking in the social polish she expected, became endearing once she understood the warmth of his heart and the affectionate nature of his temper.

As soon as she perceived the signs of his love for Elinor, Mrs. Dashwood viewed their engagement as inevitable and their marriage as imminent.

"In a few months, my dear Marianne," she told her youngest daughter, "Elinor will, in all probability, be settled for life. We shall miss her dearly, but *she* will be happy."

"Oh, Mama, how shall we do without her?" Marianne cried.

"My love, it will be scarcely a separation. We shall live within a few miles of each other, meeting every day of our lives. You will gain a brother—a real, affectionate brother. I hold the highest opinion of Edward's heart." Seeing Marianne's somber expression, she added, "Do you disapprove your sister's choice?"

"Perhaps," Marianne admitted, "I may consider it with some surprise. Edward is very amiable, and I love him tenderly. But yet... he is not the kind of young man I expected. There is something wanting. His figure is not striking; it lacks the grace I should anticipate in a man who could seriously attach my sister. His eyes lack that spirit, that fire, which at once announce virtue and intelligence. Moreover, I fear he has no real taste. Music seems to hold little attraction for him, and while he admires Elinor's drawings, it is not the admiration of a connoisseur. It is evident, despite his frequent attention while she works, that he knows nothing of the matter. He admires as a lover, not as an artist. To satisfy me, those qualities must be united. I could not be happy with a man whose taste does not coincide with my own in every point. He must enter into all my feelings; the same books, the same music must charm us both. Oh, Mama, how spiritless and tame was Edward's manner when he read to us last night! I felt for my sister most severely. Yet she bore it with such composure, seeming scarcely to notice it. I could hardly keep my seat. To hear those beautiful lines, which have frequently almost driven me wild, pronounced with such impenetrable calmness, such dreadful indifference!"

"He would certainly have done more justice to simple and elegant prose," Mrs. Dashwood interjected. "I thought so at the time; but you *would* give him Cowper."

"Nay, Mama, if he is not to be animated by Cowper!—but we must allow for difference of taste. Elinor has not my feelings, and therefore she may overlook it and be happy with him. But it would have broken *my* heart, had I loved him, to hear him read with so little sensibility. Mama, the more I know of the world, the more I am convinced that I shall never see a man whom I can truly love. I require so much! He must possess all of Edward's virtues, yet his person and manners must ornament his goodness with every possible charm."

"Remember, my love, that you are not seventeen," her mother advised softly. "It is yet too early in life to despair of such happiness. Why should you be less fortunate than your mother? In one circumstance only, my Marianne, may your destiny be different from hers!"

"What a pity it is, Elinor," Marianne sighed, her voice tinged with a melancholy that seemed to settle over the room, "that Edward should have no taste for drawing."

"No taste for drawing!" Elinor replied, her tone gentle but firm, seeking to steady her sister's enthusiasm. "Why should you think so? He does not draw himself, indeed, but he possesses a profound pleasure in witnessing the works of others. I assure you, he is by no means deficient in natural taste; he simply lacks the opportunity to cultivate it. Had he been fortunate enough to learn, I believe he would have drawn with great skill. His hesitation stems not from a lack of appreciation, but from a deep distrust of his own judgment in such matters. He is rarely willing to offer an opinion on a picture, fearing he may err. Yet, he possesses an innate propriety and a simplicity of taste that invariably guide him correctly."

Marianne, acutely aware of the danger of offending her sister, fell silent. However, the quiet approval Elinor attributed to Edward was worlds away from the rapturous ecstasy Marianne believed constituted true taste. Still, she smiled inwardly at the error, honoring her sister's blind devotion to Edward, which caused her to overlook such distinctions.

"I hope, Marianne," Elinor continued, "you do not consider him deficient in general taste. Indeed, I am certain you cannot, for your behavior toward him is perfectly cordial. If you truly held such an opinion, I am sure you could never be civil to him."

Marianne hesitated, torn between the desire not to wound her sister and the impossibility of voicing her true thoughts. Finally, she managed, "Do not be offended, Elinor, if my praise of him falls short of your perception of his merits. I have not had the same opportunities to discern the finer nuances of his mind, his inclinations, and his tastes as you have. Nevertheless, I hold the highest opinion of his goodness and sense. To me, he embodies everything worthy and amiable."

"Indeed," Elinor replied with a soft smile, "his dearest friends could not be dissatisfied with such commendation. I struggle to imagine how you could express yourself more warmly."

Relieved to see her sister so easily pleased, Marianne listened as Elinor elaborated. "Regarding his sense and goodness, no one could doubt them after engaging him in unreserved conversation. The brilliance of his understanding and the strength of his principles are often obscured only by his shyness, which keeps him silent. You know him well enough to appreciate his solid worth. As for his finer qualities, peculiar circumstances have kept you less informed than I. We have spent considerable time together, while you have been wholly absorbed in my mother's company. I have observed him closely, studied his sentiments, and listened to his opinions on literature and art. In sum, I venture to say his mind is well-informed; his enjoyment of books is great; his imagination is lively; his observations are just and correct; and his taste is delicate and pure. His abilities improve with every acquaintance, just as his manners and person do. At first glance, his address is not striking, and his appearance is hardly handsome until one perceives the uncommon goodness of his eyes and the general sweetness of his countenance. Now that I know him so well, I consider him truly handsome—or at least, almost so. What say you, Marianne?"

"I shall very soon think him handsome, Elinor, if I do not already," Marianne declared with fervor. "When you tell me to love him as a brother, I shall see no imperfection in his face, just as I see none in his heart."

Elinor started at the declaration, regretting the warmth she had inadvertently shown in speaking so highly of him. She knew Edward held a very high place in her own estimation. She believed the regard was mutual, yet she required greater certainty before she could accept Marianne's conviction of their attachment as anything more than a hopeful conjecture. She understood that for Marianne and her mother, wishing was hoping, and hoping was expecting. Elinor attempted to clarify the reality of their situation.

"I do not attempt to deny," she said gently, "that I think very highly of him—that I greatly esteem, and that I like him."

At these words, Marianne erupted in indignation. "Esteem him! Like him! Cold-hearted Elinor! Oh, worse than cold-hearted! Ashamed of being otherwise! Use those words again, and I will leave the room this very moment."

Elinor could not help but laugh softly. "Excuse me," she said, "and be assured that I meant no offense. I spoke quietly of my own feelings because I wished to be precise. Believe them to be stronger than I have declared; believe them to be such as his merit, and the suspicion—and perhaps the hope—of his affection for me may warrant, without imprudence or folly. But go no farther than this. I am by no means assured of his regard. There are moments when the extent of it seems doubtful. Until his sentiments are fully known, you cannot wonder that I wish to avoid encouraging my own partiality by believing or calling it more than it is. In my heart, I feel little—scarcely any—doubt of his preference. But there are other points to consider besides his inclination. He is far from independent. What his mother truly is, we cannot know entirely, but from Fanny's occasional remarks regarding her conduct and opinions, we have never found her amiable. I am very much mistaken if Edward is not himself aware that there would be many difficulties in his path if he were to wish to marry a woman who lacked either a great fortune or high rank."

Marianne was astonished to discover how much her mother's and her own imaginations had outstripped the truth.

"And you are not engaged to him!" she exclaimed. "Yet it certainly soon will happen. But this delay offers two advantages. First, I shall not lose you so soon. Second, Edward will have greater opportunity to improve that natural taste for your favorite pursuit, which must be indispensable to your future felicity. Oh, if he were so far stimulated by your genius as to learn to draw himself, how delightful it would be!"

Elinor had given her true opinion to her sister. She could not view Marianne's partiality for Edward in such a prosperous light as her sister believed. There were times when a certain lack of spirits about him suggested something almost unpromising, even if it did not denote indifference. A doubt of her regard, should he feel it, need not cause him more than inquietude; it would not likely produce the deep dejection that frequently attended him. A more reasonable cause lay in his dependent situation, which forbade the indulgence of his affection. She knew his mother neither behaved in a way to make his current home comfortable nor offered any assurance that he could form a home for himself without strictly adhering to her views for his aggrandizement. With such knowledge, it was impossible for Elinor to feel at ease. She was far from depending on the result of his preference for her, which her mother and sister still considered certain. Nay, the longer they remained together, the more doubtful the nature of his regard appeared; and sometimes, for a few painful minutes, she feared it was nothing more than friendship.

Yet, whatever its true limits might be, it was enough, once perceived by his sister, to make her uneasy and, all too commonly, to make her uncivil. Seizing the first opportunity, she affronted her mother-in-law, speaking expressively of her son's great expectations, of Mrs. Ferrars's resolution that both her sons should marry well, and of the peril attending any young woman who attempted to "draw him in." Mrs. Dashwood could neither pretend to be unconscious nor endeavor to remain calm. She gave an answer marked by contempt and instantly left the room, resolving that, regardless of the inconvenience or expense of such a sudden removal, her beloved Elinor should not be exposed another week to such insinuations.

In this state of spirits, a letter arrived from the post, offering a proposal particularly well timed. It was an offer of a small house on very easy terms, belonging to a relation of her own, a gentleman of consequence and property in Devonshire. The letter came from the gentleman himself, written in the true spirit of friendly accommodation. He understood her need for a dwelling; though the house he offered was merely a cottage, he assured her that everything necessary would be done to make it comfortable if the situation pleased her. He earnestly pressed her, along with his daughters, to visit Barton Park, his own residence, from where she could judge whether Barton Cottage, located in the same parish, could be altered to suit her needs. His letter was written in such a friendly style that it could not fail to please his cousin, especially at a moment when she suffered under the cold and unfeeling behavior of her nearer connections. She needed no time for deliberation or inquiry. Her resolution was formed as she read. The situation of Barton, in a county so distant from Sussex as Devonshire, which had previously been a sufficient objection to outweigh every advantage, became its first recommendation. To quit the neighborhood of Norland was no longer an evil; it was an object of desire, a blessing compared to the misery of remaining her daughter-in-law's guest. To remove forever from that beloved place would be less painful than to inhabit or visit it while such a woman remained its mistress. She instantly wrote Sir John Middleton acknowledging his kindness and accepting his proposal, then hastened to show both letters to her daughters, securing their approbation before sending her answer.

Elinor had always thought it more prudent for them to settle at some distance from Norland rather than immediately among their present acquaintances. On that point, therefore, she could not oppose her mother's intention of moving to Devonshire. The house, as described by Sir John, was on such a simple scale, and the rent so uncommonly moderate, that she had no right to object on either ground. Thus, though the plan brought no charm to her fancy and represented a removal from the vicinity of Norland beyond her wishes, she made no attempt to dissuade her mother from sending a letter of acquiescence.

The moment the letter confirming her new residence was sent, Mrs. Dashwood turned to her son-in-law and daughter-in-law with a bright, relieved smile. "I have secured a home," she announced, her voice light with the joy of independence. "I shall not trouble you any longer than until my new quarters are fully prepared."

Surprise rippled through the room. Mrs. John Dashwood remained silent, her expression unreadable, while her husband offered a polite hope that she would not settle too far from Norland. With a cheerful spirit, Mrs. Dashwood replied that she was moving to Devonshire.

Edward, unable to contain his reaction, spun quickly toward her. His voice trembled with a mixture of shock and deep concern. "Devonshire! You are really going there? How far from us! And to which part?"

She explained the location: a mere four miles north of Exeter.

"It is but a cottage," she continued, her tone warm and inviting. "Yet I look forward to welcoming many friends there. A few rooms can easily be added, and if my guests find the journey worthwhile, I shall certainly make every effort to accommodate them."

She concluded with a gracious invitation for both Mr. and Mrs. John Dashwood to visit her at Barton, but to Edward, she offered a personal summons charged with even greater affection. Although her recent talk with her daughter-in-law had hardened her resolve to leave Norland as soon as possible, it had not altered her heart regarding Edward. To separate him from Elinor was never her intention; quite the opposite. By extending this pointed invitation to her brother, she wished to demonstrate to Mrs. John Dashwood exactly how little weight she attached to her disapproval of the marriage.

Mr. John Dashwood, however, expressed his sorrow repeatedly. He lamented that the distance of the new house would prevent him from assisting with the removal of her furniture. He genuinely felt a pang of conscience, for the very exertion he had promised his father to undertake was now rendered impossible by this arrangement. The furniture itself was modest in bulk but precious in sentiment: household linens, silverware, china, books, and a beautiful pianoforte belonging to Marianne. As the crates departed, Mrs. John Dashwood sighed deeply. She could not suppress the thought that it was unfair for Mrs. Dashwood, whose income was so meager compared to theirs, to retain such fine articles.

Mrs. Dashwood had leased the cottage for a year; it was already furnished, and she could take possession immediately. There was no friction in the agreement. She waited only to clear her effects from Norland and finalize her new household before departing for the west. Being naturally swift in matters that mattered to her, these tasks were completed in short order. The horses her husband had left her had been sold shortly after his death, and following the earnest advice of her eldest daughter, she agreed to sell her carriage as well. For the sake of her children's comfort, she might have kept it, but Elinor's prudent counsel prevailed. Furthermore, Elinor's wisdom dictated that their staff be reduced to three: two maids and a man, whom they quickly recruited from among those who had served them at Norland.

One of the men and one of the maids were dispatched immediately to Devonshire to prepare the cottage for their mistress's arrival. Since Lady Middleton was entirely unknown to Mrs. Dashwood, she preferred traveling directly to the cottage rather than stopping as a guest at Barton Park. She relied so completely on Sir John's description of the property that she felt no curiosity to inspect it herself until she stepped inside as its owner.

Her eagerness to leave Norland was undiminished, fueled by the visible satisfaction of her daughter-in-law at the prospect of her removal—a happiness that was only feebly masked by a cold suggestion to delay the departure. This was the moment when Mr. John Dashwood's promise to his father could finally be fulfilled with propriety. Since he had neglected to act upon it when he first arrived at the estate, their departure seemed the most suitable time to honor it. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood soon abandoned any hope of such assistance. From the general drift of his conversation, she became convinced that his aid would extend no further than providing them with maintenance for six months at Norland. He spoke so often of the rising costs of housekeeping and the endless demands on a man's purse—claims that any gentleman of consequence faced—that he seemed to stand in need of money himself rather than possessing any desire to give it away.

Within a few weeks of receiving Sir John Middleton's first letter, everything was settled in their new abode, and Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters were ready to begin their journey.

Many tears were shed during their final farewells to a place so deeply beloved. "Dear, dear Norland!" Marianne cried out as she wandered alone before the house on the last evening of their stay. "When shall I cease to regret you? When shall I learn to feel at home elsewhere? Oh, happy house, could you know the suffering I endure now viewing you from this spot, from whence I may perhaps never view you again! And you, ye well-known trees! But you will remain unchanged. No leaf will decay because we are gone, nor any branch grow still because we can no longer watch them. No; you will continue as you are, unconscious of the pleasure or pain you cause, and insensible to any change in those who once walked beneath your shade! But who will remain to enjoy you?"

The journey began under a heavy cloud of melancholy, the road stretching out with a tedious monotony that matched their spirits. Yet, as the landscape shifted and the familiar contours of Barton Valley came into view, a fresh light seemed to break through their gloom. It was a fertile, verdant expanse, rich with pasture and sheltered by woods, promising a sanctuary against their recent losses. As they wound along the lane for over a mile, the cottage finally appeared, nestled within a small, green courtyard guarded by a neat wicket gate.

Barton Cottage itself was a study in contrasts. Though compact and comfortable, it lacked the romantic charm of their memories; its roof was tiled rather thatched, the shutters unpainted, and the walls bare of climbing vines. A narrow passage cut straight through the heart of the house to the garden behind, flanked by two sitting rooms of merely sixteen feet square. Beyond lay the offices and stairs, leading up to four bedrooms and two garrets. Compared to the grandeur of Norland, it was meager, yet as they stepped inside, the tears of recollection were quickly wiped away. The servants' eager joy and the shared resolve to find happiness in one another's company lifted their hearts. The fine weather of early September sealed their approval, casting a golden glow over the new home that made its deficiencies seem forgivable.

Mrs. Dashwood surveyed the property with a practical optimism. While the house was too small for her family's accustomed scale, she found delight in the prospect of improvement. "We shall make ourselves tolerably comfortable for the present," she declared, noting that the season was too far advanced for major changes. She dreamed of expanding the parlors, converting the passage, and adding a new drawing room and chambers above, perhaps in the spring when her finances might allow. For now, however, they were wise enough to content themselves. With an income of five hundred pounds a year—a sum she had never known how to save—they busied themselves making the space feel like home. Marianne unpacked her pianoforte, filling the air with music, while Elinor pinned her sketches to the walls, transforming the empty rooms into a sanctuary of art and sound.

Their quiet preparations were interrupted the following morning by the arrival of their landlord, Sir John Middleton. He was a man of about forty, good-looking and radiating a thoroughly good-humored nature. His manners were as warm and friendly as his letter had promised, and his genuine solicitude for their comfort was evident. He pressed them so cordially to dine daily at Barton Park until they were settled that, though his persistence bordered on impertinence, they could not refuse without offense. His kindness was immediate and tangible; within an hour of his departure, a basket brimming with garden produce and fruit arrived, followed shortly by a gift of game. He even insisted on managing their correspondence and delivering his newspaper daily, ensuring they felt cared for in every detail.

Lady Middleton followed, arriving the next day with a message of polite intent. She was a striking figure, tall and elegant, with a handsome face and graceful bearing. Yet, beneath her polished exterior lay a reserve that chilled the Dashwoods. Where Sir John possessed frank warmth, she offered only the cool, measured politeness of perfect breeding, lacking the spontaneous engagement that makes conversation alive. Her visit was long enough to temper their initial admiration, revealing a woman who had little to say beyond the most conventional inquiries.

Fortunately, conversation was not entirely absent, for Sir John proved inexhaustibly chatty. To facilitate this, Lady Middleton had wisely brought her eldest son, a fine little boy of about six. This addition became the sole topic of discourse, a lifeline for the ladies in moments of silence. They admired his beauty, asked endless questions about his name and age, and listened as his mother answered for him, while the child hung shyly by her side, holding his head down to the astonishment of his mother, who marveled at his reticence despite his boisterous nature at home. On every formal visit, a child serves as a provision for talk, and in this instance, ten minutes were spent debating whether the boy resembled his father or mother, with everyone differing in their opinion and all agreeing that the resemblance was a delightful mystery. Before the afternoon ended, Sir John secured their promise to dine at the park the following day, leaving the Dashwoods with a sense of obligation and a lingering curiosity about the future they had just begun.

Barton Park sat roughly half a mile from the cottage, a grand estate that the Dashwood sisters had glimpsed only fleetingly as they wound through the valley. From the safety of their home, however, it remained hidden behind a projecting hill, a secret fortress of hospitality and elegance. The Middletons were a household in perpetual motion, filled with guests and company, driven by a singular, shared need: Sir John required the thrill of the hunt, and his lady demanded the constant adoration of her children. Their lives were narrow orbits, revolving entirely around sport and spoiling offspring, yet they managed to fill every gap with relentless social engagement. Lady Middleton took pride in the flawless arrangement of her table, finding her greatest joy in the mechanics of entertaining, while Sir John found his true happiness in the sheer volume of youth gathered within his walls. He was a benefactor to the neighborhood's juveniles, organizing outdoor feasts in summer and private balls in winter, eager to welcome any new family that might add to his collection of guests.

Thus, the arrival of the Dashwoods was received with genuine delight. To Sir John, they were perfect: young, pretty, and utterly unaffected. He possessed a heart that relished doing kindness, and settling a family of women in his cottage satisfied his dual nature as both a benevolent cousin and a sportsman who preferred his manor to remain free of cultivated tastes. When he ushered them into the drawing room, his sincerity was warm, though his concerns for their entertainment were somewhat limited. He confessed his inability to secure "smart" young men for their company, noting that only one gentleman—a particular friend—would be present. He hoped they would forgive the smallness of the party, promising that such a lack of diversion would never recur, though his efforts to find replacements that morning had been thwarted by moonlight and prior engagements. Fortunately, Lady Middleton's mother, Mrs. Jennings, had arrived just hours before. A cheerful, talkative, and rather vulgar elderly woman, she promised to liven the atmosphere with jokes about lovers and husbands, teasing the young ladies about leaving their hearts behind in Sussex.

While Marianne felt a pang of protectiveness for her sister, turning her gaze to Elinor with anxious concern, the true dynamic of the evening began to reveal itself. Colonel Brandon, Sir John's guest, stood in stark contrast to the boisterous host. He was silent, grave, and appeared to be an absolute old bachelor, though he was merely past thirty-five. While his face lacked conventional handsomeness, his countenance was sensible, and his manners were impeccably gentlemanly. Yet, there was little in the gathering that truly appealed to the Dashwoods' sensibilities. The cold insipidity of Lady Middleton was so repulsive that even the gravity of Colonel Brandon and the raucous mirth of Sir John and his mother-in-law seemed preferable by comparison. The only moment of genuine life came when her four noisy children burst in after dinner, tearing at her clothes and reducing all discourse to matters of their own making.

That evening, when Marianne was discovered to be musical, the atmosphere shifted. The piano was unlocked, and everyone prepared to be enchanted. Marianne, who sang beautifully, performed the chief songs Lady Middleton had introduced to the family upon her marriage—pieces that had likely gathered dust on the instrument since then, despite her ladyship's own past proficiency and love for music. Marianne's performance was met with loud, enthusiastic applause. Sir John cheered at the end of every song and chattered incessantly during the intervals, while Lady Middleton frequently called him to order, marveling at how anyone could be distracted from music, and demanding Marianne repeat a piece she had just finished.

Colonel Brandon, however, was the only one who listened without falling into rapture. He offered only the quiet compliment of attention, a demeanor that earned Marianne a profound respect amidst the shameless lack of taste displayed by the others. Her pleasure in music, though it did not reach the ecstatic heights required to fully sympathize with her own soul, was estimable when contrasted with the horrible insensibility of the crowd. She was reasonable enough to acknowledge that a man of fifty-three might well have outlived the acute sensitivity and exquisite powers of enjoyment that defined her own spirit, granting him the allowance that humanity demanded.

Mrs. Jennings, a widow blessed with a generous income and two daughters already happily wedded, found her sole purpose in life to be the architect of matrimonial unions for everyone else. With the fervor of a matchmaker possessed, she scoured her circle of acquaintances for eligible bachelors and beautiful maidens, her keen eye rarely missing a flutter of affection or a blush of vanity. Upon arriving at Barton, her discernment was swift and decisive: she declared without hesitation that Colonel Brandon was deeply enamored with Marianne Dashwood. Her conviction was forged early, sparked by his rapt attention while Marianne sang, and confirmed when he listened to her again during the Middletons' dinner. To Mrs. Jennings, this was a perfect pairing; he possessed wealth, and she possessed beauty. Having long admired the Colonel since his introduction by Sir John, she was eager to secure a husband for every pretty girl in her sphere.

The personal reward for Mrs. Jennings was substantial, providing her with endless ammunition for jests. At the park, she teased the Colonel; at the cottage, she mocked Marianne. While the Colonel likely found her raillery harmless, Marianne was initially baffled by it. Once the nature of the jokes became clear, she struggled to decide whether to laugh at their absurdity or condemn their cruelty, viewing them as a stingless barb at the Colonel's advanced years and his lonely status as an old bachelor.

Mrs. Dashwood, unable to conceive of a man five years younger than herself as ancient in the eyes of her daughter's youth, stepped in to defend the Colonel's reputation. "At least, Mama," Marianne insisted, "you cannot deny the sheer absurdity of the accusation, even if you don't mean it maliciously. Colonel Brandon is younger than Mrs. Jennings, yet he is old enough to be my father. If he ever possessed the fire for love, he must have long since extinguished it. It is ridiculous! When will a man be safe from such wit if age and infirmity offer no protection?"

"Infirmity!" Elinor interjected gently. "Do you truly consider Colonel Brandon infirm? I can easily imagine his age seems much greater to you than to my mother, but you cannot deceive yourself about his physical strength."

"Did you not hear him speak of rheumatism?" Marianne countered. "Is that not the common ailment of declining years?"

"My dearest child," Mrs. Dashwood laughed, "if you proceed at this rate, you shall live in constant terror of my own decay. It must seem a miracle to you that I have reached the advanced age of forty."

"Mama, you are not doing me justice," Marianne replied. "Colonel Brandon is not so old that his friends fear losing him soon. He may live another twenty years. But thirty-five has nothing to do with matrimony."

"Perhaps," Elinor reasoned, "thirty-five and seventeen should not be paired. Yet, if there were a woman single at twenty-seven, I should not find Colonel Brandon's age an obstacle to his marrying her."

"A woman of twenty-seven," Marianne mused, "can never hope to feel or inspire affection again. If her home is uncomfortable or her fortune small, she might submit to the duties of a nurse for the security of a wife. In such a case, there is nothing unsuitable. It would be a compact of convenience, satisfying the world. To me, however, it would not be a marriage at all, but a commercial exchange where each seeks benefit at the other's expense."

"It is impossible," Elinor conceded, "to convince you that a woman of twenty-seven could feel anything approaching love for a man of thirty-five. Still, I must object to condemning Colonel Brandon and his wife to a sickroom merely because he complained yesterday, on a cold, damp day, of a slight ache in his shoulder."

"But he spoke of flannel waistcoats!" Marianne protested. "To me, a flannel waistcoat is inextricably linked to aches, cramps, rheumatism, and every ailment that afflicts the aged and feeble."

"Had he been in a violent fever, you would not despise him half so much," Elinor observed. "Confess, Marianne, is there not something fascinating in the flushed cheek, hollow eye, and quick pulse of a fever?"

Shortly after Elinor left the room, Marianne turned to her mother with a new anxiety. "I have a fear regarding illness that I cannot hide from you. I am certain Edward Ferrars is unwell. We have been here nearly two weeks, and he has not come. Nothing but genuine indisposition could cause such an extraordinary delay. What else could detain him at Norland?"

"Had you expected him sooner?" Mrs. Dashwood asked. "I had none. On the contrary, if I felt any anxiety, it was recalling that he sometimes seemed reluctant to accept my invitation. Does Elinor expect him already?"

"I never mentioned it to her, but surely she must assume so."

"I doubt you are right," Mrs. Dashwood replied. "When I spoke yesterday of getting a new grate for the spare bedroom, she remarked there was no hurry, as the room would not be needed for some time."

"How strange!" Mrs. Dashwood exclaimed. "What is the meaning of this? Their behavior toward one another has been inexplicable! How cold and composed were their final farewells! How languid their conversation on the last evening! In Edward's goodbye, there was no distinction between Elinor and me; it was merely the warm wishes of an affectionate brother. Twice I left them alone during the last morning, and each time he most strangely followed me out of the room. And Elinor, when leaving Norland and Edward, did not cry as I did. Her self-command remains unbroken. When is she dejected or melancholy? When does she seek solitude or appear restless?"

Life at Barton Hall had settled into a rhythm of tolerable comfort for the Dashwood sisters. The house, its gardens, and the rolling landscape surrounding them had ceased to be strangers; they were now woven into the fabric of their daily existence. Where Norland once held charm through its grandeur, the cottage now offered a joy born of necessity and intimacy. Sir John Middleton, a man accustomed to a life of leisure and rarely seen in active pursuit, was genuinely astonished to find the Dashwoods perpetually engaged.

Despite his earnest pleas for them to mingle with the neighborhood and his frequent offers of carriage rides, Mrs. Dashwood's spirited independence proved an insurmountable barrier. She resolved that her daughters would not venture beyond walking distance, a restriction that naturally limited their circle of acquaintances to the few families residing within reach.

Yet, nature provided ample diversion. About a mile and a half from the cottage, nestled in the narrow, winding valley of Allenham, stood an ancient, dignified mansion. Its resemblance to Norland had sparked the girls' imaginations during their earliest walks, fueling a desire to know its inhabitants. Inquiry revealed, however, that the owner, a lady of impeccable character, was too frail to leave her home, rendering the estate a silent monument rather than a social destination.

The countryside itself was a treasure trove of beauty. The high downs, visible from nearly every window, beckoned the sisters to escape the damp valleys below. On a memorable morning, when a showery sky broke into patches of brilliant sunshine, Marianne and Margaret ventured forth. They sought refuge from the lingering rain of the previous two days, drawn by the promise of clear air. While Elinor and their mother remained indoors, absorbed in books and pencils despite Marianne's confident prediction of lasting fair weather, the two younger sisters ascended the downs with glee.

They revelled in the glimpses of blue sky and the invigorating gusts of the south-westerly wind, pitying their mother and sister for missing such delights.

"Is there any felicity in the world," Marianne declared, "superior to this? Margaret, let us remain here for at least two hours."

Margaret assented, and they pressed onward against the wind, laughing with delight for some twenty minutes. Suddenly, the clouds coalesced overhead, and a driving rain descended upon them. Chagrined and surprised, they were forced to turn back, as no shelter lay closer than their own cottage. Their only solace was the prospect of racing down the steep hillside leading to the garden gate.

They launched themselves forward. Marianne initially gained the lead, but a misstep sent her crashing to the ground. Margaret, unable to halt her momentum to offer aid, was swept along by the slope and reached the bottom unharmed.

Just as Marianne fell, a gentleman carrying a gun, accompanied by two pointers, was ascending the hill. Hearing the commotion, he lowered his weapon and rushed to her aid. Marianne had managed to raise herself, but her foot was twisted, leaving her barely able to stand. The gentleman, perceiving her distress and her modesty refusing assistance, lifted her into his arms without hesitation and carried her down the hill. Passing through the open garden gate, he bore her directly into the house, where Margaret had just arrived, and did not release her until she was seated comfortably in the parlour.

Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor rose in astonishment. As the stranger spoke, explaining his intrusion with frankness and grace, a mixture of wonder and secret admiration bloomed in their eyes. His appearance was strikingly handsome, and his voice and demeanor added further charm to his figure. Even had he been older, less attractive, or of common bearing, Mrs. Dashwood's gratitude would have been secured by his kindness to her child. Yet, the combination of youth, beauty, and elegance infused his actions with a magnetic quality that touched her heart deeply.

She thanked him profusely and, with her characteristic sweetness, invited him to sit. He politely declined, citing his wet and disheveled state. When Mrs. Dashwood asked for his name, he replied, "Willoughby," and added that he resided at Allenham, hoping to call the following day to inquire after Miss Dashwood's recovery. The invitation was gladly granted, and he departed into the heavy rain, leaving an indelible impression.

News of the incident spread quickly, and Willoughby's manly beauty and graceful gallantry became the subject of universal admiration. The laughter elicited by his rescue of Marianne was heightened by his striking exterior. Marianne, though her face flushed with confusion prevented her from studying him closely once inside, had witnessed enough to join the chorus of praise with her characteristic fervor. To her, his person and bearing were the embodiment of a romantic hero. The lack of formal ceremony in carrying her indoors appealed to her sense of urgency and passion. Every detail seemed perfect: his name, his residence in their beloved village, and the fact that a shooting-jacket suited him better than any other attire. Her imagination flourished, her thoughts turned pleasant, and the pain of her sprained ankle faded into insignificance.

The next morning, as soon as the weather permitted, Sir John visited. Upon hearing of the accident, he eagerly asked if he knew any gentleman named Willoughby at Allenham.

"Willoughby!" Sir John exclaimed. "Is he in the country? That is excellent news. I shall ride over tomorrow and invite him to dinner on Thursday."

"You know him, then?" Mrs. Dashwood asked.

"Know him! Of course. He visits here every year. He is as good a fellow as ever lived—a decent shot, and perhaps the boldest rider in England."

"And is that all you can say?" Marianne interjected, indignant. "What of his manners in closer acquaintance? His talents? His genius?"

Sir John looked slightly perplexed. "Upon my soul, I know little of those finer points. But he is pleasant, good-humored, and possesses the finest black pointer I have ever seen. Was he out today?"

Marianne could not satisfy him regarding the dog's color any more than he could describe the nuances of Willoughby's mind.

"But who is he?" Elinor asked. "Where does he come from? Does he have a house at Allenham?"

Sir John provided more concrete details. Mr. Willoughby owned no property in the county; he resided at Allenham Court merely to visit an elderly relative, from whom he expected to inherit. "He is certainly worth pursuing," Sir John advised, turning to Elinor. "He owns a small estate in Somersetshire as well. If I were you, I would not let him slip away, regardless of this hill-climbing business. Miss Marianne must not expect to monopolize all the eligible men. Brandon will be jealous if she is not careful."

"I do not believe," Mrs. Dashwood smiled, "that Mr. Willoughby will be inconvenienced by the attentions of my daughters. It is not a pursuit they were raised for. Men are quite safe with us, however wealthy. Nevertheless, I am glad to hear he is a respectable young man, and his acquaintance would be entirely suitable."

"He is a very good sort of fellow," Sir John repeated. "Last Christmas, at a small dance at the park, he danced from eight until four without sitting down."

"Did he indeed?" Marianne asked, her eyes sparkling. "With elegance? With spirit?"

"Yes, and he was back at eight to ride to cover."

"That is precisely what I admire," Marianne declared. "Whatever a young man's pursuits may be, his eagerness should know no moderation, leaving him no sense of fatigue."

"Aye, aye, I see how it will go," Sir John chuckled. "You will be setting your cap at him now, and never thinking of poor Brandon."

"That is an expression, Sir John," Marianne replied warmly, "which I particularly detest. I abhor every cliché used to display wit; 'setting one's cap at a man' or 'making a conquest' are the most odious of all. Their tendency is coarse and illiberal, and even if they were clever, time has long since destroyed their ingenuity."

Sir John did not fully grasp the reproof but laughed heartily nonetheless. "Well, you will make enough conquests, one way or another. Poor Brandon! He is already smitten, and he is very well worth your attention, in spite of all this tumbling and sprained ankles."

The following morning, Willoughby arrived at the cottage, a gesture of courtesy that Margaret Dashwood, with her characteristic blend of grace and imprecision, attributed to his role as Marianne's protector. Mrs. Dashwood welcomed him with a warmth that went beyond mere politeness, fueled by Sir John's glowing endorsement and her own profound gratitude. The conversation that ensued painted a picture of a household defined by mutual affection, elegance, and domestic harmony, leaving Willoughby thoroughly charmed. Yet, he required no further introduction to appreciate the personal charms of the sisters; their allure was evident from their first encounter.

Elinor possessed a delicate complexion, regular features, and a remarkably graceful figure. Marianne, however, surpassed her in striking presence. Though her form lacked the strict correctness of her sister's, her height gave her an imposing elegance, and her face was so radiant that calling her "beautiful" felt like a truthful tribute rather than an exaggeration. Her skin, though tanned, glowed with a brilliant transparency. Every feature was pleasing, her smile inviting, and her dark eyes burned with a vibrant spirit and eagerness that was impossible to behold without delight. Initially, Marianne kept her gaze lowered, embarrassed by the memory of Willoughby's recent aid. But as the initial awkwardness faded, replaced by an appreciation of his frankness and vivacity, her demeanor shifted entirely. When he confessed his passionate love for music and dancing, she bestowed upon him a look of such approval that he found himself speaking almost exclusively to her for the remainder of his visit.

It took little to ignite Marianne's conversation; mention of a favorite pastime was enough to silence her reserve. She spoke with uninhibited enthusiasm, lacking the usual shyness of youth. Quickly, they discovered a profound alignment in their tastes for dance and music, a shared judgment that deepened their connection. Encouraged, Marianne turned to literature, questioning him on his favorite authors. Her descriptions of her beloved writers were delivered with such rapturous fervor that any man of twenty-five, regardless of prior indifference, would instantly be won over. Their intellectual tastes were strikingly identical; they idolized the same books and passages. Where minor disagreements arose, they dissolved instantly under the force of Marianne's arguments and the captivating light in her eyes. Willoughby readily acquiesced to her views, catching her enthusiasm wholeheartedly. Before his visit ended, they conversed with the ease of old friends.

"Well, Marianne," Elinor remarked as Willoughby departed, "for a single morning, you have accomplished quite a feat. You have already gauged Mr. Willoughby's opinions on nearly every subject of consequence. You know his take on Cowper and Scott, are certain he appreciates their beauties correctly, and have received assurances that he holds Pope in moderate regard. But how long can such an acquaintance endure when every topic is exhausted with such extraordinary speed? Another meeting will merely reveal his thoughts on picturesque beauty and second marriages, after which you will have nothing left to inquire."

"Elinor!" Marianne exclaimed, indignant. "Is this fair? Is this just? Are my ideas so scanty? But I understand now. I have been too comfortable, too happy, too open. I have violated every convention of decorum; I have been sincere where I should have been reserved, spirited where I should have been dull and deceitful. Had I spoken only of the weather and the roads, limiting myself to one comment every ten minutes, this reproach would have been spared."

"My dear," her mother interjected gently, "do not take offense at Elinor's jest. I would scold her myself if she wished to dampen the joy of your conversation with our new friend." In an instant, Marianne's anger melted away.

On his part, Willoughby demonstrated every sign of delight in their growing friendship, driven by an evident desire to deepen it. He visited daily. Initially, his inquiries about Marianne's health served as his pretext, but the increasing kindness of his reception rendered such an excuse unnecessary before her full recovery allowed him to drop it entirely. Confinement to the house had never been so pleasant for her. Willoughby was a man of considerable ability, quick imagination, and lively spirits, coupled with open and affectionate manners. He was perfectly suited to capture Marianne's heart. Beyond these qualities, he possessed a natural ardor of mind that was kindled and intensified by Marianne's own example, making him irresistible to her affection.

Their society became her supreme delight. They read, talked, and sang together; his musical talents were impressive, and he read with a sensitivity and spirit that Edward unfortunately lacked. In Mrs. Dashwood's eyes, he was flawless. Elinor, however, saw only one flaw: a tendency to speak too freely on every occasion, disregarding the sensibilities of others or the propriety of circumstances. His haste in forming opinions, his sacrifice of general politeness for the sake of undivided attention when his heart was engaged, and his easy dismissal of worldly forms revealed a lack of caution that Elinor could not approve, despite Marianne's vigorous defense.

Marianne began to realize that the desperate conviction she had held at sixteen—that no man could ever satisfy her ideal of perfection—had been rash and unjustified. Willoughby embodied everything her imagination had crafted in those unhappy moments and all subsequent bright ones. His behavior declared his desires as earnest as his abilities were strong. Even her mother, who had previously harbored no speculative hopes of marriage based on financial prospects, found herself within a week hoping and expecting a union. She secretly congratulated herself on securing two such sons-in-law, Edward and Willoughby.

Colonel Brandon's partiality for Marianne, which had been noted by his friends long ago, only now became apparent to Elinor as everyone else's attention shifted to the more fortunate rival. The mockery Brandon had endured before any affection arose vanished when his genuine feelings invited the ridicule so justly due to excessive sensibility. Reluctantly, Elinor had to accept that the sentiments Mrs. Jennings had attributed to him were truly stirred by her sister. While a similarity of disposition might have accelerated Willoughby's affection, the stark contrast in their characters did not hinder Colonel Brandon's regard. Elinor viewed this with concern; what hope could a silent man of thirty-five hold against a lively one of twenty? Unable to wish him success, she heartily wished him indifference. Yet, she liked him. Despite his gravity and reserve, she saw in him an object of interest. His manners, though serious, were mild, and his reserve seemed born of sorrow rather than natural gloom. Sir John's hints of past injuries and disappointments confirmed her belief that he was an unfortunate man, earning her respect and compassion.

Perhaps she pitied and esteemed him even more because he was slighted by Willoughby and Marianne, who, prejudiced against his lack of liveliness and youth, seemed determined to undervalue his merits.

"One day," Willoughby remarked while discussing him, "Brandon is precisely the kind of man whom everyone praises and nobody cares about; whom all are delighted to see, yet nobody remembers to speak to."

"That is exactly my view of him," Marianne agreed eagerly.

"Do not boast of it," Elinor chided. "It is unjust for both of you. He is highly esteemed by the entire family at the Park, and I never fail to make an effort to converse with him."

"That he is patronized by *you*," Willoughby replied, "is certainly to his credit. But regarding the esteem of the others, it is a reproach in itself. Who would submit to the indignity of being approved by women like Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings if they could command the indifference of anyone else?"

"But perhaps the disdain of men like yourself and Marianne makes up for the regard of Lady Middleton and her mother. If their praise is censure, your censure may be praise, for they are no less discerning than you are prejudiced and unjust."

"In defense of your *protégé*, you can even be saucy."

"My *protégé*, as you call him, is a sensible man; and sense always holds attractions for me. Yes, Marianne, even in a man between thirty and forty. He has seen much of the world, traveled abroad, read widely, and possesses a thinking mind. I have found him capable of providing ample information on various subjects, always answering my inquiries with readiness, good breeding, and good nature."

"That is to say," Marianne sneered, "he has told you that the climate in the East Indies is hot and the mosquitoes are troublesome."

"He *would* have told me so, I doubt not, had I asked, but those were points I had already learned."

"Perhaps," Willoughby countered, "his observations extended to the existence of nabobs, gold mohurs, and palanquins."

"I may venture to say that *his* observations stretch far beyond *your* candor. But why do you dislike him?"

"I do not dislike him. On the contrary, I consider him a very respectable man, one whom everyone praises and nobody notices; who has more money than he can spend, more time than he knows how to employ, and two new coats every year."

"Add to which," Marianne cried, "that he lacks genius, taste, and spirit. His understanding has no brilliance, his feelings no ardor, and his voice no expression."

"You judge his imperfections so broadly and rely so heavily on your own imagination," Elinor replied calmly, "that the commendation I can offer is comparatively cold and insipid. I can only pronounce him to be a sensible man, well-bred, well-informed, of gentle address, and, I believe, possessing an amiable heart."

"Miss Dashwood," Willoughby protested, "you are treating me unkindly now. You are attempting to disarm me with reason and convince me against my will. It will not work. You shall find me as stubborn as you are artful. I have three unanswerable reasons for disliking Colonel Brandon: he threatened me with rain when I needed sunshine; he criticized the arrangement of my curricle; and I cannot persuade him to buy my brown mare. If it satisfies you to know that I believe his character is otherwise irreproachable, I am willing to confess it. And in return for an acknowledgment that must cause me some pain, you cannot deny me the privilege of disliking him as much as ever."

Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters had scarcely anticipated, upon their arrival in Devonshire, that their days would be so swiftly consumed by social engagements. Instead of finding leisure for quiet reflection, they were besieged by invitations and visitors, leaving little room for serious pursuits. Once Marianne recovered from her illness, Sir John's long-planned schemes for amusement were set in motion. Private balls at the park became frequent, and parties on the water were organized whenever the October rains permitted. In every gathering, Willoughby was present, and the casual intimacy of these occasions served only to deepen his connection with the Dashwood family. He found ample opportunity to observe Marianne's virtues, to express his fervent admiration, and to receive, in her conduct toward him, unmistakable signs of her affection.

Elinor, unsurprised by the depth of their attachment, merely wished that it were displayed with less openness. On a few occasions, she ventured to suggest that Marianne exercise some self-restraint. However, Marianne detested concealment where no genuine disgrace existed. To her, attempting to suppress sentiments that were not inherently wrong seemed not just unnecessary, but a shameful submission of reason to mundane prejudice. Willoughby shared this view, and their behavior consistently reflected their convictions.

In Willoughby's presence, Marianne saw no one else. Every action he took was deemed correct; every word he spoke was considered witty. When evenings at the park concluded with card games, he would cheat himself and the entire party to ensure she held a winning hand. If dancing was the evening's entertainment, they were partners for half the night, and when forced apart, they stood close together, speaking to no one else. Such conduct inevitably drew laughter, yet ridicule failed to shame or provoke them.

Mrs. Dashwood embraced their feelings with such warmth that she felt no inclination to curb this excessive display. To her, it was simply the natural result of a passionate heart in a young, ardent mind.

For Marianne, this was a season of profound happiness. Her heart belonged entirely to Willoughby, and the deep affection she once held for Norland was beginning to soften, perhaps more than she had dared hope, under the charm of his company in her new home.

Elinor's happiness, however, was not so complete. Her heart was not at ease, nor was her satisfaction in these amusements pure. None of her companions could console her for what she had left behind, nor could they help her forget Norland with less regret. Neither Lady Middleton nor Mrs. Jennings could provide the conversation she craved. Although Mrs. Jennings was an incessant talker who regarded Elinor with kindness, she had already recounted her own history three or four times. Even if Elinor's memory matched her aunt's capacity for improvement, she might have learned early on the details of Mr. Jennings' final illness and his last words to his wife. Lady Middleton was preferable only in her silence, which Elinor perceived as a mere calmness of manner devoid of true sense. She treated her husband and mother with the same reserve, making intimacy neither expected nor desired. Lady Middleton had nothing new to say; her spirits remained unchanged, and her insipidity was constant. Though she did not oppose the parties arranged by her husband, provided everything was conducted with style and her two eldest children were present, she derived no greater enjoyment from them than she might have found sitting at home. Her presence added so little to the pleasure of others through conversation that they were sometimes reminded only of her by her concern for her troublesome sons.

Of all her new acquaintances, Elinor found only Colonel Brandon capable of earning her respect, inspiring her friendship, or providing genuine pleasure as a companion. Willoughby was out of the question. Her admiration and regard, even her sisterly affection, were entirely his, yet he was a lover whose attentions were wholly directed toward Marianne. A far less agreeable man might have been more universally pleasing. Unfortunately for himself, Colonel Brandon received no such encouragement to focus solely on Marianne. In conversing with Elinor, he found the greatest solace for the indifference shown to him by her sister.

Elinor's compassion for him grew as she suspected that the pain of disappointed love had already weighed heavily upon him. This suspicion arose from a remark he dropped one evening at the park while sitting together by mutual consent, as the others danced. His gaze was fixed on Marianne, and after a silence lasting several minutes, he said with a faint smile, "Your sister, I understand, does not approve of second attachments."

"No," replied Elinor, "her opinions are all romantic."

"Or rather, as I believe, she considers them impossible to exist."

"I believe she does. But how she manages this without reflecting on the character of her own father, who had himself two wives, I know not. A few years, however, will settle her opinions on the reasonable basis of common sense and observation; and then they may be more easily defined and justified than they are now by anyone but herself."

"This will probably be the case," he replied. "And yet, there is something so amiable in the prejudices of a young mind that one is sorry to see them yield to the reception of more general opinions."

"I cannot agree with you there," said Elinor. "There are inconveniences attending such feelings as Marianne's that all the charms of enthusiasm and ignorance of the world cannot atone for. Her systems have the unfortunate tendency of setting propriety at naught, and a better acquaintance with the world is what I look forward to as her greatest possible advantage."

After a brief pause, he resumed the conversation. "Does your sister make no distinction in her objections against a second attachment? Or is it equally criminal in everyone? Are those who have been disappointed in their first choice, whether due to the inconstancy of its object or the perverseness of circumstances, to remain equally indifferent for the rest of their lives?"
