"Upon my word, I am not acquainted with the minutiae of her principles. I only know that I have never yet heard her admit any instance of a second attachment being pardonable."

"This," he said, "cannot hold; but a change, a total change of sentiments—No, no, do not desire it; for when the romantic refinements of a young mind are obliged to give way, how frequently are they succeeded by opinions that are too common and too dangerous! I speak from experience. I once knew a lady who, in temper and mind, greatly resembled your sister, who thought and judged like her, but who, from an enforced change—from a series of unfortunate circumstances—" Here he stopped abruptly, appearing to realize he had said too much. By his countenance, he aroused conjectures that might not otherwise have occurred to Elinor. The lady would likely have passed unnoticed had he not convinced Miss Dashwood that what concerned her ought not to escape his lips. As it was, it required but a slight effort of imagination to connect his emotion with the tender recollection of past regard. Elinor attempted no further inquiry. But Marianne, in her place, would not have done so little. The whole story would have been quickly constructed under her active imagination, and everything established in the most melancholy order of disastrous love.

The morning sun broke gently over the Downs, illuminating the path where Elinor and Marianne walked side by side. It was during this quiet stroll that Marianne, with a brightness in her eyes that seemed to outshine the day itself, shared a revelation with her sister. Despite Elinor's long familiarity with Marianne's impulsive nature, the sheer extravagance of the news struck her with genuine surprise. Willoughby, Marianne announced with rapturous delight, had gifted her a horse—a fine animal he had bred personally on his Somersetshire estate, perfectly suited to carry a lady.

Marianne spoke of the gift without a single hesitation regarding the practicalities. She had not considered that her mother had no intention of keeping a horse, nor that accepting such a present would necessitate hiring an additional servant to ride it, purchasing a second mount for that servant, and constructing a stable to house them all. Yet, she had accepted the offer immediately and now described the prospect of daily rides across the Downs to Elinor with such fervor that she seemed to live entirely within the dream.

"He intends to send his groom to Somersetshire at once," Marianne continued, her voice rising with excitement. "And when the horse arrives, we shall ride every single day. You must share it with me, Elinor! Imagine the joy of a gallop on these very hills!"

Elinor found herself reluctant to shatter this vision of happiness with the sobering realities that inevitably followed. For a moment, she tried to dismiss the obstacles as trivial. The cost of an extra servant would be negligible; her mother would surely agree to it; any ordinary horse would suffice for the servant, who could likely find one at the park; and a simple shed would serve as a stable. However, Elinor eventually dared to voice her true concern: the impropriety of accepting such a substantial gift from a man she knew so little of, or at least, so recently.

"You are mistaken, Elinor," Marianne replied, her tone warming with defensive passion. "You suppose I know Willoughby very little. While I have not known him long, my acquaintance with him surpasses that of any other person in the world, save yourself and Mama. Intimacy is not determined by time or opportunity, but by disposition alone. Seven years might fail to bring two people close, while seven days can be enough for others. I would consider it far greater impropriety to accept a horse from my brother John, whom I have known for years yet know so little of, than from Willoughby, whose judgment I have long formed."

Realizing that opposing Marianne on such a tender subject would only bind her more tightly to her own opinion, Elinor wisely chose to tread lightly. Instead, she appealed to Marianne's deep affection for their mother, painting a picture of the inconvenience and burden such an expansion of their household would place upon their indulgent parent. Slowly, Marianne's enthusiasm cooled. She promised not to tempt her mother into imprudent kindness by mentioning the offer and vowed to decline Willoughby's gift the next time she saw him.

True to her word, when Willoughby visited the cottage later that day, Elinor heard Marianne express her sorrow in a low voice, explaining why she was forced to refuse his present. Her reasons were stated clearly enough to render further persuasion on Willoughby's part futile. His distress was evident; after voicing his regret earnestly, he added, still speaking softly, "But, Marianne, the horse remains yours, even if you cannot use it now. I shall keep it merely until you can claim it. When you leave Barton to establish your own home in a more permanent dwelling, Queen Mab shall welcome you."

These words, spoken in a hushed tone, were overheard by Miss Dashwood. In the cadence of his voice, the intimacy of his address—calling her sister by her Christian name alone, and the possessive nature of his promise—Elinor instantly perceived a connection so profound and direct that it signaled a complete understanding between them. From that moment, she ceased to doubt that they were engaged. The only surprise this realization caused was that two individuals of such frank tempers should remain hidden from their friends until discovered by accident.

The following day, Margaret provided confirmation that cast the matter in an even clearer light. Willoughby had spent the previous evening with the family, and Margaret, having been left alone in the parlour with just the two of them, had ample opportunity for observation. With a grave and important expression, she approached her eldest sister when they were finally alone.

"Oh, Elinor!" she exclaimed. "I have a secret to tell you about Marianne. I am certain she will be married to Mr. Willoughby very soon."

"You have said almost every day since they first met on High-church Down," Elinor replied dryly. "They had not known each other a week before you were convinced Marianne wore his picture around her neck; yet it turned out to be merely the miniature of our great uncle."

"But this is quite different," Margaret insisted. "I am sure they will marry soon because he possesses a lock of her hair."

"Be careful, Margaret. It may be the hair of some great uncle of his."

"But, indeed, Elinor, it is Marianne's. I am almost certain, for I saw him cut it off. Last night after tea, when you and Mama left the room, they were whispering and talking as fast as possible. He seemed to be begging something of her, and then he took her scissors and cut a long lock from her hair, which was tumbling down her back. He kissed it, folded it carefully into a piece of white paper, and placed it inside his pocket-book."

Given the specific details and the authority of her witness, Elinor could not withhold her belief, nor was she inclined to do so, for the circumstances aligned perfectly with what she had observed herself.

Yet, Margaret's sagacity was not always displayed to her sister's satisfaction. When Mrs. Jennings one evening pressed the family to name the young man who was Elinor's particular favorite—a question that had long been a source of great curiosity—Margaret responded by looking at her sister and asking, "Must I not tell, may I, Elinor?"

This response naturally drew laughter from everyone present, and Elinor attempted to join in, though the effort was painful. She was convinced that Margaret had selected a person whose name she could not bear to have become a standing joke among Mrs. Jennings' circle.

Marianne felt deeply for her younger sister but inadvertently caused more harm than good by turning a deep red and speaking angrily to Margaret. "Remember that whatever your conjectures may be, you have no right to repeat them."

"I never had any conjectures about it," Margaret retorted. "It was you who told me of it yourself."

This exchange only increased the mirth of the company, and Margaret was eagerly pressed to reveal more.

"Oh! pray, Miss Margaret, let us know all about it," urged Mrs. Jennings. "What is the gentleman's name?"

"I must not tell, ma'am. But I know very well what it is; and I know where he is too."

"Yes, yes, we can guess where he is; at his own house at Norland, I suppose. He is the curate of the parish, I dare say."

"No, *that* he is not. He is of no profession at all."

"Margaret," said Marianne with great warmth, "you know that all this is an invention of your own, and that there is no such person in existence."

"Well, then, he is lately dead, Marianne, for I am sure there was such a man once, and his name begins with an F."

At this critical moment, Elinor felt immense gratitude toward Lady Middleton for observing, "that it rained very hard." She believed the interruption stemmed less from any attention paid to her than from her ladyship's distinct dislike of the inelegant subjects of raillery that delighted her husband and mother. Nevertheless, the idea introduced by Lady Middleton was immediately pursued by Colonel Brandon, who was ever mindful of the feelings of others. Much was said on the subject of rain by both of them. Willoughby then opened the piano-forte and invited Marianne to sit, and thus, amidst the various efforts of different people to shift the topic, the conversation finally fell to the ground. Yet, Elinor did not so easily recover from the alarm into which the speculation had thrown her.

That evening, a party was organized to visit the following day a magnificent estate located about twelve miles from Barton. The property belonged to a brother-in-law of Colonel Brandon; without his influence, it could not be seen, as the proprietor, currently abroad, had issued strict orders prohibiting visits. The grounds were described as highly beautiful, and Sir John, who was particularly enthusiastic in his praise, could certainly be considered a tolerable judge, having hosted parties there at least twice every summer for the past decade. The plans included a noble piece of water where sailing would form a major part of the morning's amusement. Cold provisions were to be carried, only open carriages employed, and everything conducted in the usual style of a complete party of pleasure.

To some members of the company, however, the undertaking seemed rather bold, considering the season and the fact that it had rained every day for the last fortnight. Mrs. Dashwood, who already suffered from a cold, was persuaded by Elinor to remain at home.

The excursion to Whitwell, which Elinor had anticipated with a mixture of dread and resignation, ended not with rain and fatigue, but with a complete cancellation. Her preparations for discomfort were rendered unnecessary by a far more inconvenient reality: they never left the park at all.

By ten o'clock, the entire party gathered at the house, ready for breakfast. Despite the night's rain, the morning air was clearing, clouds dispersing to reveal patches of sun. Spirits were high, fueled by a collective determination to endure any hardship rather than miss the outing. They were eager, buoyant, and convinced that happiness was within reach.

However, the mood shifted abruptly when the letters arrived. Among them was one addressed to Colonel Brandon. As he took it, his face drained of color, and he excused himself, leaving the room instantly.

"What is the matter with Brandon?" Sir John asked, his tone laced with concern.

No one could answer.

"I hope he has received no bad news," Lady Middleton remarked. "Nothing short of extraordinary could make Colonel Brandon abandon my breakfast table so precipitously."

Five minutes later, he returned, looking pale but composed.

"No bad news, Colonel, I trust," Mrs. Jennings offered warmly as he entered.

"None at all, ma'am, thank you."

"Was it from Avignon? I pray it does not concern your sister's health."

"No, ma'am. It came from town; it is merely a letter of business."

"But how could a mere business letter discompose you so greatly?" Mrs. Jennings pressed, undeterred. "Come, Colonel, we deserve the truth."

"My dear madam," Lady Middleton interjected sharply, "consider your words."

"Perhaps," Mrs. Jennings speculated loudly, "it is to announce that your cousin Fanny is married?"

"No, indeed."

"Well, then, I know the sender. And I hope she is well."

"Who do you mean, ma'am?" Brandon asked, his cheeks flushing slightly.

"Oh! You know exactly who I mean."

"I am particularly sorry, ma'am," Brandon addressed Lady Middleton, "that I must receive this letter today. It concerns business requiring my immediate attendance in town."

"In town!" Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. "What possible business could keep you in town at this season?"

"My own loss is significant," Brandon continued, his voice steady despite the disappointment in his eyes, "in being forced to leave such a delightful party. Yet I am even more concerned because I fear my presence is essential to secure your admission to Whitwell."

The announcement struck the group like a physical blow.

"But if you simply write a note to the housekeeper, Mr. Brandon," Marianne suggested eagerly, "would that not suffice?"

He shook his head firmly.

"We must go," Sir John declared. "It shall not be postponed now that we are so close. You cannot travel to town until tomorrow, Brandon; that is settled."

"I wish it were so simple," Brandon replied. "But I am powerless to delay my journey by even a single day."

"If you would only tell us the nature of your business," Mrs. Jennings urged, "we might determine if it could wait."

"You would not be delayed six hours," Willoughby interjected smoothly, turning to Marianne, "if you postponed your journey until our return."

"I cannot afford to lose a single hour," Brandon insisted.

Elinor heard Willoughby whisper to Marianne, "Some souls simply cannot bear a party of pleasure. Brandon is one of them. He fears catching a cold, I daresay, and has invented this excuse to escape. I would wager fifty guineas that the letter was written by his own hand."

"I have no doubt of it," Marianne replied, her voice sharp.

"There is no persuading you to change your mind, Brandon, as I know you well," Sir John said. "Yet, I hope you will reconsider. Think of the two Miss Careys who traveled from Newton, the three Miss Dashwoods who walked up from the cottage, and Mr. Willoughby who rose two hours before his usual time solely to join us at Whitwell."

Brandon reiterated his sorrow at disappointing them but maintained that his departure was unavoidable.

"Well, then, when will you return?"

"We shall see you at Barton," Lady Addison added, "as soon as you can conveniently leave town. We shall postpone the Whitwell trip until your return."

"You are very kind. But the uncertainty of my return makes it impossible for me to promise anything."

"Oh! He must and shall come back," Sir John cried. "If he is not here by the end of the week, I shall go after him myself."

"Ay, do that, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings chimed in. "Perhaps you will discover the nature of his business then."

"I have no desire to pry into another man's affairs," Sir John muttered. "I suppose it is something he is ashamed of."

Just then, Brandon's horses were announced.

"You do not intend to ride to town, do you?" Sir John asked.

"No. Only to Honiton. From there, I shall take the post."

"Well, since you are resolved," Sir John said, "I wish you a safe journey. Though I truly hope you change your mind."

"I assure you, it is beyond my power."

He took his leave of the assembly.

"Shall I see you and your sisters in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?" he asked Elinor.

"I fear not at all."

"Then I must bid you farewell for a length of time I would rather avoid."

To Marianne, he offered only a polite bow and silence.

"Come, Colonel," Mrs. Jennings called out as he approached the door, "before you depart, do tell us what errand you are running."

He wished them a good morning and, accompanied by Sir John, exited the room.

Once he was gone, the polite restraint held by the group dissolved. Complaints and lamentations erupted universally, with everyone agreeing repeatedly on how provoking the disappointment was.

"I can guess what his business is," Mrs. Jennings declared triumphantly.

"Can you, ma'am?" nearly everyone asked in unison.

"Yes; it is about Miss Williams, I am certain."

"And who is Miss Williams?" Marianne queried.

"What! Do you not know Miss Williams? I am sure you must have heard of her. She is a relation of the Colonel's, my dear; a very close one. We shall not specify how close, for fear of shocking the young ladies." Lowering her voice, she leaned toward Elinor. "She is his natural daughter."

"Indeed!"

"Oh, yes; and she resembles him in her staring gaze. I dare say the Colonel will leave her his entire fortune."

When Sir John returned, he joined the chorus of regret regarding the ruined plans, though he concluded by suggesting that since they were all assembled, they must find some way to be happy. After brief deliberation, they agreed that while true happiness awaited them at Whitwell, they could achieve a tolerable composure of mind by driving about the countryside. Carriages were ordered; Willoughby's was first, and Marianne appeared happier than she had ever been as she stepped inside. He drove swiftly through the park, and they were soon out of sight. Nothing further was seen of them until their return, which occurred only after the rest of the party had come back. Both seemed delighted with their drive, speaking only in general terms that they had stayed in the lanes while the others ventured onto the downs.

It was decided that a dance would take place that evening, and everyone resolved to be exceedingly merry throughout the day. More of the Careys arrived for dinner, and the table seated nearly twenty guests, a number Sir John observed with great satisfaction. Willoughby took his customary place between the two elder Miss Dashwoods. Mrs. Jennings sat to Elinor's right, and they had not been seated long before she leaned forward, addressing Marianne in a voice loud enough for Willoughby to hear as well.

"I have found you out, despite all your tricks. I know where you spent the morning."

Marianne flushed and replied hastily, "Where, pray?"

"Did you not know," Willoughby added, "that we had been out in my curricle?"

"Yes, yes, Mr. Impudence, I know that perfectly well," Marianne retorted. "I was determined to find out *where* you had been. I hope you like your house, Miss Marianne. It is a very large one, I know; and when I come to visit, I hope you have furnished it anew, for it lacked furnishings greatly when I was there six years ago."

Marianne turned away in profound confusion. Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily. Meanwhile, Elinor realized that in her resolve to discover their whereabouts, she had actually instructed her maid to inquire from Mr. Willoughby's groom. Through this indirect method, she had learned that they had gone to Allenham, spending considerable time walking the gardens and touring the entire house.

Elinor struggled to believe this true, finding it highly improbable that Willoughby would propose entering the house, or that Marianne would consent, while Mrs. Smith resided there—a woman with whom Marianne shared no acquaintance whatsoever.

As soon as they left the dining room, Elinor questioned Marianne directly. Her surprise was immense when she discovered that every detail Mrs. Jennings had mentioned was accurate. Marianne was furious at her sister's doubt.

"Why should you imagine, Elinor, that we did not go there, or that we did not see the house? Is it not something you have often wished to do yourself?"

"Yes, Marianne, but I would not go while Mrs. Smith was there, and with no other companion than Mr. Willoughby."

"Mr. Willoughby, however, is the only person entitled to show that house," Marianne argued. "And since he arrived in an open carriage, it was impossible for anyone else to accompany us. I never spent a pleasanter morning in my life."

"I am afraid," Elinor replied gently, "that the pleasantness of an activity does not always prove its propriety."

"On the contrary," Marianne insisted, "nothing could be stronger proof. If there had been any real impropriety in what I did, I would have felt it at the time. We always know when we are acting wrongly, and with such conviction, I could have felt no pleasure."

"But, my dear Marianne, since this has already exposed you to such impertinent remarks, do you not now begin to question the discretion of your own conduct?"

"If the impertinent remarks of Mrs. Jennings are to serve as proof of impropriety, then we are all offending every moment of our lives," Marianne declared. "I value her censure no more than I would her commendation. I am not conscious of having done anything wrong in walking over Mrs. Smith's grounds or viewing her house. They will one day belong to Mr. Willoughby, and—"

"If they were one day to be your own, Marianne, you would not be justified in what you have done."

She blushed at the suggestion, yet it seemed visibly gratifying to her. After ten minutes of earnest reflection, she returned to her sister with great good humor.

"Perhaps, Elinor, it was rather ill-judged of me to go to Allenham," she admitted. "But Mr. Willoughby particularly wanted to show me the place, and it is a charming house, I assure you. There is one remarkably pretty sitting room upstairs; a nice, comfortable size for constant use. With modern furniture, it would be delightful. It is a corner room with windows on two sides. On one side, you look across the bowling green, behind the house, to a beautiful hanging wood; on the other, you have a view of the church and village, and beyond them, those fine bold hills we have admired so often. I did not see it to its full advantage, for the furniture was utterly forlorn—but if it were newly fitted up, a couple of hundred pounds, as Willoughby says, would make it one of the pleasantest summer rooms in England."

Had Elinor been able to listen without interruption from the others, she would have described every room in the house with equal delight.

The abrupt departure of Colonel Brandon from the park, coupled with his unyielding silence regarding the cause, occupied Mrs. Jennings' thoughts for several days. She was a woman of perpetual curiosity, finding it impossible to ignore the comings and goings of her acquaintances. With little respite, she speculated on the reason for his sudden exit, convinced that some terrible news must have arrived. She mentally cataloged every conceivable distress that could have befallen him, determined that he would not escape her worst fears.

"Something deeply melancholy must be the matter, I am certain," she declared. "I saw it in his face. Poor man! I fear his financial circumstances have turned sour. The Delaford estate was never worth much more than two thousand a year, and his brother left him in a state of ruin. I am convinced he was summoned for monetary reasons; surely nothing else could explain it. I wonder if it is true. I would give anything to know the truth. Perhaps it concerns Miss Williams, and indeed, I dare say it does, for he looked so conscious when I mentioned her name. Maybe she is ill in town; nothing is more likely, as I have always suspected she is somewhat delicate. I would wager my life it is about Miss Williams. It is not so probable he is currently distressed, for he is a prudent man and must have settled the estate by now. I wonder what it can be! Perhaps his sister is worse at Avignon and has called for him. His hurried departure seems exactly like that. Well, I wish him relief from all his troubles, and a good wife to boot."

Thus wondered and thus spoke Mrs. Jennings, her theories shifting with every fresh conjecture, all appearing equally plausible. Elinor, though genuinely concerned for Colonel Brandon's welfare, could not share her mother's endless amazement at his sudden leaving. In her view, the circumstance did not warrant such lasting confusion or variety of speculation. Her wonder was instead consumed by the extraordinary silence of her sister and Willoughby concerning the matter, a topic they knew to be of profound interest to everyone. As the days passed, this silence grew stranger and more incompatible with the evident affection between the young couple. Elinor could not fathom why they would not openly acknowledge to their mother and herself what their constant behavior clearly indicated had occurred.

She could easily understand that marriage might not be immediately feasible; although Willoughby was independent, there was no reason to believe him wealthy. His estate was valued by Sir John at roughly six or seven hundred pounds a year, yet he lived at an expense that income could scarcely sustain, often complaining of his poverty himself. However, this strange secrecy regarding their engagement, which concealed nothing, remained inexplicable. It was so wholly contrary to their general opinions and practices that a doubt occasionally entered Elinor's mind as to whether they were truly engaged, a hesitation sufficient to prevent her from inquiring of Marianne.

Nothing expressed greater attachment to them all than Willoughby's conduct. To Marianne, he offered every tenderness a lover's heart could muster; to the rest of the family, he displayed the affectionate attention of a devoted son and brother. The cottage seemed to be his true home; he spent more hours there than at Allenham. If no general engagement kept them at the park, his morning exercise invariably ended there, where the remainder of the day was spent beside Marianne, with his favorite pointer resting at her feet.

One evening, approximately a week after Colonel Brandon's departure, Willoughby's heart appeared more open than usual to the feelings surrounding him. When Mrs. Dashwood mentioned her intention to improve the cottage in the spring, he vigorously opposed any alteration to a place that affection had rendered perfect for him.

"What!" he exclaimed. "Improve this dear cottage! No. That I will never consent to. Not a stone must be added to its walls, not an inch to its size, if my feelings are to be respected."

"Do not be alarmed," said Miss Dashwood. "Nothing of the kind will be done; for my mother will never have the money to attempt it."

"I am heartily glad of it," he cried. "May she always be poor, if she can employ her riches no better."

"Thank you, Willoughby. But you may be assured that I would not sacrifice one sentiment of local attachment of yours, or of anyone I love, for all the improvements in the world. Depend upon it that whatever unemployed sum remains when I settle my accounts in the spring, I would rather lay it uselessly by than dispose of it in a manner so painful to you. But are you really so attached to this place that you see no defect in it?"

"I am," he replied. "To me, it is faultless. Nay, more, I consider it the only form of building in which happiness is attainable. Were I rich enough, I would instantly pull Combe down and rebuild it in the exact plan of this cottage."

"With dark, narrow stairs and a kitchen that smokes, I suppose," Elinor remarked.

"Yes," he cried in the same eager tone, "with all and everything belonging to it; in no convenience or inconvenience should the least variation be perceptible. Then, and then only, under such a roof, I might perhaps be as happy at Combe as I have been at Barton."

"I flatter myself," Elinor replied, "that even under the disadvantage of better rooms and a broader staircase, you will hereafter find your own house as faultless as you do this."

"There certainly are circumstances," Willoughby said, "which might greatly endear it to me; but this place will always possess one claim on my affection which no other can possibly share."

Mrs. Dashwood looked with pleasure at Marianne, whose fine eyes were fixed so expressively on Willoughby that it was plain she understood him perfectly.

"How often did I wish," he added, lowering his voice, "when I was at Allenham this time last year, that Barton cottage were inhabited! I never passed within view of it without admiring its situation and grieving that no one should live there. How little did I then think that the very first news I should hear from Mrs. Smith, upon my next return to the country, would be that Barton cottage was taken! I felt an immediate satisfaction and interest in the event, which nothing but a kind of prescience of the happiness I should experience from it can account for. Must it not have been so, Marianne?"

Continuing in his former tone, he said, "And yet this house you would spoil, Mrs. Dashwood? You would rob it of its simplicity with imaginary improvements! And this dear parlour, in which our acquaintance first began, and in which so many happy hours have since been spent by us together, you would degrade to the condition of a common entrance, and everyone would be eager to pass through the room which has hitherto contained within itself more real accommodation and comfort than any other apartment of the handsomest dimensions in the world could possibly afford."

Mrs. Dashwood again assured him that no such alteration would be attempted.

"You are a good woman," he warmly replied. "Your promise makes me easy. Extend it a little farther, and it will make me happy. Tell me that not only your house will remain the same, but that I shall ever find you and yours as unchanged as your dwelling; and that you will always consider me with the kindness which has made everything belonging to you so dear to me."

The promise was readily given, and Willoughby's behavior throughout the evening declared at once his affection and happiness.

"Shall we see you tomorrow to dinner?" asked Mrs. Dashwood as he prepared to leave. "I do not ask you to come in the morning, for we must walk to the park to call on Lady Middleton."

He engaged to be with them by four o'clock.

The following day, Mrs. Dashwood visited Lady Middleton, accompanied by two of her daughters. Marianne, however, declined to join them, citing a trivial engagement that kept her at home. Her mother, convinced that Willoughby had promised to call upon them while they were away, felt no unease regarding her absence.

Upon their return from the park, they discovered Willoughby's carriage and servant waiting at the cottage. Mrs. Dashwood, relieved that her intuition had been correct, prepared to enter the house, only to be met by a scene she had never anticipated. As soon as they crossed the threshold, Marianne emerged from the drawing-room in a state of violent distress, her handkerchief pressed to her eyes. Without a word, she fled upstairs. Alarmed by her sudden departure, Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor hastened into the room Marianne had just vacated. They found only Willoughby, leaning against the mantelpiece with his back turned. At their entrance, he turned around, his face betraying the same profound emotion that had overwhelmed his sister.

"Is anything the matter with her?" Mrs. Dashwood cried immediately upon entering. "Is she ill?"

"I hope not," Willoughby replied, striving to maintain a cheerful demeanor. With a forced smile, he added, "It is I who may rather expect to be ill—for I am now suffering under a very heavy disappointment!"

"Disappointment?"

"Yes," he answered, his voice trembling slightly. "I am unable to keep my engagement with you. Mrs. Smith has exercised the privilege of wealth upon a poor dependent cousin by sending me on urgent business to London. I have just received my dispatches and taken my farewell of Allenham; and, in a spirit of exhilaration, I have come to take my farewell of you."

"To London! And are you going this morning?"

"Almost this moment."

"This is very unfortunate. But Mrs. Smith must be obliged; and her business will not detain you from us long, I hope."

He flushed deeply as he replied, "You are very kind, but I have no idea of returning to Devonshire immediately. My visits to Mrs. Smith are never repeated within the year."

"And is Mrs. Smith your only friend? Is Allenham the only house in the neighbourhood to which you will be welcome? For shame, Willoughby, can you wait for an invitation here?"

His colour deepened further. With his eyes fixed on the floor, he could only murmur, "You are too good."

Mrs. Dashwood exchanged a look of surprise with Elinor, who felt equal astonishment. For several moments, the room fell into a heavy silence. Finally, Mrs. Dashwood spoke again.

"I have only to add, my dear Willoughby, that at Barton Cottage you will always be welcome. I will not press you to return here immediately, for only you can judge how far that might please Mrs. Smith. On this point, I shall be no more disposed to question your judgment than to doubt your inclination."

"My engagements at present," Willoughby replied, his voice confused and halting, "are of such a nature—that—I dare not flatter myself—"

He stopped abruptly. Mrs. Dashwood was too stunned to speak, and another pause ensued. It was broken by Willoughby, who offered a faint, weary smile. "It is folly to linger in this manner. I will not torment myself any longer by remaining among friends whose society it is impossible for me now to enjoy."

He then hastily took leave of them all and departed. They watched him step into his carriage, and within a minute, it was gone.

Mrs. Dashwood felt too overwhelmed for words and instantly withdrew to the adjoining room, seeking solitude to process the concern and alarm caused by this sudden departure.

Elinor's uneasiness was at least equal to her mother's. She replayed the events with growing anxiety and distrust. Willoughby's behavior in taking leave—his embarrassment, his affected cheerfulness, and above all, his reluctance to accept her mother's invitation—disturbed her greatly. Such backwardness was unlike a lover, and even more unlike the man she knew. One moment she feared that no serious design had ever been formed on his side; the next, she imagined some unfortunate quarrel had taken place between him and her sister. The distress in which Marianne had fled the room suggested a serious breach, yet considering the depth of Marianne's love, a quarrel seemed almost impossible.

Whatever the particulars of their separation, her sister's affliction was undeniable. Elinor thought with tender compassion of that violent sorrow, which Marianne was likely not merely expressing as a relief, but feeding and encouraging as a duty.

About half an hour later, her mother returned. Though her eyes were red, her countenance remained relatively cheerful.

"Our dear Willoughby is now some miles from Barton, Elinor," she said as she sat down to work. "And with how heavy a heart does he travel?"

"It is all very strange. So suddenly to be gone! It seems but the work of a moment. Last night he was with us so happy, so cheerful, so affectionate? And now, after only ten minutes' notice—gone, without intending to return! Something more than what he owned to us must have happened. He did not speak, he did not behave like himself. You must have seen the difference as well as I. What can it be? Can they have quarrelled? Why else should he have shown such unwillingness to accept your invitation here?"

"It was not inclination that he lacked, Elinor; I could plainly see that. He had not the power of accepting it. I have thought it all over, and I can perfectly account for everything that at first seemed strange to me as well as to you."

"Can you, indeed!"

"Yes. I have explained it to myself in the most satisfactory way. You, Elinor, who love to doubt where you can, will find it unsatisfying; but you shall not talk me out of my trust. I am persuaded that Mrs. Smith suspects his regard for Marianne, disapproves of it—perhaps because she has other views for him—and on that account is eager to get him away. The business she sends him to transact is likely an invention, an excuse to dismiss him. This is what I believe to have happened. He is aware that she disapproves of the connection; he dares not, in his dependent situation, confess his engagement to Marianne to her face. Therefore, he feels obliged to submit to her schemes and absent himself from Devonshire for a while. You will tell me, I know, that this may or may not have happened; but I will listen to no cavil unless you can point out any other method of understanding the affair that is equally satisfactory. And now, Elinor, what have you to say?"

"Nothing, for you have anticipated my answer."

"Then you would have told me that it might or might not have happened. Oh, Elinor, how incomprehensible are your feelings! You had rather take evil upon credit than good. You had rather look out for misery for Marianne and guilt for poor Willoughby than an apology for the latter. You are resolved to think him blameable because he took leave of us with less affection than his usual behaviour has shown. And is no allowance to be made for inadvertence, or for spirits depressed by recent disappointment? Are no probabilities to be accepted merely because they are not certainties? Is nothing due to the man whom we all have such reason to love, and no reason in the world to think ill of? To the possibility of motives unanswerable in themselves, though unavoidably secret for a while? And, after all, what is it you suspect him of?"

"I can hardly tell myself. But suspicion of something unpleasant is the inevitable consequence of such an alteration as we just witnessed in him. There is great truth, however, in what you have now urged regarding the allowances which ought to be made for him, and it is my wish to be candid in my judgment of everybody. Willoughby may undoubtedly have very sufficient reasons for his conduct, and I will hope that he has. But it would have been more like Willoughby to acknowledge them at once. Secrecy may be advisable; but still I cannot help wondering at its being practiced by him."

"Do not blame him, however, for departing from his character where the deviation is necessary. But you really do admit the justice of what I have said in his defence? I am happy—and he is acquitted."

"Not entirely. It may be proper to conceal their engagement, if they are engaged, from Mrs. Smith—and if that is the case, it must be highly expedient for Willoughby to be but little in Devonshire at present. But this is no excuse for their concealing it from us."

"Concealing it from us! My dear child, do you accuse Willoughby and Marianne of concealment? This is strange indeed, when your eyes have been reproaching them every day for incautiousness."

"I want no proof of their affection," said Elinor firmly, "but of their engagement I do."

"I am perfectly satisfied of both."

"Yet not a syllable has been said to you on the subject, by either of them."

"I have not wanted syllables where actions have spoken so plainly. Has not his behaviour to Marianne and to all of us, for at least the last fortnight, declared that he loved and considered her as his future wife, and that he felt for us the attachment of the nearest relation? Have we not perfectly understood each other? Has not my consent been daily asked by his looks, his manner, his attentive and affectionate respect? My Elinor, is it possible to doubt their engagement? How could such a thought occur to you? How is it to be supposed that Willoughby, persuaded as he must be of your sister's love, should leave her, and leave her perhaps for months, without telling her of his affection; that they should part without a mutual exchange of confidence?"

"I confess," replied Elinor, "that every circumstance except one is in favour of their engagement; but that one is the total silence of both on the subject, and with me it almost outweighs every other."

"How strange this is! You must think wretchedly indeed of Willoughby if, after all that has openly passed between them, you can doubt the nature of the terms on which they are together. Has he been acting a part in his behaviour to your sister all this time? Do you suppose him really indifferent to her?"

"No, I cannot think that. He must and does love her; I am sure of it."

"But with a strange kind of tenderness, if he can leave her with such indifference, such carelessness of the future, as you attribute to him."

"You must remember, my dear mother, that I have never considered this matter as certain. I have had my doubts, I confess; but they are fainter than they were, and they may soon be entirely done away. If we find they correspond, every fear of mine will be removed."

"A mighty concession indeed! If you were to see them at the altar, you would suppose they were going to be married. Ungracious girl! But I require no such proof. Nothing in my opinion has ever passed to justify doubt; no secrecy has been attempted; all has been uniformly open and unreserved. You cannot doubt your sister's wishes. It must be Willoughby therefore whom you suspect. But why? Is he not a man of honour and feeling? Has there been any inconsistency on his side to create alarm? Can he be deceitful?"

"I hope not, I believe not," cried Elinor. "I love Willoughby, sincerely love him; and suspicion of his integrity cannot be more painful to yourself than to me. It has been involuntary, and I will not encourage it. I was startled, I confess, by the alteration in his manners this morning—he did not speak like himself, and did not return your kindness with any cordiality. But all this may be explained by such a situation of his affairs as you have supposed. He had just parted from my sister, had seen her leave him in the greatest affliction; and if he felt obliged, from a fear of offending Mrs. Smith, to resist the temptation of returning here soon, yet aware that by declining your invitation, by saying that he was going away for some time, he should seem to act an ungenerous, a suspicious part by our family, he might well be embarrassed and disturbed. In such a case, a plain and open avowal of his difficulties would have been more to his honour, I think, as well as more consistent with his general character—but I will not raise objections against any one's conduct on so illiberal a foundation as a difference in judgment from myself, or a deviation from what I may think right and consistent."

"You speak very properly. Willoughby certainly does not deserve to be suspected. Though we have not known him long, he is no stranger in this part of the world; and who has ever spoken to his disadvantage? Had he been in a situation to act independently and marry immediately, it might have been odd that he should leave us without acknowledging everything to me at once: but this is not the case. It is an engagement in some respects not prosperously begun, for their marriage must be at a very uncertain distance; and even secrecy, as far as it can be observed, may now be very advisable."

Their conversation was interrupted by the entrance of Margaret, leaving Elinor free to reflect on her mother's representations, acknowledging the probability of many things and hoping for the justice of all.

They saw nothing of Marianne until dinner time, when she entered the room and took her place at the table without saying a word. Her eyes were red and swollen, and it seemed as if her tears were even then restrained with difficulty. She avoided the looks of everyone, could neither eat nor speak. After some time, on her mother silently pressing her hand with tender compassion, her small degree of fortitude was quite overcome; she burst into tears and left the room.

This violent oppression of spirits continued throughout the evening. She was without any power, because she was without any desire of command over herself. The slightest mention of anything relative to Willoughby overpowered her in an instant. Though her family were most anxiously attentive to her comfort, it was impossible for them, if they spoke at all, to keep clear of every subject which

Marianne felt that if she had managed even a single hour of sleep the night following her departure from Willoughby, she would have deemed herself utterly inexcusable. Yet, shame held no sway over her; she rose from her bed not merely rested, but drained, her spirit too raw for the composure that would have been required to face her family. The very emotions that rendered such stoicism a disgrace left her immune to the accusation. She had wept through the darkness, waking with a throbbing headache, speechless and repelled by the idea of food. Every attempt at comfort from her mother and sisters met only with a cold refusal, her sensitivity a fortress against their pity.

Once breakfast concluded, she fled the house alone, wandering the village of Allenham. There, she indulged in the ghost of past joys while drowning her present ruin in tears. The evening offered no respite; instead, she turned her grief into a ritual. She played every melody she had once shared with Willoughby, tracing the lines of his handwriting on the music sheets until her heart grew so heavy it could bear no further weight. Hours were lost at the piano, alternating between song and sobbing, her voice often choked by tears. Even in literature, she sought the same bitter contrast, reading only the books they had cherished together, feeding a hunger for misery that seemed insatiable.

For a few days, this violent affliction could not be sustained; it settled into a quieter, deeper melancholy. Yet, her solitary strolls and silent contemplations still summoned waves of sorrow as fierce as before. No letter arrived from Willoughby, and none was anticipated. Her mother, bewildered, found solace in rationalizations, while Elinor's unease grew.

"Remember, Elinor," Mrs. Dashwood said, "how often Sir John retrieves our mail himself. We agreed that secrecy is paramount; if their correspondence passed through his hands, it could not remain hidden."

Elinor saw the logic but felt the weight of the silence pressing down. "Mother," she ventured, "why do you not simply ask Marianne if she is engaged to Willoughby? From you, her beloved and indulgent mother, such a question could not offend. It would stem from love itself. She was once so open, so trusting with you."

"I would never ask such a thing," Mrs. Dashwood replied, her tone firm. "If they are not engaged, what agony would such a query inflict? It would be cruel. I would never deserve her trust again if I forced a confession of what she intends to keep hidden. I know Marianne's heart; she loves me deeply, and she will tell me everything when the time is right. I will not pry from a child, for duty forbids it."

Elinor found this delicacy excessive, given her sister's youth, yet her protests fell on deaf ears. Common sense and prudence were drowned by her mother's romantic idealism.

Days passed before Willoughby's name was spoken before Marianne, save for the sharp wit of Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, whose jokes only deepened her pain. One evening, however, Mrs. Dashwood, picking up a volume of Shakespeare, remarked, "We never finished *Hamlet*, Marianne. Our dear Willoughby left before we could conclude it. We shall wait until he returns..."

"Months!" Marianne cried, startled. "No—nor many weeks."

The remark brought a flicker of hope to Elinor, confirming Marianne's certainty in Willoughby's return.

A week after his departure, Marianne was finally persuaded to join her sisters for a walk, though she had previously avoided all company, slipping away whenever they planned to go to the downs or the valley. Elinor, disapproving of her isolation, insisted. They walked in silence along the road through the valley, Marianne's mind too turbulent for conversation. As they reached the point where the road opened toward Barton, they paused to survey the landscape.

Suddenly, an animated figure appeared—a man on horseback approaching them. Within moments, they recognized him as a gentleman.

"It is he! It is indeed!" Marianne exclaimed, her eyes lighting up, and she hurried forward.

"Elinor!" she cried, "I know it is!"

"Indeed, Marianne, I fear you are mistaken," Elinor interjected, quickening her pace to shield her sister. "That man is not tall enough, nor does he carry himself like Willoughby."

"He has his air! His coat! His horse!" Marianne insisted, walking eagerly ahead.

Elinor kept close behind, certain of her judgment. As they drew within thirty yards, Marianne's heart sank. She turned abruptly to flee, but her sisters' voices called her back. A third voice joined theirs, familiar and beloved, urging her to stay. Marianne turned in surprise to find Edward Ferrars.

He was the only man in the world who could be forgiven for resembling anyone other than Willoughby at that moment, yet he possessed the power to draw a smile from her. She wiped away her tears to greet him, momentarily forgetting her own disappointment in her sister's happiness.

Edward dismounted, handing his horse to a servant, and walked back with them to Barton, having come specifically to visit. He was received with warmth by all, but Marianne displayed a fervor in her greeting that even Elinor did not match. To Marianne, the encounter felt like a continuation of the coldness she had sensed at Norland. Edward, however, offered little warmth. He appeared confused, barely registering the joy of the reunion, neither rapturous nor cheerful. He spoke little, answering only when pressed, and showed no special affection for Elinor. Marianne watched with growing astonishment, a nascent dislike taking root. The contrast between his reserved demeanor and the vibrant manners of her lost lover sharpened her despair.

After a brief pause for greetings, Marianne asked if he had come straight from London.

"No," Edward replied, "I have been in Devonshire for two weeks."

"Two weeks!" Marianne repeated, surprised he had stayed in the county without seeing Elinor.

"I was staying with friends near Plymouth," he explained, looking slightly troubled.

"Have you been to Sussex lately?" Elinor asked.

"Yes, I visited Norland about a month ago."

"And how does dear, dear Norland look?" Marianne asked eagerly.

"Dear, dear Norland," Elinor said dryly, "likely looks much as it always does this time of year. The woods and paths are thick with dead leaves."

"Oh!" Marianne sighed, her voice filled with longing. "With what transporting sensation have I watched them fall! How I delighted to see them driven in showers by the wind! What feelings the season and the air inspire! Now, there is no one to appreciate them. They are seen only as a nuisance, swept away hastily."

"It is not everyone," Elinor noted, "who shares your passion for dead leaves."

"No," Marianne admitted softly. "My feelings are rarely understood. But sometimes they are." As she spoke, she drifted into a reverie, then rallied herself. "Now, Edward," she said, gesturing to the view, "look at Barton Valley. Try to find tranquility. Look at those hills! Have you ever seen their like? To the left is Barton Park, amidst those woods. You can see the end of the house. And there, beneath that distant, grand hill, lies our cottage."

"It is a beautiful country," Edward replied politely. "Though these valleys must be quite muddy in winter."

"How can you speak of mud with such scenery before you?"

"Because," he smiled faintly, "among the objects before me, I see a very dirty lane."

Marianne muttered to herself, "How strange."

"Do you find the neighborhood agreeable? Are the Middletons pleasant people?"

"No, not all," Marianne answered quietly. "We could not be more unfortunately situated."

"Marianne!" Elinor exclaimed. "How can you say that? How unjust! They are a respectable family, Mr. Ferrars, and have treated us with the utmost kindness. Have you forgotten the many pleasant days we owe them?"

"No," Marianne whispered, "nor the many painful moments."

Elinor ignored the comment, turning her attention to Edward and attempting to sustain conversation by discussing their current residence and its amenities. His continued coldness and reserve mortified her deeply. She felt vexed and half-angry, yet she resolved to treat him with the courtesy due to a family connection, suppressing any outward sign of resentment, guided by the past rather than the present.

The sight of him brought only a fleeting surprise to Mrs. Dashwood; indeed, his arrival at Barton seemed to her the most natural thing in the world. Yet, her initial wonder quickly dissolved into a joy that deepened with every passing moment. She welcomed him with such warmth and genuine affection that any lingering shyness, coldness, or reserve melted away before he even crossed the threshold. These defenses had already begun to crumble before he entered the room, completely overwhelmed by the captivating kindness of Mrs. Dashwood. It was impossible for a man to harbor love for either of her daughters without extending that tenderness to their mother as well, and Elinor found great satisfaction in seeing him soon return to his true self. His affections seemed to reawaken toward them all, and his concern for their welfare became evident once more.

However, a shadow remained. He was not in spirits. Though he praised their home, admired the view, and attended to them with kindness, a certain heaviness lingered in his demeanor. The entire family noticed it, and Mrs. Dashwood, blaming it on some lack of generosity in his mother, sat down to dinner with an indignation directed at all selfish parents.

"Pray, what are Mrs. Ferrars's views for you at present, Edward?" she asked after the meal, as they gathered around the fire. "Are you still destined to be a great orator, despite your own inclinations?"

"No," he replied. "I hope my mother is finally convinced that I possess no talent for a public life beyond my inclination!"

"But how is your fame to be established?" Mrs. Dashwood pressed gently. "For you must be famous to satisfy your family. With no desire for expense, no affection for strangers, no profession, and no assurance, you might find it a difficult path."

"I shall not attempt it," Edward said firmly. "I have no wish to be distinguished, and I have every reason to hope I never shall. Thank Heaven, I cannot be forced into genius and eloquence."

"You have no ambition, I well know," Mrs. Dashwood observed. "Your wishes are all moderate."

"As moderate as those of the rest of the world, I believe," Edward answered. "I wish, as everyone else does, to be perfectly happy; but, like everyone else, it must be in my own way. Greatness will not make me so."

"Strange that it would!" cried Marianne, her voice brightening. "What have wealth or grandeur to do with happiness?"

"Grandeur has but little to do with it," Elinor corrected softly, "but wealth has much."

"Elinor, for shame!" Marianne exclaimed. "Money can only give happiness where there is nothing else to give it. Beyond a comfortable competence, it affords no real satisfaction regarding the self alone."

"Perhaps," Elinor smiled, "we may come to the same conclusion. Your 'competence' and my 'wealth' are very much alike, I dare say. Without them, as the world goes now, we shall both agree that every external comfort must be lacking. Your ideas are simply more noble than mine. Come, what exactly is your competence?"

"About eighteen hundred, perhaps two thousand a year; not more than that."

Elinor laughed. "Two thousand a year! One is my wealth! I guessed how it would end."

"And yet," Marianne insisted, "two thousand a year is a very moderate income. A family cannot well be maintained on less. I am sure I am not extravagant in my demands. A proper establishment of servants, a carriage, perhaps two, and hunters, cannot be supported on less."

Elinor smiled again, hearing her sister describe their future expenses at Combe Magna with such accuracy.

"Hunters!" Edward repeated, shaking his head. "But why must you have hunters? Not everyone hunts."

"But most people do," Marianne blushed as she replied.

"I wish," said Margaret, striking out a novel thought, "that somebody would give us all a large fortune apiece!"

"Oh, that they would!" cried Marianne, her eyes sparkling with animation, her cheeks glowing with the delight of such imaginary happiness.

"We are all unanimous in that wish, I suppose," Elinor said, "in spite of the insufficiency of wealth."

"Oh dear!" Margaret sighed dreamily. "How happy I should be! I wonder what I should do with it!"

Marianne looked as though she had no doubt on that point.

"I should be puzzled to spend so large a fortune myself," Mrs. Dashwood admitted, "if my children were all to be rich without my help."

"You must begin your improvements on this house," Elinor observed cheerfully. "And your difficulties will soon vanish."

"What magnificent orders would travel from this family to London in such an event!" Edward mused. "What a happy day for booksellers, music-sellers, and print-shops! You, Miss Dashwood, would give a general commission for every new print of merit to be sent you. And as for Marianne, I know her greatness of soul; there would not be enough music in London to content her. And books! Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she would buy them all over and over again. She would buy up every copy, I believe, to prevent them falling into unworthy hands. And she would have every book that tells her how to admire an old twisted tree. Should not you, Marianne? Forgive me if I am very saucy. But I was willing to show you that I have not forgotten our old disputes."

"I love to be reminded of the past, Edward," Marianne said warmly. "Whether it be melancholy or gay, I love to recall it. You will never offend me by talking of former times. You are very right in supposing how my money would be spent—some of it, at least. My loose cash would certainly be employed in improving my collection of music and books."

"And the bulk of your fortune would be laid out in annuities on the authors or their heirs," Edward added playfully.

"No, Edward, I should have something else to do with it."

"Perhaps, then, you would bestow it as a reward on that person who wrote the ablest defense of your favorite maxim: that no one can ever be in love more than once in their life? Your opinion on that point is unchanged, I presume?"

"Undoubtedly," Marianne declared. "At my time of life, opinions are tolerably fixed. It is not likely that I should now see or hear anything to change them."

"Marianne is as steadfast as ever, you see," Elinor noted. "She is not at all altered."

"She is only grown a little more grave than she was," Edward replied.

"Nay, Edward," Marianne said, turning to him. "You need not reproach me. You are not very gay yourself."

"Why should you think so!" he replied with a sigh. "But gaiety has never been a part of my character."

"Nor do I think it a part of Marianne's," Elinor said gently. "I should hardly call her a lively girl. She is very earnest, very eager in all she does—sometimes talks a great deal and always with animation—but she is not often really merry."

"I believe you are right," he agreed, "and yet I have always set her down as a lively girl."

"I have frequently detected myself in such kind of mistakes," Elinor confessed. "In a total misapprehension of character in some point or other: fancying people so much more gay or grave, or ingenious or stupid than they really are. I can hardly tell why or in what the deception originated. Sometimes one is guided by what they say of themselves, and very frequently by what other people say of them, without giving oneself time to deliberate and judge."

"But I thought it was right, Elinor," Marianne interjected. "To be guided wholly by the opinion of other people. I thought our judgments were given us merely to be subservient to those of our neighbors. This has always been your doctrine, I am sure."

"No, Marianne, never," Elinor corrected. "My doctrine has never aimed at the subjection of the understanding. All I have ever attempted to influence has been the behavior. You must not confound my meaning. I am guilty, I confess, of having often wished you to treat our acquaintance in general with greater attention; but when have I advised you to adopt their sentiments or to conform to their judgment in serious matters?"

"You have not been able to bring your sister over to your plan of general civility," Edward turned to Elinor. "Do you gain no ground?"

"Quite the contrary," Elinor replied, looking expressively at Marianne.

"My judgment," Edward returned, "is all on your side of the question; but I am afraid my practice is much more on your sister's. I never wish to offend, but I am so foolishly shy that I often seem negligent when I am only kept back by my natural awkwardness. I have frequently thought that I must have been intended by nature to be fond of low company, for I am so little at my ease among strangers of gentility!"

"Marianne has not shyness to excuse any inattention of hers," Elinor said.

"She knows her own worth too well for false shame," Edward replied. "Shyness is only the effect of a sense of inferiority in some way or other. If I could persuade myself that my manners were perfectly easy and graceful, I should not be shy."

"But you would still be reserved," Marianne countered. "And that is worse."

Edward started. "Reserved! Am I reserved, Marianne?"

"Yes, very."

"I do not understand you," he replied, coloring slightly. "Reserved! How, in what manner? What am I to tell you? What can you suppose?"

Elinor looked surprised at his emotion. Trying to laugh off the subject, she said to him, "Do you not know my sister well enough to understand what she means? Do you not know she calls everyone reserved who does not talk as fast, and admire what she admires as rapturously as herself?"

Edward made no answer. His gravity and thoughtfulness returned upon him in their fullest extent, and he sat for some time silent and dull.

Elinor watched her brother-in-law with a heavy heart, noting the profound gloom that had settled over him. His visit offered her only a fraction of the comfort she craved, for his own enjoyment of the moment seemed equally diminished. It was painfully clear that he was unhappy, yet she prayed with every fiber of her being that his affection for her remained as steadfast as it had once been. Until now, however, the persistence of his regard had felt precarious, his reserved demeanor constantly contradicting the fleeting sparks of animation he occasionally showed.

The following morning, before the rest of the household had risen, Edward joined Elinor and Marianne in the breakfast room. Marianne, ever eager to foster their happiness, soon excused herself to attend to her duties upstairs. Yet, barely halfway up the stairs, she heard the parlour door swing open and turned to find Edward emerging.

"I am off to the village to check on my horses," he announced, "since you are not yet ready for breakfast. I shall be back shortly."

When he returned, his spirits were buoyed by a fresh appreciation of the countryside. His walk to the village had revealed many hidden beauties of the valley, and the elevated position of the village itself offered a sweeping panorama that delighted him. This topic naturally captured Marianne's attention; she began to describe her own awe of the scenery, probing him with questions about the specific details that had struck him. Edward, however, gently interjected.

"You must not press me too closely, Marianne," he said. "Remember, I possess no knowledge of the picturesque. If we delve into particulars, I shall only offend you with my ignorance and lack of taste. I might label a hill 'steep' when it ought to be 'bold,' or call a surface 'strange' when it should be 'irregular.' Distant objects, to me, are merely out of sight, whereas they should be indistinct through a hazy atmosphere. Please accept such admiration as I can honestly offer. To me, it is simply a very fine country—the hills are steep, the woods brim with timber, and the valley feels comfortable and snug, dotted with rich meadows and neat farmhouses. It suits my definition of beauty because it unites aesthetic pleasure with utility. I daresay it is picturesque too, simply because you admire it; I can easily imagine it teeming with rocks, promontories, grey moss, and brushwood, though all of that escapes me. I know nothing of the picturesque."

"I fear it is too true," Marianne admitted, "but why boast of such limitations?"

"I suspect," Elinor observed, "that in trying to avoid one kind of affectation, Edward has fallen into another. Believing that many people pretend to admire nature's beauties more deeply than they truly feel, he has adopted an attitude of greater indifference and less discrimination than he actually possesses. He is fastidious and has cultivated an affectation of his own."

"It is very true," Marianne agreed. "Admiration of landscape has become mere jargon. Everyone pretends to feel and strives to describe with the taste of the man who first defined picturesque beauty. I detest jargon of every sort; often I have kept my feelings to myself because I found no language to describe them except what was worn, hackneyed, and devoid of meaning."

"I am convinced," Edward replied, "that you truly feel all the delight in a fine prospect that you profess. But in return, my sister must allow me to feel no more than I express. I like a fine prospect, but not on picturesque principles. I dislike crooked, twisted, blasted trees; I prefer them tall, straight, and flourishing. I have no fondness for ruined, tattered cottages, nor for nettles, thistles, or heath blossoms. I derive more pleasure from a snug farmhouse than from a watch-tower, and a troop of tidy, happy villagers pleases me far more than the finest banditti in the world."

Marianne stared at Edward with amazement, while looking at her sister with deep compassion. Elinor, however, only laughed.

The conversation lapsed, leaving Marianne thoughtful until a new object caught her eye. Sitting beside Edward, she took his tea from Mrs. Dashwood. As his hand moved, a ring became visible on one of his fingers—a plait of hair set in the center, conspicuous against his skin.

"I never saw you wear a ring before, Edward," she exclaimed. "Is that Fanny's hair? I recall her promising to give you some. But I should have thought her hair was darker."

Marianne spoke without thinking, voicing exactly what she felt. The moment she realized the pain she had caused, her own vexation at her lack of tact was surpassed only by Edward's distress. He flushed deeply, casting a fleeting glance at Elinor before replying, "Yes; it is my sister's hair. The setting always casts a different shade upon it, you know."

Elinor had met his gaze and felt a similar consciousness. Instantly, she knew the hair was hers. The only divergence between their thoughts lay in the conclusion: where Marianne viewed it as a generous gift from her sister, Elinor was acutely aware it must have been obtained through some theft or contrivance unknown to herself. She was not in the mood to consider it an affront. Instead, feigning indifference by immediately changing the subject, she resolved internally to seize every opportunity to examine the ring and confirm beyond doubt that the shade matched her own exactly.

Edward's embarrassment lingered for some time, eventually giving way to a more settled preoccupation. He remained unusually grave throughout the morning. Marianne severely chided herself for her indiscretion, though her forgiveness might have come sooner had she known how little offense it had truly caused her sister.

Before midday, they were visited by Sir John and Mrs. Jennings. Having heard of a gentleman's arrival at the cottage, they came to inspect the guest. With the aid of his mother-in-law, Sir John quickly deduced that the name Ferrars began with an 'F.' This discovery prepared a future mine of raillery against the devoted Elinor, though the novelty of their acquaintance with Edward prevented it from being sprung immediately. For now, she gleaned only from significant glances how far their penetration, guided by Margaret's instructions, had extended.

As was his custom, Sir John never failed to invite them to dine at the park the following day or to drink tea that evening. On this occasion, desiring to entertain his visitor and contribute to his amusement, he sought to secure them for both engagements.

"You *must* drink tea with us tonight," he insisted, "for we shall be quite alone. And tomorrow you must absolutely dine with us, for we shall be a large party."

Mrs. Jennings reinforced the invitation. "And who knows but you may raise a dance," she added. "That will tempt *you*, Miss Marianne."

"A dance!" Marianne cried. "Impossible! Who is to dance?"

"Who? Why yourselves, the Careys, and the Whitakers, to be sure! What! You thought nobody could dance because a certain person who shall remain nameless has departed!"

"I wish with all my soul," Sir John declared, "that Willoughby were among us again."

These words, coupled with Marianne's sudden blush, planted new suspicions in Edward's mind. "And who is Willoughby?" he asked in a low voice to Miss Dashwood, to whom he was seated.

She offered a brief reply, but Marianne's countenance was more communicative. Edward perceived enough to understand not only the meaning behind the others' remarks but also the nuances of Marianne's expressions that had previously puzzled him. When their visitors finally departed, he immediately approached her and whispered, "I have been guessing. Shall I tell you my guess?"

"What do you mean?"

"Shall I tell you?"

"Certainly."

"Well then, I guess that Mr. Willoughby hunts."

Marianne was surprised and confused, yet she could not help smiling at the quiet archness of his manner. After a moment's silence, she said, "Oh, Edward! How can you?—But the time will come, I hope... I am sure you will like him."

"I do not doubt it," he replied, somewhat astonished by her earnestness and warmth. Had he not assumed it was a jest intended for the general good of her acquaintance, founded on something or nothing between Mr. Willoughby and herself, he would not have ventured to mention it.

Edward stayed at the cottage for a full week, though Mrs. Dashwood pleaded earnestly for him to remain longer. He seemed determined to depart the moment his time among them reached its peak, behaving as if he were undergoing some form of self-imposed penance. For the final few days, his spirits, though still fluctuating, had noticeably lifted; he grew increasingly fond of the house and its surroundings, sighing whenever the topic of leaving arose. He insisted his schedule was entirely free, yet he could not decide where he would go next. Despite this, he had to leave. No week had ever flown by so rapidly; he struggled to believe it was truly over. He spoke often of his reluctance, revealing a shift in his feelings that contradicted his rigid actions. He found no joy at Norland, detested the prospect of returning to town, yet he was bound to go to one or the other. He cherished their kindness above all else, and his greatest happiness lay in their company. Still, he was compelled to depart at the end of the week, defying their wishes and his own desires, with no constraints on his time.

Elinor attributed everything strange in Edward's conduct to his mother. It was fortunate for her that Mrs. Ferrars's character was so poorly understood by her son that she served as a convenient excuse for every oddity. Though disappointed and vexed, occasionally displeased by his inconsistent behavior toward her, Elinor remained largely willing to interpret his actions with the candor and generosity she had previously been forced to muster for Willoughby's sake. His lack of spirit, openness, and consistency was usually blamed on his lack of independence and his awareness of Mrs. Ferrars's designs. The brevity of his visit and his steadfast resolve to leave stemmed from the same fettered inclination, the same unavoidable necessity of compromising with his mother. The age-old conflict between duty and will, parent and child, was the root cause. She longed to know when these difficulties would cease, when this opposition would yield, when Mrs. Ferrars would be reformed and her son free to be happy. Forced to turn from such vain hopes for comfort, she clung to her renewed faith in Edward's affection, recalling every mark of regard in his looks and words during his stay at Barton, and above all, the flattering ring he constantly wore on his finger.

"I think, Edward," Mrs. Dashwood said as they broke their fast on the final morning, "you would be a happier man if you had a profession to occupy your time and give substance to your plans. There might be some inconvenience to your friends, indeed; you could not give them so much of your time. But (with a smile) you would be materially benefited in at least one particular—you would know where to go when you leave us."

"I assure you," Edward replied, "that I have long considered this very point, just as you do now. It has been, and remains, and likely will always be, a heavy misfortune for me to have no necessary business to engage me, no profession to provide employment or any semblance of independence. Yet, unfortunately, my own fastidiousness and that of my friends have made me what I am: an idle, helpless creature. We could never agree on a profession. I always preferred the church, and still do. But it was not 'smart' enough for my family. They recommended the army. That was far too 'smart' for me. The law was deemed genteel enough; many young men with chambers in the Temple made a fine appearance in high society and drove about town in knowing carriages. But I had no inclination for the law, even in this less abstract branch that my family approved. As for the navy, it had fashion on its side, but I was too old when the subject was first raised to join it. And finally, since there was no necessity for me to have any profession at all, as I might be just as dashing and expensive without a red coat as with one, idleness was pronounced the most advantageous and honorable course. A young man of eighteen is generally not so eager to be busy as to resist his friends' solicitations to do nothing. I was therefore entered at Oxford and have been properly idle ever since."

"The consequence of which, I suppose, will be," Mrs. Dashwood remarked, "that since leisure has not promoted your own happiness, your sons will be brought up to as many pursuits, employments, professions, and trades as Columella's."

"They will be brought up," he said seriously, "to be as unlike myself as is possible. In feeling, in action, in condition, in everything."

"Come, come; this is merely an effusion of your immediate lack of spirits, Edward. You are in a melancholy mood and fancy that anyone unlike yourself must be happy. But remember that the pain of parting from friends is felt by everyone at times, regardless of their education or station. Know your own happiness. You want nothing but patience—or give it a more captivating name, call it hope. Your mother will secure you, in time, that independence you are so anxious for; it is her duty, and it will, it must soon become her happiness to prevent your entire youth from being wasted in discontent. How much might not a few months accomplish?"

"I think," Edward replied, "that I may defy many months to produce any good for me."

This despondent turn of mind, though he hid it from Mrs. Dashwood, caused additional pain to them all during the parting, which soon took place. It left an uncomfortable impression on Elinor's feelings particularly, one that required effort and time to overcome. Yet, since it was her determination to conquer it and to prevent herself from suffering visibly more than the rest of her family, she did not adopt the method so wisely employed by Marianne on a similar occasion: to augment and fix her sorrow by seeking silence, solitude, and idleness. Their approaches were as different as their goals, yet both suited the advancement of each.

As soon as Edward was out of the house, Elinor sat down at her drawing table. She busied herself throughout the day, neither seeking nor avoiding mention of his name, appearing to interest herself almost as much as ever in the family's general concerns. If this conduct did not lessen her own grief, it at least prevented its unnecessary increase and spared her mother and sisters much solicitude regarding her.

Such behavior, so diametrically opposed to her own, appeared no more meritorious to Marianne than her own had seemed faulty to her. The business of self-command she settled very easily; with strong affections, it was impossible, and with calm ones, it could hold no merit. That her sister's affections *were* calm, she dared not deny, though she blushed to acknowledge it; and of the strength of her own, she gave a striking proof by continuing to love and respect that sister, despite this mortifying conviction.

Without isolating herself from her family or leaving the house in determined solitude to avoid them, or lying awake all night to indulge in meditation, Elinor found that every day afforded her enough leisure to think of Edward and his behavior in every possible variety that her varying moods could produce—with tenderness, pity, approval, censure, and doubt. There were abundant moments when, if not by the absence of her mother and sisters, at least by the nature of their occupations, conversation was forbidden among them, and every effect of solitude was produced. Her mind was inevitably at liberty; her thoughts could not be chained elsewhere; and the past and future, on a subject so interesting, had to be before her, forcing her attention and engrossing her memory, reflection, and fancy.

From a reverie of this kind, as she sat at her drawing table, she was roused one morning, shortly after Edward's departure, by the arrival of company. She happened to be quite alone. The closing of the small gate at the entrance of the green court in front of the house drew her eyes to the window, and she saw a large party walking up to the door. Among them were Sir John and Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings, but there were two others, a gentleman and a lady, who were quite unknown to her. Sitting near the window, as soon as Sir John perceived her, he left the rest of the party to the ceremony of knocking at the door. Stepping across the turf, he obliged her to open the casement to speak to him, though the space was so short between the door and the window that it was hardly possible to speak at one without being heard at the other.

"Well," he said, "we have brought you some strangers. How do you like them?"

"Hush! They will hear you."

"Never mind if they do. It is only the Palmers. Charlotte is very pretty, I can tell you. You may see her if you look this way."

Since Elinor was certain of seeing her in a couple of minutes without taking that liberty, she begged to be excused.

"Where is Marianne? Has she run away because we are come? I see her instrument is open."

"She is walking, I believe."

They were now joined by Mrs. Jennings, who had not patience enough to wait until the door was opened before telling *her* story. She came hallooing to the window, "How do you do, my dear? How does Mrs. Dashwood do? And where are your sisters? What! All alone! You will be glad of a little company to sit with. I have brought my other son and daughter to see you.
