Only think of their coming so suddenly! I thought I heard a carriage last night while we were drinking our tea, but it never entered my head that it could be them. I thought of nothing but whether it might not be Colonel Brandon come back again; so I said to Sir John, 'I do think I hear a carriage; perhaps it is Colonel Brandon come back again—'"

Elinor was obliged to turn from her in the middle of her story to receive the rest of the party. Lady Middleton introduced the two strangers; Mrs. Dashwood and Margaret came downstairs at the same time, and they all sat down to look at one another while Mrs. Jennings continued her story as she walked through the passage into the parlour, attended by Sir John.

Mrs. Palmer was several years younger than Lady Middleton and totally unlike her in every respect. She was short and plump, had a very pretty face, and the finest expression of good humor in it that could possibly be. Her manners were by no means so elegant as her sister's, but they were much more prepossessing. She came in with a smile, smiled all the time of her visit, except when she laughed, and smiled when she went away. Her husband was a grave-looking young man of five or six and twenty, with an air of more fashion and sense than his wife, but of less willingness to please or be pleased. He entered the room with a look of self-consequence, slightly bowed to the ladies without speaking a word, and, after briefly surveying them and their apartments, took up a newspaper from the table and continued to read it as long as he stayed.

Mrs. Palmer, on the contrary, who was strongly endowed by nature with a turn for being uniformly civil and happy, was hardly seated before her admiration of the parlour and everything in it burst forth.

"Well! What a delightful room this is! I never saw anything so charming! Only think, Mama, how it is improved since I was here last! I always thought it such a sweet place, ma'am! (turning to Mrs. Dashwood) But you have made it so charming! Only look, sister, how delightful everything is! How I should like such a house for myself! Should not you, Mr. Palmer?"

Mr. Palmer made her no answer and did not even raise his eyes from the newspaper.

"Mr. Palmer does not hear me," she said, laughing; "he never does sometimes. It is so ridiculous!"

This was quite a new idea to Mrs. Dashwood; she had never been used to find wit in the inattention of anyone and could not help looking with surprise at them both.

Mrs. Jennings, meanwhile, talked on as loud as she could and continued her account of their surprise the evening before on seeing their friends, without ceasing until everything was told. Mrs. Palmer laughed heartily at the recollection of their astonishment, and everyone agreed, two or three times over, that it had been quite an agreeable surprise.

"You may believe how glad we all were to see them," added Mrs. Jennings, leaning forward toward Elinor and speaking in a low voice as if she meant to be heard by no one else, though they were seated on different sides of the room; "but, however, I can't help wishing they had not traveled quite so fast, nor made such a long journey of it, for they came all round by London upon account of some business, for you know (nodding significantly and pointing to her daughter) it was wrong in her situation. I wanted her to stay at home and rest this morning, but she would come with us; she longed so much to see you all!"

Mrs. Palmer laughed and said it would not do her any harm.

"She expects to be confined in February," continued Mrs. Jennings.

Lady Middleton could no longer endure such a conversation and therefore exerted herself to ask Mr. Palmer if there was any news in the paper.

"No, none at all," he replied, and read on.

"Here comes Marianne," cried Sir John. "Now, Palmer, you shall see a monstrous pretty girl."

He immediately went into the passage, opened the front door, and ushered her in himself. Mrs. Jennings asked her, as soon as she appeared, if she had not been to Allenham; and Mrs. Palmer laughed so heartily at the question as to show she understood it. Mr. Palmer looked up on her entering the room, stared at her for some minutes, and then returned to his newspaper. Mrs. Palmer's eye was now caught by the drawings which hung round the room. She got up to examine them.

"Oh! Dear, how beautiful these are! Well! How delightful! Do but look, Mama, how sweet! I declare they are quite charming; I could look at them for ever." And then sitting down again, she very soon forgot that there were any such things in the room.

When Lady Middleton rose to go away, Mr. Palmer rose also, laid down the newspaper, stretched himself, and looked at them all around.

"My love

The next morning, as the Dashwood sisters stepped into the drawing-room of the park, Mrs. Palmer burst through the opposite door, her face alight with a cheerful energy that seemed entirely restored. She greeted them with an affectionate grasp of each hand, her eyes sparkling with delight at their return.

"How utterly wonderful to see you!" she exclaimed, settling herself snugly between Elinor and Marianne. "I was truly frightened you wouldn't come; the weather has been dreadfully dismal, and I feared you'd stay cooped up. But we must leave tomorrow, alas! The Westons arrive next week, and it was a sudden decision on our part. Mr. Palmer didn't tell me until the carriage was already at the door, and then he asked if I'd accompany him to Barton. He is such a peculiar man! He never reveals anything unless pressed. I am so sorry we cannot remain longer, but I trust we shall meet again in London very soon."

The Dashwoods felt compelled to dampen this hopeful expectation.

"To London?" Mrs. Palmer cried, laughing. "I shall be terribly disappointed if you do not go. I could secure the finest house in the world for you, right next door to ours in Hanover Square. You must come, indeed. I am certain I would be delighted to act as your chaperone whenever Mrs. Dashwood feels unable to venture into public society."

They offered their thanks but firmly resisted her persistent pleas.

"Oh, my dear," Mrs. Palmer called out to her husband, who had just entered the room, "you must assist me in persuading the Miss Dashwoods to spend this winter in London."

Her husband offered no reply; after a polite bow to the ladies, he turned his attention to the gloomy weather.

"Horrible!" he declared. "Such weather renders everything and everyone repulsive. Dullness is generated indoors just as readily as outdoors by the rain; it makes one detest every acquaintance. What on earth does Sir John mean by lacking a billiard room? How few people understand true comfort! Sir John is as obtuse as the weather itself."

Soon, the rest of the party filtered in.

"I fear, Miss Marianne," Sir John remarked, "that you were unable to take your customary walk to Allenham today."

Marianne's expression grew grave, and she remained silent.

"Oh, do not be so coy before us," Mrs. Palmer chided playfully. "We know all about it, I assure you. I admire your taste immensely; I find him extremely handsome. We do not live far from him in the country, you know—perhaps not ten miles."

"Much nearer thirty," her husband corrected dryly.

"Ah, well! There is little difference. I have never visited his estate, though they say it is a charming place."

"As vile a spot as I have ever seen in my life," Mr. Palmer countered sharply.

Marianne remained perfectly still, though her countenance betrayed a keen interest in the exchange.

"Is it truly so ugly?" Mrs. Palmer pressed. "Then it must be some other location that is so delightful, I suppose."

Later, seated in the dining room, Sir John expressed regret that their gathering consisted of only eight people.

"My dear," he said to his wife, "it is most vexing that we are so few. Why did you not invite the Gilberts to dine with us today?"

"Did I not tell you, Sir John, when you first suggested it, that it was impossible? They dined with us yesterday."

"You and I, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings interjected warmly, "should not stand on such formalities."

"Then you would be exceedingly ill-bred," Mr. Palmer retorted.

"My love, you contradict everyone," his wife laughed, her usual merriment undimmed. "Do you realize how rude you are?"

"I did not know I was contradicting anyone by calling your mother ill-bred."

"Ay, you may abuse me as you please," the kindly old lady replied. "You have taken Charlotte off my hands, and you cannot give her back. So, there, I hold the upper hand over you."

Charlotte laughed heartily at the thought that her husband could not escape her, declaring that she cared not how cross he became, for they were bound to live together. It was impossible to find someone more thoroughly good-natured or more determined to find happiness than Mrs. Palmer. Her husband's studied indifference, insolence, and discontent caused her no pain; rather, when he scolded or abused her, she found it highly diverting.

"Mr. Palmer is so droll!" she whispered to Elinor. "He is perpetually in a bad mood."

After observing him closely, Elinor was not inclined to credit him with genuine, unfeigned ill-nature or ill-breeding. His temper might indeed be slightly soured by discovering, as many men do, that through some inexplicable bias toward beauty, he was married to a very foolish woman. Yet, she knew such blunders were too common for any sensible man to be permanently wounded by them. Rather, she believed it was a desire for distinction that fueled his contemptuous treatment of others and his general abuse of everything around him. It was the wish to appear superior to his peers. The motive was common enough to warrant no wonder, but the means, however successful in establishing his superiority in bad manners, were unlikely to attract anyone to him save his wife.

"Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood," Mrs. Palmer said shortly afterward, "I have a favor to ask of you and your sister. Will you come to spend some time at Cleveland this Christmas? Please do—and come while the Westons are with us. You cannot imagine how happy I shall be! It will be absolutely delightful! My love," turning to her husband, "do you not long to have the Miss Dashwoods visit Cleveland?"

"Certainly," he replied with a sneer, "I came to Devonshire with no other purpose."

"There now," she said triumphantly, "you see Mr. Palmer expects you; therefore, you cannot refuse."

Both sisters eagerly and resolutely declined her invitation.

"But indeed you must and shall come. I am sure you will like it above all things. The Westons will be present, and it will be quite charming. You cannot conceive what a lovely place Cleveland is; and we are so lively now, for Mr. Palmer is constantly traveling the country canvassing for the election, and so many people have come to dine with us that I have never seen before—it is simply delightful! But, poor fellow! It is very fatiguing for him, for he is forced to make everyone like him."

Elinor could scarcely maintain her composure as she acknowledged the hardship of such an obligation.

"How charming it will be," Charlotte continued, "when he enters Parliament! Won't it? How I shall laugh! It will be so ridiculous to see all his letters addressed to him with 'M.P.' appended. But do you know, he says he will never frank for me? He declares he won't. Don't you, Mr. Palmer?"

Mr. Palmer ignored her completely.

"He cannot bear writing, you know," she persisted, "he says it is quite shocking."

"No," he replied, "I never said anything so irrational. Do not attribute all your misuse of language to me."

"There now; you see how droll he is. This is always his manner! Sometimes he won't speak to me for half a day, and then he bursts out with something so amusing—about anything in the world."

She surprised Elinor greatly as they returned to the drawing-room by asking if she did not like Mr. Palmer excessively.

"Certainly," Elinor answered. "He seems very agreeable."

"Well, I am so glad you feel that way. I thought you would; he is so pleasant. And Mr. Palmer is excessively pleased with you and your sisters, I can tell you. You cannot imagine how disappointed he will be if you do not come to Cleveland. I cannot fathom why you should object."

Elinor was once again obliged to decline her invitation, changing the subject to halt her entreaties. She hoped that, living in the same county, Mrs. Palmer might provide a more detailed account of Willoughby's general character than could be gleaned from the Middletons' limited acquaintance. She was eager to secure any confirmation of his merits that might alleviate Marianne's fears. She began by inquiring if they saw much of Mr. Willoughby at Cleveland and whether they were intimate with him.

"Oh, yes, I know him extremely well," Mrs. Palmer replied. "Not that I ever spoke to him, indeed; but I have seen him frequently in town. Somehow, I never happened to be staying at Barton while he was at Allenham. Mama saw him here once before, but I was with my uncle at Weymouth. However, I dare say we should have seen a great deal of him in Somersetshire if it had not been unluckily that we never found ourselves in the country together. He is very rarely at Combe, I believe; but even if he were there often, I do not think Mr. Palmer would visit him, for he is in the opposition, you know, and besides, it is so distant. I know why you inquire about him, very well; your sister is to marry him. I am monstrously glad of it, for then I shall have her as a neighbor."

"Upon my word," Elinor replied, "you know much more about the matter than I do, if you have any reason to expect such a match."

"Don't pretend to deny it, because you know it is what everyone is talking about. I assure you I heard of it on my way through town."

"My dear Mrs. Palmer!"

"Upon my honor, I did. I met Colonel Brandon Monday morning in Bond Street, just before we left town, and he told me of it directly."

"You surprise me very much. Colonel Brandon telling you of it! Surely you must be mistaken. To impart such intelligence to a person who could not possibly be interested in it, even if true, is not what I should expect from Colonel Brandon."

"But I do assure you it was so, for all that, and I will explain how it happened. When we met him, he turned back and walked with us. We began discussing my brother and sister, and various other topics. I said to him, 'So, Colonel, I hear a new family has arrived at Barton Cottage, and Mama sends word they are very pretty, and that one of them is to be married to Mr. Willoughby of Combe Magna. Is it true, pray? For of course you must know, as you have been in Devonshire so recently.'"

"And what did the Colonel say?"

"Oh, he did not say much; but he looked as if he knew it to be true. From that moment, I set it down as certain. It will be quite delightful, I declare! When is it to take place?"

"Was Mr. Brandon well, I hope?"

"Oh! Yes, quite well; and so full of your praises he did nothing but say fine things of you."

"I am flattered by his commendation. He seems an excellent man; and I think him uncommonly pleasing."

"So do I. He is such a charming man that it is a pity he should be so grave and so dull. Mama says *he* was in love with your sister too. I assure you it was a great compliment if he was, for he hardly ever falls in love with anyone."

"Is Mr. Willoughby much known in your part of Somersetshire?" Elinor asked.

"Oh! Yes, extremely well; that is, I do not believe many people are acquainted with him, because Combe Magna is so remote; but they all think him extremely agreeable, I assure you. No one is more liked than Mr. Willoughby wherever he goes, so you may tell your sister. She is a monstrous lucky girl to secure him, upon my honor; not that he is much more lucky in securing her, for she is so very handsome and agreeable that nothing can be good enough for her. However, I do not think she is hardly at all handsomer than you, I assure you; for I think you both excessively pretty, and so does Mr. Palmer too, I am sure, though we could not get him to admit it last night."

Mrs. Palmer's information regarding Willoughby was not particularly substantial, yet any testimony in his favor, however slight, was pleasing to her.

"I am so glad we have finally become acquainted," Charlotte continued. "And now I hope we shall always be great friends. You cannot imagine how much I longed to see you! It is so delightful that you live at the cottage! Nothing can compare to it, surely! And I am so glad your sister is going to be well married! I hope you will visit Combe Magna frequently. It is a sweet place, by all accounts."

"Have you been acquainted with Colonel Brandon for long?" Elinor asked.

"Yes, for a great while; ever since my sister married. He was a particular friend of Sir John's. I believe," she added in a low voice, "he would have been very glad to have had me, if he could. Sir John and Lady Middleton wished it very much. But Mama did not think the match good enough for me; otherwise, Sir John would have mentioned it to the Colonel, and we should have been married immediately."

"Did Colonel Brandon know of Sir John's proposal to your mother before it was made? Had he never owned his affection to you?"

"Oh, no; but if Mama had not objected to it, I dare say he would have liked it above all things. He had not seen me then more than twice, for it was before I left school. However, I am much happier as I am. Mr. Palmer is the kind of man I like."

The Palmers departed for Cleveland the following day, leaving the Dashwoods and the Middletons to their own company at Barton. Yet this solitude proved fleeting. Elinor had scarcely shaken off the lingering thoughts of the Palmers—their peculiar domestic harmony, Mr. Palmer's unassuming demeanor despite his evident talents, and the often jarring disconnect between spouses—before Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, driven by their fervent zeal for social expansion, introduced her to fresh acquaintances.

During a morning excursion to Exeter, they encountered two young ladies whom Mrs. Jennings delighted to identify as her relations. This discovery was sufficient for Sir John to extend an immediate invitation to the Park, contingent only upon the conclusion of the ladies' current engagements in Exeter. Those engagements dissolved instantly upon receiving such an offer. Lady Middleton, however, was thrown into considerable alarm upon Sir John's return, learning that she was soon to host two strangers she had never seen, whose elegance—or even their mere gentility—she could not verify. The assurances of her husband and mother held no weight with her; indeed, the fact that they were her relations made the prospect all the more unsettling. Mrs. Jennings's attempts to soothe her daughter, advising her not to mind their lack of fashion since they were all cousins bound to tolerate one another, fell on deaf ears. Resigned to the inevitability of their arrival, Lady Middleton adopted the stoic philosophy of a well-bred woman, contenting herself with issuing gentle reprimands to her husband regarding the matter five or six times daily.

The young ladies arrived, and their appearance was neither ungenteel nor unfashionable. Their attire was smart, their manners civil, and they expressed such delight at the house and furniture that Lady Middleton's good opinion was secured within an hour. She declared them to be "very agreeable girls indeed," a phrase that constituted enthusiastic admiration in her household. Sir John's confidence in his own judgment soared at this praise, and he hastened to the cottage to inform the Miss Dashwoods of the Miss Steeles' arrival, assuring them they were the sweetest girls in the world. Yet, Elinor discerned little substance in such commendation; she knew that the "sweetest girls" could be found across England in myriad forms of temperament and understanding. Sir John insisted that the entire family walk to the Park immediately to view his guests. A benevolent, philanthropic soul, he found it painful even to keep a third cousin to himself.

"Do come now," he urged. "Pray come—you must come. You can't imagine how you will like them. Lucy is monstrous pretty, so good-humoured and agreeable! The children are already clustering around her as if she were an old acquaintance. They both long to see you above all things, for they heard at Exeter that you are the most beautiful creatures in the world; I have confirmed this truth to them and added much more. You will be delighted, I am sure. They have filled the coach with playthings for the children. How can you be so cross as to refuse? Why, they are your cousins, after a fashion. You are my cousins, and they are my wife's, so you must be related."

Despite his persistence, Sir John could not prevail. He secured only a promise that they would call at the Park within a day or two, leaving the Miss Dashwoods in amazement at their indifference, while he walked home to boast anew of their attractions to them, just as he had previously boasted of them to the sisters.

When the promised visit finally occurred, the introduction revealed little to admire in the eldest, who was nearly thirty and possessed a plain, unsensible face. In contrast, the younger sister, not yet twenty-five, displayed considerable beauty. Her features were pretty, accompanied by a sharp, quick eye and a smartness of air that, while lacking actual elegance or grace, lent distinctiveness to her person. Their manners were notably civil, and Elinor quickly granted them credit for genuine sense, observing the constant, judicious attention they paid to pleasing Lady Middleton. With the children, they were in continual rapture, extolling their beauty, courting their notice, and humouring their whims. Any spare time was devoted to admiring Lady Middleton's activities or copying patterns of her elegant new dresses, which had captivated them the previous day.

Fortunately for those who court through such foibles, a fond mother, though rapacious in her pursuit of praise for her children, is also the most credulous of human beings. Her demands are exorbitant, yet she swallows anything. The excessive affection and endurance shown by the Miss Steeles toward Lady Middleton's offspring were viewed by the mother without the slightest surprise or distrust. She watched with maternal complacency as her cousins submitted to impertinent encroachments and mischievous tricks. She saw their sashes untied, their hair pulled about their ears, their work-bags searched, and their knives and scissors stolen, feeling no doubt that it was a reciprocal enjoyment. The only surprise she registered was that Elinor and Marianne sat so composedly by, claiming no share in the proceedings.

"John is in such spirits today!" she exclaimed, as he took Miss Steele's pocket-handkerchief and threw it out of the window. "He is full of monkey tricks."

Soon after, upon the second boy violently pinching the same lady's finger, she fondly observed, "How playful William is!"

"And here is my sweet little Annamaria," she added, tenderly caressing a three-year-old girl who had remained silent for the last two minutes. "She is always so gentle and quiet—never was there such a quiet little thing!"

Unfortunately, in bestowing these embraces, a pin in her ladyship's head-dress slightly scratched the child's neck. This minor injury produced screams so violent they could hardly be outdone by any creature professedly noisy. The mother's consternation was excessive, yet it could not surpass the alarm of the Miss Steeles. In this critical emergency, everything possible was done by all three, guided solely by affection, to assuage the little sufferer's agony. She was seated in her mother's lap, covered in kisses, her wound bathed with lavender-water by one of the Miss Steeles kneeling to attend her, and her mouth stuffed with sugar-plums by the other. Such rewards for her tears rendered the child too wise to cease crying; she screamed and sobbed lustily, kicking her brothers for attempting to touch her, until all their united soothings proved ineffectual.

It was only when Lady Middleton remembered that apricot marmalade had successfully treated a bruised temple in a similar distress last week that hope arose. The remedy was eagerly proposed, and a slight intermission of screams upon hearing it gave reason to believe it would not be rejected. The child was carried out of the room in her mother's arms to seek the medicine. As the two boys chose to follow, though earnestly entreated to stay, the four young ladies were left in a quietness the room had not known for many hours.

"Poor little creatures!" said Miss Steele, as soon as they were gone. "It might have been a very sad accident."

"Yet I hardly know how," cried Marianne. "Unless it had been under totally different circumstances. But this is the usual way of heightening alarm where there is nothing to be alarmed at in reality."

"What a sweet woman Lady Middleton is!" said Lucy Steele.

Marianne remained silent; it was impossible for her to utter words she did not feel, however trivial the occasion. Thus, upon Elinor, the entire task of telling lies when politeness required it always fell. She did her best when thus called upon, speaking of Lady Middleton with more warmth than she felt, though with far less enthusiasm than Miss Lucy.

"And Sir John too," cried the elder sister, "what a charming man he is!"

Here too, Miss Dashwood's commendation, being merely simple and just, came forth without éclat. She merely observed that he was perfectly good-humoured and friendly.

"And what a charming little family they have! I never saw such fine children in my life.—I declare I quite doat upon them already, and indeed I am always distractedly fond of children."

"I should guess so," said Elinor, with a smile, "from what I have witnessed this morning."

"I have a notion," said Lucy, "you think the little Middletons rather too much indulged; perhaps they may be the outside of enough; but it is so natural in Lady Middleton. And for my part, I love to see children full of life and spirits; I cannot bear them if they are tame and quiet."

"I confess," replied Elinor, "that while I am at Barton Park, I never think of tame and quiet children with any abhorrence."

A short pause followed this speech, broken first by Miss Steele, who seemed very much disposed for conversation. She now said rather abruptly, "And how do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood? I suppose you were very sorry to leave Sussex."

Surprised by the familiarity of the question, or at least the manner in which it was spoken, Elinor replied that she was.

"Norland is a prodigious beautiful place, is not it?" added Miss Steele.

"We have heard Sir John admire it excessively," said Lucy, who seemed to think some apology necessary for her sister's freedom.

"I think every one *must* admire it," replied Elinor, "whoever has seen the place; though it is not to be supposed that anyone can estimate its beauties as we do."

"And had you a great many smart beaux there? I suppose you have not so many in this part of the world; for my part, I think they are a vast addition always."

"But why should you think," said Lucy, looking ashamed of her sister, "that there are not as many genteel young men in Devonshire as Sussex?"

"Nay, my dear, I'm sure I don't pretend to say that there aren't. I'm sure there's a vast many smart beaux in Exeter; but you know, how could I tell what smart beaux there might be about Norland? And I was only afraid the Miss Dashwoods might find it dull at Barton if they had not so many as they used to have. But perhaps you young ladies may not care about the beaux, and had as lief be without them as with them. For my part, I think they are vastly agreeable, provided they dress smart and behave civil. But I can't bear to see them dirty and nasty. Now there's Mr. Rose at Exeter, a prodigious smart young man, quite a beau, clerk to Mr. Simpson, you know; and yet if you do but meet him of a morning, he is not fit to be seen. I suppose your brother was quite a beau, Miss Dashwood, before he married, as he was so rich?"

"Upon my word," replied Elinor, "I cannot tell you, for I do not perfectly comprehend the meaning of the word. But this I can say, that if he ever was a beau before he married, he is one still, for there is not the smallest alteration in him."

"Oh! dear! one never thinks of married men's being beaux—they have something else to do."

"Lord! Anne," cried her sister, "you can talk of nothing but beaux;—you will make Miss Dashwood believe you think of nothing else." Turning the discourse, she began admiring the house and the furniture.

This specimen of the Miss Steeles was sufficient. The vulgar freedom and folly of the eldest left her no recommendation, and as Elinor was not blinded by the beauty or the shrewd look of the youngest to her want of real elegance and artlessness, she left the house without any wish of knowing them better.

Not so the Miss Steeles. They came from Exeter well-provided with admiration for the use of Sir John Middleton, his family, and all his relations. No niggardly proportion was now dealt out to his fair cousins, whom they declared to be the most beautiful, elegant, accomplished, and agreeable girls they had ever beheld, with whom they were particularly anxious to be better acquainted. To be better acquainted, therefore, Elinor soon found was her inevitable lot. For as Sir John was entirely on the side of the Miss Steeles, their party would be too strong for opposition, and that kind of intimacy must be submitted to which consists of sitting an hour or two together in the same room almost every day. Sir John could do no more; but he did not know that any more was required. To be together was, in his opinion, to be intimate, and while his continual schemes for their meeting were effectual, he had no doubt of their being established friends.

To do him justice, he did everything in his power to promote their unreserve, making the Miss Steeles acquainted with whatever he knew or supposed of his cousins' situations in the most delicate particulars. Elinor had not seen them more than twice before the eldest wished her joy on her sister's having been so lucky as to make a conquest of a very smart beau since she came to Barton.

"'Twill be a fine thing to have her married so young to be sure," said she, "and I hear he is quite a beau, and prodigious handsome. And I hope you may have as good luck yourself soon,—but perhaps you may have a friend in the corner already."

Elinor could not suppose that Sir John would be more nice in proclaiming his suspicions of her regard for Edward than he had been with respect to Marianne. Indeed, it was rather his favourite joke of the two, as being somewhat newer and more conjectural. Since Edward's visit, they had never dined together without his drinking to her best affections with so much significance and so many nods and winks as to excite general attention. The letter F—had likewise been invariably brought forward, found productive of such countless jokes that its character as the wittiest letter in the alphabet had been long established with Elinor.

The Miss Steeles, as she expected, now had all the benefit of these jokes. In the eldest of them, they raised a curiosity to know the name of the gentleman alluded to, which, though often impertinently expressed, was perfectly of a piece with her general inquisitiveness into the concerns of their family. But Sir John did not sport long with the curiosity which he delighted to raise, for he had at least as much pleasure in telling the name

The Miss Steeles, with their boundless energy and lack of refinement, found Marianne increasingly unbearable. Her spirits, already low, had curdled into a sharp intolerance for their vulgarity and inferiority of taste. She met their advances with a chilling indifference that acted as a wall, effectively shutting them out. Yet, it was this very coldness that seemed to spark a peculiar affection in the sisters' hearts, particularly in Lucy. Where Marianne turned away, Lucy stepped forward, seizing every opportunity to engage Elinor in conversation, striving to deepen their acquaintance with an easy, frank manner that masked her true intentions.

Lucy possessed a natural cleverness; her remarks were often just and amusing, making her a delightful companion for a fleeting half-hour. However, Elinor quickly discerned that these powers were unaided by education. Lucy was ignorant and illiterate, her mind lacking the cultivation that would have elevated her talents into something respectable. Elinor pitied this neglect of ability, but she viewed Lucy's lack of delicacy, rectitude, and integrity with far less tenderness. The attentions, assiduous flattery, and calculated efforts Lucy displayed at the Park revealed a thorough want of principle. To Elinor, a woman who joined insincerity with ignorance, whose lack of instruction prevented equality in discourse, and whose conduct toward others rendered her deference valueless, offered no lasting satisfaction.

One afternoon, as they strolled from the park to the cottage, Lucy broke the silence with a question that struck Elinor as oddly specific. "You will think my question an odd one, I dare say," Lucy began, her eyes fixed on Elinor. "But pray, are you personally acquainted with your sister-in-law's mother, Mrs. Ferrars?"

Elinor felt the oddity immediately, her countenance betraying her surprise. "I have never seen Mrs. Ferrars," she replied.

"Indeed!" Lucy exclaimed, feigning wonder. "I thought you must have seen her at Norland sometimes. Then, perhaps, you cannot tell me what sort of a woman she is?"

"No," Elinor returned, carefully shielding her true opinion of Edward's mother and resisting what appeared to be impertinent curiosity. "I know nothing of her."

Lucy watched her closely, her expression shifting to one of wounded innocence. "I am sure you think me very strange for enquiring about her in such a way. Perhaps there are reasons... I wish I might venture, but I hope you will do me the justice of believing I do not mean to be impertinent."

After a civil reply and a few minutes of silence, Lucy resumed the topic with hesitant urgency. "I cannot bear to have you think me impertinently curious. I would rather do anything in the world than be thought so by a person whose good opinion is so well worth having as yours. I should not fear trusting *you*; indeed, I should be glad of your advice on how to manage in such an uncomfortable situation as I am. But since you do not know Mrs. Ferrars, there is no occasion to trouble you further."

"I am sorry I do *not*," Elinor said, astonishment coloring her voice. "If it could be of any use to *you* to know my opinion of her. But I never understood that you were at all connected with that family, and I am surprised at such a serious inquiry into her character."

"You are right to be surprised," Lucy admitted, lowering her gaze with an amiably bashful air. "Mrs. Ferrars is certainly nothing to me at present—but the time *may* come. How soon it will come depends on her, but when we may be very intimately connected."

"Good heavens!" cried Elinor, the shock nearly overwhelming her composure. "What do you mean? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Can you be?" She recoiled slightly at the thought of such a sister-in-law.

"No," Lucy corrected, fixing her eyes on Elinor with intense focus. "Not to Mr. *Robert* Ferrars—I never saw him in my life; but to his eldest brother."

Astonishment, sharp and painful, seized Elinor, though an immediate disbelief fought against it. She stared at Lucy in silent amazement, unable to fathom the reason or object of such a declaration. Her complexion flushed, yet she stood firm in her incredulity, refusing to succumb to hysteria.

"You may well be surprised," Lucy continued, sensing Elinor's turmoil. "He never dropped the smallest hint of it to you or your family, for it was always meant to be a great secret, faithfully kept by me to this hour. Not a soul of my relations knows but Anne. I never should have mentioned it if I had not felt the greatest dependence on your secrecy. I thought my inquiries about Mrs. Ferrars seemed odd, so I felt compelled to explain. I do not think Mr. Ferrars can be displeased when he knows I have trusted you; he holds your family in the highest esteem, regarding you and the other Miss Dashwoods as his own sisters."

Elinor remained silent for a moment, her mind reeling. Forcing herself to speak with a calmness that barely concealed her rising solicitude, she asked, "May I ask if your engagement is of long standing?"

"We have been engaged these four years."

"Four years!" Elinor gasped.

"Yes."

Though greatly shocked, Elinor still struggled to believe it. "I did not know," she said, her voice trembling slightly, "that you were even acquainted until the other day."

"Our acquaintance is of many years' date," Lucy replied smoothly. "He was under my uncle's care for a considerable while."

"My uncle?"

"Yes; Mr. Pratt. Did you never hear him talk of Mr. Pratt?"

"I think I have," Elinor managed, exerting her spirits to maintain control.

"He was four years with my uncle, who lives at Longstaple, near Plymouth. It was there our acquaintance began. My sister and I stayed often with him, and it was there our engagement was formed, though not until a year after he had left as a pupil. He was almost always with us afterwards. I was very unwilling to enter into it without my mother's knowledge and approbation, but I was too young and loved him too well to be prudent. You must have seen enough of him to know he is very capable of making a woman sincerely attached to him."

"Certainly," Elinor answered, though her mind was a chaotic mix of confusion and dread. After a moment's reflection, she added, with a desperate attempt to cling to her belief in Edward's honor, "Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars! I confess I am so totally surprised that I must assume there is some mistake of person or name. We cannot mean the same Mr. Ferrars."

"We can mean no other," Lucy cried, smiling triumphantly. "Mr. Edward Ferrars, the eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars of Park Street, and brother of your sister-in-law, Mrs. John Dashwood. You must allow that I am not likely to be deceived as to the name of the man on whom all my happiness depends."

"It is strange," Elinor murmured, in a state of painful perplexity, "that I should never have heard him mention your name."

"No; considering our situation, it was not strange. Our first care has been secrecy. You knew nothing of me or my family, so there was no occasion to mention my name. And since he was always particularly afraid of his sister suspecting anything, that was reason enough for his silence."

She fell silent. Elinor's sense of security crumbled, though her self-command held firm. "Four years you have been engaged," she said, her voice steady despite the storm within.

"Yes; and heaven knows how much longer we may have to wait. Poor Edward! It puts him quite out of heart." Lucy reached into her pocket and produced a small miniature. "To prevent the possibility of mistake, please look at this face. It does not do him justice, to be sure, but I think you cannot be deceived as to the person it drew for. I have had it for over three years."

She pressed the painting into Elinor's hands. Whatever doubts lingered in Elinor's mind—fear of a hasty decision, a desire to detect falsehood—vanished instantly upon seeing the portrait. It was unmistakably Edward's face. She returned it almost immediately, acknowledging the likeness.

"I have never been able," Lucy continued, "to give him my picture in return, which vexes me greatly, for he has always been so anxious to get it! But I am determined to send it the very first opportunity."

"You are quite in the right," Elinor replied calmly. They walked a few steps in silence before Lucy spoke again.

"I am sure," she said, "I have no doubt in the world of your faithfully keeping this secret. You must know how important it is that it not reach his mother; she would never approve of it, I dare say. I shall have no fortune, and I fancy she is an exceedingly proud woman."

"I certainly did not seek your confidence," Elinor said, "but you do me no more than justice in imagining I may be depended on. Your secret is safe with me; but pardon me if I express some surprise at such an unnecessary communication. You must feel that my knowing of it could not add to its safety."

As she spoke, Elinor looked earnestly at Lucy, hoping to find a crack in her facade, perhaps the falsehood underlying most of her story. But Lucy's countenance remained unchanged, smooth and untroubled.

"I was afraid you would think I was taking a great liberty," Lucy said, her voice dropping to a whisper. "I have not known you long personally, but I have known your family by description for a while. As soon as I saw you, I felt almost as if you were an old acquaintance. In this case, I felt some explanation was due to you after my inquiries about Edward's mother. I am so unfortunate that I have no one whose advice I can ask. Anne is the only one who knows, and she has no judgment at all; indeed, she does me more harm than good. I am in constant fear of her betraying me. She does not know how to hold her tongue, and I was in the greatest fright the other day when Edward's name was mentioned by Sir John, lest she should blurt it all out. You can't think how much I suffer in my mind from it. I only wonder I am alive after what I have suffered for Edward's sake these last four years. Everything in such suspense and uncertainty; seeing him so seldom—we can hardly meet above twice a year. I am sure I wonder my heart is not quite broke."

She pulled out her handkerchief, dabbing at her eyes, but Elinor felt little compassion.

"Sometimes," Lucy continued, after wiping her eyes, "I think it would be better for us both to break off the matter entirely. But then at other times I have not the resolution for it. I cannot bear the thought of making him so miserable, as I know the mere mention of such a thing would do. And on my own account, so dear as he is to me, I don't think I could be equal to it. What would you advise me to do, Miss Dashwood? What would you do yourself?"

"Pardon me," Elinor replied, startled by the directness of the question. "But I can give you no advice under such circumstances. Your own judgment must direct you."

After a few minutes of silence, Lucy resumed. "Of course, his mother must provide for him sometime or other; but poor Edward is so cast down by it! Did you not think him dreadful low-spirited when he was at Barton? He was so miserable when he left us at Longstaple to go to you that I feared you would think him quite ill."

"Did he come from your uncle's, then, when he visited us?"

"Oh, yes; he had been staying a fortnight with us. Did you think he came directly from town?"

"No," Elinor replied, feeling a strange sense of validation at the recollection. "I remember he told us he had been staying a fortnight with some friends near Plymouth." She recalled her own surprise at the time, at his total silence regarding even the names of those friends.

"Did not you think him sadly out of spirits?" Lucy repeated.

"We did, indeed, particularly so when he first arrived."

"I begged him to exert himself for fear you should suspect what was the matter; but it made him so melancholy, not being able to stay more than a fortnight with us, and seeing me so much affected. Poor fellow! I am afraid it is just the same with him now; for he writes in wretched spirits. I heard from him just before I left Exeter." She reached into her pocket again, pulling out a letter and casually showing the direction to Elinor. "You know his hand, I dare say—a charming one it is; but that is not written so well as usual. He was tired, I dare say, for he had just filled the sheet to me as full as possible."

Elinor examined the letter. It was undeniably his hand. Doubt vanished completely. This picture, she had allowed herself to believe, might have been accidentally obtained; it might not have been Edward's gift. But a correspondence by letter could exist only under a positive engagement, authorized by nothing else. For a few moments, she was almost overcome; her heart sank within her, and she could hardly stand. Yet, exertion was indispensable. She struggled resolutely against the oppression of her feelings, and her success was speedy and complete.

"Writing to each other," Lucy said, returning the letter to her pocket, "is the only comfort we have in such long separations. Yes, *I* have one other comfort in his picture, but poor Edward has not even *that*. If he had but my picture, he says he should be easy. I gave him a lock of my hair set in a ring when he was at Longstaple last, and that was some comfort to him, he said, but not equal to a picture. Perhaps you might notice the ring when you saw him?"

"I did," Elinor said, her voice composed but masking

Though Elinor had often questioned the reliability of Lucy's word in the past, the gravity of the current situation rendered such doubt impossible. To invent a falsehood of such magnitude, involving a secret engagement and a ruined reputation, would require a level of audacity that Lucy simply did not possess. Every probability pointed to the truth of her assertion; every piece of evidence—their history at Mr. Pratt's, Edward's recent melancholy demeanor near Plymouth, his vague behavior toward her, and the intimate knowledge Lucy held of Norland—conspired to form an undeniable chain of facts. The letter, the ring, and the picture were no longer mere rumors but tangible proofs of Edward's ill-treatment of her.

For a fleeting moment, anger flared within her, a hot indignation at having been the dupe of such deception. She asked herself if Edward had feigned affection, if his engagement to Lucy was one of the heart. Yet, as quickly as the doubt arose, it was dispelled. Whatever sentiment had once existed between them, it was clearly dead. His affection belonged entirely to her; she could not be deceived about that. Her mother, her sisters, and even Fanny had witnessed his devotion at Norland. It was not a vanity-induced illusion. He loved her. This realization acted as a balm to her wounded spirit, softening the blow and making forgiveness seem not only possible but necessary.

He had been foolish, undeniably so, in allowing his feelings to cloud his judgment while residing at Norland. He could not be excused for that imprudence. But if he had caused her pain, he had inflicted far greater harm upon himself. While she might eventually find peace, his future seemed bleak. Could he truly be happy with Lucy Steele? With a woman who was illiterate, artful, and selfish, lacking the refinement and education that years of rational living had bestowed upon her? The youth of nineteen might have blinded him to these defects, but four years of growth should have opened his eyes. Instead, Lucy's time in inferior society had likely eroded the very simplicity that once made her charming.

Furthermore, the obstacles to their union had grown insurmountable. If his mother's opposition had previously seemed daunting, it was now compounded by the reality that Lucy was inferior in both connection and fortune. Yet, the irony was bitter: while Elinor faced family opposition with dread, Edward found a strange relief in the certainty of it. Melancholy settled over her as she considered his hopeless state. Weeping for him became her primary emotion, overshadowing her own grief. Convinced that she had done nothing to deserve this unhappiness and that Edward retained her esteem, she summoned the strength to conceal the full extent of her despair from her mother and sisters.

Two hours after the news broke, she joined them at dinner with an air of such composed cheerfulness that no one suspected the storm raging within. To spare them the agony of knowing the truth, and to shield herself from their excessive, perhaps unfair, condemnation of Edward, she bore the burden alone. She knew their sorrow would only deepen her own, and their counsel would offer no solace. Alone, bolstered by her own sense, she remained unshaken.

Driven by a need to clarify the situation, Elinor soon felt a strong desire to speak with Lucy again. She needed to hear the details of their engagement anew, to gauge the sincerity of Lucy's feelings, and most importantly, to demonstrate through her calm demeanor that she was no longer a rival but a friend. She suspected Lucy's jealousy; Edward's high praise of her, coupled with Sir John's teasing, suggested it. But even without those hints, Lucy's eagerness to reveal the secret proved her intent: she wished to warn Elinor away. Resolved to honor her principles and avoid Edward, Elinor nonetheless sought to reassure Lucy that her own heart remained unwounded. She believed she could recount the particulars with composure, for there was nothing more painful to hear than what had already been told.

Opportunity, however, was scarce. The weather rarely permitted walks where they might speak privately, and their evenings at the park or cottage were dominated by the noisy games and laughter of Sir John and Lady Middleton, leaving no room for serious discourse. They met to eat, drink, and play, not to converse.

It was only when Sir John called one morning to invite them all to dine at the cottage, citing Lady Middleton's impending solitude, that Elinor saw a potential opening. She anticipated that a quieter gathering, guided by Lady Middleton's gentle manners rather than her husband's boisterous energy, might yield better results. Margaret and Marianne, the latter reluctantly persuaded by her mother, accepted the invitation.

The dinner was, as Elinor predicted, devoid of novelty or interest. The conversation in the drawing room was equally dull, interrupted only by the arrival of the children. Realizing that engaging Lucy's attention here was futile, Elinor waited until the tea things were cleared and the card tables set. As the group prepared for a game of Casino, Lady Middleton remarked kindly to Lucy that she should not work on Annamaria's basket by candlelight, promising to make amends the next day.

Lucy, seizing the moment, declared she would not disappoint the "little angel" and insisted on finishing the basket after supper. Lady Middleton, delighted by her compliance, offered working candles. Lucy immediately moved her table closer, her alacrity suggesting she found no greater joy than crafting a gift for a spoiled child.

Marianne, ever the outlier, refused to play, declaring her hatred for cards and retreating to the piano. Lady Middleton looked relieved, while Elinor smoothly intervened to praise the instrument. Seizing the lull, Elinor proposed that if she were to cut out, she would assist Lucy in rolling her papers, noting that the basket required more work than Lucy could manage alone.

Lucy gratefully accepted the offer, lamenting the prospect of disappointing Annamaria. Elinor joyfully took her seat beside her, utilizing a tact she knew Marianne lacked. Side by side, the two rivals worked in harmony on the same project. The piano, where Marianne was lost in her own music, provided a perfect shield of noise. Under its cover, Elinor felt safe to introduce the subject she had come to discuss, hoping to find the clarity she sought before the evening ended.

Elinor approached the conversation with a steady, albeit guarded, composure. "I would be unworthy of the confidence you have placed in me," she began, "if I did not share your desire to continue this dialogue, nor lack further curiosity regarding its subject. Therefore, I need offer no apology for raising it once more."

"Thank you!" Lucy exclaimed warmly, her relief palpable. "You have broken the ice; my heart is at ease now. I confess I was terrified that what I told you on Monday had offended you."

"Offend me?" Elinor replied with genuine warmth. "How could you possibly imagine that? Believe me, nothing was further from my intention than to give you such an idea. Could there be any motive for your trust that was not honorable and flattering to me?"

"And yet," Lucy countered, her sharp eyes gleaming with a mixture of vulnerability and calculation, "I felt a distinct coldness and displeasure in your manner. It made me quite uncomfortable. I was convinced you were angry with me, and I have been tormenting myself ever since for taking the liberty of troubling you with my affairs. But I am so glad to find it was merely my own fancy, and that you truly do not blame me. If you knew how much solace it was to relieve my heart by speaking to you of what occupies every moment of my life, your compassion would surely make you overlook everything else."

"I can easily believe that it was a great relief to you to acknowledge your situation to me," Elinor said gently. "And you may be assured you shall never have reason to repent it. Your case is indeed unfortunate; you appear surrounded by difficulties, and you will require all your mutual affection to sustain you. Mr. Ferrars, I believe, is entirely dependent on his mother."

"He possesses only two thousand pounds of his own," Lucy explained, her voice dropping slightly. "It would be madness to marry upon that sum, though for my part, I could give up every prospect of more without a sigh. I have always been accustomed to a very small income and could struggle with any poverty for him. Yet, I love him too well to be the selfish instrument of robbing him, perhaps, of all that his mother might bestow if he married to please her. We must wait; it may be for many years. With almost every other man in the world, this would be an alarming prospect, but Edward's affection and constancy are treasures nothing can deprive me of."

"That conviction must be everything to you," Elinor observed carefully, ensuring her countenance remained impassive. "And he is undoubtedly supported by the same trust in yours. Had the strength of your reciprocal attachment failed—as it naturally might, under many circumstances, during a four-year engagement—your situation would indeed have been pitiable."

Lucy looked up, her gaze intense. "Edward's love for me has been thoroughly tested by our long, very long absence since we were first engaged. It has stood the trial so well that I should be unpardonable to doubt it now. I can safely say he has never given me one moment's alarm on that account from the beginning."

Elinor found herself torn between a smile and a sigh at this assertion.

Lucy continued, her tone shifting to one of defensive pride. "I am rather of a jealous temper by nature. Given our different stations in life, his being so much more 'in the world' than I, and our continual separations, I was naturally inclined toward suspicion. Had there been the slightest alteration in his behavior when we met, or any inexplicable lowness of spirits, or if he had spoken more of another lady, or seemed less happy at Longstaple than usual, I would have discovered the truth instantly. I do not claim to be particularly observant in general matters, but in such a case, I am certain I could not be deceived."

*All this,* Elinor thought, *is very pretty; but it can impose upon neither of us.*

After a brief silence, she pressed further. "But what are your views? Or do you have none but that of waiting for Mrs. Ferrars's death—a melancholy and shocking extremity? Is her son determined to submit to this, and to all the tediousness of the years of suspense it may involve, rather than risk her displeasure for a while by owning the truth?"

"If we could be certain it would be only for a while!" Lucy sighed. "But Mrs. Ferrars is a very headstrong, proud woman. In her first fit of anger upon hearing the truth, she would very likely secure every inheritance to Robert. The mere idea of that, for Edward's sake, frightens away all my inclination for hasty measures. And for your own sake too, or you are carrying your disinterestedness beyond reason."

Lucy looked at Elinor again, falling silent.

"Do you know Mr. Robert Ferrars?" Elinor asked.

"Not at all; I never saw him," Lucy replied dismissively. "But I fancy he is very unlike his brother—silly and a great coxcomb."

"A great coxcomb!" repeated Miss Steele, her attention suddenly arrested by the words amidst a pause in Marianne's music. "Oh, they are talking of their favorite beaux, I dare say."

"No sister," cried Lucy, indignant, "you are mistaken there; our favorite beaux are *not* great coxcombs."

"I can answer for it that Miss Dashwood's is not," said Mrs. Jennings, laughing heartily. "He is one of the modestest, prettiest-behaved young men I have ever seen. But as for Lucy, she is such a sly little creature, there is no finding out who *she* likes."

"Oh," cried Miss Steele, casting a significant glance around the room, "I dare say Lucy's beau is quite as modest and pretty-behaved as Miss Dashwood's."

Elinor blushed involuntarily. Lucy bit her lip and shot an angry look at her sister. A mutual silence ensued for some time until Lucy broke it in a lower tone, though Marianne was then providing them with the powerful protection of a magnificent concerto.

"I will honestly tell you of one scheme which has lately come into my head for bringing matters to a conclusion. Indeed, I am bound to let you into the secret, for you are a party concerned. I dare say you have seen enough of Edward to know that he would prefer the church to every other profession. My plan is that he should take orders as soon as he can, and then, through your interest—which I am sure you would be kind enough to use out of friendship for him, and I hope out of some regard to me—your brother might be persuaded to give him the Norland living. I understand it is a very good one, and the present incumbent is not likely to live much longer. That would be enough for us to marry upon, and we might trust to time and chance for the rest."

"I should always be happy," replied Elinor, "to show any mark of my esteem and friendship for Mr. Ferrars; but do you not perceive that my interest on such an occasion would be perfectly unnecessary? He is the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood—that must be recommendation enough to her husband."

"But Mrs. John Dashwood would not much approve of Edward's going into orders."

"Then I rather suspect that my interest would do very little."

They fell silent again for many minutes. Finally, Lucy exclaimed with a deep sigh. "I believe it would be the wisest way to put an end to the business at once by dissolving the engagement. We seem so beset with difficulties on every side that, though it would make us miserable for a time, we should perhaps be happier in the end. But you will not give me your advice, Miss Dashwood?"

"No," answered Elinor, with a smile that concealed very agitated feelings. "On such a subject, I certainly will not. You know very well that my opinion would have no weight with you unless it were on the side of your wishes."

"Indeed you wrong me," replied Lucy with great solemnity. "I know nobody of whose judgment I think so highly as I do of yours. I do really believe that if you were to say to me, 'I advise you by all means to put an end to your engagement with Edward Ferrars; it will be more for the happiness of both of you,' I should resolve upon doing it immediately."

Elinor blushed at the insincerity of Edward's future wife and replied, "This compliment would effectually frighten me from giving any opinion on the subject had I formed one. It raises my influence much too high; the power of dividing two people so tenderly attached is too much for an indifferent person."

"'Tis because you are an indifferent person," said Lucy, with some pique, laying particular stress on those words, "that your judgment might justly have such weight with me. If you could be supposed to be biased in any respect by your own feelings, your opinion would not be worth having."

Elinor deemed it wisest to make no answer, lest they provoke each other to an unsuitable increase of familiarity and unreserve. She was even partly determined never to mention the subject again. Another pause of many minutes followed, and Lucy was still the first to end it.

"Shall you be in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?" she asked with her customary complacency.

"Certainly not."

"I am sorry for that," returned the other, her eyes brightening at the information. "It would have given me such pleasure to meet you there! But I dare say you will go for all that. To be sure, your brother and sister will ask you to come to them."

"It will not be in my power to accept their invitation if they do."

"How unlucky that is! I had quite depended upon meeting you there. Anne and I are to go the latter end of January to some relations who have been wanting us to visit them these several years! But I only go for the sake of seeing Edward. He will be there in February; otherwise, London would have no charms for me; I have not the spirits for it."

Elinor was soon called to the card-table by the conclusion of the first rubber, and the confidential discourse of the two ladies came to an end. Both submitted to the interruption without reluctance, for nothing had been said to make them dislike each other less than they had done before. Elinor sat down to the cards with a melancholy persuasion that Edward was not only without affection for the person destined to be his wife, but that he had not even the chance of being tolerably happy in marriage, which sincere affection on her side would have secured. For self-interest alone could induce a woman to keep a man to an engagement of which she seemed so thoroughly aware that he was weary.

From this time forth, the subject was never revived by Elinor. When entered upon by Lucy, who seldom missed an opportunity of introducing it and was particularly careful to inform her confidante of her happiness whenever she received a letter from Edward, it was treated by the former with calmness and caution, and dismissed as soon as civility would allow. She felt such conversations to be an indulgence which Lucy did not deserve, and which were dangerous to herself.

The visit of the Miss Steeles at Barton Park was lengthened far beyond what the first invitation implied. Their favor increased; they could not be spared; Sir John would not hear of their going. In spite of their numerous and long-arranged engagements in Exeter, and in spite of the absolute necessity of returning to fulfill them immediately—a necessity in full force at the end of every week—they were prevailed upon to stay nearly two months at the park, assisting in the due celebration of that festival which requires a more than ordinary share of private balls and large dinners to proclaim its importance.

Mrs. Jennings, though accustomed to spending much of the year amidst the homes of her children and friends, maintained a permanent residence of her own. Since the passing of her husband, a successful merchant of a less fashionable quarter, she had made a house near Portman Square her winter sanctuary. As January approached, her thoughts turned inevitably toward this retreat, and one day, she abruptly—and to the surprise of all present—invited the elder Miss Dashwoods to join her.

Elinor, failing to notice the stormy shift in her sister's expression or the fervent light in her eyes that betrayed a deep desire to accept, offered an immediate and polite refusal. She spoke for both sisters, citing their firm resolve not to abandon their mother during the winter season. Mrs. Jennings received this rejection with mild astonishment, only to renew her plea with renewed vigor.

"Oh, Lord! I am certain your mother can spare you quite easily," she declared warmly. "I truly beg you to honor me with your company; I have quite set my heart upon it. Do not imagine you will be a burden; I shall make no special accommodations for you. It will simply mean sending Betty by coach—a sum I can certainly afford. The three of us shall travel comfortably in my chaise. And once in town, if you prefer not to follow my exact footsteps, you are welcome to stay with one of my daughters. I am sure your mother will not object; having successfully dispatched my own children, she will likely view me as the ideal guardian for you. And if I fail to secure a marriage for either of you before I am done with you, it shall not be my fault. I shall speak highly of you to every eligible young man you meet."

"I suspect," interjected Sir John, "that Miss Marianne would welcome such an arrangement, provided her elder sister agreed. It is a pity she is denied even a small measure of pleasure simply because Miss Dashwood objects. I would advise you both to depart for town the moment you tire of Barton, without informing Miss Dashwood of your plans."

"Nay!" cried Mrs. Jennings. "I shall be overjoyed to have Miss Marianne's company, regardless of whether Miss Dashwood accompanies her. The more the merrier, as they say. It would be far more comfortable for them to be together; should they grow weary of me, they could confide in one another and laugh at my peculiarities behind my back. But I must have one, if not both of them. Good heavens, how can I endure poking around alone, accustomed as I have been until this winter to the company of Charlotte? Come, Miss Marianne, let us seal this bargain with a handshake. If Miss Dashwood changes her mind later, so much the better."

"I thank you, ma'am, most sincerely," Marianne replied with evident emotion. "Your invitation guarantees my eternal gratitude. To accept it would bring me such happiness—perhaps the greatest joy I am capable of feeling. Yet, my mother, my dearest and kindest mother... I acknowledge the validity of Elinor's argument. Were her happiness or comfort diminished by our absence? Oh, no! Nothing could tempt me to leave her. It must not, and shall not, become a struggle."

Mrs. Jennings reiterated her assurance that Mrs. Dashwood could easily spare them. Elinor, now fully understanding her sister's state of mind and recognizing how her eagerness to reunite with Willoughby had rendered her indifferent to nearly everything else, ceased her direct opposition. She deferred the decision to her mother, though she doubted she would find support in her efforts to prevent a journey she disapproved of for Marianne, and one she herself wished to avoid for personal reasons. Whatever Marianne desired, her mother was eager to facilitate; she could not expect to sway a woman who had never learned to distrust the affections of a man like Willoughby. Furthermore, Elinor dared not reveal her own reasons for hesitating to visit London. That Marianne, so fastidious in nature and thoroughly acquainted with Mrs. Jennings' mannerisms, should overlook every discomfort and disregard anything that might wound her sensitive feelings in pursuit of a single object, was a testament to the intensity of her desire that Elinor found difficult to witness, despite everything that had transpired.

Upon learning of the invitation, Mrs. Dashwood, convinced that the trip would prove delightful for both her daughters and perceiving through her own affectionate attentiveness just how deeply Marianne's heart was involved, refused to hear of their declining the offer on her account. She insisted they both accept immediately and began to anticipate, with her characteristic cheerfulness, the various advantages such a separation would bring.

"I am delighted with the plan," she exclaimed. "It is precisely what I could have wished. Margaret and I shall benefit as much as you. Once you and the Middletons depart, we shall settle into a quiet, happy routine with our books and music! You will find Margaret greatly improved upon your return. I have even planned alterations to your bedrooms that can now be executed without inconvenience. It is right that you should visit London; every young woman of your station ought to be familiar with its manners and amusements. You will be under the care of a kind, motherly woman, and I have no doubt of her goodness. Moreover, you will likely see your brother. Regardless of his faults—or those of his wife, considering his lineage—I cannot bear the thought of you remaining so estranged from one another."

"Though you act with your usual concern for our happiness," Elinor remarked gently, "there remains one objection which, in my opinion, cannot be so easily dismissed."

Marianne's countenance fell instantly.

"And what," asked Mrs. Dashwood, "is my dear, prudent Elinor suggesting now? What formidable obstacle is she bringing forward? Do not speak of the expense."

"My objection is this," Elinor continued calmly. "While I hold Mrs. Jennings' heart in high regard, her society offers us little pleasure, and her protection affords us no social standing."

"That is entirely true," her mother replied. "However, apart from the company of others, you will have little interaction with her anyway, and you will almost always appear in public accompanied by Lady Middleton."

"If Elinor is deterred by her dislike of Mrs. Jennings," Marianne interjected, "it need not prevent ME from accepting. I have no such scruples, and I am certain I could endure every unpleasantness with minimal effort."

Elinor could not suppress a smile at this display of indifference toward the manners of a woman she had often struggled to persuade Marianne to treat with basic politeness. Resolving internally that if her sister persisted, she would go as well—deeming it improper to leave Marianne solely to her own judgment or to subject Mrs. Jennings to Marianne's capricious whims for the sake of domestic comfort—she found her determination reinforced by the knowledge that Edward Ferrars, according to Lucy, would not arrive in town until February. Their visit could thus be concluded without unreasonable delay.

"I will have you BOTH go," Mrs. Dashwood declared. "These objections are nonsensical. You will find great pleasure in London, especially in each other's company. If Elinor would ever condescend to anticipate enjoyment, she would foresee it from many sources; perhaps she expects some from improving her acquaintance with her sister-in-law's family."

Elinor had long wished for an opportunity to subtly weaken her mother's dependence on the attachments of Edward and herself, hoping the eventual revelation of the truth would be less shocking. Seizing this moment, though she felt the attempt was nearly hopeless, she forced herself to begin her design with as much composure as possible. "I like Edward Ferrars very much and shall always be glad to see him," she said. "But regarding the rest of the family, it is a matter of perfect indifference to me whether I am ever known to them or not."

Mrs. Dashwood smiled and said nothing. Marianne raised her eyes in astonishment, and Elinor realized she might as well have remained silent.

After little further discussion, it was settled that the invitation would be fully accepted. Mrs. Jennings received the news with immense joy, offering countless assurances of kindness and care; nor was it merely a pleasure for her. Sir John was equally delighted; for a man whose greatest fear was solitude, the addition of two companions to his London household was a significant gain. Even Lady Middleton managed to feign delight, a feat requiring considerable effort on her part. As for the Miss Steeles, particularly Lucy, they had never been happier in their lives than this intelligence made them.

Elinor submitted to the arrangement that contradicted her wishes with less reluctance than she had anticipated. Regarding herself, it became a matter of indifference whether she traveled to town or not. Seeing her mother so thoroughly pleased and her sister so visibly exhilarated—restored to her usual animation and elevated to a gaiety exceeding her normal self—Elinor could not be dissatisfied with the cause, nor did she allow herself to doubt the consequences.

Marianne's joy surpassed mere happiness; her spirits were so perturbed and her eagerness to depart so intense that only her unwillingness to leave her mother served as a balm to her calm. At the moment of parting, her grief was excessive. Her mother's sorrow was hardly less, and Elinor was the only one of the three who seemed to regard the separation as anything short of eternal.

Their departure occurred in the first week of January. The Middletons were to follow within a week, while the Miss Steeles remained at the park, intending to leave only with the rest of the family.

Elinor found herself seated beside Mrs. Jennings in the carriage, embarking on a journey to London as her guest, yet she could not suppress a profound sense of disquiet regarding her own position. Their acquaintance was so brief, their natures so disparate in age and temperament, and her objections to such an arrangement so numerous just days prior! Yet, swept away by the same youthful ardor that animated Marianne and her mother, Elinor had allowed these reservations to dissolve. Now, however, staring into the radiant, delirious hope that illuminated Marianne's face, Elinor felt the stark emptiness of her own prospects. Her heart, despite its lingering doubts about Willoughby's fidelity, ached at the sheer vibrancy of her sister's anticipation. She envied Marianne the animating object of her gaze, the tangible possibility of happiness, and secretly wished she could trade her own cheerless state for even a fraction of Marianne's joy. She knew, with a chilling certainty, that a mere handful of days would reveal Willoughby's true intentions; he was likely already in town. Marianne's impatience to depart spoke volumes of her reliance on finding him there. Consequently, Elinor resolved to be a vigilant sentinel: she would gather every scrap of intelligence regarding his character through her own observation and the gossip of others, and she would scrutinize his behavior toward her sister with zealous attention. If her findings were unfavorable, she would be prepared to shatter Marianne's illusions; if favorable, she would strive to banish her own selfish regrets and find satisfaction in her sister's bliss.

The journey lasted three days, during which Marianne's conduct offered a glimpse of the future: a woman lost in her own reveries, speaking only to exclaim over picturesque landscapes to her sister. To compensate for this silence, Elinor assumed the role of the attentive hostess, engaging Mrs. Jennings in conversation, sharing laughter, and listening patiently. In return, Mrs. Jennings showered them with kindness, fussing over their comfort and lamenting only that she could not force them to dine on salmon instead of cod. By three o'clock on the final day, they reached London, eager to escape the carriage and bask in the warmth of a good fire.

Their lodgings were handsome and comfortably appointed, a former residence of Charlotte Palmer, evidenced by a landscape painting in colored silks hanging above the mantelpiece—a testament to her seven years at a prestigious school. With dinner hours away, Elinor took the opportunity to write to her mother. Almost simultaneously, Marianne began her own correspondence. "I am writing home, Marianne," Elinor noted. "Had you not better defer your letter for a day or two?"

"I am *not* going to write to my mother," Marianne replied sharply, clearly wishing to evade further inquiry. Elinor said nothing, but the deduction was immediate: Marianne was writing to Willoughby. The realization that they must be engaged, however mysteriously conducted, brought a strange, quiet pleasure to Elinor, and she resumed her letter with renewed vigor. Marianne's note was brief, folded, sealed, and directed with frantic speed. Elinor discerned a large 'W' in the address. Before the footman could answer the bell, Marianne instructed him to send the letter via the two-penny post. The matter was settled.

Marianne's spirits remained high, yet a fluttering agitation prevented her from sharing her joy with her sister, growing more intense as evening approached. She barely touched her dinner and later paced the drawing room, anxiously listening for the rumble of carriages. Elinor was relieved that Mrs. Jennings, absorbed in her own room, witnessed little of this turmoil. When tea was served, Marianne had already been thwarted by several knocks at neighboring doors until a loud, unmistakable rap echoed through the house. Elinor felt certain it signaled Willoughby's arrival. Marianne sprang up and moved toward the door. Silence fell, unbearable in its weight. She opened the door, stepped onto the landing, and listened for half a minute before returning to the room, trembling with the conviction that she had heard him. In her ecstasy, she cried out, "Oh, Elinor, it is Willoughby, indeed it is!" ready to throw herself into his arms, only to be met by the appearance of Colonel Brandon.

The blow was too severe to bear calmly, and Marianne fled the room. Elinor felt a pang of disappointment, yet her regard for Colonel Brandon ensured his welcome. She was particularly stung that a man so fond of her sister perceived only her grief and disappointment in his presence. She instantly recognized that he had noticed Marianne's departure, observing her with such astonishment and concern that he barely remembered the civility due to herself.

"Is your sister ill?" he asked.

Elinor answered with distress, attributing Marianne's behavior to headaches, low spirits, and fatigue. He listened with earnest attention, then, seemingly recalling himself, shifted the conversation to his pleasure at seeing them in London, asking routine questions about their journey and old friends.

In this calm, uninterested manner, they conversed, both out of spirits and distracted by thoughts elsewhere. Elinor desperately wanted to ask if Willoughby was in town but feared causing pain by inquiring about his rival. Finally, she asked if he had been in London since she last saw him.

"Yes," he replied, with evident embarrassment, "almost ever since; I have been once or twice at Delaford for a few days, but it has never been in my power to return to Barton."

These words, delivered with such hesitation, instantly recalled the circumstances of his departure from Barton and the unease they had caused Mrs. Jennings. Elinor feared her question had revealed a curiosity she did not possess.

Mrs. Jennings soon entered, her usual noisy cheerfulness intact. "Oh! Colonel," she exclaimed, "I am monstrous glad to see you—sorry I could not come before... I have been forced to look about me and settle my matters... I have been as busy as a bee ever since dinner! But pray, Colonel, how came you to conjure out that I should be in town today?"

"I had the pleasure of hearing it at Mr. Palmer's, where I have been dining."

"Oh, you did; well, and how do they all do at their house? How does Charlotte do? I warrant you she is a fine size by this time."

"Mrs. Palmer appeared quite well, and I am commissioned to tell you, that you will certainly see her to-morrow."

"Ay, to be sure... Well, Colonel, I have brought two young ladies with me, you see—that is, you see but one of them now, but there is another somewhere. Your friend, Miss Marianne, too—which you will not be sorry to hear. I do not know what you and Mr. Willoughby will do between you about her. Ay, it is a fine thing to be young and handsome. Well! I was young once, but I never was very handsome—worse luck for me. However, I got a very good husband, and I don't know what the greatest beauty can do more.
