Ah! poor man! he has been dead these eight years and better. But Colonel, where have you been to since we parted? And how does your business go on? Come, come, let's have no secrets among friends."

He responded with his customary mildness, offering no satisfying answers. Elinor began preparing the tea, and Marianne was obliged to reappear. After her entrance, Colonel Brandon grew thoughtful and silent, and Mrs. Jennings could not persuade him to stay long. No other visitors arrived that evening, and the ladies unanimously agreed to retire early.

Marianne rose the next morning with recovered spirits and a happy countenance. The disappointment of the previous night seemed forgotten in the anticipation of the day's events. Hardly had they finished breakfast when Mrs. Palmer's barouche stopped at the door. Soon, she burst into the room, laughing, delighted to see them all, struggling to decide whether she derived more pleasure from meeting her mother or the Dashwood sisters. She was surprised by their visit to town, though she had expected it; angry that they had accepted her mother's invitation after declining her own, yet she claimed she could never forgive them if they hadn't come.

"Mr. Palmer will be so happy to see you," she said. "What do you think he said when he heard of your coming with Mama? I forget what it was now, but it was something so droll!"

After an hour of comfortable chat and causeless laughter, Mrs. Palmer proposed that they accompany her to some shops where she had business. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor readily agreed, and Marianne, though initially reluctant, was induced to join them.

Throughout the shopping expedition, Marianne was constantly on the watch. In Bond Street especially, her eyes scanned the crowd in constant inquiry. In every shop, her mind was abstracted from the items before them, ignoring everything that interested her companions. Restless and dissatisfied everywhere, her sister could never obtain her opinion on any purchase. She derived no pleasure from anything; she was merely impatient to return home, struggling to contain her vexation at the tediousness of Mrs. Palmer, whose eye was caught by every pretty, expensive, or new item. Wild to buy everything, she dawdled in rapture and indecision.

It was late in the morning before they returned home. No sooner had they entered than Marianne flew eagerly upstairs. When Elinor followed, she found Marianne turning from the table with a sorrowful countenance, declaring that no Willoughby had been there.

"Has no letter been left here for me since we went out?" she asked the footman who entered with the parcels.

He answered in the negative. "Are you quite sure of it?" she insisted. "Are you certain that no servant, no porter has left any letter or note?"

The man confirmed that none had been left.

"How very odd!" she murmured in a low, disappointed voice, turning to the window.

"How odd, indeed!" Elinor repeated within herself, regarding her sister with deep unease. "If she had not known him to be in town, she would not have written to him; she would have written to Combe Magna. And if he is in town, how odd that he should neither come nor write! Oh! my dear mother, you must be wrong in permitting an engagement between a daughter so young, a man so little known, to be carried on in such a doubtful, mysterious manner! I long to inquire; and how will my interference be borne."

After some consideration, Elinor determined that if appearances remained as unpleasant as they were, she would represent to her mother the absolute necessity of a serious inquiry into the affair.

Mrs. Palmer and two elderly ladies of Mrs. Jennings's acquaintance dined with them. Mrs. Palmer left soon after tea to fulfill her evening engagements, leaving Elinor to assist in setting up a whist table for the others. Marianne was of no use on such occasions, as she would never learn the game. Though her time was at her disposal, the evening was no more productive of pleasure for her than for Elinor. It was spent in the agonizing anxiety of expectation and the sharp pain of disappointment. She occasionally attempted to read, but the book was soon discarded, and she returned to the more absorbing employment of pacing across the room, pausing only at the window in hopes of distinguishing the long-awaited rap.

"If this fine weather persists," remarked Mrs. Jennings as they gathered for breakfast the following morning, "Sir John will be miserable to depart from Barton next week. It is a tragedy for men of sport to lose a single day's pleasure. Poor fellows! I always feel a pang for them; they seem to carry the loss so deeply."

"That is precisely true," cried Marianne, her voice brightening as she hurried to the window to inspect the sky. "I hadn't considered that angle. This weather will keep countless sportsmen rooted to the countryside."

It was a fortunate thought; her spirits, previously dim, were instantly restored. "It is delightful weather for *them* indeed," she continued, settling into her chair with a radiant countenance. "They must be enjoying it immensely! But" (a flicker of anxiety crossing her face) "it cannot be expected to endure. At this season, following such relentless rain, we shall surely see little more. The frosts will arrive soon, likely with severity. Perhaps within a day or two; this extreme mildness cannot last—nay, perhaps tonight itself will freeze!"

"At any rate," interjected Elinor, hoping to veil her sister's anxious calculations from her mother's gaze, "I am certain Sir John and Lady Middleton will be back in town by the end of next week."

"Ay, my dear, I warrant it. Mary always gets her own way."

"And now," Elinor thought silently, "she will dispatch a letter to Combe by today's post."

Whether she did or not, the letter was written and sent with a discretion that evaded all of Elinor's watchful scrutiny. Regardless of the truth, and though Elinor felt no profound contentment regarding the matter, she could not be entirely uncomfortable while Marianne was in high spirits. Marianne was buoyant, delighted by the mild air, and even more so by her anticipation of the coming frost.

The morning was largely devoted to calling on Mrs. Jennings's acquaintances to announce their presence in town. Throughout the visits, Marianne remained engrossed in tracking the wind's direction, scrutinizing the shifting clouds, and envisioning atmospheric changes.

"Do you not find it colder than this morning, Elinor? There seems a marked difference. I can barely keep my hands warm in my muff. It wasn't so yesterday, I believe. The clouds appear to be parting; the sun will break through momentarily, and we shall have a clear afternoon."

Elinor felt alternately amused and pained, yet Marianne persisted, interpreting every flicker of the firelight and every nuance of the morning sky as a sure sign of impending frost.

The Dashwood sisters found no greater dissatisfaction with Mrs. Jennings's lifestyle or circle of friends than with her treatment of them, which was invariably kind. Her household arrangements were conducted on the most generous scale, and aside from a few old city friends whom, to Lady Middleton's regret, she had never abandoned, she visited no one whose acquaintance might disturb the feelings of her young cousins. Delighted to find herself more comfortably situated than anticipated, Elinor was willing to trade the lack of genuine enjoyment in their evening gatherings—which, whether at home or elsewhere, devolved solely on cards—for the comfort of their surroundings.

Colonel Brandon, holding a general invitation, visited almost daily. He came to admire Marianne and converse with Elinor, who often found greater satisfaction in his company than in any other daily occurrence, yet she observed with deep concern his unrelenting attachment to her sister. She feared it was a strengthening bond. It grieved her to witness the earnestness with which he watched Marianne, and his spirits were noticeably lower than they had been at Barton.

About a week after their arrival, it became certain that Willoughby had also arrived in London. His calling card lay on the table when they returned from their morning drive.

"Good heavens!" exclaimed Marianne. "He has been here while we were out!"

Elinor, rejoiced to confirm his presence in London, ventured to say, "Be assured, he will call again tomorrow." But Marianne seemed hardly to hear her, and upon Mrs. Jennings's entrance, she snatched up the precious card and fled the room.

This event, while lifting Elinor's spirits, restored to her sister all her former agitation and more. From that moment, her mind knew no peace; the expectation of seeing him every hour rendered her unfit for anything else. She insisted on remaining behind the next morning while the others went out.

Elinor's thoughts were filled with speculations on what might be transpiring in Berkeley Street during their absence, but a fleeting glance at her sister upon their return sufficed to inform her that Willoughby had paid no second visit. Just then, a note was delivered and placed on the table.

"For me!" cried Marianne, stepping forward hastily.

"No, ma'am, for my mistress."

But Marianne, unconvinced, seized it immediately.

"It is indeed for Mrs. Jennings; how provoking!"

"You are expecting a letter, then?" said Elinor, unable to remain silent any longer.

"Yes, a little—not much."

After a brief pause, "You have no confidence in me, Marianne."

"Nay, Elinor, this reproach from *you*—you who have confidence in no one!"

"Me!" returned Elinor, somewhat confused; "indeed, Marianne, I have nothing to tell."

"Nor I," answered Marianne with energy, "our situations are then alike. We have neither of us anything to tell; you, because you do not communicate, and I, because I conceal nothing."

Distressed by this accusation of reserve against herself, which she was not at liberty to dismiss, Elinor did not know how to press for greater openness from Marianne under such circumstances.

Mrs. Jennings soon appeared, and the note being handed to her, she read it aloud. It was from Lady Middleton, announcing their arrival in Conduit Street the previous night and requesting the company of her mother and cousins the following evening. Business on Sir John's part and a violent cold on Lady Middleton's prevented their calling at Berkeley Street. The invitation was accepted; yet when the appointed hour approached, and it became necessary, out of common civility to Mrs. Jennings, that they both attend her on such a visit, Elinor found it difficult to persuade her sister to go, for she had still seen nothing of Willoughby and was therefore no less disinclined to seek amusement abroad than unwilling to risk his calling again in her absence.

Elinor discovered, upon the evening's conclusion, that disposition is not materially altered by a change of residence, for although scarcely settled in town, Sir John had managed to gather nearly twenty young people around him and amuse them with a ball. This was an affair, however, of which Lady Middleton disapproved. In the country, an unpremeditated dance was perfectly acceptable; but in London, where the reputation for elegance was more vital and harder to attain, it was risking too much for the gratification of a few girls to have it known that Lady Middleton had hosted a small dance of eight or nine couples, accompanied by two violins and a mere side-board collation.

Mr. and Mrs. Palmer were among the guests. From the former, whom they had not seen since their arrival in town, as he carefully avoided any appearance of attention to his mother-in-law and thus never approached her, they received no mark of recognition upon entering. He glanced at them slightly, seemingly unaware of their identity, and merely nodded to Mrs. Jennings from across the room. Marianne cast one glance around the apartment as she entered; it was sufficient—he was not there—and she sat down, equally ill-disposed to receive or communicate pleasure. After they had been assembled for about an hour, Mr. Palmer sauntered toward the Miss Dashwoods to express his surprise at seeing them in town, though Colonel Brandon had been first informed of their arrival at his house, and he himself had remarked something very droll upon hearing they were coming.

"I thought you were both in Devonshire," he said.

"Did you?" replied Elinor.

"When do you go back again?"

"I do not know." And thus ended their discourse.

Never had Marianne been so unwilling to dance in her life as she was that evening, nor so fatigued by the exercise. She complained of it as they returned to Berkeley Street.

"Aye, aye," said Mrs. Jennings, "we know the reason of all that very well; if a certain person who shall be nameless had been there, you would not have been a bit tired: and to say the truth, it was not very pretty of him not to give you the meeting when he was invited."

"Invited!" cried Marianne.

"So my daughter Middleton told me, for it seems Sir John met him somewhere in the street this morning." Marianne said no more, but looked exceedingly hurt. Impatient in this situation to do something that might lead to her sister's relief, Elinor resolved to write the next morning to her mother, hoping that by awakening her fears for Marianne's health, she would procure those inquiries which had been so long delayed; and she was still more eagerly bent on this measure by perceiving, after breakfast on the morrow, that Marianne was again writing to Willoughby, for she could not suppose it to be addressed to any other person.

About midday, Mrs. Jennings went out alone on business, and Elinor began her letter immediately, while Marianne, too restless for employment and too anxious for conversation, walked from one window to another or sat by the fire in melancholy meditation. Elinor was very earnest in her appeal to her mother, relating everything that had passed, her suspicions of Willoughby's inconstancy, and urging her by every plea of duty and affection to demand from Marianne an account of her real situation regarding him.

Her letter was scarcely finished when a knock foretold a visitor, and Colonel Brandon was announced. Marianne, who had seen him from the window and who hated company of any kind, left the room before he entered. He looked more than usually grave, and though expressing satisfaction at finding Miss Dashwood alone, as if he had something particular to tell her, he sat for some time without speaking. Elinor, persuaded that he had some communication to make concerning her sister, impatiently awaited its opening. It was not the first time she had felt such a conviction; more than once before, beginning with observations like "your sister looks unwell today" or "your sister seems out of spirits," he had appeared on the verge of disclosing or inquiring about something specific. After a pause of several minutes, their silence was broken by his asking her, in a voice of some agitation, when she was to congratulate her on the acquisition of a brother? Elinor was unprepared for such a question and, having no answer ready, was obliged to adopt the simple and common expedient of asking what he meant.

He tried to smile as he replied, "Your sister's engagement to Mr. Willoughby is very generally known."

"It cannot be generally known," returned Elinor, "for her own family do not know it."

He looked surprised and said, "I beg your pardon; I am afraid my inquiry has been impertinent; but I had not supposed any secrecy intended, as they openly correspond, and their marriage is universally talked of."

"How can that be? By whom can you have heard it mentioned?"

"By many—by some of whom you know nothing, by others with whom you are most intimate, Mrs. Jennings, Mrs. Palmer, and the Middletons. But still I might not have believed it, for where the mind is perhaps rather unwilling to be convinced, it will always find something to support its doubts, if I had not, when the servant let me in today, accidentally seen a letter in his hand, directed to Mr. Willoughby in your sister's writing. I came to inquire, but I was convinced before I could ask the question. Is everything finally settled? Is it impossible to—? But I have no right, and I could have no chance of succeeding. Excuse me, Miss Dashwood. I believe I have been wrong in saying so much, but I hardly know what to do, and on your prudence I have the strongest dependence. Tell me that it is all absolutely resolved on, that any attempt, that in short concealment, if concealment be possible, is all that remains."

These words, which conveyed to Elinor a direct avowal of his love for her sister, affected her profoundly. She was not immediately able to speak, and even when her spirits were recovered, she debated for a short time on the answer it would be most proper to give. The real state of things between Willoughby and her sister was so little known to herself that, in endeavoring to explain it, she might be as liable to say too much as too little. Yet, as she was convinced that Marianne's affection for Willoughby left no hope for Colonel Brandon's success, whatever the event of that affection might be, and at the same time wished to shield her conduct from censure, she thought it most prudent and kind, after some consideration, to say more than she really knew or believed. She acknowledged, therefore, that though she had never been informed by themselves of the terms on which they stood with each other, of their mutual affection she had no doubt, and of their correspondence she was not astonished to hear.

He listened to her with silent attention, and upon her ceasing to speak, rose directly from his seat. After saying in a voice of emotion, "To your sister I wish all imaginable happiness; to Willoughby that he may endeavour to deserve her," he took his leave and departed.

Elinor derived no comforting feelings from this conversation to lessen the uneasiness of her mind on other points; she was left, on the contrary, with a melancholy impression of Colonel Brandon's unhappiness, and was prevented even from wishing it removed by her anxiety for the very event that must confirm it.

The days that followed were marked by a heavy, suspended silence. Willoughby neither visited nor wrote, leaving Elinor with no cause to regret her decision to seek her mother's counsel. By the week's end, the family was summoned to attend Lady Middleton at a gathering, an event from which Mrs. Jennings was excused due to the illness of her youngest child. Marianne, utterly despondent, cared little for her appearance or the propriety of attendance; she seemed equally indifferent to going or staying. After tea, she sat by the drawing-room fire, rigid and unchanging, lost in a private torment that rendered her blind to Elinor's presence. Only when they were summoned to the door did she start as if waking from a trance, having momentarily forgotten the world outside.

Upon arriving at the destination, they navigated the crowded carriages and ascended the stairs into a hall resplendent with light and stifling heat. The room was packed, a suffocating sea of society. After offering the requisite courtesies to the hostess, they were swept into the throng, adding to the oppressive atmosphere. While Lady Middleton engaged in a game of Cassino, Marianne, lacking the spirit to move, and Elinor, fortunate in securing nearby seats, settled themselves close to the table.

It was not long before Elinor spotted Willoughby, standing a few yards away in animated conversation with a fashionable young woman. He caught her glance and bowed, yet made no attempt to approach Marianne, despite clearly seeing her, and continued his discourse. Elinor turned instinctively to Marianne, hoping the sight would pass unnoticed. It was then that Marianne first laid eyes upon him. Her face flushed with sudden, overwhelming joy, she would have rushed toward him had not her sister's hand firmly restrained her.

"Good heavens!" Marianne cried out, her composure shattering. "He is there—he is there! Why does he not look at me? Why cannot I speak to him?"

"Be composed, pray," Elinor urged, her voice tight with anxiety. "Do not reveal your feelings to everyone here. Perhaps he has not yet seen you."

But this was a lie Marianne could not believe, nor could she summon the strength to accept. She sat in an agony of impatience that twisted every feature of her face. Finally, Willoughby turned and regarded them both. Marianne sprang up, calling his name with affectionate fervor, and extended her hand. He approached, addressing Elinor rather than Marianne, seemingly determined to avoid her gaze, and inquired hurriedly after Mrs. Dashwood and their time in London. Elinor, robbed of her wits by the shock, stood speechless. Her sister's emotions, however, poured forth immediately. Her face burned crimson as she exclaimed, "Good God, Willoughby! What is the meaning of this? Have you received my letters? Will you not shake hands with me?"

He could not evade the contact, yet her touch seemed painful to him; he held her hand for only a fleeting moment. Throughout the encounter, he appeared to be struggling desperately to maintain his composure. Elinor watched his face, noting a gradual return to tranquility. After a brief pause, he spoke calmly.

"I did myself the honor of calling in Berkeley Street last Tuesday," he said, "but regretted greatly that I was not fortunate enough to find you and Mrs. Jennings at home. I trust my card was not lost."

"But have you not received my notes?" Marianne shrieked, her anxiety wild and frantic. "There must be some mistake—some dreadful mistake! What can be the meaning of it? Tell me, Willoughby; for heaven's sake, tell me what is wrong?"

He offered no reply. His complexion drained, and his embarrassment returned in full force. Yet, as if sensing the gaze of the young lady with whom he had been speaking, he felt compelled to exert himself. Recovering his poise, he replied, "Yes, I had the pleasure of receiving the information of your arrival in town, which you were so good as to send me," before turning hastily away with a slight bow to join his friend.

Marianne, now looking dreadfully pale and unable to stand, sank back into her chair. Elinor, fearing she might faint, shielded her from the prying eyes of the crowd while administering lavender water to revive her.

"Go to him, Elinor!" Marianne cried the moment she could speak. "Force him to come to me! Tell him I must see him again—must speak to him instantly. I cannot rest; I shall have no peace until this is explained. Some dreadful misunderstanding must exist. Oh, go to him this very moment."

"How can that be done? No, my dearest Marianne, you must wait. This is not the place for explanations. Wait only till tomorrow."

With great difficulty, Elinor prevented her from following him herself. To persuade Marianne to check her agitation, to wait with the appearance of composure until she could speak to him privately and effectively, was impossible. Marianne continued to yield to the misery of her feelings, murmuring exclamations of wretchedness. Soon, Elinor saw Willoughby leave the room via the staircase. Telling Marianne he was gone, she urged the impossibility of another meeting that evening as a final plea for her to remain calm. Instantly, Marianne begged Elinor to entreat Lady Middleton to take them home, claiming she was too miserable to stay another minute.

Though Lady Middleton was mid-game, she was too polite to object to Marianne's distress. Making over her cards to a friend, the sisters departed as soon as a carriage could be secured. Not a word was spoken on the journey back to Berkeley Street. Marianne was in a silent agony, too overwhelmed even for tears. Fortunately, Mrs. Jennings had not yet returned, allowing them to go straight to their room, where hartshorn helped restore Marianne to herself. She was quickly undressed and tucked into bed, desiring solitude. Elinor left her to wait for Mrs. Jennings, finding time in the quiet to reflect on the day's events.

She could not doubt that an engagement had once existed between Willoughby and Marianne, nor that Willoughby was now weary of it. However Marianne might still nourish her own hopes, Elinor could not attribute such behavior to mere mistake or misapprehension. Nothing short of a thorough change of sentiment could explain it. Her indignation would have been far greater had she not witnessed the evident embarrassment that spoke of a consciousness of his own misconduct; this prevented her from believing him so unprincipled as to have toyed with her sister's affections from the start without a design worthy of investigation. Absence might have weakened his regard, and convenience might have driven him to overcome it, but that such a regard had once existed was a truth she could not bring herself to deny.

As for Marianne, reflecting on the pain such an unhappy meeting must have caused, and the even severer suffering that awaited her in its probable consequences, filled Elinor with the deepest concern. Her own situation seemed preferable in comparison; while she might be separated from Edward in the future, she could still esteem him, and her mind would always find support. But every circumstance seemed to unite to heighten Marianne's misery, sealing her fate in a final separation from Willoughby—an immediate and irreconcilable rupture.

The fire had not yet been kindled, and the January morning remained a cold, grey prison of gloom. Marianne, barely clad, knelt before the window, clutching a letter as if it were the only thing keeping her anchored to reality. Tears flowed continuously, blurring the page, yet she wrote with a frantic, desperate speed. Elinor, roused from sleep by the sharp intake of her sister's breath and the rising tide of sobs, watched in silent, aching anxiety.

"Marianne," Elinor whispered, her voice gentle, "may I ask—"

"No, Elinor," Marianne cut in, her voice trembling with a terrifying, fleeting calm. "Ask nothing. You will know soon enough."

That calm shattered the moment the words left her lips, replaced instantly by the crushing weight of grief. Minutes stretched into an eternity before she could lift her pen again. Each pause, each fresh burst of weeping that forced her to stop, confirmed the terrible truth: she was likely writing her final farewell to Willoughby.

Elinor offered her every ounce of quiet support, yet Marianne, driven by a nervous irritability that bordered on madness, begged her not to speak a word. In such a storm of emotion, silence was the only mercy. Marianne could not remain still; she wandered the house until breakfast, a ghost avoiding the sight of everyone, her mind too restless for solitude or stillness.

At the table, she ate nothing. Elinor, seeing her sister's devastation, turned her attention entirely to Mrs. Jennings, diverting the older woman's gaze from Marianne's hollow eyes. The meal dragged on, a favorite ritual for Mrs. Jennings, until a knock brought a letter to Marianne. She snatched it from the servant's hand, her face draining of color until she looked deathly pale, and fled the room.

Elinor saw the direction immediately. Her heart seized, a sickness rising in her throat that made holding her head upright a struggle. She sat trembling, fearing Mrs. Jennings would notice her distress. Instead, the good lady, busy measuring wool for her rug, mistook the scene for a romantic comedy.

"Upon my word," Mrs. Jennings exclaimed, laughing, "I never saw a young woman so desperately in love! My girls were nothing to her, but Miss Marianne is quite an altered creature. I hope he doesn't keep her waiting much longer; it is grievous to see her so ill. Pray, when are they to be married?"

Elinor, though her spirit was heavy, felt compelled to answer. She forced a smile, her voice steady despite the turmoil within. "And have you really, Ma'am, persuaded yourself that my sister is engaged to Mr. Willoughby? I thought it a joke, but such a serious question implies more. Please, do not deceive yourself. Nothing would surprise me more than to hear of their marriage."

"For shame!" Mrs. Jennings retorted, her laughter fading into indignation. "How can you talk so? We all know it must be a match. They were over head and ears in love from the first moment they met. Did I not see them together in Devonshire every day? Did I not know your sister came to town specifically to buy wedding clothes? Come, come. Because you are so sly, you think no one else has sense. But it has been known all over town for ages. I tell everyone, and so does Charlotte."

"Indeed, Ma'am," Elinor said, her tone grave. "You are mistaken. You are doing a very unkind thing spreading such a report, and I am sure you will find yourself wrong."

Mrs. Jennings laughed again, dismissing Elinor's plea, while Elinor, drained of spirit, hurried away to their room. She pushed open the door to find Marianne stretched across the bed, almost choking on her grief, clutching one letter while two or three others lay scattered nearby.

Elinor approached slowly, saying nothing. She sat beside her sister, taking her hand and kissing it repeatedly. Then, the dam broke. Elinor wept, her tears nearly as violent as Marianne's. Marianne, unable to speak, felt the tenderness of the gesture. After a long period of shared, silent agony, she handed all the letters to Elinor. Covering her face with a handkerchief, she screamed with such raw agony that it filled the small room.

Elinor, knowing this pain must run its course, waited until the initial shock subsided. Then, she turned to the letter from Willoughby.

*I have just had the honour of receiving your letter, for which I beg to return my sincere acknowledgments. I am much concerned to find there was anything in my behaviour last night that did not meet your approbation; and though I am quite at a loss to discover in what point I could be so unfortunate as to offend you, I entreat your forgiveness of what I can assure you to have been perfectly unintentional. I shall never reflect on my former acquaintance with your family in Devonshire without the most grateful pleasure, and flatter myself it will not be broken by any mistake or misapprehension of my actions. My esteem for your whole family is very sincere; but if I have been so unfortunate as to give rise to a belief of more than I felt, or meant to express, I shall reproach myself for not having been more guarded in my professions of that esteem. That I should ever have meant more you will allow to be impossible, when you understand that my affections have been long engaged elsewhere, and it will not be many weeks, I believe, before this engagement is fulfilled. It is with great regret that I obey your commands in returning the letters with which I have been honoured from you, and the lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me.*

The indignation that surged through Elinor was profound. She had suspected his inconstancy, but she had not anticipated such cruelty. To announce his betrayal with such cold, polite detachment, to deny any special affection while returning her tokens of love, was a violation of every honorable feeling. It was a letter of impudent cruelty, every line an insult that proclaimed its writer to be deep in hardened villainy.

She read it again and again, her abhorrence growing with each perusal. The bitterness of her feelings was so intense that she dared not speak, terrified that trying to comfort Marianne by framing this as an escape from a bad match would only wound her sister deeper. Marianne needed to know this was a loss, not a deliverance.

Lost in her meditation on the depravity of the mind that could dictate such words, Elinor forgot her sister's immediate distress, the unread letters on her lap, and the passage of time. Only when the sound of a carriage driving up to the door startled her did she realize Mrs. Jennings's chariot had arrived early. Determined not to leave Marianne alone in her despair, Elinor excused herself to the older woman, citing her sister's indisposition. Mrs. Jennings, ever good-natured, accepted the excuse readily.

Once the carriage departed, Elinor returned to find Marianne attempting to rise. Elinor caught her just in time to prevent a fall; her sister was giddy and weak from days of hunger and sleeplessness. Now that the fever of suspense had broken, the physical toll was evident: an aching head, a weakened stomach, and a general nervous faintness. Elinor fetched wine, which helped settle Marianne enough for her to whisper, "Poor Elinor! how unhappy I make you!"

"I only wish," Elinor replied, "there were anything I *could* do to comfort you."

Those words were too much for Marianne. "Oh! Elinor, I am miserable, indeed," she sobbed, her voice failing.

Elinor could no longer watch this torrent of unresisted grief in silence. "Exert yourself, dear Marianne," she cried. "If you would not kill yourself and all who love you. Think of your mother; think of her misery while *you* suffer: for her sake you must exert yourself."

"I cannot, I cannot," Marianne wailed. "Leave me, leave me, if I distress you; leave me, hate me, forget me! But do not torture me so. Oh! how easy for those, who have no sorrow of their own to talk of exertion! Happy, happy Elinor, *you* cannot have an idea of what I suffer."

"Do you call *me* happy, Marianne? Ah! if you knew!—And can you believe me to be so, while I see you so wretched!"

"Forgive me, forgive me," Marianne threw her arms around her sister's neck. "I know you feel for me; I know what a heart you have; but yet you are—you must be happy; Edward loves you—what, oh what, can do away such happiness as that?"

"Many, many circumstances," Elinor said solemnly.

"No, no, no," Marianne cried wildly. "He loves you, and only you. You *can* have no grief."

"I can have no pleasure while I see you in this state."

"And you will never see me otherwise. Mine is a misery which nothing can do away."

"You must not talk so, Marianne. Have you no comforts? no friends? Is your loss such as leaves no opening for consolation? Much as you suffer now, think of what you would have suffered if the discovery of his character had been delayed to a later period—if your engagement had been carried on for months and months, as it might have been, before he chose to put an end to it. Every additional day of unhappy confidence, on your side, would have made the blow more dreadful."

"Engagement!" Marianne gasped. "There has been no engagement."

"No engagement!"

"No, he is not so unworthy as you believe him. He has broken no faith with me."

"But he told you that he loved you."

"Yes—no—never absolutely. It was every day implied, but never professedly declared. Sometimes I thought it had been, but it never was."

"Yet you wrote to him?"

"Yes: could that be wrong after all that had passed? But I cannot talk."

Elinor said no more. Turning to the three remaining letters, her curiosity sharpened into a need for truth. She read them quickly. The first, sent upon their arrival in town, expressed excitement and expectation. The second, written after the dance at the Middletons', pleaded for an explanation of his absence and demanded a personal assurance of his presence. The third, following the incident at the ball, was a desperate plea for clarity, questioning his behavior and demanding a justification or a return of her letters and hair.

That such letters, so full of affection and confidence, could be answered with such cold dismissal, Elinor found hard to believe. Yet, she condemned the imprudence of writing them in the first place. As Marianne watched her finish, she remarked that the letters contained nothing anyone wouldn't write in her situation.

"I felt myself," Marianne added, her voice trembling, "to be as solemnly engaged to him, as if the strictest legal covenant had bound us to each other."

"I can believe it," Elinor said softly. "But unfortunately he did not feel the same."

"He *did* feel the same, Elinor—for weeks and weeks he felt it. I know he did. Whatever may have changed him now, (and nothing but the blackest art employed against me can have done it), I was once as dear to him as my own soul could wish. This lock of hair, which now he can so readily give up, was begged of me with the most earnest supplication. Had you seen his look, his manner, had you heard his voice at that moment! Have you forgot the last evening of our being together at Barton? The morning that we parted too! When he told me that it might be many weeks before we met again—his distress—can I ever forget his distress?"

For a moment, she could say nothing. When the emotion passed, she spoke in a firmer tone. "Elinor, I have been cruelly used; but not by Willoughby."

"Dearest Marianne, who but himself? By whom can he have been instigated?"

"By all the world, rather than by his own heart. I could rather believe every creature of my acquaintance leagued together to ruin me in his opinion, than believe his nature capable of such cruelty. This woman of whom he writes—whoever she be—or any one, in short, but your own dear self, mama, and Edward, may have been so barbarous to bely me. Beyond you three, is there a creature in the world whom I would not rather suspect of evil than Willoughby, whose heart I know so well?"

Elinor did not argue. "Whoever may have been so detestably your enemy, let them be cheated of their malignant triumph, my dear sister, by seeing how nobly the consciousness of your own innocence and good intentions supports your spirits. It is a reasonable and laudable pride which resists such malevolence."

"No, no," Marianne cried. "Misery such as mine has no pride. I care not who knows that I am wretched. The triumph of seeing me so may be open to all the world. Elinor, Elinor, they who suffer little may be proud and independent as they like—may resist insult, or return mortification—but I cannot. I must feel—I must be wretched—and they are welcome to enjoy the consciousness of it that can."

"But for my mother's sake and mine—"

"I would do more than for my own. But to appear happy when I am so miserable—Oh! who can require it?"

Silence fell between them again. Elinor walked thoughtfully from the fire to the window, unaware of the warmth or the view. Marianne, seated at the foot of the bed, leaned her head against the post and picked up Willoughby's letter again. Shuddering over every sentence, she exclaimed, "It is too much! Oh, Willough

Mrs. Jennings arrived at their lodging with a haste that brooked no delay, pushing open the door before anyone could offer it and stepping inside with a face etched by genuine alarm.

"How do you do, my dear?" she asked, her voice thick with pity as she looked at Marianne, who had already turned her face away, refusing to meet the gaze of the woman who meant well.

"How is she, Miss Dashwood? Poor creature! She looks dreadfully ill. No wonder. Ay, it is far too true. He is to be wed very shortly—a scoundrel! I have no patience for such a man. Mrs. Taylor confided this to me only half an hour ago, having learned it from a close confidante of Miss Grey herself; otherwise, I should have dismissed it as rumor. I was nearly fainting when I heard it. 'Well,' I thought, 'if this is true, he has treated a young lady of my acquaintance with abominable cruelty, and I pray with all my soul that his new wife may torment his heart until it breaks.' And so I shall always say, my dear; you may rely upon it. I cannot conceive of men behaving thus. If I ever cross paths with him again, I shall give him a beating he has not known in many a day. Yet, there is solace, my dear Miss Marianne: he is not the only eligible young man in the world. With your lovely face, you will never lack suitors. Well, poor thing! I shall not keep her here longer; she would do better to weep it all out and be done with it. Fortunately, the Parrys and Sandersons are arriving tonight, and their presence will provide amusement."

She departed then, moving on tiptoes as if she feared the mere sound of footsteps might deepen her young friend's sorrow.

To Elinor's surprise, Marianne resolved to dine with them. Elinor counseled against it, but "No, she would descend; she could endure it, and the commotion of the room would distract her." Elinor, delighted to see her sister momentarily governed by such a practical impulse, though doubting she could last the meal, offered no further objection. She adjusted Marianne's gown as best she could while her sister remained on the bed, ready to escort her to the dining room when summoned.

There, despite her wretched appearance, Marianne ate more than Elinor anticipated and appeared calmer. Had she attempted to speak, or had she been conscious of half of Mrs. Jennings's well-intentioned but clumsy attentions, this composure would have been impossible. Not a syllable escaped her lips; the deep abstraction of her thoughts kept her ignorant of everything happening around her.

Elinor, acknowledging Mrs. Jennings's kindness despite its often distressing and occasionally absurd nature, returned the necessary civilities that her sister could not muster. Their good hostess perceived Marianne's unhappiness and felt that every effort should be made to alleviate it. She treated her with the indulgent fondness of a parent toward a favorite child on the final day of its holidays. Marianne was granted the best seat by the fire, tempted by every delicacy in the house, and amused by the recounting of the day's news. Had Elinor not seen the tragic countenance of her sister, she might have been entertained by Mrs. Jennings's attempts to cure a broken heart with sweets, olives, and a roaring fire. However, as the weight of these efforts became undeniable through constant repetition, Marianne could stay no longer. With a hasty cry of "Misery!" and a gesture telling her sister not to follow, she rose abruptly and hurried out of the room.

"Poor soul!" cried Mrs. Jennings immediately after her departure. "It grieves me to see her! I declare she has left without finishing her wine! And the dried cherries too! Lord! nothing seems to help her. I am certain if I knew what she liked, I would scour the entire town for it. Well, it is the strangest thing to me that a man should treat such a pretty girl so badly! But when there is plenty of money on one side and next to none on the other, Lord bless you! they care no such thing!"

"The lady, then—Miss Grey, I believe you called her—is very wealthy?"

"Fifty thousand pounds, my dear. Have you seen her? They say she is smart and stylish, though not handsome. I remember her aunt very well, Biddy Henshawe; she married a very wealthy man. But the whole family is rich together. Fifty thousand pounds! And by all accounts, it won't be available until it is needed, for they say he is completely ruined. No wonder! Dashing about in his curricle and hunting horses! Well, it doesn't matter talking about it; but when a young man, whoever he may be, comes to court a pretty girl and promises marriage, he has no business to flee from his word simply because he grows poor and a richer girl is ready to accept him. Why doesn't he, in such a case, sell his horses, let his house, dismiss his servants, and make a thorough reform at once? I warrant you, Miss Marianne would have been willing to wait until matters improved. But that won't work nowadays; nothing related to pleasure can ever be sacrificed by the young men of this age."

"Do you know what kind of a girl Miss Grey is? Is she said to be amiable?"

"I have never heard any harm of her; indeed, I hardly ever hear her mentioned, except that Mrs. Taylor said this morning that one day Miss Walker hinted to her that she believed Mr. and Mrs. Ellison would not be sorry to have Miss Grey married, for she and Mrs. Ellison could never agree."

"And who are the Ellisons?"

"Her guardians, my dear. But now she is of age and may choose for herself; and what a choice she has made!—What now," she paused, "your poor sister has gone to her own room, I suppose, to mourn by herself. Is there nothing we can do to comfort her? Poor dear, it seems quite cruel to let her be alone. Well, by-and-by we shall have a few friends, and that will amuse her a little. What shall we play at? She hates whist, I know; but is there no round game she cares for?"

"Dear ma'am, this kindness is quite unnecessary. Marianne, I dare say, will not leave her room again this evening. I shall persuade her, if I can, to go to bed early, for I am sure she needs rest."

"Aye, I believe that will be best for her. Let her name her own supper and retire. Lord! No wonder she has looked so bad and so cast down for the last week or two; this matter, I suppose, has been hanging over her head as long as that. And so the letter that came today finished it! Poor soul! I am sure if I had had the slightest notion of it, I would not have joked her about it for all my money. But then you know, how could I guess such a thing? I assumed it was nothing but a common love letter, and you know young people like to be laughed at regarding them. Lord! How concerned Sir John and my daughters will be when they hear it! If I had my senses about me, I might have called in Conduit Street on my way home and told them. But I shall see them tomorrow."

"It would be unnecessary, I am sure, for you to caution Mrs. Palmer and Sir John against ever mentioning Mr. Willoughby, or making the slightest allusion to what has passed, before my sister. Their own good nature must point out to them the real cruelty of appearing to know anything about it when she is present; and the less that may ever be said to myself on the subject, the more my feelings will be spared, as you, my dear madam, will easily believe."

"Oh! Lord! yes, that I do indeed. It must be terrible for you to hear it discussed; and as for your sister, I am sure I would not mention a word about it to her for the world. You saw I did not all dinner time. Neither would Sir John, nor my daughters, for they are all very thoughtful and considerate; especially if I give them a hint, as I certainly will. For my part, I think the less that is said about such things, the better, the sooner it blows over and is forgotten. And what good does talking ever do, you know?"

"In this affair, it can only do harm; more so perhaps than in many cases of a similar kind, for it has been attended by circumstances which, for the sake of everyone concerned, make it unfit for public conversation. I must do *this* justice to Mr. Willoughby—he has broken no positive engagement with my sister."

"Law, my dear! Don't pretend to defend him. No positive engagement indeed! After taking her all over Allenham House and fixing on the very rooms they were to live in henceforth!"

Elinor, for her sister's sake, could not press the subject further, and she hoped it was not required of her for Willoughby's; since, though Marianne might lose much, he could gain very little by the enforcement of the real truth. After a short silence on both sides, Mrs. Jennings, with all her natural hilarity, burst forth again.

"Well, my dear, 'tis a true saying about an ill wind, for it will be all the better for Colonel Brandon. He will have her at last; ay, that he will. Mark my words, if they aren't married by Midsummer. Lord! How he'll chuckle over this news! I hope he will come tonight. It will be all to one a better match for your sister. Two thousand a year without debt or drawback—except the little love-child, indeed; ay, I had forgotten her; but she may be apprenticed out at a small cost, and then what does it signify? Delaford is a nice place, I can tell you; exactly what I call a nice old-fashioned place, full of comforts and conveniences; quite shut in with great garden walls covered with the best fruit-trees in the country; and such a mulberry tree in one corner! Lord! How Charlotte and I did stuff ourselves the only time we were there! Then, there is a dove-cote, some delightful stew-ponds, and a very pretty canal; and everything, in short, that one could wish for; and, moreover, it is close to the church, and only a quarter of a mile from the turnpike-road, so 'tis never dull, for if you only go and sit up in an old yew arbor behind the house, you may see all the carriages that pass along. Oh! 'tis a nice place! A butcher hard by in the village, and the parsonage-house within a stone's throw. To my fancy, a thousand times prettier than Barton Park, where they are forced to send three miles for their meat and have not a neighbor nearer than your mother. Well, I shall spirit up the Colonel as soon as I can. One shoulder of mutton, you know, drives another down. If we *can* but put Willoughby out of her head!"

"Ay, if we can do *that*, Ma'am," said Elinor, "we shall do very well with or without Colonel Brandon." And then rising, she went away to join Marianne, whom she found, as she expected, in her own room, leaning in silent misery over the small remains of a fire which, until Elinor's entrance, had been her only light.

"You had better leave me," was the only notice her sister gave.

"I will leave you," said Elinor, "if you will go to bed." But this, from the momentary perverseness of impatient suffering, she at first refused to do. Her sister's earnest, though gentle persuasion, however, soon softened her to compliance, and Elinor saw her lay her aching head on the pillow, and, as she hoped, in a way to get some quiet rest before she left her.

In the drawing-room, whither she then repaired, she was soon joined by Mrs. Jennings, holding a wine-glass filled with something in her hand.

"My dear," she said, entering, "I have just recollected that I have some of the finest old Constantia wine in the house that ever was tasted, so I have brought a glass of it for your sister. My poor husband! How fond he was of it! Whenever he had a touch of his old colicky gout, he said it did him more good than anything else in the world. Do take it to your sister."

"Dear Ma'am," replied Elinor, smiling at the difference of the complaints for which it was recommended, "how good you are! But I have just left Marianne in bed, and, I hope, almost asleep; and as I think nothing will be of so much service to her as rest, if you will give me leave, I will drink the wine myself."

Mrs. Jennings, though regretting that she had not been five minutes earlier, was satisfied with the compromise; and Elinor, as she swallowed the chief of it, reflected that though its effects on a colicky gout were, at present, of little importance to her, its healing powers on a disappointed heart might be as reasonably tried on herself as on her sister.

Colonel Brandon entered while the party were at tea, and by his manner of looking round the room for Marianne, Elinor immediately fancied that he neither expected nor wished to see her there, and, in short, that he was already aware of what occasioned her absence. Mrs. Jennings was not struck by the same thought; for soon after his entrance, she walked across the room to the tea-table where Elinor presided and whispered, "The Colonel looks as grave as ever you see. He knows nothing of it; do tell him, my dear."

He shortly afterwards drew a chair close to hers and, with a look which perfectly assured her of his good information, inquired after her sister.

"Marianne is not well," she said. "She has been indisposed all day, and we have persuaded her to go to bed."

"Perhaps, then," he hesitatingly replied, "what I heard this morning may—there may be more truth in it than

Marianne awoke the next morning to a consciousness of misery that was as heavy and unyielding as the night from which she had fled. Sleep had offered no respite, only a deeper immersion in her despair. Before breakfast was served, Elinor had exhausted every word of comfort, yet the cycle remained unbroken. With Elinor's steady, affectionate counsel on one side and Marianne's impetuous, fluctuating emotions on the other, they revisited the tragedy of Willoughby again and again. One moment Marianne could cling to the delusion of his innocence, viewing him as a victim of fate just as she was; the next, she rejected all consolation, convinced of his treachery. Her mood swung violently between a desperate indifference to the world's judgment and a frantic desire to hide away forever, only to rally with sudden energy against the very isolation she sought. Yet, in one matter, her resolve was absolute: she avoided Mrs. Jennings whenever possible, and when forced to endure her presence, she met it with a cold, determined silence. Her heart had hardened against the belief that Mrs. Jennings possessed any true compassion for her sorrow.

"No, no, no, it cannot be!" Marianne cried, her voice trembling with irritation. "She cannot feel. Her kindness is merely politeness; her good-nature is not tenderness. All she desires is gossip, and she cherishes me only because I provide it."

Elinor, observing her sister, recognized the injustice of this conclusion. She understood that Marianne, brilliant and well-intentioned though she was, lacked the candor to judge others fairly. Driven by her own intense sensibility and a polished exterior, Marianne expected everyone else to mirror her own depth of feeling, interpreting motives solely by their immediate impact on herself. This flaw became painfully evident shortly after breakfast. Mrs. Jennings entered their room with a letter held out eagerly, her face beaming with the joy of bringing comfort.

"Now, my dear, I bring you something that I am sure will do you good," she announced.

To Marianne, these words were a death knell. In an instant, her imagination conjured a letter from Willoughby, overflowing with tenderness and repentance, explaining everything, satisfying every doubt. She pictured him rushing into the room, kneeling at her feet, his eyes eloquently pleading his case. The fantasy lasted but a heartbeat before shattering into dust. Instead of Willoughby, she held the handwriting of her mother. The crushing weight of this disappointment, born of such a fleeting ecstasy, felt sharper than her initial grief. She could not bear to look at Mrs. Jennings. The kindness of the older woman seemed crueler than any malice; Marianne could only reproach her with violent tears, a silent accusation that Mrs. Jennings would never understand.

Mrs. Jennings, oblivious to the depth of the wound she had inflicted, offered only pity and urged Marianne to read the letter of comfort. But when Marianne finally calmed enough to open it, she found no solace. Every page was filled with Willoughby's name. Her mother, still convinced of their engagement and trusting in his constancy, had written only to plead for Marianne's honesty and openness. The letter was a torrent of maternal affection, of belief in their future happiness, so overwhelming that Marianne wept with agony as she read it.

The longing to return home seized her with renewed force. Her mother was dearer to her than ever, precisely because her confidence in Willoughby was so tragically misplaced. Marianne became wildly urgent to leave. Elinor, torn between the desire to keep her sister in London or send her to Barton, offered no advice of her own, suggesting only patience until her mother's wishes were clear. Eventually, Marianne agreed to wait.

Mrs. Jennings departed earlier than usual, unable to rest until the Middletons and Palmers could share in her grief. Refusing Elinor's offer of company, she left alone. Elinor, her heart heavy with the pain she was about to deliver, sat down to write to her mother, recounting the day's events and seeking guidance. Marianne, entering the drawing-room as Mrs. Jennings left, stood fixed beside her sister at the table. She watched the movement of Elinor's pen, grieving for the burden of the task, and grieving even more deeply for the effect it would have on their mother.

They sat in this quiet communion for about fifteen minutes when a sharp rap at the door shattered the stillness. Marianne's nerves, already frayed, could not withstand the sudden noise.

"Who can this be?" Elinor cried, startled. "So early! I thought we were safe."

Marianne moved to the window. "It is Colonel Brandon!" she said, her voice tinged with vexation. "We are never safe from him."

"He will not come in, as Mrs. Jennings is away," Elinor reasoned.

"I will not trust to that," Marianne retorted, retreating to her own room. "A man who has nothing to do with his own time possesses no conscience regarding his intrusion on others."

Her conjecture proved correct, though founded on error. Colonel Brandon did enter. Elinor, seeing the genuine solicitude in his disturbed, melancholy gaze and his anxious inquiry for Marianne, could not forgive her sister's harsh judgment.

"I met Mrs. Jennings in Bond Street," Brandon began after their initial greetings. "She encouraged me to come. I was eager to find you alone, for my sole wish is to offer comfort—not fleeting comfort, but lasting conviction to your sister's mind. My regard for her, for yourself, and for your mother... will you allow me to prove it by relating some circumstances? Nothing but sincere regard and an earnest desire to be useful could justify this, though after so many hours spent convincing myself of my rightness, I fear I may be wrong." He paused, looking uncertain.

"I understand," Elinor said gently. "You have something to tell me of Mr. Willoughby that will reveal his true character. Your testimony would be the greatest act of friendship for Marianne. My gratitude is immediate; hers will follow in time. Please, let me hear it."

"You shall," Brandon replied. "Briefly, when I left Barton last October... but I must go further back. I am an awkward narrator, Miss Dashwood; I hardly know where to begin. A short account of myself seems necessary. On such a subject, I have little temptation to be diffuse." He sighed heavily.

"You have probably forgotten a conversation," he continued, recovering his composure. "Between us, one evening at Barton Park, during a dance. I alluded to a lady I once knew, noting she resembled your sister, Marianne, in some measure."

"I have not forgotten it," Elinor answered.

Brandon looked pleased. "If I am not deceived by the partiality of memory, the resemblance is striking, both in mind and person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy. This lady was my nearest relation, an orphan from infancy under my father's guardianship. Our ages were nearly the same, and from our earliest years we were playfellows and friends. I cannot recall a time when I did not love Eliza. My affection for her, as we grew, was profound—perhaps so deep that, judging by my present forlorn gravity, you might think me incapable of such feeling. Hers, for me, was as fervent as Marianne's attachment to Mr. Willoughby, and though caused differently, it was equally unfortunate. At seventeen, she was lost to me forever. She was married against her will to my brother. Her fortune was large, but our family estate was encumbered. I fear this is all that can be said for the conduct of one who was both her uncle and guardian. My brother did not deserve her; he did not even love her. I had hoped her regard for me would sustain her, and for a time it did. But eventually, the misery of her situation, marked by great unkindness, overcame her resolution. Though she had promised me nothing, I have never explained how this came to pass. We were within hours of eloping to Scotland. The treachery—or folly—of my cousin's maid betrayed us. I was banished to a distant relative's house, and she was confined without liberty or society until my father's anger subsided. I had relied too heavily on her fortitude; the blow was severe. Yet, had her marriage been happy, so young as I was, a few months might have reconciled me. But it was not. My brother held no regard for her; his pleasures were base, and from the start he treated her unkindly. The consequence, upon a mind so young, lively, and inexperienced, was inevitable. She resigned herself to her misery initially, but it would have been better had she not lived to overcome the regrets my memory caused. With such a husband provoking inconstancy, and without a friend to advise or restrain her—my father died shortly after their marriage, and I was stationed in the East Indies—how could she avoid falling? Had I remained in England, perhaps... but I intended to promote their happiness by removing her from harm's way, hence my exchange to the colonies. The shock of her marriage was trifling compared to what I felt two years later when I heard of her divorce. It was that which cast this gloom upon me, even now recalling the suffering I endured."

He stopped, unable to continue, and paced the room. Elinor, deeply affected by his tale and his distress, remained silent. He approached her, taking her hand and pressing it with grateful respect. After a few moments of silent recovery, he proceeded.

"It was nearly three years after this unhappy period before I returned to England. My first care was to seek her, but the search was as melancholy as it was fruitless. I could not trace her beyond her first seducer, and feared she had only moved to sink deeper into sin. Her legal allowance was insufficient, and I learned from my brother that the power to receive it had been transferred months prior. He imagined, calmly, that her extravagance and distress had forced her to sell it for immediate relief. Finally, after six months in England, I found her. A former servant, now fallen into misfortune, directed me to a spunging-house where he was confined for debt. There, under similar confinement, lay my unfortunate sister. So altered, so faded, worn down by acute suffering of every kind! Hardly could I believe the sickly figure before me to be the remains of the lovely, blooming girl I once adored. What I endured in beholding her... I have no right to wound your feelings by describing it. That she appeared to be in the final stages of consumption was, in such a situation, my greatest comfort. Life could do nothing but grant time for a better preparation for death, and that was granted. I saw her placed in comfortable lodgings with proper attendants; I visited her daily during the rest of her short life. I was with her in her last moments."

He stopped again, and Elinor expressed her tender concern for his friend's fate.

"Your sister, I hope, cannot be offended," Brandon said, regaining his composure. "By the resemblance I fancied between her and my poor disgraced relation. Their fates and fortunes differ; had the natural sweetness of the one been guarded by a firmer mind or a happier marriage, she might have been all that you will live to see the other be. But to what does this lead? I seem to have distressed you for nothing. Ah, Miss Dashwood—a subject untouched for fourteen years is dangerous to handle! I will be more collected. She left to my care her only child, a little girl, the offspring of her first guilty connection, then about three years old. She loved the child and kept it with her. It was a precious trust. Gladly would I have educated her myself, had our situations allowed, but I had no family, no home. Thus, little Eliza was placed at school. I visited her whenever I could, and after my brother's death five years ago, she visited me at Delaford. I called her a distant relation, though I am aware I have often been suspected of a closer connection. It was three years ago, when she reached her fourteenth year, that I removed her from school to place her under the care of a respectable woman in Dorsetshire, who supervised several girls of her age. For two years, her situation gave me pleasure. But last February, almost a year ago, she suddenly disappeared. I had imprudently, at her earnest desire, allowed her to go to Bath with a young friend accompanying her father for his health. I knew the father to be a good man and thought well of his daughter—better than she deserved, for with obstinate secrecy she revealed nothing, gave no clue, though she certainly knew all. The father, well-meaning but not quick-sighted, could give no information; he had been confined to the house while the girls ranged the town, and he tried to convince me, as he convinced himself, that his daughter was entirely unconcerned. In short, I learned only that she was gone. For eight long months, the rest was left to conjecture. What I thought, what I feared, may be imagined; and what I suffered."

"Good heavens!" cried Elinor. "Could it be—could it be Willoughby?"

"The first news of her reached me in a letter from herself, last October," Brandon continued. "It was forwarded from Delaford, and I received it on the very morning of our intended party to Whitwell. This explains my sudden departure from Barton, which must have seemed strange to everyone and likely gave offense. Little did Mr. Willoughby imagine, when his looks censured me for incivility in breaking up the party, that I was called away to relieve one whom he had made poor and miserable. Had he known, what would it have availed? Would he have been less gay or less happy in the smiles of your sister? No, he had already done what no man who can feel for another would do. He left the girl whose youth and innocence he had seduced in a situation of utmost distress, with no creditable home, no help, no friends, ignorant of his address! He left her, promising to return; he neither returned, nor wrote, nor relieved her."

"This is beyond everything

When Elinor recounted the details of her conversation to her sister, the impact was not the one she had anticipated. Marianne did not question the truth of a single word; she listened with a steady, submissive stillness, offering no defense for Willoughby and shedding tears that spoke of a conviction she found impossible to shake. Yet, while Elinor saw the signs of this realization—Marianne no longer avoiding Colonel Brandon, addressing him with a newfound, compassionate respect, and her volatile temper settling into a quieter despair—she saw no lightening of her sister's wretchedness. Marianne's mind had become fixed, but it was anchored in a gloomy desolation. The loss of Willoughby's character weighed heavier on her than the loss of his affection. The memory of Miss Williams' misery, coupled with the terrifying uncertainty of what his intentions toward her once might have been, preyed upon her so relentlessly that she could not bear to speak of her pain even to Elinor. In her silent brooding, she inflicted a deeper sorrow upon her sister than any open confession could have caused.

Mrs. Dashwood's reaction to Elinor's letter mirrored the anguish of her daughters, though her indignation burned hotter. Long letters followed swiftly, pouring out her suffering and her desperate pleas for Marianne to summon fortitude. How cruel, Elinor thought, for a mother to speak of strength when her own heart was so bruised; how humiliating for Marianne to be urged to suppress the very regrets her mother wished she could feel.

Despite the pain, Mrs. Dashwood made a difficult decision. She believed it better for Marianne to be anywhere but at Barton, where every sight and sound would resurrect the past, constantly presenting Willoughby as she had always known him. She urged her daughters to extend their stay in London, hoping that the variety of city life might, however briefly, distract Marianne from her own misery. To Mrs. Dashwood, the crowded anonymity of London offered safety; Willoughby's reputation was now so ruined that no friend would seek him out, and the sheer chaos of the city made a chance encounter unlikely compared to the quiet isolation of Devonshire. Furthermore, a letter from her son-in-law indicated that the Ferrars family would be in town by mid-February, making it prudent for the sisters to remain until they could greet their brother.

Marianne, though convinced her mother's plan was misguided and that staying in London denied her the only true solace—her mother's presence—submitted without argument. Elinor, however, found a strange comfort in the arrangement. While the delay threatened her own happiness, she reasoned that it was necessary for Marianne's sake. Moreover, Elinor suspected she could not entirely avoid Edward Ferrars; thus, the extended stay, though painful, might be preferable to an immediate return to the countryside.

Elinor's efforts to shield Marianne from the name of Willoughby proved successful. Neither Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, nor Mrs. Palmer ever mentioned him before her daughter. Elinor, however, could not enjoy this silence. She was forced to endure the clamorous indignation of her circle daily. Sir John raged against the "scoundrel," vowing never to speak to him again, even if Willoughby stood beside him at Barton Covert. Mrs. Palmer, equally furious, declared she would never mention his name again and resolved to label him a "good-for-nothing" to anyone who would listen. They delighted in sharing gossip about his new carriage, his portraitist, and the location of Miss Grey's wardrobe, treating the tragedy as a source of communal entertainment.

In contrast, Lady Middleton's calm, polite indifference was a balm to Elinor's spirit. It was a profound relief to know that at least one person in their circle felt no need to pry into particulars or worry about Marianne's health. Lady Middleton's daily, gentle exclamation of "It is very shocking!" served as a perfect vent, allowing her to maintain her dignity while condemning the wrong, before turning her attention back to her own social obligations. She even planned to call on Mrs. Willoughby immediately upon her marriage, a decision that amused Sir John but pleased Lady Middleton.

Colonel Brandon's inquiries remained a source of genuine comfort for Elinor. Having earned the right to discuss Marianne's grief through his zealous efforts to soothe it, they shared their burdens with confidence. Marianne's occasional pitying glances and soft voice when addressing the Colonel assured him of his growing influence, giving Elinor hope for the future. Unaware of this subtle shift, Mrs. Jennings grew increasingly skeptical of the match. Seeing the Colonel remain grave and unable to propose, she began to doubt whether the union would happen at all, eventually ceasing to think of Mrs. Ferrars entirely, convinced the Colonel and Elinor were destined for each other.

By early February, less than two weeks after receiving Willoughby's letter, Elinor delivered the final blow: he was married. She had ensured Marianne learned of it privately, sparing her the shock of reading it in the newspapers. Marianne received the news with a resolute composure that lasted only a moment before breaking into tears, leaving her in a state of pitiable distress for the rest of the day.

With the Willoughbys gone, Elinor hoped to encourage Marianne to venture out again. Around this time, the Miss Steeles returned to London, greeted with warmth by their relatives. Only Elinor felt pain at their arrival, particularly at Lucy's delight in finding her still in town. Lucy's teasing was relentless, mocking Elinor for not leaving sooner and boasting of her own travels with Dr. Davies, whom she coyly denied being a "beau" despite everyone's laughter. When Lucy turned her attention to Elinor's sister, feigning concern for Marianne's health to invite her to visit, Elinor politely declined, citing Marianne's nervous headaches. When Miss Steele insisted on going to see Marianne herself, Elinor's temper nearly flared, but Lucy's sharp reprimand saved her the trouble, silencing her cousin's impertinence.

Though Marianne initially resisted, she eventually relented to Elinor's gentle persuasion and agreed to venture out with her sister and Mrs. Jennings for a brief hour. However, Marianne set a strict condition: they were to make no social calls, limiting their outing strictly to Gray's in Sackville Street, where Elinor was conducting business regarding the exchange of her mother's antique jewels.

As they approached the shop, Mrs. Jennings recalled a pending visit to a lady at the opposite end of the street. Since she had no errands of her own at Gray's, it was arranged that she would attend to her visitor and return promptly for the Dashwood sisters.

Upon entering the shop, the sisters found the counter crowded. With no clerk available to assist them immediately, they were forced to wait. They seated themselves at the end of the counter that appeared to promise the swiftest service. Only one gentleman stood there, and Elinor harbored a faint hope that his politeness might expedite their transaction. Yet, his keen eye and refined taste proved superior to his courtesy. He was deeply engrossed in selecting a toothpick-case, meticulously debating its dimensions, shape, and ornamentation for nearly twenty minutes. During this time, he offered the ladies little more than three or four broad, staring glances—a gaze that impressed upon Elinor the memory of a face possessing a strong, natural, and utterly insignificant charm, despite being dressed in the height of fashion.

Marianne, spared the sting of contempt and resentment by his intrusive scrutiny and the puppyish manner in which he critiqued every option, remained blissfully unconscious of the scene. She retreated inward, collecting her thoughts with such intensity that she might as well have been in her own bedroom, oblivious to the world outside.

Finally, the decision was made. The ivory, gold, and pearls were appointed, and the gentleman, having declared the day by which his existence could no longer continue without his new toothpick-case, slowly donned his gloves. Bestowing one final look upon the Miss Dashwoods—a glance that seemed to demand admiration rather than merely offer it—he departed with an air of genuine conceit and feigned indifference.

Elinor wasted no time in advancing her own business, nearly concluding it when another gentleman approached her side. Turning to him, she was surprised to recognize her brother, John Dashwood.

Their reunion was marked by a display of affection sufficient to maintain appearances in the public eye. John was genuinely pleased to see his sisters again; his inquiries regarding their mother were respectful and attentive. He learned that he and his wife, Fanny, had arrived in London only two days prior.

"I had intended to call upon you yesterday," he explained, "but it was impossible. We were obliged to take Harry to see the wild beasts at Exeter Exchange, and the rest of the day was spent with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was delighted. This morning, I fully intended to stop by if I could spare half an hour, but one is always so occupied upon first arriving in town. I am here to commission a seal for Fanny. Tomorrow, however, I shall certainly be able to visit Berkeley Street and introduce myself to your friend, Mrs. Jennings. I understand she is a woman of considerable fortune. And the Middletons, too—you must introduce me to them. As my mother-in-law's relations, I shall be happy to show them every respect. They are excellent neighbors to you in the country, I understand."

"Excellent indeed," Elinor replied. "Their attention to our comfort and their friendliness in every particular are beyond expression."

"I am extremely glad to hear it," John said warmly. "It ought to be so; they are people of large fortune, related to you, and every civility that could make your situation pleasant might reasonably be expected. And so you are comfortably settled in your little cottage and want for nothing! Edward brought us a most charming account of the place; he described it as the most complete establishment of its kind, and you all seemed to enjoy it immensely. It was a great satisfaction to us to hear it."

Elinor felt a pang of shame for her brother and was relieved when Mrs. Jennings's servant arrived to announce that his mistress awaited them at the door.

Mr. Dashwood escorted them downstairs, was introduced to Mrs. Jennings by her carriage, and, reiterating his hope to call the following day, took his leave.

His visit was duly returned. He arrived with a pretext of apologizing for his wife's absence, claiming she was too engaged with her mother to venture out. Mrs. Jennings, however, assured him that she would not stand on ceremony, noting that they were all cousins or something akin to it. She promised to visit Mrs. John Dashwood soon and bring her sisters along. His manners toward the sisters were calmly kind, while his treatment of Mrs. Jennings was attentively civil. When Colonel Brandon entered shortly after, John eyed him with a curiosity that suggested he sought only to determine if the colonel possessed wealth before offering equal civility.

After staying for half an hour, John invited Elinor to walk with him to Conduit Street to introduce him to Sir John and Lady Middleton. The weather was exceptionally fine, and she readily agreed. The moment they stepped outside, his inquiries began.

"Who is Colonel Brandon? Is he a man of fortune?"

"Yes; he has very good property in Dorsetshire."

"I am glad of it. He seems a most gentlemanlike man; and I think, Elinor, I may congratulate you on the prospect of a very respectable establishment in life."

"Me, brother? What do you mean?"

"He likes you. I observed him narrowly and am convinced of it. What is the amount of his fortune?"

"I believe about two thousand a year."

"Two thousand a year!" John exclaimed, rising to a pitch of enthusiastic generosity. "Elinor, I wish with all my heart it were twice as much, for your sake."

"Indeed I believe you," Elinor replied dryly, "but I am very sure that Colonel Brandon has not the smallest wish of marrying me."

"You are mistaken, Elinor; you are very much mistaken. A very little trouble on your side secures him. Perhaps he is undecided just now; the smallness of your fortune may make him hesitate, or his friends may advise against it. But some of those little attentions and encouragements which ladies can so easily give will fix him, in spite of himself. And there can be no reason why you should not try for him. It is not to be supposed that any prior attachment on your side—in short, you know as to an attachment of that kind, it is quite out of the question, the objections are insurmountable—you have too much sense not to see all that. Colonel Brandon must be the man; and no civility shall be wanting on my part to make him pleased with you and your family. It is a match that must give universal satisfaction. In short, it is a kind of thing that"—lowering his voice to an important whisper—"will be exceedingly welcome to all parties." Recollecting himself, he added, "That is, I mean to say—your friends are all truly anxious to see you well settled; Fanny particularly, for she has your interest very much at heart, I assure you. And her mother too, Mrs. Ferrars, a very good-natured woman, I am sure it would give her great pleasure; she said as much the other day."

Elinor refused to offer any answer.

"It would be something remarkable, now," he continued, "something droll, if Fanny should have a brother and I a sister settling at the same time. And yet it is not very unlikely."

"Is Mr. Edward Ferrars," said Elinor, with resolution, "going to be married?"

"It is not actually settled, but there is such a thing in agitation. He has a most excellent mother. Mrs. Ferrars, with the utmost liberality, will come forward and settle on him a thousand a year if the match takes place. The lady is the Hon.
