Miss Morton, only daughter of the late Lord Morton, with thirty thousand pounds. A very desirable connection on both sides, and I have not a doubt of its taking place in time. A thousand a year is a great deal for a mother to give away to make over forever; but Mrs. Ferrars has a noble spirit. To give you another instance of her liberality: the other day, as soon as we came to town, aware that money could not be very plentiful with us just now, she put bank-notes into Fanny's hands to the amount of two hundred pounds. And extremely acceptable it is, for we must live at a great expense while we are here."

He paused, expecting her assent and compassion, and she forced herself to say, "Your expenses both in town and country must certainly be considerable; but your income is a large one."

"Not so large, I dare say, as many people suppose. I do not mean to complain, however; it is undoubtedly a comfortable one, and I hope will in time be better. The enclosure of Norland Common, now carrying on, is a most serious drain. And then I have made a little purchase within this half year; East Kingham Farm, you must remember the place, where old Gibson used to live. The land was so very desirable for me in every respect, so immediately adjoining my own property, that I felt it my duty to buy it. I could not have answered it to my conscience to let it fall into any other hands. A man must pay for his convenience; and it has cost me a vast deal of money."

"More than you think it really and intrinsically worth."

"Why, I hope not that. I might have sold it again the next day for more than I gave: but, with regard to the purchase-money, I might have been very unfortunate indeed; for the stocks were at that time so low that if I had not happened to have the necessary sum in my banker's hands, I must have sold out to very great loss."

Elinor could only smile.

"Other great and inevitable expenses too we have had on first coming to Norland. Our respected father, as you well know, bequeathed all the Stanhill effects that remained at Norland—and very valuable they were—to your mother. Far be it from me to repine at his doing so; he had an undoubted right to dispose of his own property as he chose, but, in consequence of it, we have been obliged to make large purchases of linen, china, and so forth, to supply the place of what was taken away. You may guess, after all these expenses, how very far we must be from being rich, and how acceptable Mrs. Ferrars's kindness is."

"Certainly," said Elinor; "and assisted by her liberality, I hope you may yet live to be in easy circumstances."

"Another year or two may do much towards it," he gravely replied; "but however there is still a great deal to be done. There is not a stone laid of Fanny's green-house, and nothing but the plan of the flower-garden marked out."

"Where is the green-house to be?"

"Upon the knoll behind the house. The old walnut trees are all come down to make room for it. It will be a very fine object from many parts of the park, and the flower-garden will slope down just before it and be exceedingly pretty. We have cleared away all the old thorns that grew in patches over the brow."

Elinor kept her concern and her censure to herself, thankful that Marianne was not present to share the provocation.

Having now sufficiently exposed his poverty and dispelled the necessity of purchasing earrings for his sisters during his next visit to Gray's, John's thoughts took a cheerier turn, and he began to congratulate Elinor on having such a friend as Mrs. Jennings.

"She seems a most valuable woman indeed. Her house, her style of living, all bespeak an exceeding good income; and it is an acquaintance that has not only been of great use to you hitherto but may prove materially advantageous in the end. Her inviting you to town is certainly a vast thing in your favor; and indeed, it speaks altogether so great a regard for you that in all probability when she dies you will not be forgotten. She must have a great deal to leave."

"Nothing at all, I should rather suppose; for she has only her jointure, which will descend to her children."

"But it is not to be imagined that she lives up to her income. Few people of common prudence will do that, and whatever she saves, she will be able to dispose of."

"And do you not think it more likely that she should leave it to her daughters than to us?"

"Her daughters are both exceedingly well married, and therefore I cannot perceive the necessity of her remembering them farther. Whereas, in my opinion, by her taking so much notice of you and treating you in this kind of way, she has given you a sort of claim on her future consideration which a conscientious woman would not disregard. Nothing can be kinder than her behavior; and she can hardly do all this without being aware of the expectation it raises."

"But she raises none in those most concerned. Indeed, brother, your anxiety for our welfare and prosperity carries you too far."

"Why, to be sure," he said, seeming to recollect himself, "people have little, have very little in their power. But, my dear Elinor, what is the matter with Marianne? She looks very unwell, has lost her color, and is grown quite thin. Is she ill?"

"She is not well; she has had a nervous complaint on her for several weeks."

"I am sorry for that. At her time of life, anything of an illness destroys the bloom forever! Hers has been a very short one! She was as handsome a girl last September as I ever saw, and as likely to attract a man. There was something in her style of beauty to please them particularly. I remember Fanny used to say that she would marry sooner and better than you did; not but what she is exceedingly fond of you, but so it happened to strike her. She will be mistaken, however. I question whether Marianne now will marry a man worth more than five or six hundred a year, at the utmost, and I am very much deceived if you do not do better. Dorsetshire! I know very little of Dorsetshire; but, my

Mrs. John Dashwood possessed such absolute faith in her husband's judgment that she waited until the very next day to pay her calls upon Mrs. Jennings and her daughter. Her confidence was richly rewarded; she found even the former, the woman with whom her sisters were lodging, to be entirely worthy of her notice, and as for Lady Middleton, she discovered her to be one of the most charming women in the world.

Lady Middleton was equally delighted with Mrs. Dashwood. Between them, there existed a certain cold-hearted selfishness that mutually attracted them, united by a shared insipid propriety of demeanor and a general lack of deep understanding. However, the very manners that secured Mrs. John Dashwood the high opinion of Lady Middleton failed to please Mrs. Jennings. To her, Mrs. Dashwood appeared merely as a somewhat proud-looking woman of uncordial address, who greeted her husband's sisters without affection and with almost nothing to say. Of the quarter-hour spent at Berkeley Street, Mrs. Jennings noted that at least seven and a half minutes were consumed in silence.

Elinor longed to know, though she chose not to ask, whether Edward was then in town. Nothing would have induced Fanny to mention his name voluntarily until she could inform them that his marriage with Miss Morton was settled, or until her husband's expectations regarding Colonel Brandon were fulfilled; she believed them still so deeply attached that they could not be too carefully separated in word and deed. Yet, the intelligence Fanny withheld soon flowed from another source. Lucy came shortly to claim Elinor's compassion for being unable to see Edward, despite his arrival in town with Mr. and Mrs. Dashwood. He dared not visit Bartlett's Buildings for fear of detection, and though their mutual impatience to meet was undeniable, they could do nothing but write.

Edward confirmed his presence in town within a very short time by calling twice at Berkeley Street. Twice his card was found on the table upon their return from morning engagements. Elinor was pleased that he had called, and even more pleased that she had missed him.

The Dashwoods were so prodigiously delighted with the Middletons that, though not accustomed to giving entertainments, they determined to invite them to dinner. Soon after their acquaintance began, they invited them to dine in Harley Street, where they had taken a very good house for three months. Their sisters and Mrs. Jennings were likewise invited, and John Dashwood was careful to secure Colonel Brandon, who, always glad to be where the Miss Dashwoods were, received his eager civilities with some surprise but much pleasure. They were to meet Mrs. Ferrars, though Elinor could not learn if her sons would attend. The expectation of seeing her, however, made her keenly interested in the engagement; for though she could now meet Edward's mother without the strong anxiety that once attended such an introduction, and see her with perfect indifference regarding her own opinion, her desire to be in Mrs. Ferrars' company and her curiosity to know what she was like remained as lively as ever.

This interest was soon afterwards increased, more powerfully than pleasantly, by hearing that the Miss Steeles were also to be present. So well had they recommended themselves to Lady Middleton, and so agreeable had their assiduities made them to her, that though Lucy was certainly not so elegant and her sister not even genteel, Sir John was as ready as Lady Middleton to ask them to spend a week or two in Conduit Street. It happened to be particularly convenient for the Miss Steeles that their visit should begin a few days before the party took place.

Their claims to the notice of Mrs. John Dashwood, as the nieces of the gentleman who for many years had cared for her brother, might not have procured them seats at her table; but as Lady Middleton's guests, they must be welcome. Lucy, who had long wanted to be personally known to the family, to gain a nearer view of their characters and her own difficulties, and to have an opportunity of endeavoring to please them, had seldom been happier in her life than when receiving Mrs. John Dashwood's card.

On Elinor, the effect was very different. She immediately determined that Edward, who lived with his mother, must be asked as his mother was to a party given by his sister; and to see him for the first time, after all that had passed, in the company of Lucy! She hardly knew how she could bear it. These apprehensions, perhaps, were not founded entirely on reason, and certainly not at all on truth. They were relieved, however, not by her own recollection, but by the goodwill of Lucy, who believed herself to be inflicting a severe disappointment when she told Elinor that Edward certainly would not be in Harley Street on Tuesday, and even hoped to carry the pain still farther by persuading her that he was kept away by the extreme affection for herself which he could not conceal when they were together.

The important Tuesday came that was to introduce the two young ladies to this formidable mother-in-law.

"Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!" said Lucy, as they walked up the stairs together—for the Middletons arrived so directly after Mrs. Jennings that they all followed the servant at the same time—"there is nobody here but you that can feel for me. I declare I can hardly stand. Good gracious! In a moment I shall see the person that all my happiness depends on—that is to be my mother!"

Elinor could have given her immediate relief by suggesting the possibility of it being Miss Morton's mother rather than her own whom they were about to behold; but instead, she assured her, with great sincerity, that she did pity her—to the utter amazement of Lucy, who, though really uncomfortable herself, hoped at least to be an object of irrepressible envy to Elinor.

Mrs. Ferrars was a little, thin woman, upright even to formality in her figure, and serious even to sourness in her aspect. Her complexion was sallow, and her features small, without beauty and naturally without expression; but a lucky contraction of the brow rescued her countenance from the disgrace of insipidity by giving it the strong characters of pride and ill nature. She was not a woman of many words; for, unlike people in general, she proportioned them to the number of her ideas; and of the few syllables that escaped her, not one fell to the share of Miss Dashwood, whom she eyed with the spirited determination of disliking her at all events.

Elinor could not now be made unhappy by this behavior. A few months ago it would have hurt her exceedingly; but it was not in Mrs. Ferrars' power to distress her by it now; and the difference of her manners to the Miss Steeles, a difference which seemed purposely made to humble them more, only amused her. She could not but smile to see the graciousness of both mother and daughter towards the very person—for Lucy was particularly distinguished—whom of all others, had they known as much as she did, they would have been most anxious to mortify; while she herself, who had comparatively no power to wound them, sat pointedly slighted by both. But while she smiled at a graciousness so misapplied, she could not reflect on the mean-spirited folly from which it sprung, nor observe the studied attentions with which the Miss Steeles courted its continuance, without thoroughly despising them all four.

Lucy was all exultation on being so honorably distinguished; and Miss Steele wanted only to be teased about Dr. Davies to be perfectly happy.

The dinner was a grand one, the servants were numerous, and everything bespoke the Mistress's inclination for show and the Master's ability to support it. In spite of the improvements and additions making to the Norland estate, and in spite of its owner having once been within some thousand pounds of being obliged to sell out at a loss, nothing gave any symptom of that indigence which he had tried to infer from it; no poverty of any kind, except of conversation, appeared—but there, the deficiency was considerable. John Dashwood had not much to say for himself that was worth hearing, and his wife had still less. But there was no peculiar disgrace in this; for it was very much the case with the chief of their visitors, who almost all labored under one or other of these disqualifications for being agreeable—want of sense, either natural or improved; want of elegance; want of spirits; or want of temper.

When the ladies withdrew to the drawing-room after dinner, this poverty was particularly evident, for the gentlemen had supplied the discourse with some variety—the variety of politics, enclosing land, and breaking horses—but then it was all over; and one subject only engaged the ladies till coffee came in, which was the comparative heights of Harry Dashwood and Lady Middleton's second son William, who were nearly of the same age.

Had both the children been there, the affair might have been determined too easily by measuring them at once; but as Harry only was present, it was all conjectural assertion on both sides; and everybody had a right to be equally positive in their opinion and to repeat it over and over again as often as they liked.

The parties stood thus:

The two mothers, though each really convinced that her own son was the tallest, politely decided in favor of the other.

The two grandmothers, with not less partiality but more sincerity, were equally earnest in support of their own descendant.

Lucy, who was hardly less anxious to please one parent than the other, thought the boys were both remarkably tall for their age and could not conceive that there could be the smallest difference in the world between them; and Miss Steele, with yet greater address, gave it, as fast as she could, in favor of each.

Elinor, having once delivered her opinion on William's side, by which she offended Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny still more, did not see the necessity of enforcing it by any further assertion; and Marianne, when called on for hers, offended them all by declaring that she had no opinion to give, as she had never thought about it.

Before her removal from Norland, Elinor had painted a very pretty pair of screens for her sister-in-law, which being now just mounted and brought home, ornamented her present drawing room; and these screens, catching the eye of John Dashwood on his following the other gentlemen into the room, were officiously handed by him to Colonel Brandon for his admiration.

"These are done by my eldest sister," said he; "and you, as a man of taste, will, I dare say, be pleased with them. I do not know whether you have ever happened to see any of her performances before, but she is in general reckoned to draw extremely well."

The Colonel, though disclaiming all pretensions to connoisseurship, warmly admired the screens, as he would have done anything painted by Miss Dashwood; and on the curiosity of the others being of course excited, they were handed round for general inspection. Mrs. Ferrars, not aware of their being Elinor's work, particularly requested to look at them; and after they had received gratifying testimony of Lady Middleton's approbation, Fanny presented them to her mother, considerately informing her, at the same time, that they were done by Miss Dashwood.

"Hum"—said Mrs. Ferrars—"very pretty,"—and without regarding them at all, returned them to her daughter.

Perhaps Fanny thought for a moment that her mother had been quite rude enough—for, coloring a little, she immediately said, "They are very pretty, ma'am—an't they?" But then again, the dread of having been too civil, too encouraging herself, probably came over her, for she presently added, "Do you not think they are something in Miss Morton's style of painting, Ma'am?—She does paint most delightfully!—How beautifully her last landscape is done!"

"Beautifully indeed! But she does every thing well."

Marianne could not bear this.—She was already greatly displeased with Mrs. Ferrars; and such ill-timed praise of another, at Elinor's expense, though she had not any notion of what was principally meant by it, provoked her immediately to say with warmth, "This is admiration of a very particular kind!—what is Miss Morton to us?—who knows, or who cares, for her?—it is Elinor of whom we think and speak."

And so saying, she took the screens out of her sister-in-law's hands, to admire them herself as they ought to be admired.

Mrs. Ferrars looked exceedingly angry, and drawing herself up more stiffly than ever, pronounced in retort this bitter philippic, "Miss Morton is Lord Morton's daughter."

Fanny looked very angry too, and her husband was all in a fright at his sister's audacity. Elinor was much more hurt by Marianne's warmth than she had been by what produced it; but Colonel Brandon's eyes, as they were fixed on Marianne, declared that he noticed only what was amiable in it, the affectionate heart which could not bear to see a sister slighted in the smallest point.

Marianne's feelings did not stop here. The cold insolence of Mrs. Ferrars's general behavior to her sister seemed, to her, to foretell such difficulties and distresses to Elinor as her own wounded heart taught her to think of with horror; and urged by a strong impulse of affectionate sensibility, she moved after a moment to her sister's chair, and putting one arm round her neck and one cheek close to hers, said in a low, but eager, voice, "Dear, dear Elinor, don't mind them. Don't let them make you unhappy."

She could say no more; her spirits were quite overcome, and hiding her face on Elinor's shoulder, she burst into tears. Everybody's attention was called, and almost everybody was concerned.—Colonel Brandon rose up and went to them without knowing what he did.—Mrs. Jennings, with a very intelligent "Ah! poor dear," immediately gave her her salts; and Sir John felt so desperately enraged against the author of this nervous distress that he instantly changed his seat to one close by Lucy Steele and gave her, in a whisper, a brief account of the whole shocking affair.

In a few minutes, however, Marianne was recovered enough to put an end to the bustle and sit down among the rest; though her spirits retained the impression of what had passed throughout the evening.

"Poor Marianne!" said her brother to Colonel

Elinor's curiosity regarding Mrs. Ferrars was finally sated. She had discovered in the older woman everything that rendered a deeper alliance between their families not merely difficult, but impossible. She had witnessed enough of Mrs. Ferrars's pride, her meanness, and her rigid, unyielding prejudice against herself to fully comprehend the labyrinth of obstacles that had delayed Edward and her own union. Yet, she had seen almost enough to be grateful, for her own sake, that a greater barrier remained intact—one that spared her from the torment of depending on Mrs. Ferrars's caprice or seeking her approval. Perhaps she did not quite rejoice that Edward was bound to Lucy, but she resolved that, had Lucy been more amiable, she *ought* to have rejoiced.

She marveled at how Lucy's spirits could be so buoyed by Mrs. Ferrars's mere civility. How could her vanity be so blinded that she mistook attention paid solely because she was *not* Elinor for a personal compliment? How could she draw encouragement from a preference granted only because her true situation was unknown? These truths were evident in Lucy's eyes at the time, and confirmed the following morning when, at Lucy's specific request, Lady Middleton deposited her in Berkeley Street to convey her happiness.

The timing proved fortunate, for a message from Mrs. Palmer shortly after Lucy's arrival whisked Mrs. Jennings away.

"My dear friend," Lucy exclaimed the moment they were alone, "I come to speak of my happiness. Was there anything so flattering as Mrs. Ferrars's treatment of me yesterday? She was so excessively affable! You know how I dreaded the thought of seeing her; yet the instant I was introduced, her behavior radiated such affection that it seemed she had instantly taken a fancy to me. Was it not so? You saw it all; were you not struck?"

"She was certainly very civil to you."

"Civil! Did you see nothing but civility? I saw far more. Such kindness reserved exclusively for me! No pride, no hauteur, and your sister was just the same—all sweetness!"

Elinor wished to change the subject, but Lucy pressed her to admit she had cause for happiness. Reluctantly, Elinor continued.

"Undoubtedly, if they had known your engagement, nothing could be more flattering than their treatment of you; but since that was not the case—"

"I guessed you would say so," Lucy interrupted quickly. "But there was no reason in the world why Mrs. Ferrars should seem to like me if she did not, and her liking me is everything. You won't talk me out of my satisfaction. I am sure it will all end well, with no difficulties whatsoever. Mrs. Ferrars is a charming woman, and so is your sister. They are both delightful! I wonder I never heard you say how agreeable Mrs. Dashwood was!"

Elinor had no answer and offered none.

"Are you ill, Miss Dashwood? You seem low; you don't speak. Are you well?"

"I have never been in better health."

"I am glad of it with all my heart, but you certainly did not look it. I should be sorry to have *you* ill; you have been my greatest comfort in the world. Heaven knows what I should have done without your friendship."

Elinor attempted a civil reply, though doubting its success. It seemed to satisfy Lucy, who immediately responded,

"I am perfectly convinced of your regard for me, and next to Edward's love, it is my greatest comfort. Poor Edward! But now, there is one good thing: we shall be able to meet, and meet often. Lady Middleton delights in Mrs. Dashwood, so we shall be frequent visitors in Harley Street. Edward spends half his time with his sister, besides, Lady Middleton and Mrs. Ferrars will be visiting soon. Both Mrs. Ferrars and your sister have said more than once they would always be glad to see me. They are such charming women! If you ever tell your sister what I think of her, you cannot praise her too highly."

But Elinor refused to give her any encouragement to hope she *would* tell her sister. Lucy persisted.

"I am sure I would have seen it in a moment if Mrs. Ferrars had disliked me. If she had merely offered a formal courtesy, said nothing, and never looked at me pleasantly afterward—you know what I mean—if I had been treated in that forbidding manner, I would have given up in despair. I could not have stood it. Where she *does* dislike, I know it is most violent."

Elinor was prevented from replying to this civil triumph when the door opened and the servant announced Mr. Ferrars. Edward walked in immediately.

It was an awkward moment; the countenance of each betrayed it. They all looked foolish, and Edward seemed equally inclined to retreat from the room as to advance further. The very circumstance they had most anxiously sought to avoid had fallen upon them. They were not only all three together, but together without the relief of any other person. The ladies recovered themselves first. It was not Lucy's role to put herself forward, and the appearance of secrecy must be maintained. She could only offer a look of tenderness and, after a slight address, said no more.

Elinor, however, had more to do. Anxious for his sake and her own to manage the situation well, she forced herself, after a moment's recollection, to welcome him with a look and manner that were almost easy, almost open. Another struggle, another effort, improved them further. She would not allow Lucy's presence, nor the consciousness of some injustice toward herself, to deter her from expressing her happiness at seeing him and her regret at having been away when he called previously in Berkeley Street. She would not be frightened from paying him those attentions due to a friend and almost a relative by Lucy's observant eyes, though she soon perceived them watching her narrowly.

Her manners gave Edward some reassurance, and he had the courage to sit down; but his embarrassment still exceeded that of the ladies in a proportion the case rendered reasonable, though rare for his sex. His heart did not possess the indifference of Lucy's, nor could his conscience find the ease Elinor's did.

Lucy, with a demure and settled air, seemed determined to contribute nothing to the comfort of the others and remained silent. Almost everything said proceeded from Elinor, who was obliged to volunteer all information regarding her mother's health, their arrival in town, and the like—details Edward ought to have inquired about but never did.

Her exertions did not stop there. Soon, she felt so heroically disposed that she determined, under the pretext of fetching Marianne, to leave the others to themselves. She did so, and in the handsomest manner possible, loitering on the landing-place for several minutes with high-minded fortitude before descending to her sister. Once that was done, however, Edward's raptures had to cease, for Marianne's joy hurried her into the drawing-room immediately. Her pleasure in seeing him was, like every other feeling, strong in itself and strongly spoken. She met him with a hand ready to be taken and a voice expressing the affection of a sister.

"Dear Edward!" she cried. "This is a moment of great happiness! This would almost make amends for everything!"

Edward tried to return her kindness as it deserved, but before such witnesses, he dared not say half what he truly felt. Again they all sat down, and for a moment or two, silence reigned. Marianne looked with speaking tenderness, alternating between Edward and Elinor, regretting only that their delight was checked by Lucy's unwelcome presence. Edward was the first to speak, noting Marianne's altered looks and expressing his fear that London was not agreeing with her.

"Oh, don't think of me!" she replied with spirited earnestness, though tears filled her eyes. "Don't think of *my* health. Elinor is well; you see. That must be enough for us both."

This remark did little to ease Edward or Elinor, nor did it conciliate Lucy, who looked up at Marianne with no very benignant expression.

"Do you like London?" Edward asked, willing to introduce another subject.

"Not at all. I expected much pleasure, but found none. The sight of you, Edward, is the only comfort it has afforded; and thank Heaven! you are what you always were!"

She paused; no one spoke.

"I think, Elinor," she added presently, "we must employ Edward to take care of us in our return to Barton. In a week or two, I suppose, we shall be going; and I trust Edward will not be very unwilling to accept the charge."

Poor Edward muttered something, but no one knew what it was, not even himself. Marianne, seeing his agitation and easily tracing it to whatever cause best pleased her, was perfectly satisfied and soon changed the topic.

"We spent such a day in Harley Street yesterday! So dull, so wretchedly dull! But I have much to say to you on that head which cannot be said now."

With this admirable discretion, she deferred the assurance of finding their mutual relatives more disagreeable than ever and being particularly disgusted with his mother until they were more private.

"But why were you not there, Edward? Why did you not come?"

"I was engaged elsewhere."

"Engaged! But what was that when such friends were to be met?"

"Perhaps, Miss Marianne," cried Lucy, eager to take revenge, "you think young men never stand upon engagements if they have no mind to keep them, little as well as great."

Elinor was very angry, but Marianne seemed entirely insensible to the sting. Calmly, she replied,

"Not so, indeed; for seriously speaking, I am very sure conscience kept Edward from Harley Street. I really believe he *has* the most delicate conscience in the world; the most scrupulous in performing every engagement, however minute, and however it may make against his interest or pleasure. He is the most fearful of giving pain, of wounding expectation, and the most incapable of being selfish of anybody I ever saw. Edward, it is so, and I will say it. What! Are you never to hear yourself praised? Then you must be no friend of mine; for those who accept my love and esteem must submit to my open commendation."

The nature of her commendation, in this instance, happened to be particularly ill-suited to the feelings of two-thirds of her audience, and so unexhilarating to Edward that he very soon rose to go away.

"Going so soon!" said Marianne. "My dear Edward, this must not be."

Drawing him aside, she whispered her persuasion that Lucy could not stay much longer. Even this encouragement failed; he insisted on leaving, and Lucy, who would have outstayed him had his visit lasted two hours, soon departed.

"What can bring her here so often?" Marianne asked upon her leaving. "Could she not see that we wanted her gone? How teasing to Edward!"

"Why so? We were all his friends, and Lucy has been the longest known to him of any. It is but natural that he should like to see her as well as ourselves."

Marianne looked at her steadily and said, "You know, Elinor, that this is a kind of talking which I cannot bear. If you only hope to have your assertion contradicted, as I must suppose to be the case, you ought to recollect that I am the last person in the world to do it. I cannot descend to be tricked out of assurances that are not really wanted."

She then left the room, and Elinor dared not follow her to say more. Bound by her promise of secrecy to Lucy, she could give no information that would convince Marianne. Painful as the consequences of Marianne continuing in an error might be, she was obliged to submit to it. All she could hope was that Edward would not often expose himself or her to the distress of hearing Marianne's mistaken warmth, nor to the repetition of any other part of the pain that had attended their recent meeting—and this she had every reason to expect.

The newspapers soon carried the news that Mrs. Palmer had safely delivered a son and heir, a piece of intelligence that brought immense satisfaction to her circle of acquaintances. This event, so pivotal to Mrs. Jennings's joy, temporarily reshaped her schedule and, by extension, the lives of her younger friends. Determined to be with Charlotte, Mrs. Jennings visited her household every morning immediately after dressing, remaining until late evening. Consequently, the Dashwood sisters, at the specific request of the Middletons, spent their entire days in Conduit Street. Though they would have preferred to remain at Mrs. Jennings's home for at least the mornings, they could not argue against the united wishes of everyone involved. Thus, their time was surrendered to Lady Middleton and the two Miss Steeles, whose company they found far less valued than it was ostensibly sought.

They possessed too much sense to be truly desirable companions for Lady Middleton, who, despite her outward politeness, held no genuine affection for them. Unable to believe anyone could be good-natured if they refused to flatter her or her children, and suspecting that their love for reading made them satirical—a charge she leveled without fully understanding satire herself—she viewed them with suspicion. Their presence acted as a restraint on both Lady Middleton and Lucy Steele. It curbed the idleness of the former and the business of the latter; Lady Middleton felt ashamed to do nothing in their company, while Lucy feared that the flattery she usually administered would be despised. Miss Steele was the least disturbed by their arrival, and indeed, she could have been fully reconciled to it had either sister shared the full, minute details of the affair between Marianne and Mr. Willoughby. A simple account of that tragedy would have made her feel amply rewarded for sacrificing her prime spot by the fire. Yet, this conciliation was denied. Miss Steele's occasional expressions of pity to Elinor or reflections on the fickleness of beaux before Marianne elicited only indifference or disgust. Even a lighter jest about the doctor would have secured her friendship, but the sisters were too unwilling to oblige; if Sir John dined away, Miss Steele might spend a whole day without hearing a single word of raillery on the subject from them.

Mrs. Jennings, however, remained blissfully unaware of these underlying jealousies and discontents. She delighted in the girls' reunion, often congratulating them nightly on escaping the company of a "stupid old woman" for so long. Whether joining them at Sir John's or her own house, she arrived in high spirits, attributing Charlotte's success to her own care and providing such exhaustive details of the baby's condition that only Miss Steele possessed the curiosity to listen. One matter, however, did disturb her: Mr. Palmer's stubborn adherence to the unfatherly notion that all infants are identical. Despite the striking resemblance of the baby to every relative on both sides, he refused to acknowledge it, insisting it was no more special than any other child of its age, and certainly not the finest in the world.

Meanwhile, a misfortune befell Mrs. John Dashwood. While her two sisters were visiting her in Harley Street, another acquaintance dropped in. This lady, allowing her imagination to outrun truth and probability, heard the name of the Miss Dashwoods and assumed they were staying in Harley Street. Within a day or two, she sent invitations to a small musical party for the sisters, their brother, and sister. Mrs. John Dashwood was thus forced to submit to the inconvenience of sending her carriage for them and the greater unpleasantness of appearing to treat them with attention, fearing they might expect further outings. Though she retained the power to disappoint them, she felt injured by the expectation of anything less than perfection.

Marianne had grown so accustomed to daily outings that it became a matter of indifference to her whether she went or not. She prepared mechanically for every evening's engagement, expecting no amusement and often not knowing her destination until the last moment. She had also become indifferent to her dress and appearance, neglecting her toilette entirely until Miss Steele took over in the first five minutes of their meeting. Miss Steele observed everything with minute scrutiny and general curiosity, asking about the price of every item in Marianne's wardrobe, guessing the total number of her gowns with better judgment than Marianne herself, and hoping to discover her weekly washing costs and annual spending before they parted. These impertinent interrogations were invariably capped with a compliment, intended as a douceur but regarded by Marianne as the height of impertinence. After being examined on the value of her gown, the color of her shoes, and the arrangement of her hair, she was almost certain to be told that, on Miss Steele's word, she looked "vastly smart" and destined to make many conquests.

With such encouragement, Marianne was dismissed to her brother's carriage, which was ready to depart five minutes after stopping—a punctuality that annoyed Mrs. John Dashwood, who had preceded them to the hostess's house, hoping for a delay to inconvenience either herself or her coachman. The evening's events were unremarkable. The party comprised many with genuine taste for music and many without, while the performers, as usual, considered themselves the finest in England.

Elinor, neither musical nor pretending to be so, freely turned her eyes away from the grand pianoforte to fix them on any other object in the room. In one such glance, she spotted among a group of young men the very gentleman who had lectured them on toothpick-cases at Gray's. Soon after, she saw him looking at her and speaking familiarly to her brother. Just as she decided to inquire his name, Mr. Dashwood approached and introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

He addressed her with easy civility, bowing in a manner that assured her he was exactly the coxcomb Lucy had described. Had Elinor's regard for Edward depended less on his own merit and more on that of his nearest relations, his brother's bow would have delivered the final blow to what her mother and sister had begun. Yet, as she marveled at the contrast between the two young men, the emptiness and conceit of one did not diminish her charity for the modesty and worth of the other. Robert explained the difference himself during a quarter-hour conversation. Speaking of his brother, he lamented the *gaucherie* he believed kept Edward from proper society, candidly attributing it less to natural deficiency than to the misfortune of a private education. He claimed that, while perhaps lacking any material superiority by nature, his attendance at a public school had made him as fit for the world as any other man.

"Upon my soul," he added, "I believe it is nothing more; and so I often tell my mother, when she is grieving about it. 'My dear Madam,' I always say, 'you must make yourself easy. The evil is now irremediable, and it has been entirely your own doing. Why would you be persuaded by my uncle, Sir Robert, against your own judgment, to place Edward under private tuition at the most critical time of his life? If you had only sent him to Westminster as well as myself, instead of sending him to Mr. Pratt's, all this would have been prevented.' This is how I view the matter, and my mother is perfectly convinced of her error."

Elinor did not oppose his opinion, for while she generally valued the advantages of a public school, she could not think of Edward's residence in Mr. Pratt's family with any satisfaction.

"You reside in Devonshire, I think," he observed next, "in a cottage near Dawlish."

Elinor corrected him regarding the location, and it seemed surprising to him that anyone could live in Devonshire without residing near Dawlish. Nevertheless, he bestowed hearty approval on their type of house.

"For my own part," he said, "I am excessively fond of a cottage; there is always so much comfort, so much elegance about them. I protest, if I had any money to spare, I should buy a little land and build one myself, within a short distance of London, where I might drive down at any time, collect a few friends, and be happy. I advise everybody who is going to build to build a cottage. My friend Lord Courtland came to me the other day to ask my advice and laid before me three different plans of Bonomi's. I was to decide on the best. 'My dear Courtland,' I immediately said, throwing them all into the fire, 'do not adopt either of them, but by all means build a cottage.' And that, I fancy, will be the end of it."

"Some people imagine there can be no accommodations, no space in a cottage," he continued. "But this is a mistake. I was last month at my friend Elliott's, near Dartford. Lady Elliott wished to give a dance. 'But how can it be done?' she asked. 'There is not a room in this cottage that will hold ten couples, and where can the supper be?' I immediately saw there could be no difficulty. 'My dear Lady Elliott,' I said, 'do not be uneasy. The dining parlour will admit eighteen couples with ease; card-tables may be placed in the drawing-room; the library may be open for tea; and let the supper be set out in the saloon.' Lady Elliott was delighted. We measured the dining-room, and it held exactly eighteen couples. So, if people know how to set about it, every comfort may be enjoyed in a cottage as well as in the most spacious dwelling."

Elinor agreed to all this, feeling he did not deserve the compliment of rational opposition.

Since John Dashwood derived no more pleasure from music than his eldest sister, his mind was free to wander elsewhere. A thought struck him during the evening, which he communicated to his wife for her approbation upon their return home. The consideration of Mrs. Dennison's mistake in supposing his sisters were her guests suggested the propriety of inviting them to become actual guests while Mrs. Jennings was away. The expense would be negligible, the inconvenience minimal, and it was an attention his conscience deemed requisite to clear his promise to his father. Fanny was startled by the proposal.

"I do not see how it can be done," she said, "without affronting Lady Middleton, for they spend every day with her. Otherwise, I should be exceedingly glad to do it. You know I am always ready to pay them any attention in my power, as my taking them out this evening shows. But they are Lady Middleton's visitors. How can I ask them away from her?"

Her husband, with great humility, failed to see the force of her objection. "They had already spent a week in this manner in Conduit Street, and Lady Middleton could not be displeased at their giving the same number of days to such near relations."

Fanny paused, then spoke with fresh vigor. "My love, I would ask them with all my heart, if it was in my power. But I had just settled within myself to ask the Miss Steeles to spend a few days with us. They are very well-behaved, good kind of girls; and I think the attention is due to them, as their uncle did so very well by Edward. We can ask your sisters some other year, you know; but the Miss Steeles may not be in town any more. I am sure you will like them; indeed, you *do* like them, you know, very much already, and so does my mother; and they are such favourites with Harry!"

Mr. Dashwood was convinced. He saw the necessity of inviting the Miss Steeles immediately, and his conscience was pacified by the resolution to invite his sisters another year, though he slyly suspected that another year might render the invitation needless by bringing Elinor to town as Colonel Brandon's wife and Marianne as their visitor.

Fanny, rejoicing in her escape and proud of the ready wit that had procured it, wrote the next morning to Lucy, requesting her company and her sister's for some days in Harley Street as soon as Lady Middleton could spare them. This was enough to make Lucy truly and reasonably happy. Mrs. Dashwood seemed actually working for her, cherishing all her hopes and promoting all her views. Such an opportunity to be with Edward and his family was the most material to her interests, and such an invitation the most gratifying to her feelings. It was an advantage that could not be too gratefully acknowledged nor too speedily utilized; consequently, the visit to Lady Middleton, which had previously lacked precise limits, was instantly discovered to have been always meant to end in two days' time.

When the note was shown to Elinor, within ten minutes of its arrival, it gave her, for the first time, some share in the expectations of Lucy. Such a mark of uncommon kindness, vouchsafed on so short an acquaintance, seemed to declare that the goodwill towards her arose from something more than mere malice against herself, and might be brought, by time and address, to achieve everything Lucy wished. Her flattery had already subdued the pride of Lady Middleton and made an entry into the close heart of Mrs. John Dashwood; these were effects that opened the probability of greater things.

The Miss Steeles moved to Harley Street, and all that reached Elinor of their influence there strengthened her expectation of the event. Sir John, who called on them more than once, brought home such accounts of the favor they were in as must be universally striking. Mrs. Dashwood had never been so pleased with any young women in her life as she was with them; she had given each of them a needle book made by some emigrant, called Lucy by her Christian name, and did not know whether she should ever be able to part with them.

By the end of the fortnight, Mrs. Palmer had recovered sufficiently for her mother to cease her constant attendance, resuming her own domestic routine while visiting her daughter once or twice daily. Upon returning to Berkeley Street, Mrs. Jennings found the Dashwood sisters eager to resume their usual social engagements.

It was on the third or fourth morning after this resettlement that Mrs. Jennings entered the drawing-room, where Elinor sat alone. Her hurried demeanor suggested she possessed news of great import. Seizing the moment, she launched into her revelation.

"Lord! my dear Miss Dashwood, have you heard the news?" she exclaimed. "Something so strange! When I visited Mr. Palmer, I found Charlotte in a panic over the child—it was crying, fretting, covered in pimples. I assured her it was merely the red gum, and the nurse agreed. Yet Charlotte refused to be satisfied, so Mr. Donovan was summoned. Fortunately, he had just arrived from Harley Street. Upon seeing the infant, he confirmed it was nothing but the red gum, and Charlotte was relieved. However, as he departed, I impulsively asked if there were any other news. He immediately adopted a grave, smirking expression, as if guarding a secret. Finally, he whispered that, 'For fear any unpleasant report should reach the young ladies under your care regarding their sister's indisposition, I think it advisable to say there is no great reason for alarm; I hope Mrs. Dashwood will do very well.'"

"What! Is Fanny ill?" Elinor asked.

"That is precisely what I said," Mrs. Jennings replied. "Then the truth emerged. Mr. Edward Ferrars—the very young man I once joked with you about, though I am delighted there was nothing between us—has been secretly engaged to my cousin Lucy for over a year! Not a soul knew, save Nancy. It is no wonder they liked one another, but that such a union remained undetected is baffling. I never saw them together, yet I should have guessed. This secret was kept to avoid offending Mrs. Ferrars, leaving neither she, your brother, nor your sister aware until this morning. Poor Nancy, a well-meaning but simple creature, accidentally revealed it. Thinking, 'They are all so fond of Lucy, they will accept this easily,' she rushed to your sister, who was working alone on her carpet. Unbeknownst to her, your sister had only moments prior suggested matching Edward with a lord's daughter. The blow to her vanity was immediate. She fell into violent hysterics, screaming loudly enough to be heard by your brother, who was downstairs composing a letter to his steward. He rushed upstairs to find a terrible scene unfolding. Lucy had arrived, unaware of the turmoil, and was driven into a fainting fit by your sister's fury. Nancy knelt and wept bitterly, while your brother paced in despair. Mrs. Dashwood declared they must leave instantly, forcing your brother to kneel and plead for permission to pack their belongings. Only then did she relapse into hysterics, so terrified was he that he summoned Mr. Donovan. He found the house in uproar; the carriage waited, and Lucy, barely able to walk, was being helped in alongside a nearly hysterical Nancy. I confess, I have no patience with your sister. I hope, with all my heart, this becomes a match despite her. Imagine Mr. Edward's passion upon hearing this! They say he is deeply fond of her. Mr. Donovan agrees. He has already returned to Harley Street to be near when Mrs. Ferrars is informed, for your sister is certain she will also succumb to hysterics. I care not for her reaction. I pity neither of them. People make such a fuss over money and status! There is no earthly reason why Mr. Edward and Lucy should not marry. Mrs. Ferrars can easily provide for her son, and though Lucy has little, she knows how to stretch resources. With five hundred a-year, I am sure Mrs. Ferrars could maintain as grand an appearance as anyone else with eight. Imagine how snugly they might live in a cottage like yours, perhaps slightly larger, with two maids and two men. I believe I could even provide a housemaid, as my Betty has a sister seeking employment."

Mrs. Jennings paused, allowing Elinor time to compose herself. She responded with the expected observations, relieved that Mrs. Jennings no longer suspected her of romantic attachment to Edward. Above all, she was grateful for Marianne's absence, which allowed her to discuss the affair with impartiality.

Elinor struggled to determine her own expectations, though she earnestly suppressed the notion of any outcome other than the marriage of Edward and Lucy. She was anxious to hear Mrs. Ferrars's reaction and, more so, to observe Edward's conduct. She felt profound compassion for him, little for Lucy, and none for the rest of the party.

Realizing Mrs. Jennings could discuss no other topic, Elinor saw the necessity of preparing Marianne. There was no time to lose in revealing the truth and ensuring Marianne could hear such news from others without detecting her sister's distress or resentment toward Edward.

Elinor's task was painful. She intended to remove what she believed was Marianne's chief consolation, to detail facts that might ruin Edward forever in her sister's eyes, and to force Marianne to relive her own disappointment through a resemblance of their situations. Though unwelcome, it was necessary.

Elinor made no attempt to dwell on her own feelings or portray herself as suffering, relying instead on the self-command she had practiced since learning of Edward's engagement. Her narration was clear and simple. Though delivered with emotion, it lacked violent agitation or impetuous grief. That reaction belonged to the listener; Marianne listened with horror and wept excessively. Elinor offered herself as a comforter, providing assurances of her own composure and a earnest vindication of Edward from all charges but imprudence.

For some time, Marianne refused to accept either explanation. Edward seemed a second Willoughby. Acknowledging, as Elinor did, that she had loved him sincerely, how could she feel less? Regarding Lucy Steele, she deemed her so utterly unamiable and incapable of attracting a sensible man that she could not initially believe, nor later pardon, Edward's former affection for her. She refused to admit it was natural, leaving Elinor to prove it through a deeper knowledge of mankind.

Elinor's initial communication stated only the fact of the engagement and its duration. Marianne's emotions then overwhelmed her, halting further detail. For a while, the only goal was to soothe her distress, lessen her fears, and combat her resentment. The first question Marianne posed was, "How long has this been known to you, Elinor? Has he written to you?"

"I have known it for four months," Elinor replied. "When Lucy first came to Barton Park last November, she confided her engagement to me."

Marianne's eyes widened in astonishment, her lips unable to express the shock. After a pause of wonder, she exclaimed, "Four months! Have you known of this for four months?"

Elinor confirmed it.

"While attending me in all my misery, has this been on your heart? And I have reproached you for being happy!"

"It was not fit that you should then know how much I was the reverse!"

"Four months!" Marianne cried again. "So calm! So cheerful! How have you been supported?"

"By feeling that I was doing my duty. My promise to Lucy obliged me to remain silent. I owed it to her to avoid giving any hint of the truth, and to my family and friends, not to create a solicitude about me that I could not satisfy."

Marianne appeared deeply moved.

"I have often wished to undeceive you and my mother," Elinor added. "Once or twice I attempted it, but without betraying my trust, I could never have convinced you."

"Four months! And yet you loved him!"

"Yes. But I did not love only him; and while the comfort of others was dear to me, I was glad to spare them from knowing how much I felt. Now, I can think and speak of it with little emotion. I would not have you suffer on my account; for I assure you I no longer suffer materially myself. I have many things to support me. I am not conscious of having provoked this disappointment by any imprudence of my own. I have borne it as much as possible without spreading it farther. I acquit Edward of essential misconduct. I wish him very happy; and I am so sure of his always doing his duty that, though he may harbor some regret now, he must eventually overcome it. Lucy does not lack sense, and that is the foundation upon which everything good may be built. And after all, Marianne, after all that is bewitching in the idea of a single, constant attachment, and all that can be said of happiness depending entirely on one particular person, it is not meant—it is not fit—it is not possible that it should be so. Edward will marry Lucy. He will marry a woman superior in person and understanding to half her sex; and time and habit will teach him to forget that he ever thought another superior to her."

"If such is your way of thinking," Marianne said, "if the loss of what is most valued is so easily made up by something else, your resolution, your self-command, are perhaps less to be wondered at. They are brought more within my comprehension."

"I understand you. You do not suppose that I have ever felt much. For four months, Marianne, I have had all this hanging on my mind, without being at liberty to speak of it to a single creature. Knowing that it would make you and my mother most unhappy whenever explained, yet unable to prepare you for it in the least. It was forced upon me by the very person whose prior engagement ruined all my prospects, and told to me, as I thought, with triumph. Therefore, I had to oppose her suspicions by appearing indifferent where I was most deeply interested. I have had to listen to her hopes and exultation again and again. I have known myself to be divided from Edward forever without hearing one circumstance that could make me less desire the connection. Nothing has proved him unworthy; nor has anything declared him indifferent to me. I have had to contend against the unkindness of his sister and the insolence of his mother, suffering the punishment of an attachment without enjoying its advantages. And all this has occurred at a time when, as you know too well, it was not my only unhappiness. If you can think me capable of ever feeling, surely you may suppose that I have suffered now. The composure of mind with which I have brought myself to consider the matter, the consolation I have admitted, have been the effect of constant and painful exertion; they did not spring up spontaneously. No, Marianne. Then, if I had not been bound to silence, perhaps nothing could have kept me entirely—not even what I owed to my dearest friends—from openly showing that I was very unhappy."

Marianne was completely subdued.

"Oh! Elinor," she cried, "you have made me hate myself forever. How barbarous have I been to you! You, who have been my only comfort, who have borne with me in all my misery, who have seemed to be suffering only for me! Is this my gratitude? Is this the only return I can make you? Because your merit cries out upon myself, I have been trying to do it away."

Tenderest caresses followed this confession. In such a frame of mind, Elinor had no difficulty in obtaining whatever promise she required. At Marianne's request, she pledged never to speak of the affair to anyone with the slightest appearance of bitterness; to meet Lucy without betraying the smallest increase of dislike; and even to see Edward himself, if chance brought them together, without diminishing her usual cordiality. These were great concessions, but where Marianne felt she had injured, no reparation was too much.

She fulfilled her promise of discretion to admiration. She attended to everything Mrs. Jennings said with an unchanging countenance, dissented from her in nothing, and was heard three times to say, "Yes, ma'am." She listened to Mrs. Jennings's praise of Lucy with only shifting from one chair to another, and when Mrs. Jennings spoke of Edward's affection, it cost her only a spasm in her throat. Such advances toward heroism in her sister made Elinor feel equal to anything.

The next morning brought another trial in the form of a visit from their brother, who arrived with a solemn aspect to discuss the dreadful affair and bring news of his wife.

"You have heard, I suppose," he began gravely, "of the very shocking discovery that took place under our roof yesterday."

They all nodded in assent; it seemed too awful a moment for speech.

"Your sister," he continued, "has suffered dreadfully. Mrs. Ferrars too—in short, it has been a scene of such compounded distress—but I hope the storm may be weathered without us being quite overcome. Poor Fanny! She was in hysterics all yesterday. But I would not alarm you too much. Donovan says there is nothing materially to be apprehended; her constitution is good, and her resolution equal to anything. She has borne it all with the fortitude of an angel! She says she shall never think well of anybody again, and one cannot wonder at it after being so deceived! Meeting with such ingratitude where so much kindness and confidence had been shown! It was purely from the benevolence of her heart that she invited these young women to her house; merely because she thought they deserved attention, were harmless, well-behaved girls, and pleasant companions. Otherwise, we both wished very much to invite you and Marianne while your kind friend attended her daughter. And now to be rewarded thus! 'I wish, with all my heart,' says poor Fanny in her affectionate way, 'that we had asked your sisters instead of them.'"

He paused to receive thanks, then proceeded.

"What poor Mrs. Ferrars suffered when Fanny first broke it to her is indescribable. While she, with the truest affection, planned a most eligible connection for him, was it to be supposed he could be secretly engaged to another? Such a suspicion could never have entered her head! If she suspected any prepossession elsewhere, it could not be in that quarter. 'There, to be sure,' she said, 'I might have thought

Mrs. Jennings spoke of Edward's conduct with such warmth that it bordered on adoration, yet only Elinor and Marianne possessed the depth of understanding to grasp its true value. They alone knew the narrow margin he had walked to avoid disobedience, and the meager consolation awaiting him beyond the simple knowledge of having done right—a consolation all the more poignant given the total loss of friends and fortune. Elinor took pride in his unshakeable integrity; Marianne, overwhelmed by compassion for his suffering, forgave every fault he had committed. Yet, despite the restoration of their confidence following this public revelation, neither found themselves eager to dwell upon the subject when alone. Elinor avoided it on principle, fearing that Marianne's too-warm, too-positive assurances would only cement in her mind a belief of Edward's continued affection for herself that she desperately wished to dissolve. For Marianne, the courage to discuss the topic soon evaporated; every attempt to converse on it left her more dissatisfied with her own conduct than before, as the inevitable comparison between her actions and Elinor's sharpened her self-reproach.

She felt the full weight of that comparison, but not as her sister had hoped—to spur her to immediate action. Instead, it brought the torturous sting of continual regret, a bitter mourning for the fact that she had never exerted herself sooner. It offered only the agony of penitence without the faintest hope of amendment. Her mind, so thoroughly weakened, still convinced her that present effort was impossible, a realization that served only to dispirit her further.

For a day or two thereafter, no new tidings arrived regarding affairs in Harley Street or Bartlett's Buildings. Though Mrs. Jennings had ample material to spread among her circle without seeking further information, she had resolved from the outset to visit her cousins with comfort and inquiry as soon as possible. Only the hindrance of an unusually high volume of visitors had delayed her departure.

On the third day following their acquisition of these particulars, the weather proved so fine and beautiful that it drew many to Kensington Gardens, despite it being merely the second week of March. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor were among the crowd. Marianne, however, knowing that the Willoughbys had returned to town and harboring a constant dread of encountering them, chose to remain at home rather than risk venturing into such a public gathering.

Shortly after entering the gardens, an intimate acquaintance of Mrs. Jennings joined them. Elinor was relieved that this companion engaged Mrs. Jennings's attention, leaving her free for quiet reflection. She saw nothing of the Willoughbys, nothing of Edward, and for some time, nothing of anyone who might hold interest for her, whether in a serious or lighthearted capacity. Finally, however, she was surprised to be accosted by Miss Steele. Though appearing somewhat shy, Miss Steele expressed great delight at meeting them. Encouraged by Mrs. Jennings's particular kindness, she temporarily left her own party to join theirs. Mrs. Jennings immediately whispered to Elinor, "Get it all out of her, my dear. She will tell you anything if you ask. You see I cannot leave Mrs. Clarke."

Fortunately for both Mrs. Jennings's curiosity and Elinor's, Miss Steele was inclined to speak freely without prompting, for otherwise, little would have been learned.

"I am so glad to meet you," said Miss Steele, taking her arm familiarly—"for I wanted to see you of all things in the world." Lowering her voice, she added, "I suppose Mrs. Jennings has heard all about it. Is she angry?"

"Not at all, I believe, with you."

"That is a good thing. And Lady Middleton—is she angry?"

"I cannot suppose it possible that she should be."

"I am monstrous glad of it. Good gracious! I have had such a time of it! I never saw Lucy in such a rage in my life. She vowed at first she would never trim me a new bonnet or do anything else for me again so long as she lived; but now she is quite come to, and we are as good friends as ever. Look, she made me this bow to my hat and put in the feather last night. There now, you are going to laugh at me too. But why should not I wear pink ribbons? I do not care if it is the Doctor's favorite color. I am sure, for my part, I should never have known he liked it better than any other if he had not happened to say so. My cousins have been plaguing me! I declare sometimes I do not know which way to look before them."

She drifted away onto a subject on which Elinor had nothing to say, and thus soon deemed it expedient to steer the conversation back to its original course.

"Well, but Miss Dashwood," she said triumphantly, "people may say what they choose about Mr. Ferrars declaring he would not have Lucy, for it is no such thing, I can tell you; and it is quite a shame for such ill-natured reports to be spread abroad. Whatever Lucy might think of it herself, you know, it was no business of other people to set it down as certain."

"I never heard anything of the kind hinted at before, I assure you," said Elinor.

"Oh, did not you? But it was said, I know, very well, and by more than one. Miss Godby told Miss Sparks that nobody in their senses could expect Mr. Ferrars to give up a woman like Miss Morton, with thirty thousand pounds to her fortune, for Lucy Steele who had nothing at all; and I had it from Miss Sparks myself. Besides that, my cousin Richard said himself that when it came to the point, he was afraid Mr. Ferrars would be off. When Edward did not come near us for three days, I could not tell what to think myself. I believe in my heart Lucy gave it up for lost; for we left your brother's house on Wednesday and saw nothing of him until Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, not knowing what had become of him. Once Lucy thought to write to him, but then her spirits rose against that idea. However, this morning he came just as we came home from church, and then it all came out. He had been sent for on Wednesday to Harley Street, talked to by his mother and everyone, and declared before them all that he loved nobody but Lucy and that nobody but Lucy would he have. He was so worried by what passed that, as soon as he left his mother's house, he got upon his horse and rode into the country, somewhere or other, staying at an inn all Thursday and Friday on purpose to get the better of it. After thinking it all over and over again, he said it seemed to him that, now he had no fortune and nothing at all, it would be quite unkind to keep her on to the engagement, because it must be for her loss. He had nothing but two thousand pounds and no hope of anything else; if he were to go into orders, as he had some thoughts of doing, he could get nothing but a curacy, and how were they to live upon that? He could not bear to think of her doing no better, so he begged, if she had the least mind for it, to put an end to the matter directly and leave him to shift for himself. I heard him say all this as plain as could possibly be. It was entirely for her sake, and upon her account, that he said a word about being off, and not upon his own. I will take my oath he never dropped a syllable of being tired of her, or of wishing to marry Miss Morton, or anything like it. But, to be sure, Lucy would not give ear to such kind of talking. So she told him directly—with a great deal about sweet and love, you know, and all that—Oh, la! one can't repeat such kind of things, you know—that she had not the least mind in the world to be off. She said she could live with him upon a trifle, and how little so ever he might have, she should be very glad to have it all, you know, or something of the kind. So then he was monstrous happy and talked on some time about what they should do. They agreed he should take orders directly and they must wait to be married till he got a living. Just then I could not hear any more, for my cousin called from below to tell me Mrs. Richardson was come in her coach and would take one of us to Kensington Gardens. So I was forced to go into the room and interrupt them to ask Lucy if she would like to go, but she did not care to leave Edward. So I just ran upstairs and put on a pair of silk stockings and came off with the Richardsons."

"I do not understand what you mean by interrupting them," said Elinor. "You were all in the same room together, were not you?"

"No, indeed, not us. La! Miss Dashwood, do you think people make love when anybody else is by? Oh, for shame! To be sure you must know better than that. (Laughing affectedly.) No, no; they were shut up in the drawing-room together, and all I heard was only by listening at the door."

"How!" cried Elinor. "Have you been repeating to me what you only learnt yourself by listening at the door? I am sorry I did not know it before; for I certainly would not have suffered you to give me particulars of a conversation which you ought not to have known yourself. How could you behave so unfairly by your sister?"

"Oh, la! there is nothing in that. I only stood at the door and heard what I could. And I am sure Lucy would have done just the same by me; for a year or two back, when Martha Sharpe and I had so many secrets together, she never made any bones of hiding in a closet or behind a chimney-board on purpose to hear what we said."

Elinor tried to talk of something else, but Miss Steele could not be kept beyond a couple of minutes from what was uppermost in her mind.

"Edward talks of going to Oxford soon," she said. "But now he is lodging at No.—, Pall Mall. What an ill-natured woman his mother is, an't she? And your brother and sister were not very kind! However, I shan't say anything against them to you; and to be sure they did send us home in their own chariot, which was more than I looked for. And for my part, I was all in a fright for fear your sister should ask us for the huswifes she had given us a day or two before; but, however, nothing was said about them, and I took care to keep mine out of sight. Edward has got some business at Oxford, he says; so he must go there for a time. And after that, as soon as he can light upon a Bishop, he will be ordained. I wonder what curacy he will get! Good gracious! (giggling as she spoke) I'd lay my life I know what my cousins will say when they hear of it. They will tell me I should write to the Doctor to get Edward the curacy of his new living. I know they will; but I am sure I would not do such a thing for all the world. 'La!' I shall say directly, 'I wonder how you could think of such a thing? I write to the Doctor, indeed!'"

"Well," said Elinor, "it is a comfort to be prepared against the worst. You have got your answer ready."

Miss Steele was going to reply on the same subject, but the approach of her own party made another conversation necessary.

"Oh, la! here come the Richardsons. I had a vast deal more to say to you, but I must not stay away from them not any longer. I assure you they are very genteel people. He makes a monstrous deal of money, and they keep their own coach. I have not time to speak to Mrs. Jennings about it myself, but pray tell her I am quite happy to hear she is not in anger against us, and Lady Middleton the same. And if anything should happen to take you and your sister away, and Mrs. Jennings should want company, I am sure we should be very glad to come and stay with her for as long a time as she likes. I suppose Lady Middleton won't ask us any more this bout. Good-by; I am sorry Miss Marianne was not here. Remember me kindly to her. La! if you have not got your spotted muslin on!—I wonder you was not afraid of its being torn."

Such was her parting concern; for after this, she had time only to pay her farewell compliments to Mrs. Jennings before her company was claimed by Mrs. Richardson, leaving Elinor in possession of knowledge that might feed her powers of reflection for some time, though she had learned very little more than what had been already foreseen and foreplanned in her own mind. Edward's marriage with Lucy was as firmly determined upon, and the time of its taking place remained as absolutely uncertain as she had concluded it would be; everything depended, exactly after her expectation, on his obtaining that preferment, of which, at present, there seemed not the smallest chance.

As soon as they returned to the carriage, Mrs. Jennings was eager for information. But as Elinor wished to spread as little intelligence as possible that had in the first place been so unfairly obtained, she confined herself to the brief repetition of such simple particulars as she felt assured Lucy, for the sake of her own consequence, would choose to have known. The continuance of their engagement and the means that could be taken to promote its end constituted her entire communication. This produced from Mrs. Jennings the following natural remark.

"Wait for his having a living!—ay, we all know how that will end:—they will wait a twelvemonth, and finding no good comes of it, will set down upon a curacy of fifty pounds a-year, with the interest of his two thousand pounds and what little matter Mr. Steele and Mr. Pratt can give her. Then they will have a child every year! and Lord help 'em! how poor they will be! I must see what I can give them towards furnishing their house. Two maids and two men, indeed! as I talked of the other day. No, no, they must get a stout girl of all works. Betty's sister would never do for them now."

The next morning brought Elinor a letter by the two-penny

The Dashwood sisters had now resided in London for over two months, and Marianne's longing to depart grew with every passing day. She sighed for the fresh air, the freedom, and the quietude of the countryside, convinced that no place could offer her the peace she craved like Barton. Elinor shared her desire to leave, though she tempered her urgency with a practical awareness of the difficulties inherent in such a long journey—a reality Marianne refused to acknowledge. Yet, Elinor began to seriously formulate plans for their departure, having already voiced their wishes to their kind hostess, who resisted with all the eloquence of her goodwill. Then, a new proposal emerged: the Palmers were preparing to move to Cleveland near the end of March for the Easter holidays, and Mrs. Jennings, along with her friends, received a fervent invitation to accompany them. While this alone might not have satisfied Elinor's delicate sensibilities, it was reinforced by the genuine politeness of Mr. Palmer himself. Coupled with the marked improvement in his manners since learning of his sister's unhappiness, this gesture induced Elinor to accept the invitation with pleasure.

When she revealed her decision to Marianne, the younger sister's reaction was far from auspicious.

"Cleveland!" she cried, agitated. "No, I cannot go to Cleveland."

"You forget," Elinor said gently, "that its location is not—that it is not near—"

"But it is in Somersetshire," Marianne interrupted. "I cannot go into Somersetshire. There, where I had hoped to go... No, Elinor, you cannot expect me to go there."

Elinor saw no point in arguing the propriety of suppressing such feelings; instead, she sought to counteract them by appealing to others. She framed the move as a way to secure a more comfortable and timely reunion with their beloved mother, whom Marianne cherished deeply. From Cleveland, merely a few miles from Bristol, the journey to Barton would take no more than a day, albeit a long one. Their mother's servant could easily accompany them, and since their stay at Cleveland would likely be brief, they could be back home in little over three weeks. Knowing Marianne's love for her mother was sincere, Elinor believed it would triumph over the imaginary terrors Marianne conjured.

Mrs. Jennings, far from weary of her guests, pressed them earnestly to return with her from Cleveland. Elinor appreciated the kindness, yet her resolve remained unshaken. With their mother's consent readily obtained, arrangements for their return were finalized. Marianne found solace in calculating the remaining hours until she would once again be in Barton.

"Ah, Colonel," Mrs. Jennings addressed him upon his first visit after their departure was settled, "I do not know what you and I shall do without the Miss Dashwoods! They are quite resolved to go home from the Palmers. And how forlorn we shall be when I return! Lord, we shall sit and stare at one another as dull as two cats."

Perhaps Mrs. Jennings hoped this vivid sketch of future boredom would provoke an offer from the Colonel, offering him an escape from it. If so, she soon had reason to believe her aim was achieved. As Elinor moved to the window to quickly measure a print she intended to copy, the Colonel followed, his gaze laden with particular meaning. He conversed with her for several minutes. The effect on Elinor was evident; though Mrs. Jennings, too honorable to eavesdrop, had deliberately shifted her seat to the piano where Marianne played, she could not help noticing Elinor's flushed cheeks and agitated demeanor. She was too absorbed in the Colonel's words to continue her work.

Further confirmation of her hopes arrived during a pause in Marianne's practice. Words from the Colonel drifted to Mrs. Jennings' ear, in which he seemed to apologize for the inadequacies of his house. This settled the matter beyond doubt. She marveled that he would feel the need to do so, assuming it was mere etiquette. Unable to distinguish Elinor's reply, she judged from the motion of her lips that the Colonel's comment was not a material objection. Mrs. Jennings commended Elinor silently for her honesty. They spoke for a few more minutes without her catching a syllable, until another fortunate break in Marianne's performance allowed her to hear the Colonel's calm voice clearly:

"I am afraid it cannot take place very soon."

Startled and shocked by such an unloving remark, she nearly cried out, "Lord, what could possibly hinder it?" but checked herself, settling for a silent gasp.

"This is very strange! Surely he need not wait to be older."

However, this apparent delay did not seem to offend or mortify his fair companion. When the conversation ended and they parted ways, Mrs. Jennings distinctly heard Elinor say, with a voice betraying her feelings, "I shall always think myself very much obliged to you." Mrs. Jennings was delighted by this gratitude, wondering only why the Colonel could depart with such composure, making no reply, after hearing such a sentence. She had not expected her old friend to consider him such an indifferent suitor.

What truly transpired between them was as follows:

"I have heard," the Colonel said with deep compassion, "of the injustice your friend Mr. Ferrars has suffered from his family. If I understand correctly, he has been entirely cast off by them for persevering in his engagement with a very deserving young woman. Have I been rightly informed? Is it so?"

Elinor confirmed it was true.

"The cruelty, the impolitic cruelty," he replied with great feeling, "of separating, or attempting to separate, two young people long attached to each other, is terrible. Mrs. Ferrars does not know what she is doing—what she may drive her son to. I have seen Mr. Ferrars two or three times in Harley Street and am much pleased with him. He is not a man with whom one can become intimately acquainted in a short time, but I have seen enough to wish him well for his own sake, and as a friend of yours, I wish it all the more. I understand he intends to take orders. Will you be so kind as to tell him that the living of Delaford, now just vacant as I learned from today's post, is his, if he deems it worthy of acceptance? But perhaps, given his unfortunate circumstances, it may seem foolish to appear to doubt; I only wish it were more valuable.
