It is a rectory, but a small one; the late incumbent earned no more than £200 per annum. Though it is certainly capable of improvement, I fear not to such an extent as to provide a very comfortable income. Nevertheless, my pleasure in presenting it to him will be great. Please assure him of this."

Elinor's astonishment at this commission was profound, had the Colonel been genuinely making her an offer of marriage. The preferment she had considered hopeless for Edward just two days prior was now provided to enable him to marry, and she, of all people, was chosen to bestow it. Her emotion was such that Mrs. Jennings attributed it to a completely different cause. Whatever lesser, less pure feelings might have contributed to her agitation, her esteem for the Colonel's general benevolence and gratitude for his particular friendship, which prompted this act, were strongly felt and warmly expressed.

She thanked him from the bottom of her heart, praised Edward's principles and disposition with the accuracy she knew they deserved, and promised to undertake the commission with pleasure if he truly wished to delegate such an agreeable office to another. Yet, she could not help thinking that no one could perform it better than he. It was an office from which, unwilling to cause Edward pain by accepting an obligation from her, she would have gladly been spared. However, Colonel Brandon, driven by motives of equal delicacy, declined it as well. Still, he seemed so eager for it to be conveyed through her that she would not further oppose him. She believed Edward was still in town and, fortunately, had heard of his address from Miss Steele. Thus, she undertook to inform him of the news later that day.

Once this was settled, Colonel Brandon began discussing his advantage in securing such a respectable and agreeable neighbor. It was then that he mentioned, with regret, that the house was small and indifferent. As Mrs. Jennings had suspected, Elinor made very light of this, particularly regarding its size.

"The smallness of the house," she said, "I cannot imagine any inconvenience for them, as it will be proportional to their family and income."

The Colonel was surprised to find that she considered Mr. Ferrars's marriage a certain consequence of the presentation. He did not suppose the Delaford living could supply an income sufficient for anyone of his station to settle upon, and he stated as much.

"This little rectory can do no more than make Mr. Ferrars comfortable as a bachelor; it cannot enable him to marry," he said sorrowfully. "I am sorry to say that my patronage ends here; my influence is hardly more extensive. If, however, by some unforeseen chance it falls within my power to serve him further, I must think very differently of him than I do now, if I am not as ready to be useful to him then as I sincerely wish I could be at present. What I am doing now indeed seems insignificant, since it advances him so little toward what must be his principal, his only object of happiness. His marriage must still remain a distant good; at least, I am afraid it cannot take place very soon."

Such was the sentence which, when misunderstood, justifiably offended the delicate feelings of Mrs. Jennings. Yet, after recounting what truly passed between Colonel Brandon and Elinor while they stood at the window, the gratitude expressed by the latter upon their parting may perhaps appear, in general, no less reasonably excited, nor less properly worded, than if it had arisen from an offer of marriage.

"Well, Miss Dashwood," Mrs. Jennings said, her eyes crinkling with a sagacious smile the moment the gentleman had withdrawn, "I needn't ask you what the Colonel has been saying to you. Though, upon my honour, I *tried* to keep out of hearing, I couldn't help catching enough to understand his business. And I assure you, I have never been better pleased in my life. I wish you joy of it with all my heart."

"Thank you, ma'am," Elinor replied. "It *is* a matter of great joy to me, and I feel the goodness of Colonel Brandon most sensibly. There are not many men who would act as he has done. Few possess such a compassionate heart! I was never more astonished in my life."

"Lord! my dear, you are very modest. I'm not the least astonished in the world; I've often thought lately that nothing was more likely to happen."

"You judged from your knowledge of the Colonel's general benevolence," Elinor said gently. "But at least you could not foresee that the opportunity would occur so very soon."

"Opportunity!" Mrs. Jennings repeated. "Oh! as to that, when a man has once made up his mind to such a thing, somehow or other he will soon find an opportunity. Well, my dear, I wish you joy of it again and again. If ever there was a happy couple in the world, I think I shall soon know where to look for them."

"You mean to go to Delaford after them, I suppose?" Elinor asked, offering a faint smile.

"Aye, my dear, that I do, indeed. And as to the house being a bad one, I don't know what the Colonel would be at. It is as good a one as ever I saw."

"He spoke of its being out of repair."

"Well, and whose fault is that? Why doesn't he repair it? Who should do it but himself?"

Their conversation was interrupted by the servant entering to announce the carriage at the door. Mrs. Jennings immediately prepared to depart.

"Well, my dear, I must be gone before I've had half my talk out. However, we may have it all over in the evening, for we shall be quite alone. I don't ask you to come with me; I dare say your mind is too full of the matter to care for company, and besides, you must long to tell your sister all about it."

Marianne had already left the room before the conversation began.

"Certainly, ma'am, I shall tell Marianne of it; but I shall not mention it to anyone else at present."

"Oh! very well," Mrs. Jennings said, looking slightly disappointed. "Then you wouldn't have me tell it to Lucy? I think of going as far as Holborn today."

"No, ma'am, not even Lucy, if you please. One day's delay won't be material, but until I have written to Mr. Ferrars, I think it ought not to be mentioned to anyone else. I shall do that directly. It is of importance that no time be lost with him, for he will, of course, have much to do relative to his ordination."

This speech puzzled Mrs. Jennings exceedingly at first. Why Mr. Ferrars was to be written to in such a hurry was incomprehensible to her. But a few moments' reflection produced a very happy idea, and she exclaimed, "Oh, ho!—I understand you. Mr. Ferrars is to be the man. Well, so much the better for him. Ay, to be sure, he must be ordained in readiness. I am very glad to find things are so forward between you. But, my dear, is this rather out of character? Should not the Colonel write himself? Surely, he is the proper person."

Elinor did not quite understand the beginning of Mrs. Jennings's speech, nor did she think it worth inquiring into; she only replied to its conclusion.

"Colonel Brandon is so delicate a man that he rather wished anyone to announce his intentions to Mr. Ferrars than himself."

"And so *you* are forced to do it. Well, *that* is an odd kind of delicacy! However, I will not disturb you (seeing her prepare to write). You know your own concerns best. So goodby, my dear. I haven't heard of anything to please me so well since Charlotte was brought to bed."

She departed, but returned in a moment.

"I have just been thinking of Betty's sister, my dear. I should be very glad to get her such a good mistress. But whether she would do for a lady's maid, I am sure I can't tell. She is an excellent housemaid and works very well at her needle. However, you will think of all that at your leisure."

"Certainly, ma'am," replied Elinor, not hearing much of what she said and more anxious to be alone than to be mistress of the subject.

Now, how she should begin—how she should express herself in her note to Edward—was all her concern. The particular circumstances between them made a difficulty of that which to any other person would have been the easiest thing in the world. She equally feared to say too much or too little, and sat deliberating over her paper, pen in hand, until she was broken in upon by the entrance of Edward himself.

He had met Mrs. Jennings at the door on her way to the carriage as he came to leave his farewell card. After apologizing for not returning himself, she had obliged him to enter by saying that Miss Dashwood was above and wanted to speak with him on very particular business.

Elinor had just been congratulating herself, amidst her perplexity, that however difficult it might be to express herself properly by letter, it was at least preferable to giving the information by word of mouth, when her visitor entered to force her upon this greatest exertion of all. Her astonishment and confusion were very great at his sudden appearance. She had not seen him since his engagement became public, and therefore not since his knowing her to be acquainted with it. This, combined with the consciousness of what she had been thinking of and what she had to tell him, made her feel particularly uncomfortable for some minutes. He too was much distressed, and they sat down together in a most promising state of embarrassment. Whether he had asked her pardon for his intrusion on first coming into the room, he could not recollect; but determining to be on the safe side, he made his apology in form as soon as he could speak.

"Mrs. Jennings told me," he said, "that you wished to speak with me, at least I understood her so—or I certainly should not have intruded on you in such a manner; though at the same time, I should have been extremely sorry to leave London without seeing you and your sister, especially as it will most likely be some time—it is not probable that I should soon have the pleasure of meeting you again. I go to Oxford tomorrow."

"You would not have gone, however," Elinor said, recovering herself and determined to get over what she so much dreaded as soon as possible, "without receiving our good wishes, even if we had not been able to give them in person. Mrs. Jennings was quite right in what she said. I have something of consequence to inform you of, which I was on the point of communicating by paper. I am charged with a most agreeable office." She breathed rather faster than usual as she spoke. "Colonel Brandon, who was here only ten minutes ago, has desired me to say that, understanding you mean to take orders, he has great pleasure in offering you the living of Delaford, now just vacant, and only wishes it were more valuable. Allow me to congratulate you on having so respectable and well-judging a friend, and to join in his wish that the living—it is about two hundred a-year—were much more considerable, and such as might better enable you to... as might be more than a temporary accommodation to yourself—such, in short, as might establish all your views of happiness."

What Edward felt, as he could not say it himself, it cannot be expected that anyone else should say for him. He *looked* all the astonishment which such unexpected, such unthought-of information could not fail of exciting; but he said only these two words, "Colonel Brandon!"

"Yes," Elinor continued, gathering more resolution as some of the worst was over, "Colonel Brandon means it as a testimony of his concern for what has lately passed—for the cruel situation in which the unjustifiable conduct of your family has placed you—a concern which I am sure Marianne, myself, and all your friends, must share; and likewise as a proof of his high esteem for your general character and his particular approbation of your behaviour on the present occasion."

"Colonel Brandon give *me* a living!—Can it be possible?"

"The unkindness of your own relations has made you astonished to find friendship anywhere."

"No," he replied with sudden consciousness, "not to find it in *you*; for I cannot be ignorant that to you, to your goodness, I owe it all.—I feel it—I would express it if I could—but, as you well know, I am no orator."

"You are very much mistaken. I do assure you that you owe it entirely, at least almost entirely, to your own merit and Colonel Brandon's discernment of it. I have had no hand in it. I did not even know, till I understood his design, that the living was vacant; nor had it ever occurred to me that he might have had such a living in his gift. As a friend of mine, of my family, he may, perhaps—indeed I know he *has*, still greater pleasure in bestowing it; but, upon my word, you owe nothing to my solicitation."

Truth obliged her to acknowledge some small share in the action, but she was at the same time so unwilling to appear as the benefactress of Edward that she acknowledged it with hesitation; which probably contributed to fix that suspicion in his mind which had recently entered it. For a short time he sat deep in thought after Elinor had ceased to speak; at last, and as if it were rather an effort, he said, "Colonel Brandon seems a man of great worth and respectability. I have always heard him spoken of as such, and your brother I know esteems him highly. He is undoubtedly a sensible man, and in his manners perfectly the gentleman."

"Indeed," replied Elinor, "I believe that you will find him, on farther acquaintance, all that you have heard him to be. And as you will be such very near neighbours—for I understand the parsonage is almost close to the mansion-house—it is particularly important that he *should* be all this."

Edward made no answer; but when she had turned away her head, he gave her a look so serious, so earnest, so uncheerful, as seemed to say that he might hereafter wish the distance between the parsonage and the mansion-house much greater.

"Colonel Brandon, I think, lodges in St. James Street," he said soon afterwards, rising from his chair.

Elinor told him the number of the house.

"I must hurry away then, to give him those thanks which you will not allow me to give *you*; to assure him that he has made me a very—an exceedingly happy man."

Elinor did not offer to detain him, and they parted with a very earnest assurance on *her* side of her unceasing good wishes for his happiness in every change of situation that might befall him; on *his*, with rather an attempt to return the same good will than the power of expressing it.

"When I see him again," Elinor said to herself as the door shut him out, "I shall see him the husband of Lucy."

And with this pleasing anticipation, she sat down to reconsider the past, recall the words, and endeavour to comprehend all the feelings of Edward; and, of course, to reflect on her own with discontent.

When Mrs. Jennings came home, though she returned from seeing people whom she had never seen before and of whom therefore she must have a great deal to say, her mind was so much more occupied by the important secret in her possession than by anything else that she reverted to it again as soon as Elinor appeared.

"Well, my dear," she cried, "I sent you up the young man. Did not I do right?—And I suppose you had no great difficulty—You did not find him very unwilling to accept your proposal?"

"No, ma'am; *that* was not very likely."

"Well, and how soon will he be ready?—For it seems all to depend upon that."

"Really," said Elinor, "I know so little of these kind of forms that I can hardly even conjecture as to the time or the preparation necessary; but I suppose two or three months will complete his ordination."

"Two or three months!" cried Mrs. Jennings. "Lord! my dear, how calmly you talk of it! And can the Colonel wait two or three months? Lord bless me!—I am sure it would put *me* quite out of patience!—And though one would be very glad to do a kindness by poor Mr. Ferrars, I do think it is not worth while to wait two or three months for him. Sure somebody else might be found that would do as well; somebody that is in orders already."

"My dear ma'am," said Elinor, "what can you be thinking of? Why, Colonel Brandon's only object is to be of use to Mr. Ferrars."

"Lord bless you, my dear! Sure you do not mean to persuade me that the Colonel only marries you for the sake of giving ten guineas to Mr. Ferrars!"

The deception could not continue after this, and an explanation immediately took place, by which both gained considerable amusement for the moment without any material loss of happiness to either, for Mrs. Jennings only exchanged one form of delight for another, and still without forfeiting her expectation of the first.

"Aye, aye, the parsonage is but a small one," she said after the first ebullition of surprise and satisfaction was over, "and very likely *may* be out of repair; but to hear a man apologising, as I thought, for a house that to my knowledge has five sitting

Edward, buoyed by his recent triumph, hastened to share his joy with Lucy. By the time he arrived at Bartlett's Buildings, his elation was so profound that when Mrs. Jennings visited the following day with her congratulations, Lucy could assure her hostess that she had never witnessed such spirits in her life. Her own happiness was absolute, and she eagerly joined Mrs. Jennings in anticipating a comfortable reunion at Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas. Yet, Lucy's gratitude extended far beyond mere sentiment. She was eager to acknowledge the debt owed to both Edward and Elinor, declaring with warm sincerity that no effort Elinor might make for their sake would ever surprise her, for she believed her friend capable of anything for those she truly valued. In Lucy's eyes, Colonel Brandon was not merely a suitor but a saintly figure; she was fervently anxious that he be treated with the utmost reverence in worldly matters, even resolving to exploit his resources at Delaford—his servants, carriage, cows, and poultry—to the fullest extent possible.

It had been over a week since John Dashwood had visited Berkeley Street, and with no further attention paid to his wife's indisposition save for a single verbal inquiry, Elinor felt compelled to pay a visit. This obligation, however, clashed violently with her inclinations and lacked the support of her companions. Marianne, refusing to go herself, urged her sister not to go at all, while Mrs. Jennings, despite her carriage standing ready, harbored such a deep aversion to Mrs. John Dashwood that neither curiosity nor a desire to champion Edward could overcome her reluctance. Consequently, Elinor set out alone, bracing herself for a tête-à-tête with a woman whom the others detested.

Upon arrival, Mrs. Dashwood was initially denied, but before the carriage could depart, her husband emerged. He expressed great pleasure at meeting Elinor, claiming he had intended to visit Berkeley Street himself. Assuring her that Fanny would be delighted to see her, he invited her inside. They ascended to the drawing-room, only to find it empty.

"Fanny is in her own room, I suppose," John remarked, turning to Elinor. "I shall go to her presently; she will have no objection to seeing you. Indeed, she won't. Now, especially, there cannot be—but however, you and Marianne were always great favorites. Why did Marianne not come?"

Elinor offered a polite excuse for her sister's absence.

"I am not sorry to see you alone," John replied, his tone shifting. "For I have much to say to you. This living of Colonel Brandon's—can it be true? Has he really given it to Edward? I heard it by chance yesterday and came specifically to inquire."

"It is perfectly true," Elinor confirmed. "Colonel Brandon has presented the living of Delaford to Edward."

"Really! Well, this is astonishing! No relationship, no connection between them! And now that livings fetch such prices! What was the value of this?"

"About two hundred a year."

"Very well. For the next presentation to a living of that value—supposing the incumbent were old and sickly, likely to vacate it soon—one might expect fourteen hundred pounds. How did he fail to settle this before this person's death? Now, indeed, it would be too late to sell it, but a man of Colonel Brandon's sense! I wonder he should be so improvident in a matter of such common, natural concern! Well, I am convinced there is inconsistency in almost every human character. However, on recollection, the case may be this: Edward is only to hold the living until the person to whom the Colonel sold the presentation is old enough to take it. Aye, that must be the fact."

Elinor contradicted him positively, explaining that she had personally conveyed the offer from Colonel Brandon to Edward and thus understood the terms. She obliged him to submit to her authority.

"It is truly astonishing!" he exclaimed. "What could be the Colonel's motive?"

"A very simple one—to be of use to Mr. Ferrars."

"Well, well; whatever Colonel Brandon may be, Edward is a very lucky man. You will not mention this to Fanny, however. Though I have broken it to her and she bears it well, she will not like it much talked of."

Elinor struggled to refrain from noting that she believed Fanny could have borne the news of her brother's acquisition of wealth with composure, an event that impoverished neither her nor her child.

"Mrs. Ferrars," John added, lowering his voice to a tone befitting such an important subject, "knows nothing of this at present, and I believe it best to keep it entirely concealed from her as long as possible. When the marriage takes place, I fear she must hear of it all."

"But why such precaution? Though it is not to be supposed that Mrs. Ferrars can derive satisfaction from knowing her son has money enough to live upon—for that must be out of the question—why, upon her late behavior, is she supposed to feel at all? She has cast her son off forever and influenced others to do the same. Surely, after such an act, she cannot be imagined liable to any impression of sorrow or joy regarding him. She cannot be interested in anything that befalls him. She would not be so weak as to throw away the comfort of a child while retaining the anxiety of a parent!"

"Ah, Elinor," John said gently, "your reasoning is sound, but it is founded on ignorance of human nature. When Edward's unhappy match takes place, depend upon it his mother will feel as much as if she had never discarded him; and therefore, every circumstance accelerating that dreadful event must be concealed from her. Mrs. Ferrars can never forget that Edward is her son."

"You surprise me; I should think it must nearly have escaped her memory by this time."

"You wrong her exceedingly. Mrs. Ferrars is one of the most affectionate mothers in the world."

Elinor fell silent.

"We think now," John continued after a pause, placing his hand kindly on hers and speaking in an awed whisper, "of Robert's marrying Miss Morton."

Elinor, smiling at the grave importance her brother assigned to the statement, calmly replied, "The lady, I suppose, has no choice in the affair."

"Choice? How do you mean?"

"I only mean that, judging by your manner, it must be the same to Miss Morton whether she marries Edward or Robert."

"Certainly, there can be no difference; for Robert will now, to all intents and purposes, be considered the eldest son. As to anything else, they are both very agreeable young men; I do not know that one is superior to the other."

Elinor said no more, and John remained silent for a time. His reflections concluded thus: "Of one thing, my dear sister, I may assure you—and I will, because I know it must gratify you. I have good reason to think, indeed I have it from the best authority, or I should not repeat it, for otherwise it would be very wrong to say anything about it—but I have it from the very best authority. Not that I ever precisely heard Mrs. Ferrars say it herself, but her daughter did, and I have it from her—that in short, whatever objections there might be against a certain connection, you understand me, it would have been far preferable to her. It would not have given her half the vexation that this does. I was exceedingly pleased to hear that Mrs. Ferrars considered it in that light; a very gratifying circumstance for us all. 'It would have been beyond comparison,' she said, 'the least evil of the two, and she would be glad to compound now for nothing worse.' But however, all that is quite out of the question—not to be thought of or mentioned—as to any attachment, it never could be: all that is gone by. But I thought I would just tell you of this, because I knew how much it must please you. Not that you have any reason to regret, my dear Elinor. There is no doubt of your doing exceedingly well—quite as well, or better, perhaps, all things considered. Has Colonel Brandon been with you lately?"

Elinor had heard enough to agitate her nerves and fill her mind, whether to gratify her vanity or raise her self-importance. She was therefore glad to be spared the necessity of replying at length and the danger of hearing more from her brother by the entrance of Mr. Robert Ferrars. After a few moments' chat, John Dashwood, remembering that Fanny was yet uninformed of her sister's presence, left the room to find her. Elinor was left to deepen her acquaintance with Robert, whose gay unconcern and happy self-complacency, enjoyed while dividing his mother's love and liberality unfairly against his banished brother—an advantage earned solely by his dissipated lifestyle and his brother's integrity—only served to confirm Elinor's most unfavorable opinion of his head and heart.

Scarcely two minutes had passed before Robert began to speak of Edward. He, too, had heard of the living and was inquisitive. Elinor repeated the particulars she had given to John, and their effect on Robert, though different, was equally striking. He laughed immoderately. The idea of Edward becoming a clergyman, living in a small parsonage-house, diverted him beyond measure. Adding to this the fanciful imagery of Edward reading prayers in a white surplice and publishing the banns of marriage between John Smith and Mary Brown, he could conceive nothing more ridiculous.

Elinor, waiting in silence with immovable gravity for the conclusion of such folly, could not restrain her gaze from fixing on him with a look that spoke all the contempt it inspired. It was a look well bestowed, relieving her own feelings without giving him intelligence. He was recalled from wit to wisdom not by her reproof, but by his own sensibility.

"We may treat it as a joke," he said finally, recovering from the affected laugh that had prolonged the moment's genuine gaiety. "But, upon my soul, it is a most serious business. Poor Edward! He is ruined forever. I am extremely sorry for it; for I know him to be a very good-hearted creature; as well-meaning a fellow perhaps as any in the world. You must not judge of him, Miss Dashwood, from your slight acquaintance. Poor Edward! His manners are certainly not the happiest in nature. But we are not all born with the same powers, the same address. Poor fellow! To see him in a circle of strangers! To be sure, it was pitiable enough; but upon my soul, I believe he has as good a heart as any in the kingdom. I declare and protest to you I was never so shocked in my life as when it all burst forth. I could not believe it. My mother was the first to tell me, and feeling myself called on to act with resolution, I immediately said to her, 'My dear madam, I do not know what you intend to do on the occasion, but as for myself, I must say that if Edward does marry this young woman, I never will see him again.' That was what I said immediately. I was most uncommonly shocked, indeed! Poor Edward! He has done for himself completely, shut himself out forever from all decent society. But, as I directly said to my mother, I am not in the least surprised at it; from his style of education, it was always to be expected. My poor mother was half frantic."

"Have you ever seen the lady?"

"Yes; once, while she was staying in this house, I happened to drop in for ten minutes; and I saw quite enough of her. The merest awkward country girl, without style, or elegance, and almost without beauty. I remember her perfectly. Just the kind of girl I should suppose likely to captivate poor Edward. I offered immediately, as soon as my mother related the affair to me, to talk to him myself and dissuade him from the match; but it was too late then, I found, to do anything, for unluckily I was not in the way at first and knew nothing of it till after the breach had taken place, when it was not for me, you know, to interfere. But had I been informed of it a few hours earlier, I think it is most probable that something might have been hit on. I certainly should have represented it to Edward in a very strong light. 'My dear fellow,' I should have said, 'consider what you are doing. You are making a most disgraceful connection, and such a one as your family are unanimous in disapproving.' I cannot help thinking, in short, that means might have been found. But now it is all too late. He must be starved, you know, that is certain; absolutely starved."

He had just settled this point with great composure when the entrance of Mrs. John Dashwood ended the subject. Though she never spoke of it outside her own family, Elinor could see its influence on her mind in the confusion of countenance with which she entered and an attempted cordiality in her behavior toward Elinor. She even proceeded to express concern that Elinor and her sister were leaving town so soon, hoping to see more of them—an exertion in which her husband, attending her into the room and hanging enamored over her accents, seemed to distinguish everything that was most affectionate and graceful.

The final farewell to London was marked by a brief, cheerful stop at Harley Street, where Henry Dashwood congratulated his sisters on their progress toward Barton without financial strain, and announced that Colonel Brandon would join them for Cleveland within a day or two. This concluded their urban interactions, leaving only a tentative invitation from Fanny to visit Norland—a prospect so unlikely it was barely worth mentioning—and a warm, private promise from John to Elinor that he would soon seek her out at Delaford.

It was amusing to Elinor how every friend seemed intent on sending her to Delaford, a destination she now dreaded above all others. To her brother and Mrs. Jennings, it was her future home; even Lucy, upon their parting, pressed an invitation to visit her there.

By early April, the two parties departed from their respective homes in Hanover Square and Berkeley Street, meeting on the road as planned. Given the need to accommodate Charlotte and her child, the journey would take more than two days, while Mr. Palmer, traveling with greater speed alongside Colonel Brandon, intended to catch up to them at Cleveland shortly after their arrival.

Marianne, though she had spent few comfortable hours in London and had longed desperately to escape it, felt a profound pang of sorrow as she finally bid adieu to the house where she had last harbored her hopes and confidence in Willoughby—hopes now extinguished forever. Leaving the place where Willoughby remained, busy with new engagements and schemes from which she was excluded, brought forth many tears.

Elinor's relief, however, was absolute and positive. She held no lingering attachments that would cause regret, parted from no creature she loved, and welcomed the freedom from the persecution of Lucy's friendship. Grateful to have removed her sister unseen by Willoughby since his marriage, she looked forward with hope to the tranquility of Barton, confident it would restore Marianne's peace and confirm her own.

Their journey proceeded safely. On the second day, they crossed into Somerset, a county that alternated between being cherished and prohibited in Marianne's imagination. By the forenoon of the third day, they drove up to Cleveland.

Cleveland was a spacious, modern residence situated on a sloping lawn. Lacking a park, it possessed extensive pleasure grounds, complete with an open shrubbery and a closer wood walk. A smooth gravel road wound around a plantation to the front, while the lawn was dotted with timber. The house itself stood under the protective canopy of firs, mountain-ashes, and acacias, screened by a thick wall of these trees interspersed with tall Lombardy poplars that hid the offices from view.

Marianne entered the house with a heart swelling with emotion, conscious of being merely eighty miles from Barton and not thirty from Combe Magna. Within five minutes of entering, while the others assisted Charlotte in showing the housekeeper her child, she slipped away again. She wandered through the winding shrubberies, which were just beginning to display their beauty, seeking a distant eminence. From a Grecian temple there, her gaze wandered over a vast tract of country to the southeast, resting fondly on the farthest ridge of hills in the horizon, imagining that from their summits, Combe Magna might be visible.

In these moments of precious, invaluable misery, she rejoiced in tears of agony to be at Cleveland. Returning by a different path, she savored the happy privilege of country liberty—the freedom to wander in luxurious solitude. She resolved to spend nearly every hour of her stay indulging in such solitary rambles.

She returned just in time to join the others as they prepared to explore the immediate premises. The remainder of the morning passed easily as they lounged around the kitchen garden, admiring the bloom on its walls and listening to the gardener's lamentations about blights. They dawdled through the green-house, where Charlotte laughed at the loss of favorite plants nipped by lingering frost, and visited the poultry-yard, where the dairy-maid's disappointed hopes regarding forsaken nests, foxes, or dwindling broods provided fresh sources of merriment.

The morning had been fine and dry, and Marianne had not anticipated a change in weather. With great surprise, she found herself confined indoors after dinner by a settled rain. She had planned a twilight walk to the Grecian temple and perhaps all over the grounds; while a merely cold or damp evening might not have deterred her, a heavy, persistent rain was unbearable to her.

The party was small, and the hours passed quietly. Mrs. Palmer attended to her child, and Mrs. Jennings worked on her carpet-work. They discussed the friends they had left, arranged Lady Middleton's engagements, and wondered if Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon would travel farther than Reading that night. Elinor, though little concerned, joined their discourse. Marianne, possessing the knack of finding her way to the library regardless of the family's avoidance of it, soon procured a book.

Mrs. Palmer displayed constant and friendly good humor, making them feel welcome. The openness and heartiness of her manner more than compensated for her lack of recollection and elegance, which often rendered her deficient in polite forms. Her kindness, recommended by a pretty face, was engaging; her folly, though evident, was not disgusting because it lacked conceit. Elinor could have forgiven everything but her laugh.

The two gentlemen arrived the next day for a late dinner, providing a pleasant expansion of the party and a welcome variety to the conversation, which had been reduced to a low ebb by a long morning of continuous rain.

Elinor had seen so little of Mr. Palmer and observed such variety in his address to her sister and herself that she knew not what to expect in his own family. She found him perfectly gentlemanly in his behavior to all visitors, though occasionally rude to his wife and her mother. He proved capable of being a pleasant companion, hindered only by an excessive tendency to fancy himself superior to everyone else, particularly to Mrs. Jennings and Charlotte. Otherwise, his character and habits showed no unusual traits for his sex and time of life. He was fastidious in his eating, uncertain in his hours, fond of his child yet affecting to slight it, and idled away mornings at billiards instead of business. Nevertheless, she liked him much better than expected and was not sorry she could like him no more—not sorry to be driven by the observation of his Epicureanism, selfishness, and conceit to rest complacently on the remembrance of Edward's generous temper, simple taste, and diffident feelings.

Regarding Edward, or at least some of his concerns, Elinor received intelligence from Colonel Brandon, who had recently visited Dorsetshire. Treating her as both the disinterested friend of Mr. Ferrars and the kind confidante of himself, he spoke extensively of the parsonage at Delaford, describing its deficiencies and detailing his plans to remedy them. His behavior in this matter, as well as in every other, including his open pleasure in meeting her after only ten days' absence, his readiness to converse, and his deference for her opinion, justified Mrs. Jennings's persuasion of his attachment. It would have been sufficient proof had not Elinor, from the first, believed Marianne to be his true favorite, causing her to suspect it herself. Yet, such a notion had scarcely entered her head except through Mrs. Jennings's suggestion. She prided herself as the nicer observer of the two; she watched his eyes while Mrs. Jennings focused only on his general behavior. While his looks of anxious solicitude regarding Marianne's unexpressed head and throat ailments—the beginnings of a heavy cold—entirely escaped the latter lady's notice, Elinor discovered in them the quick feelings and needless alarm of a lover.

Two delightful twilight walks on the third and fourth evenings, traversing not merely the dry gravel of the shrubbery but all over the grounds, especially the most distant parts where the trees were oldest and the grass longest and wettest, combined with the greater imprudence of sitting in wet shoes and stockings, gave Marianne a violent cold. Though initially trifled with or denied, it forced itself upon the concern of everyone and her own attention through increasing ailments. Prescriptions poured in from all quarters, as usual declined. Though heavy and feverish, with pain in her limbs, a cough, and a sore throat, she was told a good night's rest would cure her entirely. It was with difficulty that Elinor prevailed upon her, when she went to bed, to try one or two of the simplest remedies.

The next morning, Marianne rose at her accustomed hour, declaring herself better and attempting to resume her usual duties. Yet, a day spent shivering before the fire, unable to read, or lying listless upon a sofa, offered little evidence of her recovery. By the time she retired early, her condition had worsened. Colonel Brandon marveled at Elinor's composure; despite nursing Marianne all day against her sister's wishes and forcing medicine upon her at night, Elinor, like Marianne, placed her faith in the healing power of sleep and felt no genuine alarm.

However, a restless and feverish night shattered their expectations. When Marianne, after insisting on rising, confessed her inability to sit and returned to bed, Elinor readily accepted Mrs. Jennings's counsel to summon the Palmers' apothecary. Mr. Harris arrived, examined the patient, and while encouraging Miss Dashwood to expect a swift recovery, his mention of a "putrid tendency" and the word "infection" sent immediate panic through Mrs. Palmer regarding her infant. Mrs. Jennings, who had suspected the illness was grave from the start, grew grave indeed at Mr. Harris's report. Confirming Charlotte's fears, she insisted on the immediate removal of the child and her nurse to a relative's house a few miles outside Bath. Mr. Palmer, though dismissing their fears as idle, found his wife's anxiety insurmountable. Her departure was arranged within the hour. She left with her son and nurse, promising to follow in a day or two, and urging her mother to accompany her. Mrs. Jennings, however, with a kindness that earned Elinor's deep affection, resolved to stay at Cleveland as long as Marianne remained ill, offering her own attentive care to replace the absent mother. Elinor found her a willing and active helper, eager to share the burden and skilled in nursing matters.

Poor Marianne, weak and low from the malady, could no longer hope for recovery by tomorrow. The thought of what that day would have brought—without this illness, they were to have begun their journey home, surprising their mother the following morning—made every ailment seem more severe. Her few words were lamentations of this inevitable delay, though Elinor tried to lift her spirits, believing as she truly did then that the delay would be short.

The next day brought little change; Marianne was neither better nor worse. The household was further diminished as Mr. Palmer, though reluctant to leave due to his humanity and dislike of appearing frightened, was finally persuaded by Colonel Brandon to fulfill his promise. As he prepared to depart, Colonel Brandon himself spoke of going as well. Here, Mrs. Jennings intervened with perfect tact. To send the Colonel away while his heart ached for his sister would deprive them both of comfort. She urged him to stay, citing her need for his company in the evenings and his ability to assist Elinor in any emergency. Mr. Palmer warmly seconded her plea, finding relief in knowing someone capable of advising Miss Dashwood remained. Marianne, of course, was kept ignorant of these arrangements. She knew not that she had caused the owners of Cleveland to depart within a week of their arrival. It surprised her nothing that she saw no sign of Mrs. Palmer, nor did it concern her enough to mention the name.

Two days passed since Mr. Palmer's departure, and Marianne's condition remained static. Mr. Harris still spoke boldly of a speedy recovery, and Elinor shared his sanguinity, though the others were far less cheerful. Mrs. Jennings had early decided Marianne would never recover, and Colonel Brandon, listening to her forebodings, found himself unable to resist their influence. He tried to reason away fears that the apothecary's judgment rendered absurd, but the long hours of solitude favored melancholy thoughts, and he could not shake the conviction that he would never see Marianne again.

On the morning of the third day, gloomy anticipations nearly vanished. Mr. Harris declared the patient materially better; her pulse was stronger, and symptoms more favorable. Elinor, confirmed in her hopes, was full of cheerfulness, rejoicing that her letters to her mother had minimized the indisposition. She almost fixed a date for Marianne's travel. But the day did not end auspiciously. Towards evening, Marianne grew ill again, becoming heavier, restless, and uncomfortable. Elinor, still optimistic, attributed the change to the fatigue of sitting up for her bed to be made. Administering cordials, she watched with satisfaction as Marianne sank into a slumber, expecting beneficial results. The sleep lasted a considerable time, though not as quiet as desired. Elinor, anxious to observe the outcome, resolved to stay with her throughout. Mrs. Jennings, unaware of the change, went to bed early; the maid, one of the principal nurses, was resting elsewhere, leaving Elinor alone with Marianne.

Marianne's repose became increasingly disturbed. Her sister, watching her continual shifts in posture and hearing frequent, inarticulate cries of pain, almost wished to rouse her from such a painful sleep. Suddenly, awakened by a noise in the house, Marianne started up with feverish wildness and cried, "Is mama coming?"

"Not yet," Elinor replied, concealing her terror and helping Marianne lie down again. "But she will be here, I hope, before long. It is a great way from here to Barton."

"But she must not go round by London!" Marianne cried in the same hurried manner. "I shall never see her if she goes by London."

Elinor perceived with alarm that Marianne was not quite herself. While attempting to soothe her, she eagerly felt her pulse. It was lower and quicker than ever! Marianne's wild talk of her mother increased Elinor's alarm so rapidly that she determined to send instantly for Mr. Harris and dispatch a messenger to Barton for her mother. Consulting Colonel Brandon on the best means to achieve this followed immediately. As soon as she had rung for the maid to take her place, she hastened to the drawing-room, where she knew he was usually found later in the day.

There was no time for hesitation. Her fears and difficulties were immediately before him. He had no courage or confidence to remove her fears; he listened to them in silent despondence. But her difficulties were instantly resolved. With a readiness that seemed pre-arranged for the occasion, he offered himself as the messenger to fetch Mrs. Dashwood. Elinor made no resistance that was not easily overcome. She thanked him with brief, fervent gratitude. While he hurried his servant to arrange post-horses directly, she wrote a few lines to her mother.

The comfort of such a friend at that moment—Colonel Brandon, or such a companion for her mother—was felt with profound gratitude. A companion whose judgment would guide, whose attendance would relieve, and whose friendship might soothe! His presence would lessen the shock of such a summons. Meanwhile, whatever he felt, he acted with the firmness of a collected mind, making every necessary arrangement with despatch and calculating exactly when she might expect his return. Not a moment was lost. The horses arrived even sooner than expected. Colonel Brandon pressed her hand with a look of solemnity, spoke a few words too low for her to hear, and hurried into the carriage. It was about twelve o'clock. She returned to Marianne's apartment to wait for the apothecary and watch through the night. It was a night of almost equal suffering: sleepless pain and delirium for Marianne, cruel anxiety for Elinor, before Mr. Harris appeared. Her apprehensions, once raised, paid for all her former security. The servant who sat with her, refusing to wake Mrs. Jennings, only tortured her more with hints of what her mistress had always feared.

Marianne's ideas remained fixed incoherently on her mother. Whenever she mentioned her name, it gave a pang to Elinor's heart. Reproaching herself for trifling with so many days of illness and wretched for lack of immediate relief, she fancied all relief might soon be in vain, that everything had been delayed too long, and pictured her suffering mother arriving too late to see this darling child or to see her rational.

She was on the point of sending for Mr. Harris again, or another doctor, when he finally arrived—not until after five o'clock. His opinion made some amends for his delay. Though acknowledging a very unexpected and unpleasant alteration in his patient, he would not allow the danger to be material. He spoke confidently of the relief a fresh mode of treatment would procure, and this confidence, in a lesser degree, was communicated to Elinor. He promised to return in three or four hours, leaving both patient and attendant more composed than he had found them.

With strong concern and many reproaches for not being called sooner, Mrs. Jennings heard in the morning of what had passed. Her former apprehensions, now restored with greater reason, left her no doubt of the event. Though trying to speak comfort to Elinor, her conviction of her sister's danger prevented her from offering hope. Her heart was truly grieved. The rapid decay and early death of a girl so young and lovely as Marianne must strike even an uninterested person with concern. On Mrs. Jennings's compassion, she had other claims: she had been Marianne's companion for three months, was still under her care, and knew how greatly injured and unhappy Marianne had been. The distress of her sister, particularly a favorite, was before her. And regarding their mother, when Mrs. Jennings considered that Marianne might be to her what Charlotte was to herself, her sympathy was very sincere.

Mr. Harris was punctual for his second visit, but he came to be disappointed in his hopes. His medicines had failed; the fever was unabated. Marianne remained in a heavy stupor, only more quiet—not more herself. Elinor, catching all his fears in a moment, proposed calling in further advice. He judged it unnecessary, claiming he had something more to try, a fresh application of which he was as confident as the last. His visit concluded with encouraging assurances that reached her ear but could not enter Elinor's heart. She was calm, except when thinking of her mother, but almost hopeless. In this state she continued until noon, scarcely stirring from her sister's bed, her thoughts wandering from one image of grief to another, her spirits oppressed by Mrs. Jennings's conversation, who scrupled not to attribute the severity of the attack to the weeks of previous indisposition caused by Marianne's disappointment. Elinor felt the reasonableness of the idea, and it added fresh misery to her reflections.

About noon, however, she began—with a dread of disappointment that kept her silent even to her friend—to fancy, to hope she could perceive a slight amendment in her sister's pulse. She waited, watched, and examined it again and again. Finally, with an agitation more difficult to bury under exterior calmness than all her foregoing distress, she ventured to communicate her hopes. Mrs. Jennings, forced by examination to acknowledge a temporary revival, tried to keep her young friend from indulging the thought of its continuance. Elinor, reciting every injunction of distrust, told herself not to hope. But it was too late. Hope had already entered, and feeling all its anxious flutter, she bent over her sister to watch—she hardly knew for what. Half an hour passed, and the favorable symptom still blessed her. Others arose to confirm it. Her breath, her skin, her lips all flattered Elinor with signs of amendment. Marianne fixed her eyes on her with a rational, though languid, gaze. Anxiety and hope now oppressed her in equal degrees, leaving her no moment of tranquility until Mr. Harris's arrival at four o'clock. His assurances and felicitations on a recovery surpassing his expectation gave her confidence, comfort, and tears of joy.

Marianne was in every respect materially better, and he declared her entirely out of danger. Mrs. Jennings, perhaps satisfied with the partial justification of her forebodings found in their recent alarm, allowed herself to trust in his judgment and admitted, with unfeigned joy and soon unequivocal cheerfulness, the probability of an entire recovery.

Elinor could not be cheerful. Her joy was of a different kind, leading to anything rather than gaiety. Marianne restored to life, health, friends, and her doting mother was an idea to fill her heart with sensations of exquisite comfort and expand it in fervent gratitude; but it led to no outward demonstrations of joy, no words, no smiles. All within Elinor's breast was satisfaction, silent and strong.

She continued by her sister's side with little intermission all afternoon, calming every fear, satisfying every inquiry of her enfeebled spirits, supplying every succour, and watching almost every look and every breath. The possibility of a relapse would occasionally remind her of her anxiety, but when she saw, on her frequent and minute examinations, that every symptom of recovery continued, and saw Marianne sink at six o'clock into a quiet, steady, and apparently comfortable sleep, she silenced every doubt.

Time was now drawing on when Colonel Brandon might be expected back. At ten o'clock, she trusted, or at least not much later, her mother would be relieved from the dreadful suspense in which she must now be traveling towards them. The Colonel, too! Perhaps scarcely less an object of pity! Oh, how slow was the progress of time that yet kept them in ignorance!

At seven o'clock, leaving Marianne still sweetly asleep, she joined Mrs. Jennings in the drawing-room for tea. She had been kept from breakfast by her fears and from dinner by their sudden reverse, so this refreshment, with such feelings of content, was particularly welcome. Mrs. Jennings would have persuaded her to rest before her mother's arrival and allow her to take her place by Marianne, but Elinor had no sense of fatigue, no capability of sleep at that moment, and was not to be kept away from her sister unnecessarily. Mrs. Jennings therefore attended her upstairs to the sick chamber to verify that all continued right, left her there to her charge and her thoughts, and retired to her own room to write letters and sleep.

The night was cold and stormy. The wind roared around the house, and the rain beat against the windows; but Elinor, all happiness within, regarded it not. Marianne slept through every blast; and the travelers—they had a rich reward in store for every present inconvenience.

The clock struck eight. Had it been ten,

Elinor recoiled, a look of genuine horror freezing her features at the sight of him. Before she could turn to flee, her hand already reaching for the door latch, he stepped forward with urgent haste. His voice, shifting rapidly from supplication to command, cut through the silence.

"Miss Dashwood, for half an hour—for ten minutes—I beg you to stay."

"No, sir," she replied, her tone steady despite the tremor in her hands. "I shall not stay. Your business cannot be with me. The servants, I suppose, forgot to inform you that Mr. Palmer is not in the house."

"Had they told me," he cried, his voice rising with vehemence, "that Mr. Palmer and every relation of his were consigned to hell, it would not have driven me from this door. My business is with you, and with you alone."

"With me!" Elinor exclaimed, her amazement bordering on disbelief. "Well, sir, be quick—and if you can, be less violent."

"Sit down, and I shall be both."

She hesitated, paralyzed by indecision. The terrifying possibility that Colonel Brandon might arrive and find her in this compromising position flashed through her mind. Yet, she had promised to listen, and her curiosity, coupled with a sense of duty, held her fast. Concluding that prudence demanded speed and that her compliance would facilitate his departure, she moved silently toward the table and sat. He took the chair opposite, and for a tense half-minute, neither spoke.

"Pray, be quick, sir," Elinor said impatiently. "I have no time to spare."

He sat in a posture of deep meditation, seemingly deaf to her urgency. Finally, he spoke abruptly.

"Your sister," he said, "is out of danger. I heard it from the servant. God be praised!—But is it true? Is it really true?"

Elinor remained silent. He repeated the question with increasing eagerness.

"For God's sake, tell me—is she out of danger, or is she not?"

"We hope she is."

He rose and paced the room, then returned to his seat with a forced liveliness. "Had I known this truth an hour ago... but since I am here, what does it signify? For once, Miss Dashwood—it may be the last time—let us be cheerful. I am in a fine mood for gaiety. Tell me honestly," a flush spreading across his cheeks, "do you think me most a knave or a fool?"

Elinor stared at him with greater astonishment than ever. She began to suspect he must be intoxicated; the strangeness of the visit and his erratic manners seemed intelligible only through that lens. With this impression, she rose.

"Mr. Willoughby, I advise you to return to Combe. I am not at leisure to remain with you longer. Whatever your business may be, it will be better recollected and explained tomorrow."

"I understand you," he replied, his voice perfectly calm and accompanied by an expressive smile. "Yes, I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was enough to unsettle me."

"At Marlborough!" Elinor cried, utterly bewildered.

"Yes. I left London this morning at eight. The only ten minutes I have spent outside my carriage since then procured me a luncheon at Marlborough."

The steadiness of his manner and the intelligence in his eyes convinced Elinor that, whatever unpardonable folly had brought him to Cleveland, it was not intoxication. After a moment's reflection, she spoke again.

"Mr. Willoughby, you ought to feel, and I certainly do, that after what has passed, your coming here in this manner, forcing yourself upon my notice, requires a very particular excuse. What is it that you mean?"

"I mean," he said with serious energy, "if I can, to make you hate me one degree less than you do now. I mean to offer an explanation, an apology for the past; to open my whole heart to you, and by convincing you that though I have always been a blockhead, I have not always been a rascal, to obtain something like forgiveness from Marianne."

"Is this the real reason of your coming?"

"Upon my soul it is," he answered, with a warmth that summoned the former Willoughby to her memory, and in spite of herself, made her believe in his sincerity.

"If that is all, you may be satisfied already; for Marianne *has*, she has *long* forgiven you."

"Has she?" he cried in the same eager tone. "Then she has forgiven me before she ought to have done. But she shall forgive me again, and on more reasonable grounds. *Now* will you listen to me?"

Elinor bowed her assent.

"I do not know," he said after a pause of expectation on her part and thoughtfulness on his, "how *you* may have accounted for my behavior to your sister, or what diabolical motive you may have imputed to me. Perhaps you will hardly think the better of me; it is worth the trial, however, and you shall hear everything. When I first became intimate in your family, I had no other intention, no other view in the acquaintance than to pass my time pleasantly while I was obliged to remain in Devonshire, more pleasantly than I had ever done before. Your sister's lovely person and interesting manners could not but please me; and her behavior to me, almost from the first, was of a kind—it is astonishing, when I reflect on what it was, and what *she* was, that my heart should have been so insensible! But at first, I must confess, my vanity only was elevated by it. Careless of her happiness, thinking only of my own amusement, giving way to feelings which I had always been too much in the habit of indulging, I endeavored, by every means in my power, to make myself pleasing to her, without any design of returning her affection."

Miss Dashwood, turning her eyes on him with angry contempt, stopped him.

"It is hardly worth while, Mr. Willoughby, for you to relate, or for me to listen any longer. Such a beginning as this cannot be followed by anything. Do not let me be pained by hearing anything more on the subject."

"I insist on you hearing the whole of it," he replied. "My fortune was never large, and I had always been expensive, always in the habit of associating with people of better income than myself. Every year since my coming of age, or even before, had added to my debts; and though the death of my old cousin, Mrs. Smith, was to set me free, that event being uncertain and possibly far distant, it had been for some time my intention to re-establish my circumstances by marrying a woman of fortune. To attach myself to your sister, therefore, was not a thing to be thought of; and with a meanness, selfishness, cruelty—which no indignant, no contemptuous look, even of yours, Miss Dashwood, can ever reprobate too much—I was acting in this manner, trying to engage her regard, without a thought of returning it. But one thing may be said for me: even in that horrid state of selfish vanity, I did not know the extent of the injury I meditated, because I did not *then* know what it was to love. But have I ever known it? Well may it be doubted; for, had I really loved, could I have sacrificed my feelings to vanity, to avarice? Or, what is more, could I have sacrificed hers? But I have done it. To avoid a comparative poverty, which her affection and her society would have deprived of all its horrors, I have, by raising myself to affluence, lost everything that could make it a blessing."

"You did then," said Elinor, a little softened, "believe yourself at one time attached to her?"

"To have resisted such attractions, to have withstood such tenderness! Is there a man on earth who could have done it? Yes, I found myself, by insensible degrees, sincerely fond of her; and the happiest hours of my life were what I spent with her when I felt my intentions were strictly honorable, and my feelings blameless. Even *then*, however, when fully determined on paying my addresses to her, I allowed myself most improperly to put off, from day to day, the moment of doing it, from an unwillingness to enter into an engagement while my circumstances were so greatly embarrassed. I will not reason here—nor will I stop for *you* to expatiate on the absurdity, and the worse than absurdity, of scrupling to engage my faith where my honor was already bound. The event has proved that I was a cunning fool, providing with great circumspection for a possible opportunity of making myself contemptible and wretched forever. At last, however, my resolution was taken, and I had determined, as soon as I could engage her alone, to justify the attentions I had so invariably paid her, and openly assure her of an affection which I had already taken such pains to display. But in the interim—in the interim of the very few hours that were to pass before I could have an opportunity of speaking with her in private—a circumstance occurred—an unlucky circumstance, to ruin all my resolution, and with it all my comfort. A discovery took place,"—here he hesitated and looked down. "Mrs. Smith had somehow or other been informed, I imagine by some distant relation, whose interest it was to deprive me of her favor, of an affair, a connection—but I need not explain myself farther," he added, looking at her with heightened color and an enquiring eye—"your particular intimacy—you have probably heard the whole story long ago."

"I have," returned Elinor, coloring likewise and hardening her heart anew against any compassion for him, "I have heard it all. And how you will explain away any part of your guilt in that dreadful business, I confess is beyond my comprehension."

"Remember," cried Willoughby, "from whom you received the account. Could it be an impartial one? I acknowledge that her situation and her character ought to have been respected by me. I do not mean to justify myself, but at the same time cannot leave you to suppose that I have nothing to urge—that because she was injured she was irreproachable, and because *I* was a libertine, *she* must be a saint. If the violence of her passions, the weakness of her understanding—I do not mean, however, to defend myself. Her affection for me deserved better treatment, and I often, with great self-reproach, recall the tenderness which, for a very short time, had the power of creating any return. I wish—I heartily wish it had never been. But I have injured more than herself; and I have injured one, whose affection for me (may I say it?) was scarcely less warm than hers; and whose mind—Oh! how infinitely superior!"

"Your indifference, however, towards that unfortunate girl—I must say it, unpleasant to me as the discussion of such a subject may well be—your indifference is no apology for your cruel neglect of her. Do not think yourself excused by any weakness, any natural defect of understanding on her side, in the wanton cruelty so evident on yours. You must have known that while you were enjoying yourself in Devonshire pursuing fresh schemes, always gay, always happy, she was reduced to the extremest indigence."

"But, upon my soul, I did *not* know it," he warmly replied. "I did not recollect that I had omitted to give her my direction; and common sense might have told her how to find it out."

"Well, sir, and what said Mrs. Smith?"

"She taxed me with the offence at once, and my confusion may be guessed. The purity of her life, the formality of her notions, her ignorance of the world—everything was against me. The matter itself I could not deny, and vain was every endeavor to soften it. She was previously disposed, I believe, to doubt the morality of my conduct in general, and was moreover discontented with the very little attention, the very little portion of my time that I had bestowed on her, in my present visit. In short, it ended in a total breach. By one measure I might have saved myself. In the height of her morality, good woman! she offered to forgive the past if I would marry Eliza. That could not be—and I was formally dismissed from her favor and her house. The night following this affair—I was to go the next morning—was spent by me in deliberating on what my future conduct should be. The struggle was great—but it ended too soon. My affection for Marianne, my thorough conviction of her attachment to me—it was all insufficient to outweigh that dread of poverty, or get the better of those false ideas of the necessity of riches, which I was naturally inclined to feel, and expensive society had increased. I had reason to believe myself secure of my present wife, if I chose to address her, and I persuaded myself to think that nothing else in common prudence remained for me to do. A heavy scene however awaited me before I could leave Devonshire;—I was engaged to dine with you on that very day; some apology was therefore necessary for my breaking this engagement. But whether I should write this apology, or deliver it in person, was a point of long debate. To see Marianne, I felt, would be dreadful, and I even doubted whether I could see her again, and keep to my resolution. In that point, however, I undervalued my own magnanimity, as the event declared; for I went, I saw her, and saw her miserable, and left her miserable—and left her hoping never to see her again."

"Why did you call, Mr. Willoughby?" said Elinor, reproachfully. "A note would have answered every purpose. Why was it necessary to call?"

"It was necessary to my own pride. I could not bear to leave the country in a manner that might lead you, or the rest of the neighborhood, to suspect any part of what had really passed between Mrs. Smith and myself—and I resolved therefore on calling at the cottage, in my way to Honiton. The sight of your dear sister, however, was really dreadful; and, to heighten the matter, I found her alone. You were all gone I do not know

For a long time after Willoughby's carriage had vanished, Elinor remained paralyzed by a chaotic storm of conflicting thoughts, all converging on a single, heavy note of sorrow. She could not summon the energy to turn her mind to her sister. The man she had despised only moments prior as the epitome of worthlessness now stirred a strange, aching pity within her. Despite his glaring faults, she found herself imagining his eternal separation from her family with a tenderness that she quickly admitted was born less of his virtues than of his desperate plight. She recognized that his hold on her mind was fueled by charms that reason demanded she ignore: his striking appearance, his open and affectionate demeanor, and that burning, albeit misguided, love for Marianne. Yet, she felt the pull of these influences long before she could muster the strength to resist them.

When she finally returned to the sleeping Marianne, she found her just awakening, revitalized by a deep, restorative slumber. Elinor's heart swelled with emotion. The weight of the past, the precariousness of the present, the memory of Willoughby's visit, Marianne's survival, and the imminent arrival of her mother created a whirlwind of agitation that banished all signs of exhaustion. Her only fear was revealing her turmoil to her sister. But this fear lasted barely half an hour before another carriage rattled up the drive. Driven by a desperate need to spare her mother further agony, Elinor rushed to the hall and met Mrs. Dashwood at the threshold, steadying her as she stumbled inside.

Mrs. Dashwood, whose terror upon approaching the house had nearly convinced her that Marianne was dead, possessed no breath for questions or greetings. Instead, Elinor, seizing the moment, poured out the joyful news. Her mother caught the words with her characteristic fervor, and in an instant, the crushing weight of fear was replaced by overwhelming elation. Supported between her daughter and Colonel Brandon, she wept tears of relief, embracing Elinor repeatedly before turning to press the Colonel's hand with a gaze that spoke of profound gratitude and the certainty that he shared in their bliss. He offered his congratulations in a silence even deeper than hers.

Once her mother regained her composure, her sole desire was to see Marianne. Within minutes, she was beside her beloved child, who had become even dearer to her through the trials of absence, suffering, and danger. Elinor's own delight at the reunion was tempered by a fear that the emotional intensity might rob Marianne of needed rest. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood, pragmatic in the face of mortal peril, remained calm. Satisfied by her mother's presence and too weak for conversation, Marianne submitted willingly to the quiet prescribed by the nurses. Mrs. Dashwood insisted on staying by her side through the night, while Elinor, yielding to her mother's plea, retired to her room.

However, the rest that a sleepless night and hours of agonizing anxiety demanded was denied her by a restless spirit. "Poor Willoughby" became a constant refrain in her thoughts. She found herself torn between a desire to hear his defense and a self-reproach for judging him so harshly before. The promise she had made to tell Marianne the truth now loomed as a terrifying prospect; she dreaded the impact it might have on her sister's fragile heart, doubting whether Marianne could ever find happiness again. For a fleeting, dark moment, she wished Willoughby were a widower. Then, recalling Colonel Brandon, she chastised herself, realizing that the reward of her sister's affection belonged to Brandon's enduring constancy, not her rival's fleeting passion, and praying fervently against the death of Mrs. Willoughby.

The shock of Colonel Brandon's mission at Barton had been significantly softened for Mrs. Dashwood by her own preceding panic. So consumed was she with worry for Marianne that she had already resolved to travel to Cleveland that very day, settling her arrangements before Brandon even arrived. Consequently, the Careys were expected any moment to escort Margaret away, as her mother refused to take her to a place potentially tainted by infection.

Marianne improved daily, and the radiant cheerfulness of Mrs. Dashwood's countenance declared her, as she repeatedly proclaimed, to be one of the happiest women alive. Elinor could not hear these declarations without occasionally wondering if her mother remembered Edward. But Mrs. Dashwood, trusting Elinor's temperate report of her own disappointment, was swept away by the exuberance of her joy, focusing solely on what would amplify it. Marianne had been rescued from a danger in which her mother's own poor judgment regarding the attachment to Willoughby had played a part; yet, in her recovery, she discovered another source of joy that Elinor had not anticipated.

It was revealed during a rare moment of privacy between them.

"At last we are alone, my Elinor. You do not yet know the full extent of my happiness. Colonel Brandon loves Marianne. He has told me so himself."

Her daughter, feeling a complex mix of pleasure and pain, surprise and lack thereof, listened in silent attention.

"You are never like me, dear Elinor; I should wonder at your composure now. If I had sat down to wish for any possible good for my family, I would have fixed on Colonel Brandon marrying one of you as the most desirable outcome. And I believe Marianne will be the happier of the two."

Elinor was half-inclined to ask for the reasoning behind this, satisfied that no argument based on an impartial assessment of their ages, characters, or feelings could support it. However, her mother was always carried away by her imagination on such subjects. Instead of an inquiry, Elinor merely smiled and let the thought pass.

"He opened his whole heart to me yesterday as we traveled. It came quite unawares, quite undesignedly. I, you may well believe, could talk of nothing but my child; he could not conceal his distress. I saw that it equaled my own, and he perhaps, thinking that mere friendship, as the world goes, would not justify such warm sympathy—or rather, not thinking at all, I suppose—gave way to irresistible feelings and made me acquainted with his earnest, tender, constant affection for Marianne. He has loved her, my Elinor, ever since the first moment he saw her."

Here, Elinor perceived—not the actual words or professions of Colonel Brandon, but the natural embellishments of her mother's active fancy, which shaped every detail to suit her delight.

"His regard for her infinitely surpasses anything Willoughby ever felt or feigned. It is far warmer, far more sincere, or whatever we are to call it. It has persisted through all knowledge of dear Marianne's unhappy infatuation for that worthless young man! Without selfishness, without encouraging false hope! Could he have seen her happy with another? Such a noble mind! Such openness, such sincerity! No one can be deceived by him."

"Colonel Brandon's character," said Elinor, "as an excellent man, is well established."

"I know it is," replied her mother seriously, "or after such a warning, I should be the last to encourage such affection, or even to be pleased by it. But his coming for me as he did, with such active, such ready friendship, is enough to prove him one of the worthiest of men."

"His character, however," answered Elinor, "does not rest on one act of kindness, to which his affection for Marianne, were humanity out of the question, would have prompted him. To Mrs. Jennings, to the Middletons, he has been long and intimately known; they equally love and respect him. Even my own knowledge of him, though recently acquired, is considerable. I value and esteem him so highly that if Marianne can be happy with him, I shall be as ready as yourself to consider our connection the greatest blessing in the world. What answer did you give him? Did you allow him to hope?"

"Oh! my love, I could not then speak of hope to him or to myself. Marianne might have been dying at that moment. But he did not ask for hope or encouragement. His was an involuntary confidence, an irrepressible effusion to a soothing friend, not an application to a parent. Yet, after a time, I did say—for at first I was quite overcome—that if she lived, as I trusted she might, my greatest happiness would lie in promoting their marriage. Since our arrival, since our delightful security, I have repeated it to him more fully, giving him every encouragement in my power. I tell him, a very little time will do everything; Marianne's heart is not to be wasted forever on such a man as Willoughby. His own merits must soon secure it."

"To judge from the Colonel's spirits, however, you have not yet made him equally sanguine."

"No. He thinks Marianne's affection is too deeply rooted for any change, even under a great length of time. And even supposing her heart were free again, he is too diffident of himself to believe that, with such a difference in age and disposition, he could ever attach her. There, however, he is quite mistaken. His age is only slightly beyond hers, making it an advantage that ensures his character and principles are fixed. His disposition, I am well convinced, is exactly the one to make your sister happy. And his person, his manners too, are all in his favor. My partiality does not blind me; he certainly is not so handsome as Willoughby; but at the same time, there is something much more pleasing in his countenance. There was always something in Willoughby's eyes at times which I did not like."

Elinor could not remember it; but her mother, without waiting for her assent, continued.

"And his manners, the Colonel's manners, are not only more pleasing to me than Willoughby's ever were, but they are of a kind I well know to be more solidly attaching to Marianne. Their gentleness, their genuine attention to others, and their manly, unstudied simplicity are much more accordant with her real disposition than the liveliness, often artificial and often ill-timed, of the other. I am very sure myself that had Willoughby turned out as really amiable as he has proved himself the contrary, Marianne would yet never have been so happy with him as she will be with Colonel Brandon."

She paused. Her daughter could not quite agree with her, but her dissent went unheard and thus caused no offense.

"At Delaford, she will be within an easy distance of me," added Mrs. Dashwood, "even if I remain at Barton. And in all probability—for I hear it is a large village—indeed there certainly must be some small house or cottage close by that would suit us quite as well as our present situation."

Poor Elinor! Here was a new scheme to send her to Delaford! But her spirit was stubborn.

"His fortune too! For at my time of life, you know, everybody cares about that; and though I neither know nor desire to know what it really is, I am sure it must be a good one."

Their conversation was interrupted by the entrance of a third person, and Elinor withdrew to think it all over in private, to wish success to her friend, and yet, in wishing it, to feel a pang for Willoughby.

Marianne's illness, though severe in its onset, had not lingered long enough to stall her recovery. Armed with her youth, innate vitality, and the devoted presence of her mother, she rallied with remarkable speed. Within four days of Mrs. Dashwood's arrival, Marianne was strong enough to transfer to Mrs. Palmer's dressing room. There, driven by an impatience to express her gratitude for the kindness that had fetched her mother, she eagerly invited Colonel Brandon to visit.

When the Colonel entered, the sight of Marianne's altered countenance and the pale hand she immediately extended to him struck Elinor with a profound emotion. It was clear to her that his reaction stemmed from something deeper than mere affection or the knowledge that his love was known; it was a resurgence of past misery, triggered by the striking resemblance between Marianne and his lost love, Eliza. The hollow eyes, the sickly complexion, and the posture of weakness brought those tragic memories flooding back, intensified by Marianne's warm acknowledgment of her debt to him.

Mrs. Dashwood, observing the scene with equal vigilance but a different perspective, interpreted the Colonel's demeanor through the lens of simple, self-evident duty. Yet, she found herself convinced that Marianne's actions and words hinted at a sentiment rising above mere gratitude.

As days passed and Marianne grew visibly stronger, the topic of returning to Barton arose. With the departure of Mrs. Jennings from Cleveland imminent, the Dashwoods' plans became fixed. Colonel Brandon, urged by the united requests of the women, accepted that his residence at Barton would be equally necessary. In a gesture of reciprocal hospitality, he agreed to provide his carriage for their journey, ensuring his sick charge would travel comfortably. He also pledged to visit the cottage within a few weeks, a promise made with genuine pleasure.

The day of departure finally arrived. Marianne took a prolonged and heartfelt leave of Mrs. Jennings, her gratitude laced with a newfound respect for the woman she had previously neglected.
