She bid Colonel Brandon farewell with the warmth of a true friend. Assisted gently by him into the carriage, she ensured she occupied at least half of the space, a testament to her lingering frailty. Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed, leaving the others to their quiet farewells and the dullness of parting.

The journey to Barton spanned two days. Marianne endured the travel with surprising resilience, free from essential fatigue. Every effort of her companions to ensure her comfort was met with her calm acceptance. For Elinor, witnessing this composure was a source of immense relief. Having watched her sister suffer in silence for weeks, burdened by anguish she lacked the courage to voice, Elinor now rejoiced in Marianne's apparent mental stability. She trusted that this serenity, born of serious reflection, would eventually lead to lasting cheerfulness.

As they neared Barton, the landscape itself seemed to summon painful memories. Marianne grew silent, turning her face away from the windowless interior to gaze earnestly through the glass. Elinor felt only pity for this natural emotion, which, in its quiet dignity, deserved praise. Upon entering the sitting room, Marianne's demeanor shifted with resolute firmness. She scanned the room as if determined to acclimate herself to the sight of everything connected to Willoughby. She spoke little, yet every sentence was crafted for cheerfulness; when a sigh escaped, it was invariably followed by a smile.

After dinner, she attempted to play the piano. However, her eyes rested upon a score of an opera procured by Willoughby, containing their favorite duets and inscribed with her name. "That will not do," she declared, shaking her head. She set the music aside, feigned feeble fingers, and closed the instrument, vowing firmly that she would practice much in the future.

The following morning brought no diminution of this hopeful spirit. Strengthened by rest, she spoke with genuine animation, anticipating Margaret's return and the restoration of their family circle. "When the weather settles," she said, outlining a rigorous plan of daily walks to the farm, Sir John's plantations, and the old Priory ruins. "I shall rise by six and divide every moment between music and reading," she continued, detailing a schedule of serious study to cure her previous idleness. "By reading six hours a day, I shall gain in a year the instruction I so desperately lack."

Elinor admired the nobility of this plan, though she smiled sadly at the familiar spark of eager fantasy that now directed it toward virtuous ends rather than languid complaint. Her smile faded into a sigh as she remembered the unfulfilled promise to Willoughby. She feared that revealing the truth would shatter Marianne's newfound tranquility. Resolving to wait until her sister's health was fully secure, Elinor intended to delay the difficult conversation. Yet, resolution proved fragile.

It took a few days for the weather to soften sufficiently for an invalid to venture out. Finally, a gentle morning broke, tempting Marianne to walk. Leaning on Elinor's arm, she was permitted to stroll along the lane before the house. They had barely advanced far enough to see the prominent hill behind the cottage when Marianne paused, pointing with a steady hand.

"There, exactly there," she said, her voice dropping. "On that projecting mound—I fell; and there I first saw Willoughby."

She revived quickly, adding, "I am thankful to find I can look upon the spot with so little pain. Shall we ever talk of that subject, Elinor? Or is it wrong? I *can* talk of it now, I hope, as I ought."

Elinor tenderly encouraged her openness. "As for regret," Marianne continued, "I have done with that regarding him. I do not mean to recount my past feelings, but what they are *now*. If I could be satisfied on one point—if I could believe he was not *always* acting a part, not *always* deceiving me; if I could be assured he was not so wicked as my fears imagined, especially concerning that unfortunate girl—"

She stopped. Elinor, seizing the moment, answered joyfully, "If you could be assured of that, you would find peace."

"Yes. My peace is doubly involved. It is horrible to suspect someone who has been so to me of such designs, but what must it make me appear to myself? Only a shamefully unguarded affection could expose me to such ruin."

"How then," Elinor asked gently, "would you account for his behavior?"

"I would suppose him... Oh, how gladly would I suppose him, only fickle. Very, very fickle."

Elinor said nothing, debating internally whether to reveal the truth immediately or wait for stronger health. They walked in silence for a few minutes.

"I am not wishing him too much good," Marianne sighed at last. "I only hope his secret reflections are no more unpleasant than my own. He will suffer enough in them."

"Do you compare your conduct with his?"

"No. I compare it with what it *ought* to have been; I compare it with yours."

"Our situations have borne little resemblance."

"They have borne more than our conduct. Do not let your kindness defend what your judgment must censure. My illness has made me think. It has given me leisure for serious recollection. Long before I was recovered enough to speak, I was able to reflect. I saw in my own behavior since last autumn nothing but a series of imprudence towards myself and a lack of kindness to others. I saw that my own feelings prepared my suffering, and my lack of fortitude nearly led me to the grave. My illness was entirely brought on by my own negligence. Had I died, it would have been self-destruction. I did not know my danger until it was removed, yet I wonder at my recovery—I wonder that the very eagerness of my desire to live, to atone to God and to you, did not kill me at once. Had I died, in what peculiar misery should I have left you, my nurse, my friend, my sister! You, who had seen the fretful selfishness of my latter days! How should I have lived in your remembrance? And my mother! How could you have consoled her? I cannot express my abhorrence of myself. Whenever I looked to the past, I saw duty neglected or failing indulged. Everyone seemed injured by me. I repaid Mrs. Jennings's kindness with ungrateful contempt. To the Middletons, the Palmers, the Steeles, to every acquaintance, I was insolent and unjust, with a heart hardened against their merits and a temper irritated by their attention. Even to John and Fanny, I gave less than their due. But you, above all, had been wronged by me. I knew your heart and its sorrows, yet did it influence me? Not to compassion. Your example was before me, but to what avail? Was I more considerate? Did I imitate your forbearance? No. Not less when I knew you were unhappy than when I thought you at ease, did I turn away from every exertion of duty or friendship. I scarcely allowed sorrow to exist but with me, regretting only that heart which had deserted and wronged me, leaving you, for whom I professed an unbounded affection, to be miserable for my sake."

Her rapid flow of self-reproach ceased. Elinor, too honest to flatter but eager to soothe, instantly offered the praise and support Marianne's frankness and contrition deserved. Marianne pressed her hand. "You are very good. The future must be my proof. I have laid down my plan. If I am capable of adhering to it, my feelings shall be governed and my temper improved. They shall no longer worry others nor torture myself. I shall now live solely for my family. You, my mother, and Margaret, must henceforth be all the world to me. From you and my home, I shall never again have the smallest incitement to move. If I mix in other society, it will be only to show that my spirit is humbled, my heart amended, and that I can practice the civilities of life with gentleness. As for Willoughby—to say I shall soon or ever forget him would be idle. His remembrance can be overcome by no change of circumstance. But it shall be regulated, checked by religion, by reason, by constant employment."

She paused, adding in a low voice, "If I could but know *his* heart, everything would become easy."

Elinor, who had been weighing the propriety of revealing the truth, heard this and realized that reflection had yielded nothing; resolution must act. She found herself leading to the fact. She managed the recital with address, preparing her anxious listener with caution. She related simply and honestly the chief points of Willoughby's apology, doing justice to his repentance while softening only his protestations of present regard.

Marianne said not a word. She trembled, her eyes fixed on the ground, her lips turning whiter than sickness had left them. A thousand inquiries surged in her heart, yet she dared not utter one. She caught every syllable with panting eagerness; her hand, unknowingly to herself, pressed tightly against her sister's, and tears covered her cheeks.

Dreading her exhaustion, Elinor led her toward home. Until they reached the cottage door, they spoke only of Willoughby and their conversation, Elinor being meticulously minute in every detail where safety allowed. As soon as they entered, Marianne, with a kiss of gratitude and two words barely audible through her tears—"Tell mama"—withdrew from her sister and walked slowly upstairs. Elinor, respecting the solitude Marianne now sought, turned into the parlour to fulfill her parting injunction, her mind anxiously pre-arranging the result, ready to revive the subject again should Marianne fail to do so.

Mrs. Dashwood listened to Willoughby's defense with a heart that refused to remain unmoved. She rejoiced that he was cleared of some of the heavy guilt imputed to him; she felt genuine sorrow for him, and she wished him happiness. Yet, the past could not be erased. No amount of vindication could restore him to Marianne with a faith unbroken or a character unblemished. Nothing could undo the pain she had suffered through his actions, nor wash away the stain of his conduct toward Eliza. Consequently, he could never regain her former esteem, nor could his innocence harm the standing of Colonel Brandon.

Had Mrs. Dashwood, like her daughter, heard Willoughby's tale directly from his lips—had she witnessed his distress and been swayed by the pathos of his manner—her compassion might have been deeper. But it was neither in Elinor's power nor her wish to stir such feelings in another through her second-hand account as she had initially felt herself. Reflection had brought calmness to her judgment and sobered her opinion of Willoughby's worth. She wished only to state the simple truth, laying bare facts inherent to his character without any embellishment of tenderness that might mislead the imagination.

That evening, when the three were gathered together, Marianne voluntarily brought up the subject again. It required effort; the restless, unquiet thoughtfulness that had plagued her for days was evident in her demeanor, as was the rising flush on her cheeks and the unsteadiness of her voice.

"I wish to assure you both," she said, "that I see everything—as you can desire me to do."

Mrs. Dashwood would have interrupted her instantly with soothing tenderness, had not Elinor, wishing to hear her sister's unbiased opinion, signaled her to hold her tongue. Marianne continued slowly.

"It is a great relief to me," she said. "What Elinor told me this morning—I have now heard exactly what I wished to hear." For a moment, her voice failed her, but recovering herself, she added with greater calm than before, "I am now perfectly satisfied. I wish for no change. I never could have been happy with him, after knowing, as I must have known sooner or later, all this. I should have had no confidence, no esteem. Nothing could have done it away to my feelings."

"I know it—I know it," cried her mother. "Happy with a man of libertine practices! With one who so injured the peace of the dearest of our friends and the best of men! No—my Marianne has not a heart to be made happy with such a man! Her conscience, her sensitive conscience, would have felt all that the conscience of her husband ought to have felt."

Marianne sighed and repeated, "I wish for no change."

"You consider the matter," said Elinor, "exactly as a good mind and a sound understanding must consider it. And I dare say you perceive, as well as myself, not only in this but in many other circumstances, reason enough to be convinced that your marriage must have involved you in many certain troubles and disappointments, in which you would have been poorly supported by an affection on his side, much less certain. Had you married, you must have been always poor. His expensiveness is acknowledged even by himself, and his whole conduct declares that self-denial is a word hardly understood by him. His demands and your inexperience together, on a small, very small income, must have brought on distresses which would not be the less grievous to you from having been entirely unknown and unthought of before. Your sense of honour and honesty would have led you, I know, when aware of your situation, to attempt all the economy that would appear to you possible: and perhaps, as long as your frugality retrenched only on your own comfort, you might have been suffered to practice it, but beyond that—and how little could the utmost of your single management do to stop the ruin which had begun before your marriage? Beyond that, had you endeavoured, however reasonably, to abridge his enjoyments, is it not to be feared that instead of prevailing on feelings so selfish to consent to it, you would have lessened your own influence on his heart and made him regret the connection which had involved him in such difficulties?"

Marianne's lips quivered, and she repeated the word "Selfish?" in a tone that implied, "Do you really think him selfish?"

"The whole of his behaviour," replied Elinor, "from the beginning to the end of the affair, has been grounded on selfishness. It was selfishness which first made him sport with your affections; which afterwards, when his own were engaged, made him delay the confession of it, and which finally carried him from Barton. His own enjoyment, or his own ease, was, in every particular, his ruling principle."

"It is very true. My happiness never was his object."

"At present," continued Elinor, "he regrets what he has done. And why does he regret it? Because he finds it has not answered towards himself. It has not made him happy. His circumstances are now unembarrassed—he suffers from no evil of that kind; and he thinks only that he has married a woman of a less amiable temper than yourself. But does it follow that had he married you, he would have been happy? The inconveniences would have been different. He would then have suffered under the pecuniary distresses which, because they are removed, he now reckons as nothing. He would have had a wife of whose temper he could make no complaint, but he would have been always necessitous—always poor; and probably would soon have learned to rank the innumerable comforts of a clear estate and good income as of far more importance, even to domestic happiness, than the mere temper of a wife."

"I have not a doubt of it," said Marianne; "and I have nothing to regret—nothing but my own folly."

"Rather say your mother's imprudence, my child," said Mrs. Dashwood; "she must be answerable."

Marianne would not let her proceed; and Elinor, satisfied that each felt their own error, wished to avoid any survey of the past that might weaken her sister's spirits. She therefore, pursuing the first subject, immediately continued, "One observation may, I think, be fairly drawn from the whole of the story—that all Willoughby's difficulties have arisen from the first offence against virtue in his behaviour to Eliza Williams. That crime has been the origin of every lesser one, and of all his present discontents."

Marianne assented most feelingly to the remark, and her mother was led by it to an enumeration of Colonel Brandon's injuries and merits, warm as friendship and design could unitedly dictate. Her daughter, however, did not look as if much of it were heard by her.

Elinor, according to her expectation, saw on the two or three following days that Marianne did not continue to gain strength as she had done; but while her resolution was unsubdued and she still tried to appear cheerful and easy, her sister could safely trust to the effect of time upon her health.

Margaret returned, and the family were again restored to each other, quietly settled at the cottage. If they were not pursuing their usual studies with quite so much vigour as when they first came to Barton, they at least planned a vigorous prosecution of them in the future.

Elinor grew impatient for some tidings of Edward. She had heard nothing of him since leaving London, nothing new of his plans, nothing certain even of his present abode. Some letters had passed between her and her brother in consequence of Marianne's illness; and in the first of John's, there had been this sentence: "We know nothing of our unfortunate Edward, and can make no enquiries on so prohibited a subject, but conclude him to be still at Oxford." This was all the intelligence of Edward afforded her by the correspondence, for his name was not even mentioned in any of the succeeding letters. She was not doomed, however, to be long in ignorance of his measures.

Their man-servant had been sent one morning to Exeter on business. When, as he waited at table, he had satisfied the inquiries of his mistress as to the event of his errand, this was his voluntary communication:

"I suppose you know, ma'am, that Mr. Ferrars is married."

Marianne gave a violent start, fixed her eyes upon Elinor, saw her turning pale, and fell back in her chair in hysterics. Mrs. Dashwood, whose eyes, as she answered the servant's inquiry, had intuitively taken the same direction, was shocked to perceive by Elinor's countenance how much she really suffered, and a moment afterwards, alike distressed by Marianne's situation, knew not on which child to bestow her principal attention.

The servant, who saw only that Miss Marianne was taken ill, had sense enough to call one of the maids, who, with Mrs. Dashwood's assistance, supported her into the other room. By that time, Marianne was rather better, and her mother leaving her to the care of Margaret and the maid, returned to Elinor, who, though still much disordered, had so far recovered the use of her reason and voice as to be just beginning an inquiry of Thomas as to the source of his intelligence. Mrs. Dashwood immediately took all that trouble on herself; and Elinor had the benefit of the information without the exertion of seeking it.

"Who told you that Mr. Ferrars was married, Thomas?"

"I see Mr. Ferrars myself, ma'am, this morning in Exeter, and his lady too, Miss Steele as was. They was stopping in a chaise at the door of the New London Inn, as I went there with a message from Sally at the Park to her brother, who is one of the post-boys. I happened to look up as I went by the chaise, and so I see directly it was the youngest Miss Steele; so I took off my hat, and she knew me and called to me, and inquired after you, ma'am, and the young ladies, especially Miss Marianne, and bid me I should give her compliments and Mr. Ferrars's, their best compliments and service, and how sorry they was they had not time to come on and see you, but they was in a great hurry to go forwards, for they was going further down for a little while, but howsever, when they come back, they'd make sure to come and see you."

"But did she tell you she was married, Thomas?"

"Yes, ma'am. She smiled, and said how she had changed her name since she was in these parts. She was always a very affable and free-spoken young lady, and very civil behaved. So, I made free to wish her joy."

"Was Mr. Ferrars in the carriage with her?"

"Yes, ma'am, I just see him leaning back in it, but he did not look up;—he never was a gentleman much for talking."

Elinor's heart could easily account for his not putting himself forward; and Mrs. Dashwood probably found the same explanation.

"Was there no one else in the carriage?"

"No, ma'am, only they two."

"Do you know where they came from?"

"They come straight from town, as Miss Lucy—Mrs. Ferrars told me."

"And are they going farther westward?"

"Yes, ma'am—but not to bide long. They will soon be back again, and then they'd be sure and call here."

Mrs. Dashwood now looked at her daughter; but Elinor knew better than to expect them. She recognised the whole of Lucy in the message and was very confident that Edward would never come near them. She observed in a low voice to her mother that they were probably going down to Mr. Pratt's, near Plymouth.

Thomas's intelligence seemed over. Elinor looked as if she wished to hear more.

"Did you see them off, before you came away?"

"No, ma'am—the horses were just coming out, but I could not bide any longer; I was afraid of being late."

"Did Mrs. Ferrars look well?"

"Yes, ma'am, she said how she was very well; and to my mind she was always a very handsome young lady—and she seemed vastly contented."

Mrs. Dashwood could think of no other question, and Thomas and the tablecloth, now alike needless, were soon afterwards dismissed. Marianne had already sent to say that she should eat nothing more. Mrs. Dashwood's and Elinor's appetites were equally lost, and Margaret might think herself very well off that, with so much uneasiness as both her sisters had lately experienced, so much reason as they had often had to be careless of their meals, she had never been obliged to go without her dinner before.

When the dessert and the wine were arranged, and Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor were left by themselves, they remained long together in a similarity of thoughtfulness and silence. Mrs. Dashwood feared to hazard any remark and ventured not to offer consolation. She now found that she had erred in relying on Elinor's representation of herself; and justly concluded that everything had been expressly softened at the time to spare her from an increase of unhappiness, suffering as she then had suffered for Marianne. She found that she had been misled by the careful, considerate attention of her daughter to think the attachment, which once she had so well understood, much slighter in reality than she had been wont to believe, or than it was now proved to be. She feared that under this persuasion she had been unjust, inattentive, nay, almost unkind to her Elinor—that Marianne's affliction, because more acknowledged and more immediately before her, had too much engrossed her tenderness and led her away to forget that in Elinor she might have a daughter suffering almost as much, certainly with less self-provocation and greater fortitude.

The agony of anticipation had finally yielded to the sharp, clear sting of reality. Elinor understood now the chasm between expecting a blow and receiving it. While Edward remained unmarried, her heart had clung to a fragile hope—a secret prayer that fate might intervene, that some timely resolution or better opportunity would spare her sister-in-law and preserve her own peace. But that hope was extinguished. He was married. The news struck her with a violence that made her condemn the lingering flattery in her soul, the foolish belief that she had ever truly wished for his happiness if it meant losing him.

It surprised her, initially, that the wedding had occurred so swiftly, before he could even be ordained or possess the living. Yet, logic quickly followed the shock. Lucy, driven by her own frantic need to secure him, had surely overlooked every obstacle save the fear of delay. They were wed in London and were now hastening toward her uncle's estate. Elinor tried to imagine the scene: Edward, standing within four miles of Barton, hearing Lucy's message, seeing her mother's servant. The image was unbearable.

She pictured their new home at Delaford—a place that simultaneously fascinated and repelled her. In her mind's eye, she saw Lucy as the bustling architect of their domestic life, balancing a desire for smart appearances with ruthless frugality, eager to court the favor of Colonel Brandon, Mrs. Jennings, and every wealthy acquaintance. As for Edward, she saw nothing she wished to see. Whether he was happy or miserable, it mattered not; she turned her gaze away from every sketch of him.

Days passed without a letter. Elinor blamed the silence on the thoughtlessness or indolence of their London connections. Her mother, sensing her daughter's restlessness, asked why she hadn't written to Colonel Brandon. "I did last week," her mother replied, hoping to see him arrive soon. This offered a sliver of comfort; Brandon *must* have news.

Scarcely had Elinor resolved this when a figure on horseback appeared at the window. A gentleman stopped at the gate. Was it Brandon? No, the air and height were wrong. Could it be Edward? She looked again. He had dismounted. There was no mistake. It was Edward. She moved away, sitting down, steeling herself. "He comes from Mr. Pratt's to see us," she told herself. "I will be calm. I will be mistress of myself."

Moments later, she realized her mother and Marianne had seen the error. They exchanged anxious glances, their faces flushing with distress. Elinor wanted to speak, to assure them that no coldness would be shown, but her throat was closed. She was forced to leave their behavior to chance.

Silence filled the room as Edward's footsteps approached. When he entered, his countenance was pale with agitation, his eyes avoiding hers, as if he feared rejection. Mrs. Dashwood, striving to follow Elinor's unspoken lead, offered a forced smile, took his hand, and wished him joy. Edward stammered an unintelligible reply. Elinor's lips had moved in unison with her mother's, and afterward, she regretted not shaking his hand herself. Instead, she sat down and spoke of the weather, her voice a hollow shield against the storm inside.

Marianne hid herself in the shadows, while Margaret, confused by the partial truth, sat rigidly distant. Then, an awful pause descended upon them, broken only by Mrs. Dashwood's desperate attempt to inquire about Mrs. Ferrars. Edward answered hurriedly that she was well.

Elinor, summoning every ounce of courage, asked, "Is Mrs. Ferrars at Longstaple?"

"At Longstaple!" Edward exclaimed, surprised. "No, my mother is in town."

"I meant," Elinor said, picking up her work, her voice trembling slightly, "to enquire for Mrs. *Edward* Ferrars."

She dared not look up, but her mother and Marianne turned their eyes sharply to him. Edward flushed, looking perplexed and doubtful. After a hesitation, he said, "Perhaps you mean—my brother—you mean Mrs.—Mrs. *Robert* Ferrars."

"Mrs. Robert Ferrars!" the cry rang out from Marianne and her mother in tones of sheer amazement. Even Elinor, unable to speak, stared at him with impatient wonder. Rising abruptly, Edward walked to the window, fumbling with a pair of scissors, snipping the sheath to pieces as he spoke in a hurried rush.

"Perhaps you do not know... you may not have heard that my brother is lately married to—to the youngest—to Miss Lucy Steele."

The news echoed through the room with unspeakable astonishment, leaving everyone breathless except Elinor. She sat with her head bowed over her work, her heart hammering so violently she felt detached from her body.

"Yes," he continued, "they were married last week, and are now at Dawlish."

Elinor could endure it no longer. She rushed from the room. The moment the door closed behind her, she collapsed into tears of pure, overwhelming joy, a release she feared would never cease. Edward, who had been staring anywhere but at her, saw her flee. Perhaps he saw her emotion, or perhaps he heard it; for immediately after, he fell into a deep reverie, impervious to his mother's inquiries or affectionate words. Finally, without a word, he left the room and walked out toward the village, leaving the others in a state of bewildered confusion, trying to piece together the sudden and wonderful shift in their fortunes.

Edward was free. The circumstances of his release, though unaccountable to the rest of the family, were a certainty to him, and the purpose of that freedom was immediately clear to all: having endured the misery of one imprudent engagement contracted without his mother's consent, nothing less than the immediate formation of another could be expected. His errand to Barton was singularly simple; he was there to ask Elinor to marry him. Yet, despite his experience in such matters, he felt a profound discomfort, needing encouragement and fresh air to summon the courage for the moment.

How quickly he resolved himself, how swiftly an opportunity arose, and how he was received, need not be detailed. By four o'clock, three hours after his arrival, he had secured his lady, won his mother's consent, and stood not merely as a rapturous lover but as one of the happiest men alive. His joy was exceptional; he possessed the triumph of accepted love, but also the relief of being released from an entanglement that had long been his misery, a union with a woman he no longer loved. Elevated instantly to a security he had once deemed impossible, he moved from despair to happiness, and the change was evident in a genuine, flowing cheerfulness his friends had never seen before.

His heart was now open to Elinor. With philosophical dignity, he confessed his weaknesses and treated his first boyish attachment to Lucy Steele as a youthful folly born of ignorance and idleness. "It was a foolish, idle inclination on my side," he explained, attributing it to a lack of employment when removed from Mr. Pratt's care at eighteen. "Had my mother given me some active profession... I should very soon have outgrown the fancied attachment." Instead, he had returned home to complete idleness, spending his time at Longstaple where he felt at home, unaware of the defects in Lucy until he saw others. "Considering everything," he concluded, "it was not at the time an unnatural or an inexcusable piece of folly."

The transformation in the Dashwood household was palpable. Mrs. Dashwood was too happy to be comfortable, struggling to praise Elinor enough without wounding Edward's delicacy. Marianne spoke her happiness only through tears, her joy sincere but lacking the language to express it fully. But Elinor? Her feelings were a tumultuous storm. From the moment she learned Lucy was married to another, she had been anything but tranquil. Yet, when the second moment passed, seeing Edward honorably released and instantly declaring an affection as tender and constant as she had always hoped, she was overwhelmed by her own felicity. It took several hours for her spirits to settle into a serene tranquility.

Edward remained at the cottage for at least a week, for lovers require time to exhaust the subjects of the past, present, and future. Their conversations turned inevitably to Lucy's marriage, a circumstance Elinor found utterly baffling. How could Robert be drawn to marry a girl of whom he had never spoken admiringly, a girl already engaged to his brother? To Elinor's reason, it was a complete puzzle. Edward suggested that vanity and flattery had led them on, perhaps initially with the hope of securing Robert's good offices. He admitted he had no prior knowledge, having remained at Oxford with no means of hearing news until Lucy's letter arrived, bursting upon him with wonder, horror, and joy.

He handed Elinor the letter, written by Lucy Ferrars herself:

Being very sure I have long lost your affections, I have thought myself at liberty to bestow my own on another... I scorn to accept a hand while the heart was another's. Sincerely wish you happy in your choice... Your brother has gained my affections entirely, and as we could not live without one another, we are just returned from the altar... I have burnt all your letters, and will return your picture the first opportunity. Please to destroy my scrawls—but the ring with my hair you are very welcome to keep."

Elinor read it silently. Edward remarked that he would have burned such a letter in former days, but now, in a sister, it was bad enough; in a wife, it was a source of blushes. "Since the first half year of our foolish business," he noted, "this is the only letter I ever received from her of which the substance made me any amends for the defect of the style."

Elinor observed that Mrs. Dashwood had brought upon herself an appropriate punishment; by bribing Robert with a thousand-a-year pension out of resentment toward Edward, she had inadvertently enabled the very match she had sought to prevent. Edward agreed that Mrs. Dashwood would be more hurt by Robert's marriage to Lucy, her favorite, and would likely forgive him sooner.

Edward knew little of his family's current state, having left Oxford immediately upon receiving Lucy's letter. He had traveled with one object: the nearest road to Barton. He did not expect a cruel reception from Elinor, despite his past jealousy of Colonel Brandon and his modesty regarding his own worth. He spoke of his intentions prettily, leaving the details of a year hence to the imagination of husbands and wives.

Elinor and Edward discussed Lucy's deception. It was clear to Elinor that Lucy had meant to deceive, going off with a flourish of malice. Edward, now enlightened on her character, believed her capable of the utmost meanness. He recalled that he had only offered her the option to continue the engagement because, in his desperate situation, he had assumed her insistence on sharing his fate stemmed from disinterested affection. He could not comprehend why she would remain bound to a man she did not regard, possessing only two thousand pounds, unaware that Colonel Brandon might provide a living. Elinor countered that she might have hoped for a change in Edward's family fortunes, and that remaining engaged had gained her consideration among friends. Edward conceded that nothing could have been more natural than her conduct.

Elinor gently scolded Edward for his imprudence in spending so much time at Norland while feeling his own inconstancy. "Your behaviour was certainly very wrong," she said, noting that their relations had been led to expect what was impossible given his situation. Edward pleaded ignorance of his own heart, claiming he felt safe because his faith was plighted to another. "I felt that I admired you, but I told myself it was only friendship," he admitted, realizing too late that comparing himself to Lucy had changed everything.

News arrived that Colonel Brandon was expected at the cottage. Edward wished to be better acquainted with him and to convince him that he no longer resented the living of Delaford. Now, he felt astonished that he had never visited the place; he owed all his knowledge of the house and parish to Elinor, who had listened with such attention to Colonel Brandon's descriptions.

One difficulty remained: money. Edward had two thousand pounds, Elinor one, and the Delaford living, totaling three hundred and fifty pounds a year. It was insufficient for comfort, and Mrs. Dashwood could not advance anything. Edward hoped for a favorable change in his mother's attitude, but Elinor feared Robert's offense would only enrich Fanny.

Four days after Edward's arrival, Colonel Brandon appeared, completing Mrs. Dashwood's satisfaction. Edward retained the privilege of the first comer, while Colonel Brandon walked nightly to his old quarters at the Park, returning early enough to interrupt the lovers' tête-à-tête before breakfast. A three-week residence at Delaford, where he calculated the disparity between thirty-six and seventeen, brought him to Barton in a mood needing Marianne's kindness and her mother's encouragement to become cheerful. Among such friends, he revived. No rumor of Lucy's marriage had reached him; he knew nothing of what had passed, and his first hours were spent in wonder. Mrs. Dashwood explained everything, and he rejoiced in having promoted Elinor's interest.

The gentlemen advanced in good opinion of each other as their acquaintance deepened. Their shared principles and manners would have united them in friendship, but their mutual love for two sisters made that regard inevitable and immediate. Letters from town, once sources of transport for Elinor, now arrived to be read with mirth. Mrs. Jennings wrote of the wonderful tale, venting indignation against the jilting girl and pity for poor Mr. Edward, whom she believed was broken-hearted at Oxford. She recounted how Lucy had borrowed all her money to make a show, leaving Nancy destitute, and how Lucy's refusal to take them in the chaise was worse than all. She urged Elinor to send for Edward and have Marianne comfort him.

Mr. Dashwood's strains were more solemn. He viewed Mrs. Ferrars as the most unfortunate of women and considered the existence of each under such a blow with grateful wonder. Robert's offense was unpardonable, but Lucy's was infinitely worse. Neither should ever be mentioned to Mrs. Ferrars; if she forgave her son, his wife should never be acknowledged. The secrecy of the affair heightened the crime in his eyes. He called on Elinor to regret that Lucy's engagement with Edward had not been fulfilled, fearing she would spread misery further. He proposed sending a letter to Edward urging a submission to his mother.

"A letter of proper submission!" Edward repeated. "Would they have me beg my mother's pardon for Robert's ingratitude to her, and breach of honour to me? I can make no submission. I am grown neither humble nor penitent... I am grown very happy; but that would not interest."

"You may certainly ask to be forgiven," Elinor suggested, "because you have offended;—and I should think you might _now_ venture so far as to profess some concern for having ever formed the engagement which drew on you your mother's anger."

He agreed he might. Elinor added that once forgiven, a little humility might be convenient when acknowledging a second engagement, which she deemed almost as imprudent in her mother's eyes as the first. Edward resisted the idea of a written submission, preferring verbal concessions. Thus, it was resolved that instead of writing to Fanny, he should go to London and personally intreat her good offices. Marianne, in her new character of candour, noted that if they truly interested themselves in reconciliation, even John and Fanny were not entirely without merit.

After a brief stay of three or four days, the two gentlemen quitted Barton together. They were to proceed immediately to Delaford, so Edward might gain personal knowledge of his future home and assist his patron in planning improvements. From there, after staying a couple of nights, he would journey to town.

After a spirited and unyielding resistance on Mrs. Ferrars's part—a defense so fierce and steadfast that it shielded her from the dreaded accusation of being "too amiable"—Edward was finally granted an audience. He was once more pronounced her son.

The Ferrars household had recently undergone a turbulent fluctuation in its composition. For years, Mrs. Ferrars had been blessed with two sons; yet the recent annihilation of Edward had deprived her of one, and the prior loss of Robert had left her bereft of both for a fortnight. Now, through Edward's resuscitation, she possessed one again.

Yet, despite this restoration, Edward did not feel his existence secure until he revealed his current engagement. He feared that the mere publication of his intention to marry Miss Dashwood might shock his constitution, hastening his demise as swiftly as his previous crisis. With apprehensive caution, he disclosed the news, only to be met with an unexpected calmness. Mrs. Ferrars initially attempted to dissuade him, marshaling every argument in her arsenal. She urged him toward Miss Morton, a woman of higher rank and greater fortune, emphasizing that Miss Morton was the daughter of a nobleman with thirty thousand pounds, whereas Miss Dashwood was merely the daughter of a private gentleman with a modest three thousand. When she realized that, while acknowledging the truth of her assertions, Edward was unmoved by them, she deemed it wiser, based on past experience, to submit. After an ungracious delay born of her own dignity, designed to prevent any suspicion of goodwill, she issued her decree of consent to the marriage of Edward and Elinor.

The next matter to be settled was the augmentation of their income. Here, it became plain that although Edward was now her only son, he was by no means her eldest. While Robert was inevitably endowed with a thousand pounds a year, no objection was raised against Edward taking holy orders for a mere two hundred and fifty at the utmost. Beyond the ten thousand pounds already given with Fanny, nothing further was promised, either for the present or the future. This was, however, precisely what Edward and Elinor desired, and more than they had expected. Indeed, Mrs. Ferrars herself, through her shuffling excuses, seemed the only one surprised that she did not offer more.

Secured with an income sufficient for their needs, they awaited only the completion of the Parsonage, which Colonel Brandon was eagerly improving to accommodate Elinor. After enduring the usual thousand disappointments and delays caused by the unaccountable dilatoriness of the workmen, Elinor broke through her first firm resolution to postpone marriage until everything was perfect. The ceremony took place in Barton church early in the autumn.

Their first month of wedded life was spent at the Mansion-house with their friend, allowing them to superintend the progress of the Parsonage and direct every detail from afar. Mrs. Jennings's prophecies, though somewhat jumbled, were largely fulfilled; by Michaelmas, she was able to visit her new daughter-in-law and found in Elinor and her husband one of the happiest couples in the world. They lacked for nothing but the marriage of Colonel Brandon and Marianne, and perhaps better pasturage for their cows.

They were visited on their first settling by nearly all their relations and friends. Mrs. Ferrars came to inspect the happiness she had authorized with some shame, and even the Dashwoods undertook a journey from Sussex to pay their respects.

"I will not say that I am disappointed, my dear sister," said John, as they walked together one morning before the gates of Delaford House. "That would be saying too much, for certainly you have been one of the most fortunate young women in the world, as it is. But, I confess, it would give me great pleasure to call Colonel Brandon brother. His property here, his place, his house, everything is in such respectable and excellent condition! And his woods—I have not seen such timber anywhere in Dorsetshire as there is now standing in Delaford Hanger! And though, perhaps, Marianne may not seem exactly the person to attract him, yet I think it would altogether be advisable for you to have them stay frequently with you. As Colonel Brandon seems so much at home, nobody can tell what may happen; for when people are much thrown together and see little of anyone else, opportunities arise. You may as well give her a chance; you understand me."

Though Mrs. Ferrars did come to see them, treating them with a make-believe of decent affection, they were never insulted by her real favor or preference. That was reserved for the folly of Robert and the cunning of his wife, who earned it within months. The selfish sagacity of the latter, which had initially drawn Robert into his scrape, became the principal instrument of his deliverance. Her respectful humility, assiduous attentions, and endless flatteries, seizing the smallest opening for their exercise, reconciled Mrs. Ferrars to his choice and re-established him completely in her favor.

The entire course of Lucy's behavior in the affair, and the prosperity that crowned it, stands as an encouraging instance of what earnest, unceasing attention to self-interest can achieve, even when progress appears obstructed. It secures every advantage of fortune with no sacrifice but that of time and conscience. When Robert first sought her acquaintance in Bartlett's Buildings, it was with the view imputed to him by his brother: merely to persuade her to break her engagement. Expecting that one or two interviews would settle the matter, he erred in his assumption. Though Lucy soon gave him hopes that his eloquence would convince her in time, another visit, another conversation, was always required to produce this conviction. Doubts lingered in her mind upon parting, removable only by another half-hour of discourse. Thus, his attendance was secured, and the rest followed in course. Instead of speaking of Edward, they gradually spoke only of Robert—a subject on which he had always more to say, and in which she soon betrayed an interest equal to his own. It became speedily evident to both that he had entirely supplanted his brother. He was proud of his conquest, proud of tricking Edward, and very proud of marrying privately without his mother's consent.

What immediately followed is known. They passed some months in great happiness at Dawlish, where she had many relations and old acquaintances to court, and he drew several plans for magnificent cottages. Returning to town, they procured the forgiveness of Mrs. Ferrars by the simple expedient of asking it, an action adopted at Lucy's instigation. The forgiveness, at first, as was reasonable, comprehended only Robert. Lucy, who had owed her mother no duty and therefore could have transgressed none, remained unpardoned for some weeks longer. But perseverance in humility, in self-condemnation for Robert's offense, and in gratitude for the unkindness she was treated with, eventually procured her the haughty notice that overcame her by its graciousness. This led, by rapid degrees, to the highest state of affection and influence. Lucy became as necessary to Mrs. Ferrars as either Robert or Fanny. While Edward was never cordially forgiven for having once intended to marry her, and Elinor, though superior in fortune and birth, was spoken of as an intruder, *she* was in everything considered, and always openly acknowledged, as a favorite child. They settled in town, received very liberal assistance from Mrs. Ferrars, and were on the best terms imaginable with the Dashwoods. Setting aside the jealousies and ill-will continually subsisting between Fanny and Lucy, in which their husbands of course took a part, as well as the frequent domestic disagreements between Robert and Lucy themselves, nothing could exceed the harmony in which they all lived together.

What Edward had done to forfeit the right of the eldest son might have puzzled many to find out; and what Robert had done to succeed to it might have puzzled them still more. It was an arrangement, however, justified in its effects, if not in its cause. Nothing ever appeared in Robert's style of living or talking to give suspicion that he regretted the extent of his income, as either leaving his brother too little or bringing himself too much. If Edward might be judged from the ready discharge of his duties, from an increasing attachment to his wife and home, and from the regular cheerfulness of his spirits, he might be supposed no less contented with his lot, no less free from every wish of exchange.

Elinor's marriage divided her as little from her family as could well be contrived without rendering the cottage at Barton entirely useless, for her mother and sisters spent much more than half their time with her. Mrs. Dashwood acted on motives of policy as well as pleasure in the frequency of her visits to Delaford; for her wish of bringing Marianne and Colonel Brandon together was hardly less earnest, though rather more liberal, than what John had expressed. It was now her darling object. Precious as the company of her daughter was to her, she desired nothing so much as to give up its constant enjoyment to her valued friend. To see Marianne settled at the mansion-house was equally the wish of Edward and Elinor. They each felt his sorrows, and their own obligations, and Marianne, by general consent, was to be the reward of all.

With such a confederacy against her—with a knowledge so intimate of his goodness—with a conviction of his fond attachment to herself, which at last, though long after it was observable to everybody else, burst upon her—what could she do?

Marianne Dashwood was born to an extraordinary fate. She was born to discover the falsehood of her own opinions and to counteract, by her conduct, her most favorite maxims. She was born to overcome an affection formed so late in life as at seventeen, with no sentiment superior to strong esteem and lively friendship, voluntarily to give her hand to another! And *that* other, a man who had suffered no less than herself under the event of a former attachment, whom, two years before, she had considered too old to be married, and who still sought the constitutional safeguard of a flannel waistcoat!

But so it was. Instead of falling a sacrifice to an irresistible passion, as once she had fondly flattered herself with expecting, instead of remaining forever with her mother and finding her only pleasures in retirement and study as she had later determined in her more calm and sober judgment, she found herself at nineteen submitting to new attachments, entering on new duties, placed in a new home, a wife, the mistress of a family, and the patroness of a village.

Colonel Brandon was now as happy as all those who best loved him believed he deserved to be. In Marianne he was consoled for every past affliction; her regard and her society restored his mind to animation and his spirits to cheerfulness. That Marianne found her own happiness in forming his was equally the persuasion and delight of each observing friend. Marianne could never love by halves, and her whole heart became, in time, as much devoted to her husband as it had once been to Willoughby.

Willoughby could not hear of her marriage without a pang, and his punishment was soon afterwards complete in the voluntary forgiveness of Mrs. Smith. By stating his marriage with a woman of character as the source of her clemency, she gave him reason for believing that had he behaved with honor toward Marianne, he might at once have been happy and rich. That his repentance of misconduct, which thus brought its own punishment, was sincere, need not be doubted; nor that he long thought of Colonel Brandon with envy and of Marianne with regret. But that he was forever inconsolable, that he fled from society, or contracted a habitual gloom of temper, or died of a broken heart, must not be depended on—for he did neither. He lived to exert and frequently to enjoy himself. His wife was not always out of humor, nor his home always uncomfortable; and in his breed of horses and dogs, and in sporting of every kind, he found no inconsiderable degree of domestic felicity.

For Marianne, however, in spite of his incivility in surviving her loss, he always retained that decided regard which interested him in everything that befell her, making her his secret standard of perfection in woman. Many a rising beauty would be slighted by him in after-days as bearing no comparison with Mrs. Brandon.

Mrs. Dashwood was prudent enough to remain at the cottage without attempting a removal to Delaford. Fortunately for Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, when Marianne was taken from them, Margaret had reached an age highly suitable for dancing, and not very ineligible for being supposed to have a lover.

Between Barton and Delaford, there was that constant communication which strong family affection would naturally dictate. Among the merits and the happiness of Elinor and Marianne, let it not be ranked as the least considerable that, though sisters and living almost within sight of each other, they could live without disagreement between themselves or producing coolness between their husbands.
